ABOUT THIS VOLUME

This is volume 3 of five, and it continues the catalogue at Chapter 120.

The front matter and Part One, the general account of the neuron, are given in Volume 1 and are not repeated here.

The tags and flags used throughout are explained in Volume 1, and are summarised again in one line each below.

• (PUC) marks a Previously Unnamed Construct: a neuron type that is real, functionally necessary and relied upon in the literature, but which had no accepted proper name until this work gave it one.

• (COG) marks a type involved in cognition, and (NON-COG) marks a type that is not.


PART EIGHT: CEREBELLUM - COORDINATION AND TIMING (CONTINUED FROM VOLUME 2)

CHAPTER 117. GLOBULAR CELLS (Feedback-Loop Wake-Boosting Neuron) (COG)

Six Purkinje cells hold this one small neuron pinned to the floor, and only a surge of noradrenaline can lift it.

Globular records only that the cell body is round, which teaches nothing about what the cell does.

Feedback-Loop names the one connection that most cleanly distinguishes it, because it receives strong monosynaptic inhibition from the recurrent axon collaterals of the cortex's own output cells, so it hears an echo of the message the cerebellar cortex has just sent.

Wake-Boosting names its other established property, excitation by monoamines such as serotonin and noradrenaline, the chemicals that report the animal's behavioural state.

Neuron marks it as a granular layer interneuron whose role in the circuit is, beyond these two facts, genuinely not established, and the name deliberately claims nothing more.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The globular cell is a small inhibitory interneuron that sits just beneath the Purkinje cell layer and spends almost all its life silent.

It was separated from the Lugaro cell only in 2007, and much of what is known about it comes from a handful of studies, so this is one of the least understood named neurons in the human brain.

Its distinctive job is to report on the cerebellar cortex's own output.

It receives direct inhibitory input from about six Purkinje cells, so whenever the cortex is speaking loudly, the globular cell is held down.

When Purkinje cells fall quiet, or when noradrenaline arrives from the locus coeruleus during arousal, the globular cell fires and inhibits basket cells, stellate cells and Golgi cells across a wide transverse strip.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is small and round, which is where the name comes from, and it is measurably rounder than the spindle-shaped Lugaro cell, with a shape index around 0.56 to 0.63.

It sits within about 10 micrometres of the lower border of the Purkinje cell layer, essentially touching it.

An average of 4.8 processes emerge from the soma, most of them spreading sideways along the Purkinje cell layer and a few climbing up into the molecular layer.

The axon travels long distances in the transverse direction, which distinguishes it immediately from the basket cell, whose axon runs in the sagittal plane.

SIZE:

The soma is on the order of 8 to 12 micrometres across, making it one of the smaller cerebellar interneurons, clearly smaller than a Golgi cell.

For comparison, small Golgi cells recorded in the same preparations had capacitances of 19.8 plus or minus 1.4 picofarads and large Golgi cells 32.8 plus or minus 3.8 picofarads, and globular cells sit at the low end of that scale.

The dendritic processes are short, typically under 100 micrometres.

The axon, by contrast, spreads several hundred micrometres to over a millimetre transversely, so like the Lugaro cell this is a tiny cell with a wide reach.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No accepted subtypes exist, and this is a genuine gap rather than a settled finding.

The literature is still resolving whether globular cells are a single type or the small end of a continuum that includes small fusiform Lugaro cells, because the two share the glycine transporter 2 marker and both are negative for metabotropic glutamate receptor type 2 and neurogranin.

One reported chemical division is that a subset is calretinin positive, calretinin being a calcium-binding protein used as a marker.

A simulation should model globular cells as one type with wide parameter variance rather than as several types.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The uppermost granular layer of the cerebellar cortex, within about 10 micrometres of the interface with the Purkinje cell layer, and in some sections apparently inside that layer.

That interface position is the whole story of the cell, because it places the soma directly in the path of Purkinje cell axon collaterals as they descend.

Globular cells have been described throughout the cerebellar cortex, in vermis and hemispheres, though systematic mapping across all lobules I to X has not been done.

Their axons run transversely through the granular layer and send branches to targets in the granular layer and the molecular layer, so they too bridge the two.

NUMBER:

On the order of 1 to 2 million in the human cerebellum, and confidence is low.

The estimate comes from the finding that globular cells represent at least 15 percent of granule cell layer interneurons in the mouse, combined with an estimate of roughly 10 million Golgi cells and 1 million Lugaro cells in the human.

One to two million is about 0.002 percent of the 69.03 billion cerebellar neurons and about 0.0017 percent of all brain neurons.

The honest bound is 0.5 to 4 million, and no human count of any kind has been published, so this figure should be flagged as provisional in any model.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Globular cells are silent at rest, with no spontaneous firing recorded in any of the cells tested, which sets them apart from small Golgi cells that idle at 2.9 plus or minus 1.0 hertz.

Serotonin induces firing in most cells but only weakly, taking them from 0.0 hertz to about 2.1 plus or minus 0.8 hertz.

Noradrenaline is the powerful driver, facilitating firing in five of six cells tested and taking two previously silent cells to maximum rates of 12.7 and 14.0 hertz.

This is the mirror image of the Lugaro cell, which responds strongly to serotonin and not at all to noradrenaline, and the two cells therefore divide the cerebellum's neuromodulatory labour between them.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The dominant input is monosynaptic inhibition from Purkinje cell axon collaterals, arriving as a barrage of inhibitory currents at about 53.1 plus or minus 4.3 hertz with amplitudes of 84.8 plus or minus 8.3 picoamperes.

The delay between a Purkinje cell spike and the resulting current is under 2 milliseconds, confirming a direct connection, and about 6 Purkinje cells converge on each globular cell.

Excitatory input arrives from mossy fibres, at a much lower spontaneous rate of about 2.0 plus or minus 0.6 hertz and a smaller amplitude of 16.2 plus or minus 1.8 picoamperes.

Outputs go to basket cells and stellate cells in the sagittal direction and to Golgi cells in the transverse direction, so a single globular cell inhibits both the molecular layer and the granular layer inhibitory systems.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin, unmyelinated, and oriented transversely, which is the opposite of the basket cell and the same axis as the parallel fibres.

Its transverse reach means it aligns with the parallel fibre beam rather than with the Purkinje cell fan, so it can act on a whole beam of activated cortex.

Terminals are varicose and symmetrical, the classic appearance of an inhibitory ending.

Conduction is slow, in the range of tenths of a metre per second, consistent with a modulatory rather than a time-critical role.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Globular cells are inhibitory and use GABA, and they are positive for glycine transporter 2 and glutamic acid decarboxylase 67, so they are best described as mixed glycinergic and GABAergic.

Some studies identify them as calretinin-positive GABAergic neurons, and the discrepancy between the glycinergic and purely GABAergic descriptions is an unresolved point in the literature that a modeller should represent as a mixing parameter.

They are negative for metabotropic glutamate receptor type 2 and negative for neurogranin, the two markers that identify Golgi cells.

On the receiving side the key receptors are GABA type A receptors for Purkinje cell input, AMPA receptors for mossy fibre input, and alpha-1 adrenergic receptors for the noradrenaline that switches them on.

MYELINATION:

None.

The globular cell axon is unmyelinated over its entire transverse course, as is true of every cerebellar cortical interneuron.

The absence of myelin here is expected rather than surprising: the axon never leaves the cortex, its longest run is around a millimetre, and its function is spatially broad suppression rather than precisely timed delivery.

This is a positive finding from ultrastructural work, not a gap in the data.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very low, and this is the most economical neuron in this batch after the granule cell.

A cell that is silent at rest, held down by six Purkinje cells, and fires at most in the low teens of hertz spends almost nothing on action potentials.

Its major standing cost is handling the constant barrage of about 53 inhibitory events per second, which means continuous chloride extrusion by the potassium chloride cotransporter, itself powered indirectly by adenosine triphosphate.

Because the cell is so small, total consumption remains trivial compared with a Purkinje cell.

PLASTICITY:

Largely unstudied, and this must be stated plainly rather than filled in by analogy.

The one established dynamic property is short-term plasticity at its inputs: the Purkinje cell collateral synapse shows paired-pulse facilitation of 1.18 plus or minus 0.04 at a 10 millisecond interval, meaning the second of two rapid Purkinje spikes inhibits harder than the first.

The mossy fibre input, by contrast, shows paired-pulse depression, meaning the second of two rapid excitatory events is weaker.

No long-term potentiation or depression has been demonstrated at any globular cell synapse, so a simulation should model long-term plasticity as absent and short-term plasticity as present with those measured signs.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing uniquely specialised has been described for the glia surrounding globular cells, and that plain statement is the accurate one.

The cells sit at the granular layer border, so they are wrapped by the same velate astrocytes that sheath glomeruli and by the descending shafts of Bergmann glia.

Because their principal excitatory drive is noradrenaline arriving by volume transmission, astrocytic noradrenaline transporters and adrenergic receptors in the surrounding tissue will shape how much reaches the cell, exactly as serotonin clearance shapes the Lugaro cell.

Beyond that, no globular-cell-specific glial specialisation has been reported, and a modeller should not invent one.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the globular cell and the cerebellar cortex loses a specific piece of self-knowledge: the ability to detect its own silence and act on it.

Because six Purkinje cells hold it down and it inhibits the inhibitory interneurons, the globular cell fires precisely when Purkinje output collapses, and its firing then removes inhibition from Purkinje cells and helps them recover.

Concretely, the region would lose an automatic recovery reflex, the mechanism that catches the cortex when it falls silent and pushes it back towards its baseline.

The tissue would still work, but it would be slower to return from a strong pause, and arousal would no longer be able to reset the balance between excitation and inhibition in a transverse beam.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The globular cell is a disinhibitory element, which means its practical effect is to increase activity in the cells two steps downstream even though everything it does is inhibitory.

Its principal excitatory drive is a neuromodulator arriving by volume transmission rather than a wired synapse, so like the Lugaro cell it converts a chemical concentration into a firing rate.

Its co-release of GABA and glycine means the identity of the receiving cell determines the size of the effect, since Golgi cells respond to the glycinergic component while Purkinje cells do not.

It closes a genuine feedback loop with the Purkinje cell, receiving direct collateral inhibition and indirectly modulating Purkinje output through molecular layer interneurons, which makes it part of a control system rather than a relay.

Its transverse axon aligned with the parallel fibre beam means it can act on the same geometric population that a burst of granule cell activity just excited, an example of computation by axonal orientation.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Purkinje cell axon collateral inhibition, about 6 sources, event rate about 53 hertz, amplitude about 85 picoamperes, with paired-pulse facilitation of 1.18 at 10 milliseconds.

◦ Mossy fibre excitation, spontaneous event rate about 2 hertz, amplitude about 16 picoamperes, with paired-pulse depression.

◦ Noradrenaline concentration, treated as a scalar 0 to 1, strong effect.

◦ Serotonin concentration, treated as a scalar 0 to 1, weak effect.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Mixed GABA and glycine release onto basket and stellate cells in the sagittal direction and onto Golgi cells in the transverse direction, at 0 to 14 hertz.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, chloride load from the inhibitory barrage, short-term facilitation factor for Purkinje input, short-term depression factor for mossy fibre input.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential about minus 62 millivolts, threshold about minus 44 millivolts, spontaneous rate 0 hertz, maximum noradrenaline-driven rate about 14 hertz, maximum serotonin-driven rate about 2 hertz, Purkinje convergence 6.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane about 20 milliseconds, inhibitory current decay about 5 to 10 milliseconds, paired-pulse facilitation window about 10 to 50 milliseconds, noradrenaline rise and fall in tissue hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ No human disease is known to target globular cells selectively, which should be recorded as a known unknown rather than as evidence of robustness.

◦ Any condition that abolishes Purkinje cell firing, including severe spinocerebellar ataxia, will release globular cells from their dominant inhibition and drive them tonically, an effect that has not been measured but follows directly from the wiring.

◦ Drugs acting on alpha-1 adrenergic receptors, including some antihypertensives and antipsychotics, will alter globular cell drive.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to work out when a globular cell escapes Purkinje cell inhibition and fires, given a noradrenaline level and a Purkinje cell firing rate.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -62.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called collateral probability with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called facilitation with initial value 1.
Set collateral probability to the product of 6 and the quotient of the product of the Purkinje rate and 0.5 divided by 1000.
Repeat the following steps exactly 8000 times.
    Set drive to the sum of the product of 20 and the noradrenaline level and the product of 8 and the serotonin level.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the quotient of the sum of drive and the difference of -62 and membrane voltage divided by 40.
    Check if a random number between zero and one is less than collateral probability.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to the difference of membrane voltage and the product of 0.9 and facilitation.
        Set facilitation to 1.18.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set facilitation to 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -44.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to -65.
        Set spike count to the sum of spike count and 1.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

// JavaScript was chosen because it runs in any browser with no build step, which suits a teaching demonstration of this feedback loop.
let seed = 1;
function rnd() { seed = (seed * 16807) % 2147483647; return seed / 2147483647; }  // reproducible
const DT = 0.5, TAU = 20.0;                       // milliseconds
const V_REST = -62.0, V_TH = -44.0, V_RESET = -65.0;   // millivolts
const NA_DRIVE = 20.0;    // millivolts at saturating noradrenaline
const HT_DRIVE = 8.0;     // serotonin pushes far more weakly than noradrenaline
const PC_IPSP = 0.9;      // millivolts removed by one Purkinje collateral event
const N_PC = 6;           // about six Purkinje cells innervate one globular cell
function run(noradrenaline, serotonin, pcRateHz) {
  let v = V_REST, spikes = 0, facilitation = 1.0;
  const steps = 8000;                                    // 4 seconds
  const pPerStep = N_PC * pcRateHz * DT / 1000.0;        // at most one event per step here
  for (let i = 0; i < steps; i++) {
    const drive = NA_DRIVE * noradrenaline + HT_DRIVE * serotonin;
    v += DT * ((V_REST - v) + drive) / TAU;
    v += (rnd() - 0.5) * 0.6;                            // synaptic background noise
    if (rnd() < pPerStep) {                              // a Purkinje collateral event lands
      v -= PC_IPSP * facilitation;
      facilitation = 1.18;                               // paired pulse facilitation
    } else {
      facilitation = 1.0;
    }
    if (v >= V_TH) { v = V_RESET; spikes++; }
  }
  return (spikes / (steps * DT / 1000.0)).toFixed(1);
}
console.log("no monoamine, Purkinje cells at 50 Hz:", run(0, 0, 50), "Hz");
console.log("serotonin only, Purkinje cells at 50 Hz:", run(0, 1, 50), "Hz");
console.log("noradrenaline, Purkinje cells at 50 Hz:", run(1, 0, 50), "Hz");
console.log("noradrenaline, Purkinje cells silenced:", run(1, 0, 0), "Hz");

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is a small round cell pressed against the underside of the Purkinje cell layer, round rather than spindle-shaped, which is the single feature that separates it from the Lugaro cell beside it.

It is silent at rest and stays silent when you add serotonin, then fires when you add noradrenaline, which is the exact opposite of its neighbour.

Its axon runs transversely while the basket cell axon it might be confused with runs sagittally.

And it is the cerebellar neuron most completely owned by the Purkinje cell, wired to about six of them and pinned down by 53 inhibitory events every second.

CHAPTER 118. CANDELABRUM CELLS (COG)

Wedged like a slice of cheese between two Purkinje cell bodies, this neuron sends up a row of vertical branches that look exactly like a many-armed candlestick.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The candelabrum cell is an inhibitory interneuron of the Purkinje cell layer, described by Lainé and Axelrad in 1994 and characterised physiologically only in 2022.

It turns out to be the most abundant interneuron in the Purkinje cell layer, present in every cerebellar lobule, and yet it was missing from textbook diagrams of the cerebellum for a quarter of a century.

Its function is disinhibition, which means it increases activity by removing inhibition.

It is excited by mossy fibres and granule cells, strongly inhibited by Purkinje cells, and its own output goes mainly onto molecular layer interneurons.

Because those interneurons inhibit Purkinje cells, the net effect of a candelabrum cell firing is that Purkinje cells fire faster, so this cell is an amplifier built entirely out of brakes.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The soma is small and pear-shaped, oriented vertically, and squeezed in a sandwich-like manner between the Purkinje cell bodies themselves.

One or two thick dendrites rise from the upper pole of the cell body and run through the entire thickness of the molecular layer, dividing into a few slightly oblique branches that can reach the pia mater at the surface.

These ascending dendrites are covered with irregularly distributed spines.

Some slimmer dendrites leave the lower part of the cell body and spread tortuously for a short distance in the upper granular layer, so the cell samples both layers.

The axon is the namesake: a thick initial segment leaves the soma or a proximal dendrite, then winds horizontally through or just above the Purkinje cell layer, giving off a row of vertically oriented beaded branches that ascend through most of the molecular layer, exactly like the arms of a candelabrum.

SIZE:

The soma is small, roughly 8 to 12 micrometres in its long axis, which is a fraction of the 33 micrometre Purkinje cell body it is wedged against.

The ascending dendrites traverse the full molecular layer, which is 300 to 500 micrometres thick in the human cerebellum, so the cell is tall and extremely thin.

The horizontal axonal trunk runs for a few hundred micrometres before and while it throws up its vertical arms.

The cell has a very high input resistance, which for a neuron is a direct consequence of small size, and that high resistance is functionally the most important number about it.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No named subtypes exist.

Single-nucleus ribonucleic acid (RNA) sequencing shows candelabrum cells forming a single molecularly distinct cluster, clearly separate from molecular layer interneurons, Golgi cells and Purkinje cells.

The cells are uniformly distributed through all cerebellar folia, with no evidence of the parasagittal striping that divides Purkinje cells.

There is variation in the number of ascending dendrites, one or two, and in how far the axon travels, but neither variation has been shown to define a subtype.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The Purkinje cell layer itself, physically interleaved between Purkinje cell somata, which makes it a resident of the thinnest layer in the nervous system.

That position is an interface by definition: the Purkinje cell layer is the boundary between the molecular layer and the granular layer, and the candelabrum cell has dendrites in both, plus a soma in the boundary.

Candelabrum cells are present in all cerebellar lobules I to X and in all folia examined, across vermis, paravermis and hemispheres.

Their axons remain within the molecular layer and Purkinje cell layer of the same and immediately neighbouring folia.

NUMBER:

On the order of 10 to 20 million in the human cerebellum, with low confidence.

The estimate comes from the finding that candelabrum cells are the most abundant Purkinje cell layer interneuron in the mouse, at densities roughly comparable to Purkinje cells themselves, combined with the well-measured human figure of 15 million Purkinje cells.

Fifteen million would be about 0.022 percent of the 69.03 billion cerebellar neurons and about 0.017 percent of all brain neurons.

No human count exists, and a defensible bound is 5 to 30 million, so this should be treated as an order of magnitude claim.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The defining electrical property is very high input resistance, which means that a tiny synaptic current produces a large voltage swing.

The practical consequence is that candelabrum cell firing is exquisitely sensitive to synaptic input, so a handful of granule cell spikes can drive it and a handful of Purkinje cell spikes can silence it.

Candelabrum cells fire at low to moderate rates and are strongly and reliably suppressed by Purkinje cell activity.

Because Purkinje cells fire tonically at tens of hertz, the candelabrum cell spends most of its time held near silence, and it becomes active mainly when granule cell drive is strong or Purkinje output dips.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory inputs come from mossy fibres, reaching the basal dendrites in the upper granular layer, and from granule cells, reaching the spiny ascending dendrites in the molecular layer.

The dominant inhibitory input is from Purkinje cells, and this inhibition is strong enough to dominate the cell's behaviour.

Outputs go primarily to molecular layer interneurons, the stellate and basket cells, with a smaller projection onto Purkinje cells themselves.

The circuit motif is therefore granule cell to candelabrum cell to molecular layer interneuron to Purkinje cell, a three-step path in which two inhibitory steps cancel to produce excitation.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon begins with an unusually thick initial segment and may emerge from a dendrite rather than the soma, which is rare and diagnostic.

It runs horizontally through or just above the Purkinje cell layer, then sends up a series of parallel vertical beaded branches into the molecular layer.

Those branches are unmyelinated and studded with varicosities, so transmitter is released along their whole ascending length rather than at a single terminal.

The vertical geometry means one candelabrum cell contacts interneurons at every depth of the molecular layer along one narrow line, which is the opposite of the parallel fibre's horizontal sweep.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Candelabrum cells are GABAergic, releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid, confirmed both electrophysiologically and by their molecular expression profile.

They form a molecularly distinct cluster in single-nucleus RNA sequencing, expressing a combination of inhibitory neuron genes that does not match Golgi cells, Lugaro cells or molecular layer interneurons.

On the receiving side they carry AMPA receptors for granule cell and mossy fibre input and GABA type A receptors for Purkinje cell input.

Their high input resistance means each of these receptor populations exerts a disproportionately large voltage effect for its size.

MYELINATION:

None.

Candelabrum cell axons remain entirely within the cerebellar cortex and are unmyelinated along their horizontal trunk and all their ascending arms.

This is required by the cell's design, because the ascending arms release transmitter continuously along their length from beaded varicosities, and a myelin sheath would insulate exactly the membrane that must be exposed.

The absence of myelin here is an established ultrastructural finding, not a gap in the record.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low.

The cell is small, fires at modest rates, and is held quiet much of the time by Purkinje cell inhibition.

Its main standing cost is maintaining a long, thin, highly branched axonal arbor whose surface area is large relative to the soma, so mitochondria are found along the ascending arms.

Nothing about candelabrum cell metabolism has been shown to be unusual, and a simulation can treat its energy budget as that of a generic small cortical interneuron.

PLASTICITY:

Essentially unstudied, and it would be dishonest to present a mechanism as established.

The synapses from granule cells onto candelabrum cells are of the same anatomical class as granule cell synapses onto other interneurons, which do show long-term potentiation, so plasticity is plausible but unproven here.

What is established is that the cell's very high input resistance means that small changes in synaptic strength produce large changes in output, so whatever plasticity exists will have an outsized effect.

A modeller should implement plasticity at these synapses as an optional, disabled-by-default feature and flag it as speculative.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The candelabrum cell soma sits in the Purkinje cell layer wedged among Bergmann glial cell bodies, and its ascending dendrites and axonal arms run alongside Bergmann glial fibres for their whole length through the molecular layer.

That means this cell's entire receptive and output surface is bathed in the glutamate-clearing field of Bergmann glia, which express very high levels of the glutamate aspartate transporter.

Because candelabrum cells are driven by granule cell glutamate and have such high input resistance, the efficiency of Bergmann glial glutamate uptake sets how much of a parallel fibre burst the candelabrum cell actually sees.

No candelabrum-cell-specific glial specialisation has been described beyond this shared Bergmann glial environment.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the candelabrum cell and the cerebellar cortex loses its brake on its own brake.

Molecular layer interneurons would inhibit Purkinje cells without any counterweight, so strong granule cell input would suppress Purkinje output instead of shaping it, and the sign of the cortex's response to a strong input could invert.

Concretely, the region would lose its gain control at high input levels, the mechanism that stops feedforward inhibition from swamping the output when the granular layer is very active.

The cortex would still work at low activity and would choke at high activity, which in behaviour would look like coordination that fails exactly when a movement becomes fast or complex.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The candelabrum cell is the cerebellum's clearest disinhibitory circuit element, and disinhibition is a computation, not a wiring accident: it converts an inhibitory population into a gain controller.

Its extremely high input resistance makes it operate near the limit where individual synaptic events matter, so its behaviour is dominated by fluctuations rather than by averages, and a rate-based model will systematically misrepresent it.

Its axon originating from a dendrite means the site of spike initiation is displaced away from the soma, so dendritic input on that particular branch has privileged access to the output.

Its vertically oriented axonal arms sample the molecular layer at every depth along a single line, which is a geometric strategy for contacting interneurons that receive from parallel fibres of every age and depth at once.

It sits inside a closed loop with the Purkinje cell, and because that loop contains an even number of inhibitory steps in one path and an odd number in another, the same cell can push Purkinje firing up or down depending on which path dominates.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Granule cell and parallel fibre excitation onto spiny ascending dendrites, 0 to 300 hertz per fibre.

◦ Mossy fibre excitation onto basal dendrites in the upper granular layer, 0 to 500 hertz in bursts.

◦ Purkinje cell inhibition, 20 to 200 hertz, dominant and reliable.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GABA onto molecular layer interneurons, stellate and basket cells, at 0 to 60 hertz, distributed at all depths of the molecular layer along a narrow vertical line.

◦ A smaller direct GABAergic projection onto Purkinje cells.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, synaptic conductance for excitation and inhibition, output rate.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Input resistance high, in the range of one to several gigaohms in recording conditions, granule cell weight low, Purkinje cell weight high, resting potential about minus 60 millivolts, threshold about minus 45 millivolts.

◦ In a rate model, gain about 2.2, granule weight about 0.25, Purkinje weight about 0.15, weight onto molecular layer interneurons about 0.9.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds, excitatory synaptic decay 1 to 3 milliseconds, inhibitory synaptic decay 5 to 15 milliseconds, loop settling time about 50 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ No candelabrum-cell-specific disease is known, which reflects how recently the cell was characterised rather than any established resistance to disease.

◦ Loss of candelabrum cells in a model produces excess feedforward inhibition of Purkinje cells at high input rates, predicting incoordination that worsens with movement speed.

◦ Because the cell is dominated by Purkinje cell inhibition, any disease that silences Purkinje cells will release candelabrum cells and further suppress molecular layer interneurons, a secondary effect that has not been measured in patients.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to settle the granule cell, candelabrum cell, molecular layer interneuron and Purkinje cell loop and show how much the candelabrum cell disinhibits the Purkinje cell.
Create a number variable called candelabrum rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called interneuron rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called purkinje rate with initial value 50.
Create a number variable called candelabrum target with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called interneuron target with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called purkinje target with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 600 times.
    Set candelabrum target to the product of 2.2 and the difference of the product of granule rate and 0.25 and the product of purkinje rate and 0.15.
    Check if candelabrum target is less than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set candelabrum target to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set interneuron target to the difference of the product of granule rate and 0.6 and the product of candelabrum rate and 0.9.
    Check if interneuron target is less than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set interneuron target to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set purkinje target to the difference of the sum of 50 and the product of granule rate and 0.7 and the product of interneuron rate and 0.8.
    Check if purkinje target is less than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set purkinje target to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set candelabrum rate to the sum of candelabrum rate and the quotient of the difference of candelabrum target and candelabrum rate divided by 15.
    Set interneuron rate to the sum of interneuron rate and the quotient of the difference of interneuron target and interneuron rate divided by 15.
    Set purkinje rate to the sum of purkinje rate and the quotient of the difference of purkinje target and purkinje rate divided by 15.
End the loop.
Display purkinje rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python was chosen because this is a small loop of coupled firing rates and Python keeps the sign of every arrow visible.
DT = 1.0            # milliseconds per step
TAU = 15.0          # how fast each rate follows its drive, milliseconds
CC_GAIN = 2.2       # the high input resistance of a candelabrum cell shows up as a high gain
W_GC_CC = 0.25      # granule cell drive onto the candelabrum cell
W_PC_CC = 0.15      # Purkinje collateral inhibition onto the candelabrum cell
W_GC_MLI = 0.60     # granule cell drive onto molecular layer interneurons
W_CC_MLI = 0.90     # candelabrum inhibition of molecular layer interneurons
W_MLI_PC = 0.80     # molecular layer inhibition of the Purkinje cell
W_GC_PC = 0.70      # parallel fibre excitation of the Purkinje cell
PC_PACE = 50.0      # a Purkinje cell fires at 50 Hz with no input at all
def run(cc_present, granule_rate):
    cc = mli = 0.0
    pc = PC_PACE
    for _ in range(600):                      # 600 ms, long enough to settle
        cc_target = CC_GAIN * max(0.0, W_GC_CC * granule_rate - W_PC_CC * pc)
        if not cc_present:
            cc_target = 0.0                   # simulate deleting the candelabrum cell
        mli_target = max(0.0, W_GC_MLI * granule_rate - W_CC_MLI * cc)
        pc_target = max(0.0, PC_PACE + W_GC_PC * granule_rate - W_MLI_PC * mli)
        cc += DT * (cc_target - cc) / TAU
        mli += DT * (mli_target - mli) / TAU
        pc += DT * (pc_target - pc) / TAU
    return round(cc, 1), round(mli, 1), round(pc, 1)
for rate in (0.0, 100.0, 300.0):
    with_cc = run(True, rate)
    without_cc = run(False, rate)
    print("granule rate", rate, "Hz")
    print("  with candelabrum cell, rates CC/MLI/PC:", with_cc)
    print("  without candelabrum cell, rates CC/MLI/PC:", without_cc)

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron squeezed edgewise between Purkinje cell bodies like a slice of cheese in a sandwich.

Its axon runs sideways and then throws up a row of evenly spaced vertical arms, a shape no other neuron in the body produces, and once you have seen a candelabrum you cannot mistake it.

It is the only cerebellar cell whose main job is to switch off the cells that switch off the Purkinje cell.

And it is the most abundant interneuron in the thinnest layer of the nervous system, a cell that hid in plain sight between the two most famous neurons in neuroscience for a hundred and thirty years.

CHAPTER 119. UNIPOLAR BRUSH CELLS (UBCS) (COG)

This neuron shakes hands with one incoming fibre, then keeps talking about that handshake for a whole second.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A unipolar brush cell (UBC) is a small excitatory neuron of the cerebellum's input layer, excitatory meaning it makes its targets more likely to fire.

Its job is time: it stretches a few milliseconds of input from a mossy fibre, one of the cerebellum's two great input cables, into hundreds of milliseconds of output spread across hundreds of granule cells.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Its one short dendrite ends in a tuft of twenty to fifty fine tips that looks like a shaving brush, buried inside a single mossy fibre rosette, the swollen bead ending an incoming cable.

Its axon branches nearby into rosettes of its own, so the cell manufactures new mossy fibres inside the cortex.

SIZE:

The cell body is nine to twelve micrometres across, a tenth the width of a human hair, with a capacitance of ten to twenty picofarads and an axon rarely longer than two millimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• ON cells carry metabotropic glutamate receptor type 1 alpha (mGluR1 alpha), a slow chemical detector rather than a fast pore, and answer with a delayed burst.

• OFF cells carry mGluR2 and mGluR3, which open potassium leaks, so they answer with a pause.

• Intermediate cells carry both and pause before accelerating, giving the population a continuum of delays.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the granular layer, densest in the nodulus (lobule ten), ventral uvula (lobule nine c) and flocculus, moderate in ventral paraflocculus and lingula, nearly absent from the hemispheres.

Those lobules form the vestibulocerebellum, the interface where inner ear balance fibres meet cerebellar tissue, and the same cell reappears in the dorsal cochlear nucleus.

NUMBER:

No human count exists, so rodent ratios must serve: these cells roughly equal Purkinje cells in number and outnumber Golgi cells three to one.

Against fifteen million human Purkinje cells that gives fifteen to thirty million, bounded at ten to fifty million, moderate confidence in the order of magnitude, three hundredths of one percent of cerebellar neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

It is a quiet pacemaker at one to thirty spikes per second whose input resistance is so high that a single synapse dominates it.

Its signature is a current tail lasting hundreds of milliseconds, after which ON cells open TRPC3 pores admitting sodium and calcium while OFF cells open silencing GIRK potassium pores.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input is one mossy fibre rosette with hundreds of release sites, plus Golgi cells, the inhibitory gatekeepers of this layer, plus Purkinje collaterals inhibiting about twenty nine percent of mGluR1 cells.

Output reaches hundreds of granule cells and other brush cells, forming chains in which every link adds delay.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin at zero point two to zero point five micrometres and confined to its own lobule, it forms two to ten rosettes indistinguishable from true mossy fibre endings.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases glutamate, the brain's main excitatory chemical, through the transporters VGLUT1 and VGLUT2, and listens with AMPA, NMDA, metabotropic glutamate, GABA type A and dopamine receptors.

MYELINATION:

None worth counting, because myelin, the fatty insulation that speeds conduction, is not worth buying for so short an axon, and the slowness adds yet more delay.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate but unusual, since the slow cascade refills calcium stores continuously and spends energy as a trickle, with heavy calretinin to mop up the calcium TRPC3 admits.

PLASTICITY:

Its mossy fibre input changes strength with use, and in the flocculus the mGluR1 to inositol trisphosphate to ERK pathway drives vestibular compensation, the recovery of balance after one inner ear is damaged.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Velate astrocytes, the sheet like support cells of the granular layer, wrap the glomerulus and clear glutamate unusually slowly.

Everywhere else fast clearance is prized; here glial restraint is the computation.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and the vestibulocerebellum loses its echo: head velocity signals that normally persist for seconds collapse to the raw pulse, and balance turns jerky and reactive.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Glutamate spills onto receptors on non synaptic appendages of the brush, which is volume transmission, and metabotropic receptors release internal calcium to gate TRPC3 or GIRK.

The cell computes with a chemical cascade lasting hundreds of milliseconds, and being intrinsically rhythmic it has each input shift a running oscillator rather than start one.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: one mossy fibre rosette at zero to three hundred hertz; Golgi inhibition zero to two nanosiemens; Purkinje collateral inhibition in about a third of ON cells.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto one hundred to five hundred granule cells and neighbouring brush cells, zero to one hundred hertz.

• STATE: membrane potential; cleft glutamate; AMPA occupancy; metabotropic activation; internal calcium; TRPC3 and GIRK conductances.

• PARAMETERS: subtype flag ON, OFF or intermediate; capacitance ten to twenty picofarads; resting rate one to thirty hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS: synaptic current one to five milliseconds; cleft clearance fifty to two hundred milliseconds; metabotropic current two hundred to one thousand milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: losing the channel ASIC5 causes ataxia, meaning uncoordinated movement, in mice; losing the transcription factor Tbr2 deletes the cells; their progenitors are implicated in medulloblastoma, a childhood cerebellar tumour.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn a brief mossy fibre burst into the long delayed response of an ON type unipolar brush cell.
Create a number variable called glutamate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called current with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called rate with initial value 5.
Create a number variable called time with initial value 0.
While time is less than 3000 is true, repeat the following steps.
    If time is less than 50 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set glutamate to the sum of glutamate and 0.2.
    End the conditional block.
    Set glutamate to the difference of glutamate and the quotient of glutamate divided by 120.
    Set current to the difference of current and the quotient of current divided by 400.
    Set current to the sum of current and the product of glutamate and 0.02.
    Set rate to the sum of 5 and the product of current and 45.
    Set time to the sum of time and 1.
End the loop.
Display rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because this is a small set of coupled first order equations where readability matters more than speed.

# ON type unipolar brush cell: brief input, very long output.
DT = 1.0            # ms per step
TAU_GLUT = 120.0    # slow clearance from the sealed cleft
TAU_SLOW = 400.0    # mGluR1 to TRPC3 cascade
BASE, GAIN = 5.0, 45.0

def simulate(on_cell=True, burst=(100.0, 150.0), duration=3000.0):
    glut, slow, trace = 0.0, 0.0, []
    for i in range(int(duration / DT)):
        t = i * DT
        glut += DT * (-glut / TAU_GLUT) + (0.2 if burst[0] <= t < burst[1] else 0.0)
        slow += DT * (-slow / TAU_SLOW + 0.02 * glut)
        sign = 1.0 if on_cell else -1.0     # OFF cells open potassium instead
        trace.append(max(BASE + sign * GAIN * slow, 0.0))
    return trace

on_trace = simulate(True)
print("ON peak %.1f Hz at %d ms" % (max(on_trace), on_trace.index(max(on_trace))))
print("ON still %.1f Hz at 1000 ms" % on_trace[1000])
print("OFF minimum %.1f Hz" % min(simulate(False)))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

One dendrite ending in a brush of fine tips, buried in a giant mossy fibre ending in the nodulus, uvula or flocculus, answering a five millisecond question with a five hundred millisecond answer.

CHAPTER 120. STELLATE CELLS (COG)

Float a tiny star in the outermost skin of the cerebellum, hang it on the branches of the brain's largest neuron, and you have the cell that decides which Purkinje dendrites may listen.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A stellate cell is a small inhibitory interneuron of the cerebellar molecular layer, inhibitory meaning it makes targets less likely to fire and interneuron meaning it never leaves the cortex.

Parallel fibres, the millimetre long axons of granule cells, excite it, and it turns that excitation into inhibition of Purkinje dendrites, sharpening their response in time and narrowing it in space.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small round cell body with four to eight thin dendrites radiating like the points of a star, all cutting across the parallel fibre beam so the cell samples a wide swathe of traffic.

Its axon stays in the molecular layer as a local cloud of boutons and, unlike its cousin the basket cell, never descends to wrap a Purkinje cell body.

SIZE:

The cell body is eight to twelve micrometres across with a capacitance of only four to twelve picofarads, a dendritic field near one hundred and fifty micrometres, and an axon usually within two hundred.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• By position, superficial cells sit in the outer third of the molecular layer with the smallest dendritic trees, while deeper cells are larger and grade into basket cells.

• By gene expression, mouse sequencing splits molecular layer interneurons into MLI1, carrying Sorcs3 and Gjd2, the gene for the coupling protein connexin 36, and MLI2, carrying Nxph1 and no Gjd2.

• Stellate shape occurs in both, and because MLI2 cells inhibit MLI1 cells this apparently uniform star field is really a two layer network capable of disinhibition.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Only in the molecular layer of the cerebellar cortex, preferentially its outer two thirds, in every lobule of the vermis, the paravermis, both hemispheres and the flocculonodular lobe.

That layer is itself an interface, the sheet where parallel fibres running left to right cross Purkinje dendritic fans standing front to back, and this cell referees the crossing.

NUMBER:

The human molecular layer interneuron population is on the order of one hundred million and stellate forms are the majority, so a defensible allocation is fifty five to seventy million, roughly four per Purkinje cell.

Confidence is moderate for the order of magnitude and low for the stellate to basket split, since no human study separates them cleanly; this is a tenth of one percent of cerebellar neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

It fires spontaneously at five to thirty spikes per second with all input blocked, and its input resistance near one gigaohm lets a few tens of picoamps move it measurably.

A type potassium channels, fast opening potassium pores, hold it back between spikes, so identical inputs can produce different spike latencies depending on recent history.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are parallel fibres, glutamate spilling from nearby climbing fibre contacts, and inhibition from other molecular layer interneurons.

Its few hundred output contacts land mostly on Purkinje dendrites, and because its axon spreads across the beam rather than along it, it inhibits Purkinje cells just off the beam that excited it.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin at zero point two to zero point five micrometres and conducting well under one metre per second, over distances so short that inhibition still lands within two milliseconds of the spike.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main inhibitory chemical, packaged by the transporter VGAT and read by GABA type A receptors, while listening through unusual calcium permeable AMPA receptors.

MYELINATION:

None, because the axon is short and local, and the cerebellum reserves myelin for fibres that must leave the cortex.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low in absolute terms because the cell is tiny, but high per unit volume because it never stops firing, dominated by the sodium pump restoring gradients spent on tonic spiking.

PLASTICITY:

Its parallel fibre input strengthens and weakens with use and can swap calcium permeable AMPA receptors for calcium impermeable ones, while its output shows rebound potentiation after climbing fibre activity.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Bergmann glia, the radial astrocytes of this layer, clamp glutamate spillover, which sets how much crosstalk excitation the stellate cell receives, and their processes define the narrow corridors in which its axon finds a Purkinje dendrite.

They carry calcium permeable AMPA receptors and change shape with activity, so this glial sheet is a moving part of the circuit rather than packing material.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove stellate cells and the molecular layer loses its contrast control: Purkinje responses would still occur but broad, smeared across the beam and slow to stop, blurring the millisecond edges that time a movement.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

MLI1 stellate cells are joined by connexin 36 gap junctions, protein tunnels passing current directly between cells, so groups behave partly as one synchronised unit.

Calcium admitted by calcium permeable AMPA receptors stays confined to single dendritic branches and drives branch specific plasticity, and endocannabinoids released backwards by the Purkinje cell silence these terminals for seconds.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: several hundred parallel fibres at zero to two hundred hertz; climbing fibre glutamate spillover; GABA from other interneurons; gap junction current in MLI1 cells.

• OUTPUTS: GABA onto a few hundred Purkinje dendritic contacts and onto neighbouring interneurons, zero to two nanosiemens each.

• STATE: membrane potential; A type potassium inactivation; sodium channel availability; per branch calcium; short term depression of the output.

• PARAMETERS: capacitance four to twelve picofarads; input resistance about one gigaohm; spontaneous rate five to thirty hertz; coupling nonzero for MLI1 only.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane ten to twenty milliseconds; excitatory current one to three milliseconds; inhibitory output five to ten milliseconds; endocannabinoid suppression five to twenty seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: weakened molecular layer inhibition unleashes Purkinje dendritic calcium and broadens Purkinje firing, a pattern seen in ataxia models, and this synapse is among the first altered by alcohol.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute how a stellate cell turns an on beam parallel fibre burst into off beam inhibition of Purkinje cells.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value -60.
Create a number variable called excitation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spikes with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called time with initial value 0.
While time is less than 200 is true, repeat the following steps.
    If both time is greater than or equal to 50 and time is less than 100 are true, then perform the following actions.
        Set excitation to the sum of excitation and 0.35.
    End the conditional block.
    Set excitation to the difference of excitation and the quotient of excitation divided by 2.
    Set voltage to the sum of voltage and the product of excitation and 8.
    Set voltage to the difference of voltage and the quotient of the sum of voltage and 60 divided by 15.
    If voltage is greater than -45 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set spikes to the sum of spikes and 1.
        Set voltage to -65.
    End the conditional block.
    Set time to the sum of time and 1.
End the loop.
Display spikes to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the useful output is both a spike train and a list of which Purkinje columns get inhibited.

# Stellate cell: on-beam excitation produces off-beam inhibition.
DT = 1.0                     # ms
TAU_M, TAU_E = 15.0, 2.0     # membrane and synaptic time constants
V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = -60.0, -45.0, -65.0
SPREAD = 3                   # columns reached each side of the beam

def stellate(beam_hz, duration=200.0, beam=(50.0, 100.0)):
    v, ge, spikes = V_REST, 0.0, []
    for i in range(int(duration / DT)):
        t = i * DT
        if beam[0] <= t < beam[1]:
            ge += (beam_hz * DT / 1000.0) * 0.7    # quanta per step
        ge -= DT * ge / TAU_E
        v += DT * (-(v - V_REST) / TAU_M) + ge * 8.0 * DT / TAU_M
        if v > V_TH:
            spikes.append(t)
            v = V_RESET
    return spikes

s = stellate(120.0)
print("spikes:", len(s), "first at", s[0] if s else None, "ms")
print("columns inhibited:", [c for c in range(-SPREAD, SPREAD + 1) if c != 0])

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A four to twelve picofarad star in the outer molecular layer, dendrites cutting across the parallel fibre beam, never touching a Purkinje cell body, inhibiting the dendrites just beside the beam that excited it.

CHAPTER 121. CEREBELLAR BASKET CELLS (COG)

This is the only neuron in the human brain that can silence another without using a chemical at all, by throwing an electrical shadow across the spot where its target's spike is born.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A basket cell is an inhibitory interneuron of the deep molecular layer, excited by parallel fibres exactly as a stellate cell is.

It aims its output at the Purkinje cell body and axon initial segment, the short stretch of output cable where every spike is generated, so its inhibition vetoes the whole cell instead of trimming one dendrite.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Its axon runs horizontally front to back just above the row of Purkinje cell bodies, dropping collaterals that weave a dense cage, the basket, around each soma it passes, and at the foot of each cage the branches converge into the pinceau, French for paintbrush, a tight brush of terminals gripping the axon initial segment and almost devoid of chemical synapses.

SIZE:

The cell body is ten to twenty micrometres across, the main axon runs five hundred to one thousand micrometres past about ten Purkinje cells, and the pinceau is twenty to forty micrometres long.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• By pinceau size, basket cells form parasagittal zonal modules, and blocking Purkinje transmitter release changes that size, so the target patterns its own input.

• By molecular class, basket morphology occurs in both the MLI1 and MLI2 families, only the first carrying connexin 36 gap junctions.

• By depth, the lowest cells build the most complete baskets and pinceaux while higher ones grade toward stellate form.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the lower third of the molecular layer throughout the vermis, both hemispheres and the flocculonodular lobe, with its axon straddling an interface: the trunk lies in the molecular layer while the baskets and pinceaux occupy the Purkinje cell layer, the point where all cortical computation funnels into one output cell.

NUMBER:

Within the roughly one hundred million human molecular layer interneurons, basket cells are the minority form, allocated here at thirty to forty five million, or two to three per Purkinje cell, at moderate confidence for the order of magnitude and low confidence for the figure, since human counts do not separate basket from stellate cells; that is five hundredths of one percent of cerebellar neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

It fires spontaneously at ten to thirty spikes per second, shaped by L type calcium channels and by calcium activated potassium channels, while its pinceau terminals are packed with the Kv1.1 and Kv1.2 potassium channels that generate the inhibitory electric field.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It receives parallel fibres, climbing fibre spillover and other interneurons, and delivers both large fast GABA currents onto the Purkinje soma and a transmitter free ephaptic effect, meaning one carried by the electric field, that arrives with zero synaptic delay.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The main axon is zero point five to one micrometre wide and runs a long straight parasagittal course, so one cell inhibits a strip of Purkinje cells lying within a single zone.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA), packaged by the transporter VGAT and read by GABA type A receptors on the Purkinje soma, supplemented by the pinceau's potassium driven electrical inhibition, which uses no transmitter at all.

MYELINATION:

None, because the axon never leaves the cortex, and the cerebellum compensates for the resulting slow conduction by making pinceau inhibition instantaneous rather than merely fast.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and concentrated at the pinceau, where a dense cluster of terminals must pump sodium and potassium back across the membrane after every spike and mitochondria are correspondingly crowded.

PLASTICITY:

Parallel fibre input is plastic, the basket to Purkinje synapse undergoes rebound potentiation, a lasting strengthening of inhibition after climbing fibre activity, and the pinceau itself is structurally remodelled across a lifetime.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

At the pinceau, Bergmann glia leave a very narrow sealed extracellular space around the axon initial segment, and that confinement is what lets a small ionic current make a large local voltage, so here the glia act as the insulation of a capacitor rather than as housekeepers.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove basket cells and the cortex loses its off switch and its clock: Purkinje cells would keep firing, but nothing could stop them quickly or synchronise their pauses, and it is the precisely timed pause that releases the deep nuclei to launch a movement.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The ephaptic pinceau is an entire mode of inhibition that no integrate and fire model contains, faster than any chemical synapse because there is no synapse.

MLI1 basket cells are coupled by connexin 36 gap junctions into synchronised groups, they are silenced for seconds by endocannabinoids released backwards from the Purkinje cell, and their own presynaptic architecture is dictated by that same target.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: parallel fibres at zero to two hundred hertz; climbing fibre glutamate spillover; interneuron GABA; gap junction current in MLI1 cells.

• OUTPUTS: GABA onto the Purkinje soma up to about five nanosiemens; an ephaptic field of roughly one to several millivolts at the axon initial segment, with zero delay.

• STATE: membrane potential; calcium; calcium activated potassium conductance; presynaptic Kv1 current; short term depression of the somatic synapse.

• PARAMETERS: spontaneous rate ten to thirty hertz; ten to thirty Purkinje targets; pinceau length twenty to forty micrometres; zone identity setting pinceau size.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane ten to twenty milliseconds; GABA type A decay five to ten milliseconds; ephaptic effect under one millisecond; rebound potentiation tens of minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: in episodic ataxia type one, caused by mutations in KCNA1, the gene for Kv1.1, this ephaptic coupling is abolished and attacks of incoordination follow; in essential tremor, dense tangled hairy baskets are scored high in about a quarter of cases and almost never in controls, rising with Purkinje axonal swellings called torpedoes.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to combine chemical and ephaptic basket cell inhibition of one Purkinje cell and report the deepest pause produced.
Create a number variable called inhibition with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called field with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called rate with initial value 60.
Create a number variable called lowest with initial value 60.
Create a number variable called time with initial value 0.
While time is less than 100 is true, repeat the following steps.
    Set field to 0.
    If either time is equal to 20 or time is equal to 24 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set inhibition to the sum of inhibition and 2.5.
        Set field to 3.
    End the conditional block.
    Set inhibition to the difference of inhibition and the quotient of inhibition divided by 7.
    Set rate to the difference of 60 and the product of inhibition and 9.
    Set rate to the difference of rate and the product of field and 6.
    If rate is less than lowest is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set lowest to rate.
    End the conditional block.
    Set time to the sum of time and 1.
End the loop.
Display lowest to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because the field effect must be applied inside the same time step as the presynaptic spike, and C makes that strict ordering explicit.

#include <stdio.h>

#define TAU_GABA 7.0     /* ms, decay of somatic inhibition */
#define BASE 60.0        /* Purkinje spikes per second */

int main(void) {
    int spikes[3] = {20, 24, 28};   /* basket cell spike times, ms */
    double g = 0.0, field, rate, low = BASE;
    for (int t = 0; t < 100; t++) {
        field = 0.0;                /* the field lasts one step only */
        for (int k = 0; k < 3; k++)
            if (spikes[k] == t) { g += 2.5; field = 3.0; }
        g -= g / TAU_GABA;
        rate = BASE - 9.0 * g - 6.0 * field;
        if (rate < 0.0) rate = 0.0;
        if (rate < low) low = rate;
    }
    printf("deepest pause: %.2f Hz\n", low);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A horizontal axon running just above the Purkinje row, dropping woven cages around cell bodies and ending in a paintbrush of terminals that grips the axon initial segment and inhibits it with an electric field.

CHAPTER 122. LARGE GLUTAMATERGIC PROJECTION NEURONS (Movement-Dispatching Output Neuron) (COG)

Sixty nine billion cerebellar neurons compute in silence, and everything they work out leaves through fewer than a million cells, of which this is the main one.

The old label is a committee minute, stating size, transmitter and projection while saying nothing a student could use.

Movement-Dispatching names what the message is about and the act of sending it, since these cells drive the red nucleus, reticular formation, vestibular nuclei and motor thalamus, and the entire computation of the cerebellar cortex leaves through this small population and nowhere else.

Output states the plain structural truth that these few cells are the only means by which the cerebellum influences behaviour.

Neuron marks it as a projection cell of the deep cerebellar nuclei.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A large glutamatergic projection neuron is an excitatory output cell of the cerebellar nuclei, the grey clusters buried in the white matter at the core of the cerebellum, and projection means its axon leaves the region for distant targets.

It fires fast and without pause, so its message is carried by the moments when Purkinje inhibition makes it fall silent and by the surge that follows.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A large multipolar cell body carries six to ten thick dendrites in a roughly spherical field so densely caked in Purkinje terminals that the cell almost disappears beneath its own inhibitory input, and one thick axon leaves through the superior cerebellar peduncle, the great outflow cable of the cerebellum.

SIZE:

The cell body is twenty to thirty five micrometres across, with a typical large nuclear soma measured near twenty two micrometres, although the sizes of GABA making and glutamate making nuclear cells overlap so much that only molecular markers identify the type.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Dentate large neurons project to thalamus and the parvocellular red nucleus, serving dexterity and cognition.

• Interposed large neurons project to the magnocellular red nucleus and thalamus, serving limb reflex timing.

• Fastigial large neurons project to vestibular nuclei, reticular formation and spinal premotor regions, and separate into distinct motor and non motor output modules.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the dentate, emboliform, globose and fastigial nuclei of each side, with the lateral vestibular nucleus of the brainstem behaving as a displaced fifth nucleus, each of them sitting at an interface inside cerebellar white matter where passing mossy and climbing fibres shed collaterals, so the nucleus receives the raw input and the cortex's verdict together.

NUMBER:

This manuscript works to an allowance of one to two million neurons for the cerebellar nuclei and gives this type the largest share, seven hundred thousand to one point two million at moderate confidence, while noting honestly that a stereological study of human brains reported about three point four to three point seven million neurons in the dentate nucleus alone on one side, so the allowance is conservative; either way this is about one thousandth of one percent of cerebellar neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

It is a tonic pacemaker averaging around seventy spikes per second in the awake animal, carrying T type calcium channels that recover only when the cell is hyperpolarised and HCN channels that open on hyperpolarisation, which together produce a rebound burst after inhibition whose real size in behaving animals is debated and should stay tunable.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Tens of Purkinje cells converge on each nuclear neuron with inhibitory currents decaying in about two milliseconds, mossy and climbing fibre collaterals supply excitation, and the output is glutamate onto ventrolateral thalamus, red nucleus, zona incerta, superior colliculus, reticular formation, vestibular nuclei and neighbouring nuclear cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thick and fast at one to five micrometres and tens of metres per second, ascending in the superior cerebellar peduncle and crossing the midline in the midbrain, which is why one cerebellar hemisphere ends up serving the same side of the body.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate loaded by the vesicular transporter VGLUT2, with GABA type A receptors for Purkinje input, AMPA and NMDA receptors for collateral excitation, and modulation by acetylcholine, serotonin and noradrenaline from the brainstem.

MYELINATION:

Heavily myelinated by oligodendrocytes, the cells that make insulating wraps in the central nervous system, because the value of the entire cerebellar computation depends on the answer arriving on time.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high, since firing seventy times a second without pause runs the sodium pump continuously, so these neurons are packed with mitochondria and are acutely vulnerable when blood flow fails.

PLASTICITY:

The mossy fibre input strengthens lastingly when excitation is paired with the hyperpolarisation and rebound caused by Purkinje inhibition, which is thought to be how a motor memory first learned in the cortex is consolidated for long term storage.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The distinguishing feature is the perineuronal net, a dense lattice of sugar rich extracellular matrix encasing the soma like a string bag, which stabilises an enormous inhibitory synapse load, buffers ions around a cell that never stops firing, and restricts new synapse formation.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the cerebellum becomes a beautiful machine wired to nothing: the cortex would still compute, but nothing would reach thalamus, red nucleus or spinal cord, and movement would become slow, broken into separate joint motions and dysmetric, meaning it overshoots or undershoots.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Rebound makes the output depend on the history of inhibition rather than its present level, so the cell differentiates in time instead of merely summing.

Synchronous Purkinje pauses are far more effective than the same amount of scattered inhibition, so it reads the correlation structure of its inputs, while its activity drives local blood flow, instructs myelination of its own axon, and remodels its perineuronal net.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: Purkinje GABA from tens of sources at zero to two hundred hertz with two millisecond decay; mossy fibre collateral glutamate at zero to three hundred hertz; climbing fibre collaterals at about one hertz.

• OUTPUTS: a glutamate spike train resting near seventy hertz and ranging zero to two hundred hertz, to thalamus, red nucleus, reticular formation, colliculus, vestibular nuclei and local nucleo olivary cells.

• STATE: membrane potential; T type inactivation; HCN activation; internal calcium; short term depression at the Purkinje input.

• PARAMETERS: baseline rate sixty to ninety hertz; rebound gain zero to one, tunable; soma diameter twenty to thirty five micrometres; conduction velocity ten to fifty metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane ten to twenty milliseconds; inhibitory current two milliseconds; T type recovery fifty to two hundred milliseconds; rebound burst one hundred to three hundred milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: damage to the dentate nucleus or superior cerebellar peduncle in children causes cerebellar mutism syndrome, a temporary loss of speech, and disturbance of this pathway produces intention tremor and dysmetria, which is why cerebellar strokes disable so much despite an intact cortex.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert a synchronous Purkinje cell pause into a rebound burst in a large glutamatergic nuclear neuron.
Create a number variable called inhibition with initial value 40.
Create a number variable called availability with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called rate with initial value 70.
Create a number variable called time with initial value 0.
While time is less than 600 is true, repeat the following steps.
    If both time is greater than or equal to 100 and time is less than 200 are true, then perform the following actions.
        Set inhibition to 0.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set inhibition to 40.
    End the conditional block.
    If inhibition is less than 10 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set availability to the sum of availability and the quotient of the difference of 1 and availability divided by 80.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set availability to the difference of availability and the quotient of availability divided by 25.
    End the conditional block.
    Set rate to the difference of 70 and inhibition.
    Set rate to the sum of rate and the product of availability and 55.
    Set time to the sum of time and 1.
End the loop.
Display rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the point is the shape of a rate trace over time, and its three phases are easy to read off and check.

# Large glutamatergic nuclear neuron: pause in, rebound out.
DT = 1.0            # ms
BASE, INHIB = 70.0, 40.0
TAU_DEINACT = 80.0  # T-type channels recover while hyperpolarised
TAU_INACT = 25.0    # and inactivate again once depolarised
GAIN = 55.0         # tunable: in vivo rebound size is debated

def run(pause=(100.0, 200.0), duration=600.0):
    h, trace = 0.0, []          # h = fraction of T-type channels available
    for i in range(int(duration / DT)):
        t = i * DT
        inhib = 0.0 if pause[0] <= t < pause[1] else INHIB
        if inhib < 10.0:
            h += DT * (1.0 - h) / TAU_DEINACT
        else:
            h -= DT * h / TAU_INACT
        trace.append(max(BASE - inhib + GAIN * h, 0.0))
    return trace

trace = run()
print("baseline %.1f Hz, in pause %.1f Hz" % (trace[50], trace[150]))
print("rebound  %.1f Hz at %d ms" % (max(trace), trace.index(max(trace))))
print("recovered %.1f Hz" % trace[-1])

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A big multipolar neuron buried in cerebellar white matter, caked in Purkinje terminals, wearing a perineuronal net, firing seventy times a second forever, with one thick myelinated axon that leaves for good.

CHAPTER 123. GABAERGIC NUCLEO-OLIVARY NEURONS (Climbing-Input Damping Neuron) (COG)

This is the cell that tells the cerebellum's teacher to be quiet, and it is how a brain learns that nothing surprising happened.

The old name is a transmitter barcode bolted to two Latin addresses.

Climbing-Input names what it acts upon, the climbing fibre error signal from the inferior olive that instructs cerebellar learning.

Damping names the action, an inhibitory projection back onto the olive that closes a loop within each narrow zone, and experiments show that driving this cerebello-olivary inhibition alone is sufficient to extinguish an already learned conditioned eyeblink response.

Neuron marks it as a cell whose job is to tell the teacher to stop teaching once the lesson is learned.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A GABAergic nucleo olivary neuron is a small inhibitory cell of the cerebellar nuclei whose axon runs back down to the inferior olive, the brainstem nucleus that generates climbing fibres.

Climbing fibres are the cerebellum's teaching signal, so by silencing the olive when a predicted error fails to arrive, this cell turns silence itself into the message that the prediction was right.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small sparsely branched neuron with three to five smooth dendrites, scattered among the large output cells, whose axon ends inside the olivary glomerulus, a knot in which olivary dendrites are already joined electrically.

SIZE:

The cell body is ten to eighteen micrometres across, smaller than the large projection neurons but overlapping them, with a thin axon running several millimetres to a few centimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• By target microzone, these cells occupy the same narrow parasagittal strips as Purkinje cells and climbing fibres, forming closed loops.

• By nucleus of origin, dentate, interposed and fastigial cells target the principal, dorsal accessory and medial accessory olives respectively.

• A separate excitatory nucleo olivary pathway also exists, but that is a different cell type and is not counted here.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Scattered through all four cerebellar nuclei on both sides, with terminals in the contralateral inferior olive of the medulla, forming one arm of the Guillain Mollaret triangle that joins dentate nucleus, red nucleus and olive.

NUMBER:

Within the one to two million neuron allowance for the cerebellar nuclei, one hundred and fifty thousand to three hundred thousand is defensible at low to moderate confidence, since rodent work makes these cells roughly one in five to one in eight nuclear neurons and no human count exists; that is a few ten thousandths of one percent of cerebellar neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

It fires tonically but slowly at a few to about twenty spikes per second, and Purkinje evoked inhibitory currents in it decay in about twenty five milliseconds, more than ten times slower than in large nuclear cells, so it reports an average rather than an instant.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It receives slow Purkinje inhibition, mossy fibre collaterals, local excitation from large nuclear cells and climbing fibre collaterals, and it releases GABA onto olivary dendrites inside the glomerulus, right beside the gap junctions that couple olivary cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin at zero point five to one micrometre and only lightly myelinated, and its slowness is not a flaw but part of the roughly one hundred millisecond clock of the olivo cerebellar loop.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA) alone, released asynchronously, meaning vesicles keep fusing for tens to hundreds of milliseconds after the spike, so a brief burst becomes a long smeared cloud acting on GABA type A receptors.

MYELINATION:

Thin at best, with much of the terminal arbor bare, because this system wants the signal slow, spread out and timed to the loop rather than fast.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest, since the cell is small and slow, but unusual at the terminals, which must sustain asynchronous release and therefore keep large recycling vesicle pools and high residual calcium.

PLASTICITY:

Nucleo olivary inhibition strengthens as a conditioned response is learned, and activating this cerebello olivary signal alone is enough to extinguish a conditioned eyeblink, one of the clearest negative prediction error signals known in any brain.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes seal each olivary glomerulus into a tiny chamber, so GABA lingers and shunts the electrical coupling between olivary cells, which means the glial wrapping converts a chemical signal into a change of connectivity.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the cerebellum can learn but cannot stop learning: the olive would fire unrestrained and in unnatural synchrony, error signals would keep arriving after the error was corrected, and conditioned responses would never extinguish.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Asynchronous release makes the output a low pass filtered version of the spike train rather than a copy of it.

Its main postsynaptic effect is not hyperpolarisation but modulation of gap junction coupling, so this cell edits a network's wiring rather than only its excitability, and its climbing fibre collateral input makes the whole loop self limiting.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: Purkinje GABA from one microzone with twenty five millisecond decay; mossy fibre collateral glutamate; climbing fibre collaterals at about one hertz; local nuclear excitation.

• OUTPUTS: asynchronous GABA into contralateral olivary glomeruli, as a slow conductance envelope and a multiplicative cut in olivary gap junction coupling.

• STATE: membrane potential; slow inhibitory conductance; residual presynaptic calcium; asynchronous release pool; running estimate of recent Purkinje drive.

• PARAMETERS: baseline rate two to twenty hertz; asynchronous release time constant fifty to two hundred milliseconds; maximum coupling reduction up to about half; microzone identity.

• TIME CONSTANTS: inhibitory input twenty five milliseconds; asynchronous output fifty to two hundred milliseconds; loop period near one hundred milliseconds; learning minutes to days.
