• FAILURE MODE: interrupting the dentato rubro olivary pathway causes hypertrophic olivary degeneration and palatal tremor, a rhythmic involuntary movement of the soft palate that can last years, and losing this inhibition removes the ability to extinguish maladaptive learned responses.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the asynchronous inhibition a nucleo olivary neuron delivers and the resulting drop in olivary coupling.
Create a number variable called pool with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called inhibition with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called coupling with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called released with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called time with initial value 0.
While time is less than 500 is true, repeat the following steps.
    If either time is equal to 10 or time is equal to 30 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set pool to the sum of pool and 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Set released to the quotient of pool divided by 120.
    Set pool to the difference of pool and released.
    Set inhibition to the difference of inhibition and the quotient of inhibition divided by 60.
    Set inhibition to the sum of inhibition and released.
    Set coupling to the difference of 1 and the product of inhibition and 3.
    Set time to the sum of time and 1.
End the loop.
Display coupling to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

JavaScript is chosen because this signal is naturally described as events queued for delayed delivery, which is how JavaScript programmers already think.

// Nucleo-olivary neuron: asynchronous GABA release onto the inferior olive.
const TAU_RELEASE = 120.0; // vesicles keep leaving the pool for 100+ ms
const TAU_GABA = 60.0;     // decay of the inhibitory conductance
const GAIN = 3.0;          // how strongly GABA shunts olivary gap junctions

function run(spikeTimes, duration = 500) {
  let pool = 0, g = 0, minCoupling = 1.0, peak = { t: 0, g: 0 };
  for (let t = 0; t < duration; t++) {
    if (spikeTimes.includes(t)) pool += 1;   // a spike loads the pool
    const released = pool / TAU_RELEASE;     // slow, smeared release
    pool -= released;
    g += released - g / TAU_GABA;
    const coupling = Math.max(1 - GAIN * g, 0.5);  // reduced, not abolished
    if (coupling < minCoupling) minCoupling = coupling;
    if (g > peak.g) peak = { t, g };
  }
  return { minCoupling, peak, residual: g };
}

const out = run([10, 30, 50]);
console.log("peak inhibition at", out.peak.t, "ms");
console.log("lowest coupling", out.minCoupling.toFixed(3));
console.log("inhibition remaining at end", out.residual.toFixed(4));

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small cell hiding among giants in the cerebellar nuclei, receiving Purkinje inhibition ten times slower than its neighbours, dribbling GABA into a sealed glial chamber in the olive for a tenth of a second after every spike.

CHAPTER 124. GLYCINERGIC NEURONS (Same-Side Restraint Output Neuron) (COG)

For a century the cerebellum was said to speak two chemical words, glutamate for yes and GABA for no, until a mouse engineered to glow green wherever glycine is made lit up a hidden population of output cells.

The old label names only the chemical the cell releases, glycine, which tells a student nothing about what the cell does.

Same-Side names the one anatomical fact that separates this population from every other cerebellar output cell, since these cells send their axons to vestibular and reticular targets on the same side of the brainstem while their glutamate-releasing neighbours in the very same nucleus cross the midline to the opposite side.

Restraint names the work, because glycine opens chloride channels that hold the target cell below its firing threshold, so this is the cerebellum's braking line rather than its driving line.

Output records that these are projection cells leaving the cerebellum rather than local circuit cells.

Neuron marks it as the reason the cerebellum can push posture on one side and hold it back on the other through two parallel and oppositely directed channels.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A glycinergic neuron inhibits its targets with glycine, the smallest amino acid in biology, which opens chloride channels called glycine receptors that the poison strychnine blocks.

The cerebellum has two families, projection neurons of the fastigial nucleus and cortical interneurons that co release glycine with gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA), and the first matters for a reason of pure geometry: their glutamate releasing neighbours cross the midline while they stay on the same side.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The nuclear cells are medium to large multipolar neurons indistinguishable by eye from the glutamatergic output cells beside them, while the cortical ones are Golgi cells of the granular layer and spindle shaped Lugaro cells lying just beneath the Purkinje layer.

SIZE:

Fastigial glycinergic projection neurons are fifteen to thirty micrometres across with axons one to three micrometres wide, Lugaro cells are ten to twenty micrometres with dendrites running three hundred micrometres along the Purkinje row, and Golgi cells are fifteen to twenty five micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Fastigial glycinergic projection neurons, sending inhibitory axons to the ipsilateral vestibular nuclei and reticular formation through the juxtarestiform body.

• Cortical interneurons positive for the glycine transporter GlyT2, chiefly Golgi and Lugaro cells, which co release glycine and GABA.

• Small mixed neurons inside the cerebellar nuclei that contain both glycine and GABA and act as local interneurons.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The projection type is concentrated in the fastigial nucleus beside the roof of the fourth ventricle with fewer in the interposed nucleus, while the cortical type occupies the granular layer and its boundary with the Purkinje layer throughout the vermis and hemispheres, and the defining interface is the border between cerebellum and vestibular brainstem.

NUMBER:

Within the one to two million neuron allowance for the cerebellar nuclei, one hundred thousand to two hundred thousand is defensible at low confidence, based on mouse evidence that a large share of fastigial output neurons are glycinergic and on the absence of any human count; the cortical glycine releasing cells are Golgi and Lugaro cells already counted inside the ten million Golgi allowance, so they are not added again, and this leaves about two ten thousandths of one percent of cerebellar neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Fastigial glycinergic cells fire tonically at tens of spikes per second, indistinguishable electrically from their glutamatergic neighbours, while Golgi and Lugaro cells fire at a few to about ten hertz and Lugaro cells are switched from near silence into steady firing by serotonin.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Nuclear glycinergic cells take direct Purkinje inhibition and mossy and climbing fibre collateral excitation and inhibit ipsilateral vestibular and reticular neurons, while Golgi and Lugaro cells inhibit granule cells and each other, producing mixed events that carry both GABA and glycine.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Nuclear axons are myelinated at one to three micrometres and descend on the same side while their glutamatergic siblings cross, and Lugaro cell axons run transversely for one to two millimetres across many parasagittal zones.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glycine, recaptured by GlyT2 and loaded by the same vesicular transporter that loads GABA, so one vesicle can carry both; glycine is also an obligatory co agonist at NMDA glutamate receptors, meaning the same molecule inhibits some cells while licensing excitation in others.

MYELINATION:

The nuclear projection axons are well myelinated so they arrive in register with the crossed glutamatergic pathway, while cortical Golgi and Lugaro axons are unmyelinated like all intracortical cerebellar axons.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate for the tonically firing nuclear cells and low for the slow cortical ones, and glycine itself is cheap, made in one step from serine, which makes this among the least expensive signalling systems in the brain.

PLASTICITY:

Developing hindbrain neurons shift the balance of GABA and glycine they release as they mature, the ratio stays adjustable in adults, and GlyT2 positive interneurons have been shown to regulate the timing and trial to trial variability of a cerebellar behavioural loop.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here express glycine transporter 1, GlyT1, which sets ambient glycine and therefore simultaneously ends an inhibitory signal and sets the gain of NMDA receptor excitation nearby, and they also supply the serine from which glycine is made.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove glycinergic neurons and the fastigial nucleus loses the ability to say no on its own side of the body, so posture would become symmetric and unstable and the reciprocal eye and head control that keeps the visual world still while you walk would degrade.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Co release of two transmitters from one vesicle means the same spike means different things to different partners, depending on which receptors each target carries.

Glycine escaping the cleft acts by volume transmission as a co agonist at nearby NMDA receptors, GlyT2 can run backwards and release glycine with no vesicle at all, and serotonin decides whether the Lugaro cell layer of inhibition exists at any given moment.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: Purkinje GABA for the nuclear type; mossy and climbing fibre collateral glutamate; parallel fibre glutamate for the cortical type; serotonin level as a gate for Lugaro cells.

• OUTPUTS: glycine, optionally with GABA in the same vesicle, onto ipsilateral vestibular and reticular neurons or onto Golgi cells, plus a contribution to ambient glycine near NMDA receptors.

• STATE: membrane potential; vesicular glycine to GABA ratio; cytoplasmic glycine set by GlyT2; short term depression; serotonin gate state.

• PARAMETERS: glycine fraction of the mixed vesicle zero to one; strychnine sensitive conductance up to two nanosiemens per contact; rate tens of hertz for nuclear cells, a few hertz for cortical cells.

• TIME CONSTANTS: glycine receptor decay ten to thirty milliseconds, slower than the GABA component of the same event; GlyT2 refilling hundreds of milliseconds; serotonergic gating seconds to minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: mutations in the glycine receptor gene GLRA1 cause hyperekplexia, or startle disease, in which any small surprise triggers whole body stiffening; antibodies against the same receptor cause progressive encephalomyelitis with rigidity and myoclonus; strychnine poisoning blocks these receptors and causes fatal convulsions.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to build the mixed inhibition produced by a neuron that co releases glycine and gamma aminobutyric acid, and to show what strychnine does to it.
Create a number variable called gaba with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called glycine with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called total with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called peak with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called strychnine with initial value false.
Create a number variable called time with initial value 0.
While time is less than 200 is true, repeat the following steps.
    If time is equal to 20 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set gaba to 0.8.
        Set glycine to 1.2.
    End the conditional block.
    Set gaba to the difference of gaba and the quotient of gaba divided by 7.
    Set glycine to the difference of glycine and the quotient of glycine divided by 20.
    If strychnine is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set total to gaba.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set total to the sum of gaba and glycine.
    End the conditional block.
    If total is greater than peak is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set peak to total.
    End the conditional block.
    Set time to the sum of time and 1.
End the loop.
Display peak to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the useful result is a comparison of three drug conditions, which sit side by side in a few readable lines.

# Mixed glycine plus GABA inhibition from a co-releasing cerebellar neuron.
TAU_GABA, TAU_GLY = 7.0, 20.0   # ms; the glycine component is slower
AMPLITUDE = 2.0                 # nS delivered by one release event

def mixed_ipsc(gly_fraction=0.6, strychnine=False, bicuculline=False, dur=200):
    g_gaba = g_gly = 0.0
    trace = []
    for t in range(dur):
        if t == 20:                            # one co-release event
            g_gaba += AMPLITUDE * (1.0 - gly_fraction)
            g_gly += AMPLITUDE * gly_fraction
        g_gaba -= g_gaba / TAU_GABA
        g_gly -= g_gly / TAU_GLY
        a = 0.0 if bicuculline else g_gaba     # bicuculline blocks GABA-A
        b = 0.0 if strychnine else g_gly       # strychnine blocks glycine
        trace.append(a + b)
    return trace

def summarise(name, trace):
    peak = max(trace)
    above = [i for i, v in enumerate(trace) if v >= peak / 2.0]
    print("%-12s peak %.3f nS, half-width %d ms" % (name, peak, above[-1] - above[0]))

summarise("control", mixed_ipsc())
summarise("strychnine", mixed_ipsc(strychnine=True))
summarise("bicuculline", mixed_ipsc(bicuculline=True))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

An inhibitory cerebellar neuron whose current is only half abolished by blocking GABA receptors and whose remaining half vanishes under strychnine, and in the fastigial nucleus it is the one output cell that stays on its own side of the body.

CHAPTER 125. SMALL LOCAL INTERNEURONS (Close-Quarters Inhibiting Neuron) (COG)

Deep in the cerebellum, among giant cells wrapped in armour, sit tiny neurons that never leave home.

The old label is a size note and a placeholder rather than a name, and honesty requires admitting that the function of these cells is not established.

Close-Quarters records the one solid fact that is beyond dispute, that their somata are small and their axonal territory stays within a narrow patch of cortex rather than reaching across lobules.

Inhibiting records the second solid fact, that they release glycine and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), so whatever computation they serve, their effect on neighbours is to hold firing down.

Neuron marks it as a granular layer interneuron of a group not characterised physiologically at all until 2012, each of these small classes accounting for at least fifteen percent of granular layer interneurons.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is a small braking cell of the cerebellar nuclei whose axon, its output cable, never leaves the nucleus.

It releases two brakes at once, gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) and glycine, onto the giant cells that carry cerebellar output to the brain.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small round body carries three to six short dendrites, the branches that gather signals, all inside the nucleus.

Its endings crowd giant-cell dendrites at contacts packed with glycine receptors and nearly free of the usual GABA receptor subunits.

SIZE:

Cell bodies here span 14.7 to 50 micrometres, and this class sits below about 21.

A micrometre is a thousandth of a millimetre, so forty of these would span a page.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two of the five conserved neuron types in every cerebellar nucleus are local GABA and glycine interneurons.

One fires slowly and carries glutamic acid decarboxylase 67 (GAD67), the enzyme that makes GABA, while the other rests silent and bursts when pushed.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It is scattered among the giant cells of all four nuclei: dentate, emboliform, globose and fastigial.

Two interfaces matter: where nuclear grey meets the white matter carrying Purkinje axons, and where the fastigial nucleus abuts the brainstem vestibular nuclei.

NUMBER:

Of the one to two million neurons allotted to the human cerebellar nuclei, rodent proportions make a third to a half these interneurons, so 300,000 to 600,000.

Confidence is low, and this is roughly 0.0005 percent of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Its action potential, the brief pulse a neuron fires, is over a millisecond wide, twice its neighbours'.

Slow pulses cap it near thirty per second while those neighbours run past a hundred.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Side branches of mossy fibres, the cerebellum's main input cables, and of climbing fibres from the inferior olive excite it.

Its glycine receptor clusters are about 5 percent of the inhibitory receptor clusters on a giant cell.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Under a micrometre thick, it branches within a few hundred micrometres, so conduction is slow yet delays stay under a millisecond.

One group, carrying glycine transporter type 2 (GlyT2), instead sends a long axon to the cortex.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The vesicular inhibitory amino acid transporter loads GABA and glycine into one vesicle, so both leave together, glycine acting faster.

Glycine is common below the brain and rare above it, which makes these cells chemical outsiders.

MYELINATION:

The local axon is unmyelinated, lacking the fatty wrap that speeds conduction, because over such short distances insulation buys nothing.

Only the long nucleocortical axons are partly wrapped.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest, since twenty pulses a second cost a fraction of a neighbour's hundred.

The steady bill is transport, because pumping chloride and recapturing glycine draw on sodium gradients.

PLASTICITY:

Inhibitory synapses in these nuclei do change strength with experience, and stripping the surrounding matrix alters them measurably.

Whether its own glycine synapse has a learning rule is unknown, so model slow activity-dependent scaling and say so.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Glia here help build perineuronal nets, cages of sugar and protein rich in aggrecan, around the giant cells only.

So this cell works bare among armoured neighbours, and digesting the cages strengthens Purkinje inhibition and impairs eyeblink learning.

Local astrocytes also recover glycine, not just GABA.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete it and the nucleus loses its private, selective brake, leaving every output cell inhibited on one common timetable.

The nucleus would still relay, but it would dim and brighten as one, losing the contrast that lets a single channel speak.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

One pulse writes a two-part waveform, fast glycine then slower GABA, both depending on the chloride gradient held by the KCC2 pump.

Spilled glycine reaches neighbours as volume transmission, and trimming spike jitter sets how tightly cerebellar output is synchronised.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: mossy fibre collaterals 0 to 200 per second, climbing fibre collaterals 0 to 10, Purkinje inhibition 20 to 100.

• OUTPUTS: mixed glycine and GABA conductance onto giant cells, 0.2 to 1.0 nanosiemens per contact.

• STATE: voltage, refractory countdown, glycine conductance, GABA conductance, internal chloride.

• PARAMETERS: soma 8 to 21 micrometres, input resistance 400 to 1000 megaohms, spike width 1.0 to 1.5 milliseconds, ceiling near 30 per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 to 30 milliseconds, glycine decay 10, GABA decay 30 to 50, refractory 25.

• FAILURE MODE: without this brake the giant cells fire in unison, the burst signature of dystonia and tremor models.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to fire one small local interneuron and release glycine onto a giant output cell.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value -65.
Create a number variable called glycine with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
While clock is less than 1000 remains true, repeat the following steps.
    Set voltage to the sum of voltage and 0.7.
    Set glycine to the product of glycine and 0.99.
    Check if voltage is greater than or equal to -48.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set voltage to -70.
        Set glycine to the sum of glycine and 0.8.
    End the conditional block.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 1.
End the loop.
Display glycine to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because this cell is slow and simple and needs no framework.
DT, TAU, VR, VTH, VRESET, REFRAC, RIN = 0.1, 25.0, -65.0, -48.0, -70.0, 25.0, 700.0

def run(pa, ms=1000.0):
    v, refr, gly, t, spikes = VR, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0, 0
    while t < ms:
        gly -= gly * DT / 10.0        # glycine decays in about 10 ms
        if refr > 0.0:
            refr -= DT
            v = VRESET
        else:
            drive = RIN * pa * 1e-3   # megaohms times picoamps gives millivolts
            v += DT * (-(v - VR) + drive) / TAU
            if v >= VTH:
                v, refr, spikes = VRESET, REFRAC, spikes + 1
                gly += 0.8            # glycine dominant release
        t += DT
    return spikes, gly

for pa in (20.0, 40.0, 80.0):
    n, g = run(pa)
    print("%4.0f pA gives %2d spikes, glycine %.2f" % (pa, n, g))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A body under 21 micrometres among giants of 50, a spike over a millisecond wide, a ceiling near thirty per second.

It alone puts glycine receptors on the output cells, its axon never leaves home, and it alone wears no aggrecan cage.

CHAPTER 126. HUSH-LIFTING LEARNING NEURON (COG) (PUC)

This is the cell that learns from silence, writing a memory in the instant a long hush is lifted off it.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is a Previously Unnamed Construct, a name for something real but unlabelled: the cerebellar nuclear output neuron in its learning mode.

Purkinje cells, the cortex's only output, do nothing but inhibit, and Pugh and Raman showed in 2006 that this cell learns only when excitation is followed by deep inhibition and release.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A large neuron with five to fifteen thick dendrites radiating like a sea urchin, its body blanketed by Purkinje endings.

Low threshold calcium channels are denser on the dendrites, so the learning signal is born where the excitation lands.

SIZE:

The body is 25 to 50 micrometres across, among the cerebellum's largest, and the dendritic tree spans 300 to 600.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The construct is strongest in the large glutamate-releasing output neurons, of two conserved forms called class A and class B.

Interposed variants time reflexes, dentate variants time skilled sequences, fastigial variants time balance.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the dentate, emboliform, globose and fastigial nuclei, the anterior interposed best studied for eyeblink timing.

The defining interface is where Purkinje axons leave the white matter for the nuclear grey.

NUMBER:

This is not an extra population but a mode of the one to two million neurons already allotted to the cerebellar nuclei.

Perhaps half to three quarters show it, so 500,000 to 1,000,000, about 0.0009 percent of all neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Left alone it paces itself at 30 to over 100 narrow pulses a second.

Purkinje inhibition drags it to minus 75 millivolts, and 150 milliseconds there resets the T-type calcium channels built on CaV3.1, so release fires a burst.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are Purkinje inhibition on the body and mossy fibre excitation on the dendrites, through AMPA and calcium-passing NMDA receptors.

Outputs reach the ventrolateral thalamus, red nucleus and vestibular nuclei, and a nucleo-olivary branch quietens the inferior olive.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

One to three micrometres thick, it leaves through the superior cerebellar peduncle, the cerebellum's outflow cable.

At roughly 10 to 40 metres per second, a correction reaches the thalamus in milliseconds.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases glutamate, the brain's main accelerator, and receives GABA from Purkinje cells plus glycine from local interneurons.

MYELINATION:

Its outgoing axon is among the most heavily myelinated in the brain, which is why cerebellar timing is measured in single milliseconds.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high, because it fires 50 to 100 times a second whether or not anything happens, and repeated calcium loads are met by astrocyte lactate.

PLASTICITY:

Mossy fibre stimulation at 100 per second, paired with a 150 to 250 millisecond hyperpolarisation and rebound, potentiated its currents by 43 percent.

Calcium arrives first through NMDA receptors, then through low threshold channels during rebound, so the cell multiplies two signals separated in time.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

These neurons, not their small neighbours, wear perineuronal nets rich in aggrecan, built by neuron and glia together.

Removing the nets enlarges Purkinje currents and impairs eyeblink conditioning, so the cage is learning machinery.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this mode and the nuclei become a relay: fast, accurate and permanently naive.

A conditioned blink would never shift to land before the puff, and a skill would fade when the cortex fell silent.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The dendrites compute, holding a chemical tag of recent excitation and multiplying it by a rebound hundreds of milliseconds later.

One caveat: rebounds are robust in slices but hard to find in intact animals, so make rebound strength tunable.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: Purkinje inhibition 20 to 100 per second, mossy fibre excitation 0 to 200, climbing fibres 0 to 10.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate spikes 0 to 300 per second to thalamus, red nucleus and vestibular nuclei, plus a nucleo-olivary branch.

• STATE: voltage, T-type availability 0 to 1, calcium tag, hush counter, synaptic weight.

• PARAMETERS: soma 25 to 50 micrometres, resting rate 30 to 100 per second, spike width 0.5 to 1.0 milliseconds, hush threshold 150 milliseconds, gain 0.43.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds, calcium tag 100, channel recovery 150 to 250.

• FAILURE MODE: CACNA1G mutations cause spinocerebellar ataxia type 42, and disordered nuclear firing underlies ataxia, dystonia and tremor.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to strengthen a mossy fibre synapse only after a long Purkinje hush is lifted.
Create a number variable called weight with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called calcium_tag with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called hush_length with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
While clock is less than 1000 remains true, repeat the following steps.
    Set calcium_tag to the sum of calcium_tag and 1.
    Check if both clock is greater than 300 and clock is less than 550 are true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set hush_length to the sum of hush_length and 1.
    Else if both hush_length is greater than 150 and calcium_tag is greater than 1 are true, then perform the following actions.
        Set weight to the product of weight and 1.43.
        Set hush_length to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 1.
End the loop.
Display weight to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>
/* C is chosen because a cerebellum simulator runs this rule millions of times a second. */
int main(void) {
    double weight = 1.0, tag = 0.0, hush = 0.0;
    int t;
    for (t = 0; t < 1000; t++) {
        int mf = (t >= 100 && t < 600);   /* mossy fibre burst */
        int pc = (t >= 300 && t < 550);   /* Purkinje hush, 250 ms */
        if (mf) tag += 1.0;               /* NMDA calcium tag */
        tag *= 0.99;
        if (pc) { hush += 1.0; continue; }
        if (hush >= 150.0 && tag > 1.0) { /* 43 percent gain, Pugh and Raman 2006 */
            weight += 0.43 * (1.0 - exp(-(hush - 150.0) / 100.0)) * tag / (tag + 5.0);
            printf("t=%d ms: hush lifted, synapse grew\n", t);
        }
        hush = 0.0;
    }
    printf("final weight=%.3f\n", weight);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron known that refuses to learn from excitation alone and insists on being silenced first.

Look for a large pacemaker caged in aggrecan, answering a released silence with a burst and a stronger synapse.


PART NINE: BRAINSTEM - MIDBRAIN, PONS, AND MEDULLA

CHAPTER 127. GENERAL SOMATIC EFFERENT MOTOR NEURONS (Eye-And-Tongue Aiming Neuron) (NON-COG)

Every time your eyes flick across this line of text, a few thousand cells buried in your brainstem fire in a precisely graded chorus, and the world obligingly stays still.

The existing label is a committee minute built from embryology, so it tells a student nothing about what the cell does.

Eye-And-Tongue names the only two territories this column serves, because the general somatic efferent nuclei, the oculomotor, trochlear, abducens and hypoglossal, drive just the six extraocular muscles of each eye and the muscles of the tongue, all of them descended from somites rather than from the pharyngeal arches.

Aiming names the work, since both jobs are acts of pointing, the eyeball at a target and the tongue tip at a sound or a morsel.

Neuron marks it as the final common path, so that when one dies the eye or the tongue simply cannot be aimed.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A general somatic efferent (GSE) motor neuron is a brainstem nerve cell whose long output fibre leaves the skull and ends directly on a skeletal muscle fibre.

Efferent simply means carrying signals outward, away from the brain, and somatic means the muscle it controls is one you can in principle move on purpose.

These cells drive the six muscles that aim each eye, the muscle that lifts the upper eyelid, and the muscles that shape the tongue.

They are the final common path: no matter how clever the decision upstream, the eye moves only if these cells fire.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is a plump multipolar shape, meaning many branches radiate from it in several directions.

Ten to twenty stout dendrites, the tree-like input branches, spread across the nucleus and often bundle together with the dendrites of neighbours into cables that share incoming signals.

A single thick axon, the output cable, leaves the base of the cell and heads straight for the skull base.

The cytoplasm is packed with dark-staining Nissl substance, which is stacked protein-making machinery, betraying the enormous manufacturing load of a cell that must keep a metre-long-scale process supplied.

SIZE:

Cell bodies measure roughly 25 to 60 micrometres across, where a micrometre is one thousandth of a millimetre.

That makes them among the largest neurons in the brainstem, comparable to spinal motor neurons.

Axons are 5 to 15 micrometres in diameter including their fatty sheath, and run 3 to 8 centimetres from brainstem to muscle.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two families exist in every extraocular pool.

Singly innervated fibre motor neurons are large, fast, and end in one compact contact per muscle fibre, generating twitch force for saccades, the rapid darting eye movements.

Multiply innervated fibre motor neurons are smaller, end in strings of small contacts along a muscle fibre, and produce slow graded tension for steady gaze holding.

Tongue motor neurons split into protrusor and retrusor pools, plus a small population driving the tongue open during breathing.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Four nuclei hold them: the oculomotor nucleus in the midbrain at the level of the superior colliculus, the trochlear nucleus in the midbrain at the inferior colliculus, the abducens nucleus in the pons beneath the facial colliculus, and the hypoglossal nucleus in the medulla beside the midline.

All four sit in a column immediately next to the midline, the most medial position in the brainstem, which is the anatomical signature of this class.

The abducens nucleus sits at a genuine interface: the axon bundle of the facial nerve loops over it, so pons motor territory and branchial motor territory are physically wrapped around each other there.

NUMBER:

The human abducens nucleus contains about 6,454 cells per side by classical counting, and cell number does not change appreciably after birth.

Adding oculomotor, trochlear and hypoglossal pools, a defensible total is 30,000 to 45,000 GSE motor neurons across both sides, and confidence is moderate because modern unbiased stereology has not been applied to all four nuclei in humans.

That is roughly one in two million of the body's neurons, and well under one thousandth of one percent of the brainstem's approximately 200 million neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage sits near minus 65 millivolts, and threshold near minus 50 millivolts.

Extraocular motor neurons are extraordinarily fast, sustaining 300 to 500 spikes per second during a saccade, far above the 50 spikes per second ceiling of most limb motor neurons.

Their firing rate encodes two things added together: a rate step proportional to where the eye is being held, and a rate pulse proportional to how fast it is moving.

Hypoglossal motor neurons are slower, firing 10 to 60 spikes per second, and are strongly modulated by the breathing rhythm.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from the paramedian pontine reticular formation for horizontal saccade pulses, from the nucleus prepositus hypoglossi and medial vestibular nucleus for the position step, from the vestibular nuclei for reflex gaze stabilisation, and from the superior colliculus indirectly.

A typical extraocular motor neuron carries several thousand synapses, the tiny junctions where one neuron speaks to another.

Output is a single kind of junction, the neuromuscular junction, one per muscle fibre for twitch fibres.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thick, straight and almost unbranched until its terminal arbour.

Conduction speed reaches 60 to 80 metres per second, so the command arrives at the eye muscle in about one millisecond.

An extraocular motor neuron contacts only 3 to 10 muscle fibres, the smallest motor unit in the body, which is why eye position can be set so finely.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine, the classic muscle-commanding chemical messenger, released onto nicotinic acetylcholine receptors on the muscle fibre.

Calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) is co-released and helps maintain the junction.

There is no known inhibitory transmitter released by these cells.

MYELINATION:

Heavily myelinated, meaning the axon is wrapped in a fatty insulating sheath that makes signals jump between gaps rather than crawl.

Oligodendrocytes make the sheath inside the brainstem, and Schwann cells take over the moment the axon leaves at the nerve root, at a boundary called the Obersteiner-Redlich zone.

Internodes, the insulated stretches between gaps, run 800 to 1,500 micrometres.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high, and unusual in kind.

Sustaining 400 spikes per second means pumping sodium and potassium back across the membrane relentlessly, and extraocular motor neurons carry mitochondrial densities among the highest of any neuron.

They rely heavily on oxidative phosphorylation, the oxygen-using energy pathway, which is one reason they fail early in mitochondrial diseases.

PLASTICITY:

Modest but real.

The position-holding gain is recalibrated continuously by the cerebellar flocculus, so that if you wear magnifying spectacles the reflex gain adjusts over days.

Neuromuscular junctions remodel after nerve injury, sprouting to reoccupy vacated muscle fibres.

Long-term potentiation of the classic hippocampal kind is not a feature here.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes around these nuclei are unusually rich in glutamate transporters, because the excitatory input rates are extreme and spillover would be catastrophic for gaze accuracy.

At the neuromuscular junction a specialised glial cell, the terminal Schwann cell, caps each junction, senses transmitter release, and repairs the junction after damage.

Microglia in the hypoglossal nucleus are famously reactive, clustering around injured motor neurons within hours in a response called synaptic stripping.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the midline brainstem loses its only direct line to the outside world.

The oculomotor nucleus becomes a silent map with no eyes to move: the eye drifts outward and downward, the eyelid drops, and reading, tracking and even balance degrade because gaze can no longer be stabilised.

The medulla without hypoglossal motor neurons is a brain that can plan speech and swallowing perfectly and execute neither.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Dendrites of neighbouring motor neurons bundle into shared cables, so excitation leaks sideways between cells and helps a pool fire as a unit.

Some pairs are electrically coupled through gap junctions, tiny protein tunnels that let current pass directly.

The axon terminal instructs the muscle fibre what kind of fibre to become, controlling its gene expression from a distance through activity pattern and released peptides.

These cells also signal retrogradely to their own inputs using nitric oxide, a gas that diffuses backwards across the synapse.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: burst signal from paramedian pontine reticular formation, 0 to 800 spikes per second; position signal from nucleus prepositus hypoglossi, 0 to 200 spikes per second; vestibular head-velocity signal, plus or minus 150 spikes per second; inhibitory gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) input from omnipause neurons.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine onto 3 to 10 extraocular muscle fibres, or 20 to 100 tongue muscle fibres; recurrent collaterals are sparse or absent in extraocular pools.

• STATE: membrane voltage; spike-frequency adaptation variable; calcium-activated potassium activation; synaptic conductance per input class.

• PARAMETERS: recruitment threshold minus 15 to plus 10 degrees of eye position; position sensitivity 2 to 8 spikes per second per degree; velocity sensitivity 0.5 to 1.5 spikes per second per degree per second; maximum rate 400 to 500 spikes per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 3 to 6 milliseconds; synaptic 1 to 2 milliseconds; muscle mechanical filter 8 to 12 milliseconds; neural integrator leak 20 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) the hypoglossal pool degenerates early, giving slurred speech and a wasted tongue, while the oculomotor pool is famously resistant; in myasthenia gravis the junction fails and the eyelid droops; in mitochondrial disease the eye muscles freeze in chronic progressive external ophthalmoplegia.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert a desired eye position and eye velocity into the firing rate of one extraocular motor neuron using the pulse and step rule.
Create a number variable called recruitment threshold with initial value -10.
Create a number variable called position sensitivity with initial value 4.
Create a number variable called velocity sensitivity with initial value 0.9.
Create a number variable called maximum rate with initial value 400.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called rate history with initial value empty list.
Define a function called compute rate that performs the following operations.
    Check if desired position is less than or equal to recruitment threshold.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 0.
        Return firing rate to the calling function.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of desired position and recruitment threshold.
    Set position term to the product of that difference and position sensitivity.
    Set velocity term to the product of desired velocity and velocity sensitivity.
    Set firing rate to the sum of position term and velocity term.
    Check if firing rate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if firing rate is greater than maximum rate.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to maximum rate.
    End the conditional block.
    Return firing rate to the calling function.
End the function definition.
For each sample in the movement plan, perform the following operations.
    Call the function named compute rate.
    Add firing rate to the end of rate history.
    Display firing rate to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the pulse-step rule is arithmetic, and a reader with no programming background can follow it line by line.

# Extraocular motoneuron: firing rate carries eye position and eye velocity.
def motoneuron_rate(position_deg, velocity_deg_per_s,
                    threshold_deg=-10.0, k=4.0, r=0.9, max_rate=400.0):
    """Firing rate in spikes per second for one extraocular motor neuron."""
    if position_deg <= threshold_deg:
        return 0.0                      # below recruitment threshold, the cell is silent
    rate = k * (position_deg - threshold_deg) + r * velocity_deg_per_s
    if rate < 0.0:
        rate = 0.0                      # a cell cannot fire at a negative rate
    if rate > max_rate:
        rate = max_rate                 # ceiling set by the refractory period
    return rate

def saccade_then_hold(start_deg, end_deg, peak_velocity):
    """Three snapshots: old fixation, mid-saccade pulse, new fixation step."""
    midpoint = (start_deg + end_deg) / 2.0
    return [("old fixation",   motoneuron_rate(start_deg, 0.0)),
            ("saccade pulse",  motoneuron_rate(midpoint, peak_velocity)),
            ("new fixation",   motoneuron_rate(end_deg, 0.0))]

for phase, rate in saccade_then_hold(0.0, 20.0, 400.0):
    print("%-15s %6.1f spikes per second" % (phase, rate))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A very large multipolar cell sitting almost on the brainstem midline, stuffed with mitochondria, stained strongly for choline acetyltransferase, the enzyme that makes acetylcholine, with a thick fast axon that leaves the brain entirely and a motor unit so tiny that a handful of muscle fibres is its whole world.

CHAPTER 128. SPECIAL VISCERAL EFFERENT, ALSO CALLED BRANCHIAL MOTOR, NEURONS (Face-And-Throat Moving Neuron) (NON-COG)

Your smile, your voice and your swallow are all commanded by cells that descend from the machinery ancient fish used to work their gills.

The old name records an embryological filing category, the pharyngeal or branchial arches, and no student hearing it learns which muscles move.

Face-And-Throat names the actual territory, because these cells sit in the trigeminal motor nucleus, the facial motor nucleus and the nucleus ambiguus and supply the muscles of chewing, the muscles of facial expression, and the palate, pharynx and larynx across five cranial nerves.

Moving names the work plainly, since every chew, smile, swallow and spoken syllable is executed by this one small population.

Neuron marks it as the cell whose loss makes a facial palsy droop and a voice go hoarse.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A special visceral efferent (SVE) neuron, better called a branchial motor neuron, is a brainstem cell that drives a skeletal muscle derived from the embryonic pharyngeal arches, the tissue blocks that became gill supports in fish and became jaws, face, larynx and pharynx in us.

Visceral in the old name is misleading, because these muscles are fully voluntary striated muscle, not gut muscle.

They chew, blink, smile, speak, swallow, tense the eardrum and turn the head.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Large multipolar cells with 6 to 15 thick dendrites that often stay within their own subnucleus rather than spreading widely, which keeps each muscle's command pool electrically separate.

The cell body is rich in Nissl substance and has a large pale nucleus with a single prominent nucleolus.

Their axons take famously roundabout courses, the facial nerve axons looping right over the abducens nucleus before turning forward.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 25 to 55 micrometres across.

Axons are 6 to 14 micrometres in diameter with sheath, and travel 4 to 15 centimetres, the longest reaching the larynx and the sternocleidomastoid muscle of the neck.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The facial nucleus divides into at least four subnuclei, each for one facial territory, with the dorsal subnuclei driving the forehead and the lateral subnuclei driving the lower face.

The trigeminal motor nucleus has a jaw-closing division for masseter and temporalis and a jaw-opening division for digastric and mylohyoid.

The nucleus ambiguus is organised head to tail by target: pharynx first, then larynx, and it also contains a completely different cell class, cardiac vagal neurons, that is described under preganglionic parasympathetic neurons.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The trigeminal motor nucleus in the mid pons, the facial motor nucleus in the caudal pons, the nucleus ambiguus in the ventrolateral medulla, and the spinal accessory nucleus in the upper cervical spinal cord.

All sit in a column just lateral and ventral to the general somatic efferent column, having migrated away from the midline during development.

The nucleus ambiguus lies at an important interface: it is embedded in the ventrolateral medullary reticular formation, so respiratory rhythm generators and swallowing pattern generators surround its cells directly.

NUMBER:

The human facial motor nucleus contains about 10,000 neurons per side by comparative stereological counting.

Adding the trigeminal motor nucleus, nucleus ambiguus and the cranial accessory pool, a defensible total is 50,000 to 70,000 across both sides, with moderate confidence for the facial nucleus and low confidence elsewhere.

That is about one in one and a half million of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the nervous system, and about 0.03 percent of the brainstem's 200 million.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage near minus 68 millivolts, threshold near minus 50 millivolts, input resistance 3 to 12 megohms.

They obey the size principle, meaning small cells with high input resistance are recruited first and large cells later as demand grows.

Sustained rates run 10 to 60 spikes per second, with brief bursts to 100 spikes per second during a swallow or a startle blink.

Laryngeal cells of the nucleus ambiguus fire in tight bursts locked to the breathing cycle.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from the reticular formation pattern generators for chewing, blinking, swallowing and vocalising, from the sensory trigeminal nuclei for reflexes, from the red nucleus and, in humans uniquely strongly, directly from the motor cortex onto lower facial and laryngeal pools.

Each cell carries several thousand synapses, roughly 60 percent inhibitory using GABA and glycine.

Outputs go exclusively to neuromuscular junctions on branchiomeric muscle.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thick and fast, conducting at 50 to 70 metres per second.

Motor unit size varies enormously: about 25 muscle fibres per neuron for the delicate muscles around the eye, and over 1,000 for the powerful masseter.

Axon collaterals within the nucleus are sparse, so there is little of the recurrent inhibition seen in the spinal cord, one reason branchial motor pools can fire so synchronously.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction, acting on nicotinic acetylcholine receptors.

Calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) is co-stored and released with heavy use, promoting receptor clustering.

MYELINATION:

Thickly myelinated by oligodendrocytes centrally and Schwann cells peripherally.

The facial nerve is unusual in having a very long bony canal, so its myelinated axons are uniquely vulnerable to swelling, which is the basis of Bell palsy, a sudden one-sided facial paralysis.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High but not extreme, lower than extraocular motor neurons because sustained rates are lower.

Chewing muscles impose long duty cycles, so trigeminal motor neurons maintain large mitochondrial pools and depend on lactate handed over by neighbouring astrocytes during prolonged activity.

PLASTICITY:

Considerable at the periphery and modest centrally.

After facial nerve injury, surviving axons sprout and reinnervate wrong targets, producing synkinesis, in which a person blinks and the corner of the mouth twitches.

Central synaptic strength onto facial motor neurons is adjusted during motor learning of speech, and infants remodel these pools heavily during babbling.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The facial nucleus is the classic laboratory model for microglial behaviour: after axon injury, microglia proliferate within 24 hours, insert themselves between synapses and the cell body, and physically detach inputs, a process called synaptic stripping that is far more vigorous here than in most brain regions.

Astrocytes in the nucleus ambiguus are tightly coupled to local blood vessels and help match blood flow to the bursts of a swallow.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take them away and the pons and medulla become a brain that thinks and feels and can show none of it.

The face goes slack and expressionless, the jaw hangs, the voice disappears, and swallowing fails so that saliva enters the lungs.

The region loses its entire capacity for social signalling, which in humans is arguably as important as limb movement.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Dendrites of facial motor neurons bundle into shared cables that let a subnucleus behave as a coherent unit for expression.

Motor neurons in the nucleus ambiguus release nitric oxide that dilates nearby capillaries, tuning their own blood supply.

They perform local protein synthesis in dendrites during recovery from injury, and they release neuregulin at the axon surface, a signal that instructs Schwann cells how thick to build the myelin sheath.

They also express receptors for immune signals, so systemic inflammation directly changes facial motor excitability.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: descending corticobulbar drive, 0 to 100 spikes per second; central pattern generator drive from the pontomedullary reticular formation with chewing at 1 to 2 hertz and swallowing as single 0.5 second bursts; trigeminal sensory reflex input; inhibitory GABA and glycine input.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine onto 25 to 1,000 muscle fibres depending on the target muscle.

• STATE: membrane voltage; recruitment status; calcium-dependent afterhyperpolarisation; fatigue variable of the motor unit.

• PARAMETERS: recruitment threshold current 0.5 to 6.5 nanoamperes across the pool; firing gain 8 to 22 spikes per second per nanoampere; maximum sustained rate 60 to 120 spikes per second; twitch force per unit 0.4 to 8 millinewtons.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 5 to 10 milliseconds; afterhyperpolarisation 40 to 100 milliseconds; muscle twitch 20 to 40 milliseconds; tetanic fusion above about 25 spikes per second.

• FAILURE MODE: bulbar ALS destroys these pools, giving slurred speech, a weak cough and fatal aspiration; Möbius syndrome is congenital absence of facial and abducens nuclei, producing a face that cannot smile; Bell palsy is a reversible conduction block of their axons.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to recruit a pool of branchial motor neurons in size order and to convert the recruited firing into muscle force.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 3.
Create a number variable called active count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called total force with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called active rates with initial value empty list.
For each cell in the motor pool, perform the following operations.
    Check if drive is less than or equal to the threshold of cell.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of drive and the threshold of cell.
    Set cell rate to the product of that difference and the gain of cell.
    Check if cell rate is greater than 120.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set cell rate to 120.
    End the conditional block.
    Set fusion to the quotient of cell rate divided by the sum of cell rate and 25.
    Set unit force to the product of fusion and the twitch force of cell.
    Calculate the sum of total force and unit force.
    Set total force to that sum.
    Calculate the sum of active count and 1.
    Set active count to that sum.
    Add cell rate to the end of active rates.
End the loop.
Display active count to the console.
Display total force to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the size principle is best shown as a plain loop over a list of cells with different thresholds, which reads almost like the English version above.

# Facial motor pool: orderly recruitment by cell size, then rate coding to force.
pool = [{"id": i,
         "threshold_nA": 0.5 + 0.30 * i,      # bigger cell needs more drive
         "gain_hz_per_nA": 22.0 - 0.7 * i,    # bigger cell has a flatter rate curve
         "twitch_mN": 0.4 * (i + 1)}          # bigger cell pulls harder
        for i in range(20)]

def pool_output(drive_nA):
    """Return active unit count, summed firing rate and predicted muscle force."""
    active, summed_rate, force = 0, 0.0, 0.0
    for cell in pool:
        if drive_nA <= cell["threshold_nA"]:
            continue                          # not yet recruited
        rate = cell["gain_hz_per_nA"] * (drive_nA - cell["threshold_nA"])
        rate = min(rate, 120.0)               # firing ceiling
        fusion = rate / (rate + 25.0)         # twitches merge into smooth force
        active += 1
        summed_rate += rate
        force += cell["twitch_mN"] * fusion
    return active, summed_rate, force

for drive in (0.4, 1.5, 3.0, 6.0):
    n, r, f = pool_output(drive)
    print("drive %.1f nA -> %2d units, %7.1f Hz summed, %6.2f mN force"
          % (drive, n, r, f))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A large acetylcholine-making motor neuron that has migrated away from the midline into the lateral brainstem, whose axon takes a bizarre curving detour before leaving the skull, whose target muscle came from a gill arch, and whose nucleus responds to injury with the most dramatic microglial synaptic stripping anywhere in the nervous system.

CHAPTER 129. GENERAL VISCERAL EFFERENT PREGANGLIONIC PARASYMPATHETIC NEURONS (Rest-And-Digest Outflow Neuron) (NON-COG)

Between every heartbeat, a few thousand quiet cells in your medulla lean on the brake, and the reason a resting human heart beats 70 times a minute instead of 100 is that they never let go.

The old label is a committee minute of embryological categories that names no organ and no effect.

Rest-And-Digest names the branch of the autonomic nervous system these cells begin, the parasympathetic side that slows the heart, narrows the pupil, wets the mouth and pushes food along.

Outflow names the architecture and corrects the usual misconception that these neurons reach the organ themselves, because they do not, stopping short at a peripheral ganglion and handing the order on, which is exactly what preganglionic means.

Neuron marks it as one of a small and specific set, the Edinger-Westphal nucleus for pupil and lens, the superior and inferior salivatory nuclei for the tear, submandibular and parotid glands, and the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus, which alone supplies the heart, lungs and gut down to the transverse colon.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A general visceral efferent (GVE) preganglionic parasympathetic neuron is a brainstem cell whose axon leaves the brain and stops at a relay station called a ganglion, a small cluster of nerve cells outside the central nervous system, where a second neuron takes over and finishes the job.

Preganglionic means before that relay.

Parasympathetic means it belongs to the rest-and-digest half of the automatic nervous system.

These cells constrict the pupil, focus the lens, make tears and saliva, slow the heart, tighten the airways and drive the stomach and gut.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Small to medium multipolar or fusiform cells, fusiform meaning spindle-shaped with a branch at each end.

Dendrites are long, thin and sparsely branched, and in the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus they extend outward into the overlying nucleus of the solitary tract, where they collect sensory information directly.

That dendritic reach across a nuclear border is the structural signature of a reflex arc built into one cell.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 15 to 35 micrometres across, distinctly smaller than the somatic motor neurons beside them.

Axons are thin, 1.5 to 4 micrometres including sheath, and can be very long: a vagal preganglionic axon travels 30 to 40 centimetres to reach a ganglion in the wall of the stomach.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Four anatomical pools with distinct jobs.

Edinger-Westphal preganglionic neurons in the midbrain constrict the pupil and focus the lens through the ciliary ganglion.

Superior salivatory neurons in the pons make tears and submandibular saliva; inferior salivatory neurons in the medulla drive the parotid gland.

Dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus cells are the gut and airway pool, while a separate and physiologically distinct pool of cardiac vagal neurons sits in the nucleus ambiguus and produces the fast, breath-locked slowing of the heart.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Edinger-Westphal nucleus in the midbrain periaqueductal grey; superior salivatory nucleus in the caudal pons near the facial genu; inferior salivatory nucleus in the rostral medulla; dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus in the dorsal medulla in the floor of the fourth ventricle; and cardiac vagal neurons in the external formation of the nucleus ambiguus in the ventrolateral medulla.

The dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus sits at a critical interface with the nucleus of the solitary tract directly above it and the area postrema beside it, a spot where the blood-brain barrier is leaky, so circulating hormones and toxins reach these cells directly.

NUMBER:

Careful human counting gives 13,802 plus or minus 844 presumed motor neurons per side in the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus, within a total nuclear population of 16,826 plus or minus 967.

Adding Edinger-Westphal, both salivatory nuclei and the cardiac vagal pool, a defensible total is 40,000 to 60,000 across both sides, with high confidence for the vagal nucleus and low confidence for the others.

That is roughly one in 1.7 million of the body's neurons, and about 0.025 percent of the brainstem's 200 million.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage near minus 65 millivolts and a very high input resistance of 200 to 800 megohms, meaning a tiny current makes a large voltage change.

They are slow, firing 0.5 to 15 spikes per second, and many are spontaneously active without any input at all.

A powerful A-type potassium current, an outward current that switches on when the cell depolarises and then slowly fades, delays the first spike and caps the maximum rate, which is why vagal output is inherently unhurried.

Cardiac vagal neurons in the nucleus ambiguus are the exception: they fire in sharp bursts and are silenced during each inspiration, which is the origin of respiratory sinus arrhythmia, the normal speeding of the heart on breathing in.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from the nucleus of the solitary tract carrying gut, lung, blood pressure and taste information, from the hypothalamic paraventricular nucleus carrying stress and hunger state, from the central nucleus of the amygdala carrying emotion, and from respiratory neurons carrying the breathing rhythm.

Outputs are cholinergic synapses onto postganglionic neurons in the ciliary, pterygopalatine, submandibular and otic ganglia, and onto the thousands of small ganglia embedded in the walls of the heart, airways and gut.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin and slow, conducting at 3 to 15 metres per second, so a vagal command takes 30 to 100 milliseconds to reach the heart.

Each preganglionic axon branches to contact many postganglionic cells, a divergence of roughly 1 to 8 for precise pupil control but far higher for the gut.

Cardiac vagal axons are the faster subgroup, which is what allows beat-to-beat heart rate control.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine onto nicotinic acetylcholine receptors on the postganglionic cell.

Nitric oxide is co-released by many vagal preganglionic neurons and diffuses to modulate neighbours.

Peptides including vasoactive intestinal peptide (VIP) and pituitary adenylate cyclase-activating polypeptide (PACAP) are co-released during strong activity and prolong the effect for seconds.

MYELINATION:

Thinly myelinated, classified as B fibres, with sheaths only two to eight wraps thick.

This is the reason the vagus is preferentially recruited at low stimulation currents in clinical vagus nerve stimulation, and the reason vagal conduction is slow.

Some gut-projecting axons lose their sheath entirely before reaching their ganglion.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low by neuronal standards because firing rates are low, but the axon is extremely long, so the cost of axonal transport, the conveyor system that carries proteins along the fibre, dominates the budget.

These cells are unusually dependent on mitochondrial health along the axon, and are among the first neurons to accumulate damage in Parkinson disease.

PLASTICITY:

Substantial and clinically important.

Baroreflex gain, the strength of the heart-slowing response to a rise in blood pressure, is reset over days by exercise training and over hours by illness.

Chronic vagus nerve stimulation for epilepsy and depression produces lasting changes in synaptic strength onto these cells.

Their peripheral targets remodel too: after gut inflammation, the number of contacts onto enteric ganglia changes measurably.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes in the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus are specialised glucose sensors, releasing lactate that directly changes vagal firing and thereby links blood sugar to gut motility, a job astrocytes elsewhere do not perform.

Because the neighbouring area postrema lacks a normal blood-brain barrier, microglia here are chronically primed and respond to circulating inflammatory signals faster than microglia in protected regions.

Around the axons in the neck, Schwann cells face constant mechanical strain from swallowing and pulsation and maintain unusually elastic sheaths.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the dorsal medulla becomes a listening post with no voice.

The nucleus of the solitary tract would still receive every signal from heart, lung and gut, and nothing could be done about any of it.

The pupil would sit wide and fixed, the mouth would go dry, the heart would race at its intrinsic pacemaker rate of about 100 beats per minute, and the stomach would stop emptying.

The medulla would lose its entire calming, restorative flavour.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Dendrites reach across into the solitary nucleus and receive sensory input directly, so a reflex can complete without an interneuron.

Nitric oxide released from these cells acts as a retrograde messenger, travelling backwards to change how strongly the solitary nucleus speaks to them.

They release peptides from the cell body and dendrites, not only from axon terminals, producing slow volume transmission through the local tissue.

They are targets for circulating hormones including leptin, insulin, ghrelin and glucagon-like peptide-1, so this is one of the few places where the bloodstream talks directly to a motor neuron.

Alpha-synuclein aggregates, the protein clumps of Parkinson disease, appear here earlier than almost anywhere else in the brain.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: excitatory glutamate from the nucleus of the solitary tract, 0 to 30 spikes per second; inhibitory GABA from local medullary interneurons, gated by the respiratory cycle; hormonal input including glucose 3 to 8 millimoles per litre, leptin, and cholecystokinin.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine onto postganglionic neurons of the ciliary, pterygopalatine, submandibular, otic, cardiac and enteric ganglia, at 0.5 to 15 spikes per second.

• STATE: membrane voltage; A-type potassium inactivation; calcium concentration; peptide vesicle pool; slow respiratory gate phase.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 200 to 800 megohms; A-current conductance 40 to 80 nanosiemens; spike threshold minus 45 millivolts; conduction velocity 3 to 15 metres per second; divergence ratio 1 to 8 or more.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 25 to 40 milliseconds; A-current recovery 100 to 200 milliseconds; respiratory gate 3 to 5 seconds per cycle; peptide action 5 to 60 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: early alpha-synuclein pathology here causes the constipation and gastroparesis that precede Parkinson disease motor symptoms by a decade; loss of cardiac vagal tone flattens heart rate variability and predicts cardiac death; damage to the Edinger-Westphal pathway gives a fixed dilated pupil, a warning sign of brain herniation.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to produce the slow, breath-gated output of a preganglionic parasympathetic neuron whose first spike is delayed by an A-type potassium current.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -65.
Create a number variable called a current availability with initial value 0.3.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.3.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called spike times with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 8000, repeat the following steps.
    Set breathing phase to the respiratory gate value at step.
    Set gated drive to the product of drive and breathing phase.
    Set potassium brake to the product of a current availability and the difference of membrane voltage and -90.
    Set leak to the product of 0.01 and the difference of membrane voltage and -65.
    Calculate the difference of gated drive and the sum of leak and potassium brake.
    Set voltage change to the quotient of that difference divided by 0.30.
    Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and voltage change.
    Set membrane voltage to that sum.
    Set target availability to the availability value for membrane voltage.
    Calculate the difference of target availability and a current availability.
    Set recovery to the quotient of that difference divided by 150.
    Calculate the sum of a current availability and recovery.
    Set a current availability to that sum.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -45.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add step to the end of spike times.
        Calculate the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set spike count to that sum.
        Set membrane voltage to -70.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of step and 1.
    Set step to that sum.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the A-current delay only makes sense when you watch voltage evolve step by step, and Python's readable loop makes each millisecond of that evolution visible.

import math

DT_MS = 1.0                                    # one millisecond per step
C, G_LEAK, E_LEAK = 0.30, 0.010, -65.0         # nanofarad, microsiemens, millivolts
G_A, E_K, TAU_H = 0.060, -90.0, 150.0          # A-current strength, reversal, recovery
V_TH, V_RESET = -45.0, -70.0

def a_inf(v):                                  # A-current activation, tiny at rest
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp(-(v + 40.0) / 6.0))

def h_inf(v):                                  # A-current availability, high when hyperpolarised
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp((v + 70.0) / 6.0))

def simulate(ms=8000, drive_nA=0.30, resp_hz=0.25, gate_depth=0.8):
    """Vagal preganglionic cell: A-current delays spikes, breathing gates them."""
    v, h, spikes = E_LEAK, h_inf(E_LEAK), []
    for step in range(ms):
        t_s = step * DT_MS / 1000.0
        gate = 1.0 - gate_depth * max(0.0, math.sin(2.0 * math.pi * resp_hz * t_s))
        i_syn = drive_nA * gate                # inspiration silences the cell
        i_a = G_A * a_inf(v) * h * (v - E_K)   # the potassium brake
        v += DT_MS * (-G_LEAK * (v - E_LEAK) - i_a + i_syn) / C
        h += DT_MS * (h_inf(v) - h) / TAU_H    # brake re-arms only slowly
        if v >= V_TH:
            spikes.append(step)
            v = V_RESET
    return spikes

sp = simulate()
gaps = [b - a for a, b in zip(sp, sp[1:])]
print("spikes in 8 s: %d, mean rate %.2f Hz" % (len(sp), len(sp) / 8.0))
print("longest silent gap: %d ms (the inspiratory pause)" % (max(gaps) if gaps else 0))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small, slow, thinly myelinated acetylcholine-making cell in the dorsal medulla or midbrain that never talks to a muscle directly but always hands off to a ganglion, whose dendrites poke into the sensory nucleus next door, whose firing pauses every time you breathe in, and which is one of the very first neurons in the entire brain to accumulate the protein clumps of Parkinson disease.

CHAPTER 130. SENSORY RELAY NEURONS OF THE TRIGEMINAL, SOLITARY, COCHLEAR, AND VESTIBULAR NUCLEI (First-Stop Sense-Sorting Neuron) (COG)

You can tell which ear a whisper came from because a cell in your brainstem can measure a time difference of ten millionths of a second, which is shorter than a single one of its own spikes.

The existing label is a list of four addresses that no one could speak aloud, yet the four share one job.

First-Stop names the position in the chain, since these are second-order cells, the first place inside the brain where a signal from the face, the tongue and gut, the ear or the balance organ is put down after leaving its ganglion.

Sense-Sorting names the work, because these nuclei do not merely pass signals on but split each stream into parallel channels before sending it upward, so touch on the face goes to the thalamus while pain and temperature branch also to the parabrachial nucleus for the emotional side of the message.

Neuron marks it as a second-order sensory cell, and naming it by function rather than by four Latin nuclei lets a student see the common plan.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are the brainstem's second-order sensory cells, the first cells inside the brain to receive news from the face, the tongue, the inner organs, the ear and the balance organs.

Second-order means the message has already passed through exactly one cell, the sensory nerve fibre coming in from the body.

They are marked cognitive here because they do not merely pass the message along: they sharpen it, compare it across two ears, subtract predictable self-generated signals, and decide what deserves to reach consciousness.

Every conscious sensation from the head, and every reflex that keeps your gaze steady while you walk, begins in this population.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Morphology is deliberately diverse because each computation needs a different shape.

Spherical bushy cells of the cochlear nucleus have one or two short, densely tufted dendrites and a round soma engulfed by a giant synapse.

Octopus cells have all their dendrites projecting from one side of the cell like tentacles, sweeping across the incoming nerve fibres in frequency order.

Vestibular nucleus cells are large multipolar neurons with wide dendritic fields, and solitary nucleus cells are small fusiform or ovoid cells packed tightly around the incoming sensory tract.

SIZE:

Soma diameters span 10 to 60 micrometres.

Solitary nucleus cells are the smallest at 10 to 20 micrometres; bushy cells are 20 to 25 micrometres; octopus cells reach 30 micrometres; and the giant Deiters cells of the lateral vestibular nucleus reach 60 micrometres, among the largest neurons in the brain.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The cochlear nucleus alone contains spherical bushy cells for sound timing, globular bushy cells for sound level comparison, octopus cells for detecting onsets, T-stellate cells for coding sound level over time, D-stellate cells for inhibition across frequencies, and fusiform cells of the dorsal cochlear nucleus for detecting where a sound is in the vertical plane.

The vestibular complex has four nuclei: superior, lateral, medial and inferior.

The trigeminal sensory complex has the principal nucleus for touch and the spinal nucleus in three parts, with the caudal part being effectively the dorsal horn of the face and handling pain and temperature.

The solitary nucleus has a rostral gustatory portion for taste and a caudal portion for heart, lung and gut.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Trigeminal principal nucleus in the mid pons; trigeminal spinal nucleus running from pons to the second cervical spinal segment in three subnuclei called oralis, interpolaris and caudalis; nucleus of the solitary tract in the dorsal medulla; cochlear nuclei on the lateral surface of the pontomedullary junction in dorsal and ventral divisions; vestibular nuclei in the floor of the fourth ventricle spanning pons and medulla.

Two interfaces matter greatly.

The trigeminal nucleus caudalis merges seamlessly into the spinal cord dorsal horn, so face pain and body pain are processed by one continuous sheet.

The nucleus of the solitary tract abuts the area postrema, where the blood-brain barrier is absent, letting circulating nausea-inducing chemicals meet sensory processing directly.

NUMBER:

The human ventral cochlear nucleus and its dorsal partner together hold on the order of 100,000 neurons per side.

The medial vestibular nucleus alone averaged 122,241 neurons in a morphometric study of younger adults, falling to about 75,959 by age 89, roughly a 3 percent loss per decade after forty.

Summing all four sensory families across both sides gives a defensible total of 1.5 to 2.5 million neurons, which is confident to within about a factor of two and represents roughly 1 percent of the brainstem's 200 million neurons and about 0.002 percent of the nervous system's 86 billion.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Extremely varied, and that variety is the point.

Bushy cells have a huge low-threshold potassium conductance that makes the membrane leak the moment it depolarises, giving a membrane time constant under 1 millisecond and forcing the cell to act as a coincidence detector rather than an adder.

Octopus cells are the most extreme neurons known in this respect, with input resistances under 5 megohms and time constants near 200 microseconds.

Medial vestibular neurons are spontaneous pacemakers firing 20 to 100 spikes per second at rest, so they can signal head rotation in both directions by speeding up or slowing down.

Solitary nucleus cells show delayed-onset firing from an A-type potassium current, matching the slow timescale of blood pressure and gut signals.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are the primary sensory fibres, and some of these are the largest synapses in the brain: the endbulb of Held wraps almost entirely around a spherical bushy cell and can drive it to threshold with a single release event.

Octopus cells take small inputs from more than 60 auditory nerve fibres spanning many frequencies.

Outputs go to the superior olivary complex and inferior colliculus for hearing, to the oculomotor nuclei and spinal cord for balance, to the thalamus for conscious touch and taste, and to the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus for autonomic reflexes.

Descending feedback from cortex and from the cerebellum is heavy and continuous.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thick and very fast for the timing pathways, 4 to 8 micrometres and 40 to 70 metres per second for bushy and octopus cells, with axon lengths tuned so that signals from both ears arrive at the comparison point at matched times.

Vestibulospinal axons run the length of the spinal cord.

Trigeminothalamic axons cross the midline and ascend 8 to 10 centimetres.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Most are glutamatergic, using glutamate, the brain's main excitatory messenger, released onto AMPA receptors that in the timing pathways are built from unusually fast subunits so the synaptic current is over in under a millisecond.

A large minority are inhibitory, using glycine in the cochlear and vestibular nuclei and GABA in the solitary and trigeminal nuclei.

A distinct catecholaminergic group in the solitary nucleus, the A2 cell group, makes noradrenaline and co-releases glutamate.

Substance P and CGRP mark the pain-carrying inputs to trigeminal nucleus caudalis.

MYELINATION:

Their axons are among the most heavily myelinated in the brain, with unusually short internodes in the auditory timing pathway that make conduction speed precisely tunable.

Oligodendrocytes in the auditory brainstem adjust myelin thickness in response to sound experience during development, a rare case of glia being tuned by the environment to preserve microsecond accuracy.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Among the highest in the entire nervous system.

Auditory brainstem tissue has the highest cytochrome oxidase activity, a marker of oxygen-based energy production, of any region measured, because these cells fire continuously at high rates for a lifetime.

Vestibular pacemakers never rest, so their sodium-potassium pumps run permanently.

PLASTICITY:

Vestibular compensation is the most striking example in clinical neurology: after one balance organ is destroyed, the vestibular nuclei rebalance their resting firing over days to weeks and vertigo resolves even though the organ never recovers.

The dorsal cochlear nucleus behaves like a small cerebellum, with long-term potentiation and depression at its parallel fibre synapses, learning to subtract the sounds your own chewing and head movements produce.

Trigeminal caudalis undergoes central sensitisation, a lasting increase in gain, which underlies chronic facial pain and migraine.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes in the auditory brainstem clear glutamate faster than anywhere else measured, which is essential because slow clearance would blur microsecond timing.
