Specialised astrocytes form glial caps around the giant endbulb synapses, physically confining released transmitter.

In the nucleus of the solitary tract, loss of astrocytes alone abolishes normal responses to glutamate and disrupts blood pressure control, showing these glia are part of the circuit rather than merely supporting it.

Microglia in trigeminal caudalis become activated after nerve injury and release signals that sustain chronic facial pain.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Strip them out and the lateral brainstem stops being a sense organ for the head.

Sound would reach the ear and go nowhere, so the person would be deaf despite a perfect cochlea.

The head could rotate without the eyes compensating, so vision would smear with every step.

The face would be numb, taste would vanish, blood pressure would swing wildly, and the reflexive gag and cough that protect the airway would disappear.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Octopus cells perform an analogue computation with their dendrites, using the physical time it takes signals to travel along a dendrite to compensate for the delay of the travelling wave in the cochlea.

Bushy cells are electrically coupled to each other by gap junctions, so a network of them fires more coherently than any one alone.

Ephaptic effects are documented in the auditory brainstem, where the electrical field of one large synapse influences its neighbour without any synapse at all.

Solitary nucleus neurons control local blood flow through nitric oxide and sense glucose and hormones directly.

Vestibular neurons undergo intrinsic plasticity, changing their own ion channel gene transcription within hours after a balance lesion.

Dorsal cochlear nucleus fusiform cells receive volume-transmitted serotonin that shifts their gain with arousal.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: primary afferent spike trains, cochlear 0 to 400 spikes per second with phase locking up to about 4 kilohertz; vestibular 90 spikes per second at rest, plus or minus 300 for head rotation; trigeminal 0 to 200 spikes per second; solitary tract afferents 0 to 50 spikes per second.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate or glycine or GABA onto superior olive, inferior colliculus, thalamic ventral posteromedial nucleus, oculomotor nuclei, spinal cord, and dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus.

• STATE: membrane voltage; low-threshold potassium activation; synaptic depression level at the endbulb; adaptation variable; for vestibular cells, the resting pacemaker set point.

• PARAMETERS: bushy cell membrane time constant 0.3 to 1.0 milliseconds; input resistance 5 to 60 megohms; endbulb synaptic strength 5 to 20 millivolts per event; coincidence window 100 to 500 microseconds; vestibular resting rate 20 to 100 spikes per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: synaptic current decay 0.2 to 1 millisecond in auditory cells, 5 to 20 milliseconds elsewhere; synaptic depression recovery 20 to 200 milliseconds; vestibular adaptation 5 to 20 seconds; central sensitisation hours to months.

• FAILURE MODE: auditory neuropathy destroys timing and makes speech unintelligible in noise despite a normal audiogram; vestibular nucleus damage causes intractable vertigo and nystagmus; trigeminal caudalis sensitisation produces trigeminal neuralgia and migraine allodynia, in which even a light touch on the face hurts; solitary nucleus failure produces baroreflex failure with dangerous blood pressure swings.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to make a bushy cell fire only when several auditory nerve inputs arrive within a few hundred microseconds of each other.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -65.
Create a number variable called synaptic drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called refractory steps with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called spike times with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 500, repeat the following steps.
    Set arrivals to the number of inputs landing at step.
    Set added drive to the product of arrivals and 12.
    Calculate the sum of synaptic drive and added drive.
    Set synaptic drive to that sum.
    Set decay to the product of synaptic drive and 0.1.
    Calculate the difference of synaptic drive and decay.
    Set synaptic drive to that difference.
    Check if refractory steps is greater than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of refractory steps and 1.
        Set refractory steps to that difference.
        Set membrane voltage to -65.
        Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of membrane voltage and -65.
    Set depolarisation to that difference.
    Set potassium leak to the product of depolarisation and 0.09.
    Calculate the difference of synaptic drive and the sum of depolarisation and potassium leak.
    Set voltage change to the product of that difference and 0.0667.
    Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and voltage change.
    Set membrane voltage to that sum.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -50.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add step to the end of spike times.
        Set membrane voltage to -65.
        Set synaptic drive to 0.
        Set refractory steps to 40.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of step and 1.
    Set step to that sum.
End the loop.
Display the total number of items in spike times to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the whole point is a side-by-side comparison of two input patterns, and Python lets that comparison sit in two readable lines at the end.

# Spherical bushy cell: a coincidence detector, not an integrator.
# A low-threshold potassium current makes the membrane leak as soon as it
# depolarises, so only near-simultaneous endbulb inputs can reach threshold.
DT = 0.02            # 20 microsecond steps: microsecond timing must be resolved
TAU_SYN = 0.25       # endbulb synaptic decay in milliseconds: extremely fast
TAU_FAST = 0.30      # membrane time constant in milliseconds
V_REST, V_TH = -65.0, -50.0
G_KLT = 0.30         # low-threshold potassium strength

def run(input_times_ms, duration_ms=10.0, weight_mV=20.0):
    """Return the times of any spikes produced by this pattern of inputs."""
    arrivals = {}
    for ti in input_times_ms:
        k = int(round(ti / DT))
        arrivals[k] = arrivals.get(k, 0) + 1
    v, g, spikes, refractory = V_REST, 0.0, [], 0
    for k in range(int(duration_ms / DT)):
        g += weight_mV * arrivals.get(k, 0)      # endbulb current arrives
        g -= g * DT / TAU_SYN                    # and vanishes almost at once
        if refractory > 0:
            refractory -= 1
            v = V_REST
            continue
        depol = v - V_REST
        klt = G_KLT * max(0.0, depol) / 5.0      # potassium opens as voltage rises
        v += DT * (-depol * (1.0 + klt) + g) / TAU_FAST
        if v >= V_TH:
            spikes.append(round(k * DT, 2))
            v, g, refractory = V_REST, 0.0, int(0.8 / DT)
    return spikes

print("four endbulbs within 150 microseconds ->", run([2.0, 2.05, 2.1, 2.15]))
print("the same four spread over 3 ms       ->", run([2.0, 3.0, 4.0, 5.0]))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A second-order sensory cell sitting in the lateral brainstem, wearing either the largest synapse in the brain wrapped around its cell body or a one-sided fan of tentacle dendrites, burning more oxygen than almost any other neuron, firing continuously from before birth until death, and built so leaky that it can only respond to events that happen at the same instant.

CHAPTER 131. MESENCEPHALIC TRIGEMINAL NUCLEUS NEURONS (Brain-Resident Jaw-Stretch Neuron) (NON-COG)

There is exactly one place in the entire human brain where a sensory cell from the body has taken up residence inside the central nervous system instead of outside it, and it is a thin string of cells that tells you how hard you are biting.

The old name is pure address, giving the midbrain location and the nerve number and nothing else.

Brain-Resident records the single most remarkable fact about this cell and the reason it is famous, that it is a primary sensory neuron whose cell body sits inside the central nervous system instead of in a ganglion outside it, the only example of that architecture in the human brain.

Jaw-Stretch names what it senses, since it carries the primary afferents from the muscle spindles of the jaw-closing muscles, the masseter, temporalis and medial pterygoid, plus mechanoreceptors of the periodontal ligament around the tooth roots.

Neuron marks it as the cell whose central branch synapses directly on the trigeminal motor nucleus, forming the one-synapse arc of the jaw-jerk reflex.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A mesencephalic trigeminal nucleus neuron, usually shortened to Mes V neuron, is a first-order sensory cell, meaning its own peripheral fibre is the one that touches the sense organ, with no cell in between.

Every other first-order sensory cell in your body sits outside the brain in a ganglion.

These cells sit inside the midbrain, a developmental oddity produced when their precursors failed to migrate out.

They report muscle stretch from the jaw-closing muscles and biting pressure from receptors around the roots of the teeth, and they drive the jaw jerk reflex and stop you from crushing your own teeth.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is a smooth sphere or oval with a very large pale nucleus and one strikingly prominent nucleolus.

It is pseudounipolar, meaning a single stalk leaves the cell and immediately forks in two: one branch runs out to the jaw muscle or tooth socket, the other runs down into the brainstem.

The cell body is therefore not on the signal path at all; it hangs off the side of the wire like a bulb on a string.

Cell bodies frequently touch one another directly, soma to soma, forming clusters.

SIZE:

Soma diameters are 30 to 70 micrometres, making them among the largest cell bodies in the brainstem and easily visible in a routine histological section.

The peripheral branch is 8 to 12 micrometres thick and runs 8 to 12 centimetres to the jaw muscles.

The nucleus itself is a thin column: in the rat it stretches about 4.5 millimetres, and in the human it runs the length of the midbrain into the upper pons.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two functional populations share one anatomy.

Muscle spindle afferents supply the stretch receptors inside masseter, temporalis and medial pterygoid, and dominate numerically.

Periodontal mechanoreceptor afferents supply pressure receptors in the ligament that slings each tooth in its socket, and these are the cells that let you feel a grain of sand between your teeth.

A small third group sends collateral branches to both the eyelid-lifting muscle and the masseter, which is thought to explain Marcus Gunn jaw-winking syndrome, in which chewing makes the eyelid rise.

WHERE IT LIVES:

A narrow column at the lateral edge of the periaqueductal grey in the midbrain, running alongside the cerebral aqueduct from the level of the superior colliculus down to the level of the trigeminal motor nucleus in the pons.

It sits at a genuine and unique interface: the boundary between peripheral sensory identity and central nervous system location.

Its lower end lies immediately adjacent to the locus coeruleus, so noradrenaline released there reaches these cells by diffusion.

Its axon terminals reach the trigeminal motor nucleus, the reticular formation, the hypoglossal nucleus and the cerebellum.

NUMBER:

Rat counts give 1,000 to 1,600 cells per side over a 4.5 millimetre column.

Scaling by brainstem size and column length, a defensible human estimate is 2,000 to 4,000 per side, or 4,000 to 8,000 in total, and confidence is low because no modern unbiased human count has been published.

That is about one in fifteen million of the nervous system's neurons and roughly 0.003 percent of the brainstem's 200 million, making this one of the smallest named neuron populations in the human brain.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage near minus 60 millivolts.

A persistent sodium current, an inward current that does not switch itself off, works against a slowly activating D-type potassium current to produce subthreshold membrane oscillations at 20 to 100 hertz, meaning the voltage ripples rhythmically without spiking.

Spikes ride on the peaks of these ripples, so the cell naturally fires in bursts locked to the oscillation, which matches the rhythm of chewing.

They also carry a resurgent sodium current that lets them fire at very high rates without inactivating.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Chemical synapses onto the cell body are essentially absent, which is extraordinary: the classic description is that the mesencephalic nucleus contains no chemical synapses on its somata.

Instead the cell bodies are joined to each other by gap junctions, direct protein tunnels that pass electrical current, so the population is electrically coupled into a network.

Output is a conventional excitatory synapse onto trigeminal motor neurons, forming a two-neuron reflex arc, the shortest reflex loop in the head.

There is also a modulatory input from the locus coeruleus delivered not through synapses but by diffusion.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Large and extremely fast, conducting at 60 to 80 metres per second, which is what makes the jaw jerk one of the shortest-latency reflexes measurable in a clinic.

The central branch descends as the mesencephalic tract and gives collaterals to several targets along the way.

The peripheral branch leaves through the motor root of the trigeminal nerve rather than the sensory root, an anatomical trap for medical students.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate is the transmitter at the central terminals.

These cells are also unusual in expressing high levels of the calcium-binding proteins parvalbumin and calretinin.

They carry alpha-2 adrenergic receptors that respond to noradrenaline arriving by diffusion from the neighbouring locus coeruleus.

MYELINATION:

Both branches are heavily myelinated.

The central branch is wrapped by oligodendrocytes and the peripheral branch by Schwann cells, and the transition happens unusually far into the brain because the cell body itself is central.

The cell body is left bare of myelin, which is what allows the soma-to-soma gap junctions to work.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High during chewing and low otherwise, giving one of the most sharply duty-cycled energy profiles in the brainstem.

The persistent sodium current imposes a constant background sodium load even at rest, so the sodium-potassium pump runs continuously.

PLASTICITY:

Limited synaptic plasticity, because there are almost no chemical synapses onto them.

Instead they show intrinsic plasticity: gap junction strength and the size of the persistent sodium current change during development and after tooth loss.

Losing teeth removes the periodontal input permanently, and the reflex map does not fully recover, which is part of why chewing changes after dental extraction.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The astrocytes here are specialised in a way seen nowhere else in the brain: they wrap individual cell bodies in near-complete sheaths that isolate them from ordinary synaptic contact while leaving small windows where somata meet each other at gap junctions.

Astrocytes also actively shape the noradrenaline volume signal arriving from the locus coeruleus, taking up the transmitter and setting how far it spreads.

Microglia are sparse and quiet here in health.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the midbrain loses its only direct physical contact with the outside world.

The jaw jerk reflex disappears entirely, so the jaw no longer protects itself and biting force becomes unregulated.

You would lose the exquisite sense of how hard your teeth are pressing, so eating would become clumsy and self-injury common.

The periaqueductal grey region would keep all its emotional and pain-modulating functions and lose its one strand of raw mechanical truth about the jaw.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Almost everything about these cells is beyond integrate and fire.

They generate subthreshold oscillations intrinsically, so the cell has its own rhythm before any input arrives.

They are electrically coupled through gap junctions, so a group of them synchronises and behaves as one oscillator, and the D-type potassium current sets how tightly they lock together.

They receive noradrenaline by volume transmission from the locus coeruleus, meaning the chemical simply diffuses through the tissue rather than crossing a synapse.

They release glutamate from the cell body and from the tract, not only from axon terminals, and this somatic release is thought to signal to neighbouring astrocytes.

Their soma-to-soma contacts also permit ephaptic interaction, in which the electrical field of one cell nudges the next without any junction at all.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: jaw muscle spindle stretch, encoded 0 to 200 spikes per second over 0 to 20 millimetres of muscle length change; periodontal pressure, 0 to 150 spikes per second over 0 to 20 newtons of bite force; electrical current through gap junctions from coupled neighbours; diffused noradrenaline, 10 nanomolar to 1 micromolar.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto trigeminal motor neurons, hypoglossal motor neurons, the parvocellular reticular formation and the cerebellum.

• STATE: membrane voltage; persistent sodium activation; D-type potassium activation; oscillation phase; coupling current from each neighbour.

• PARAMETERS: persistent sodium conductance 0.2 to 0.35 millisiemens per square centimetre; D-type potassium conductance 0.4 to 0.8; gap junction conductance 0.03 to 0.1; oscillation frequency 20 to 100 hertz; conduction velocity 60 to 80 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 8 to 12 milliseconds; D-type potassium activation 10 to 15 milliseconds; oscillation period 10 to 50 milliseconds; jaw jerk reflex loop latency 6 to 8 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis a brisk jaw jerk is an early clinical sign that the descending control of this loop has failed; loss of periodontal input after full dental extraction permanently degrades bite force control; abnormal coupling in this nucleus is implicated in sleep bruxism, the involuntary night-time grinding of teeth.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to make two electrically coupled mesencephalic trigeminal neurons oscillate and then lock into the same rhythm.
Create a number variable called voltage one with initial value -64.
Create a number variable called voltage two with initial value -50.
Create a number variable called potassium one with initial value 0.02.
Create a number variable called potassium two with initial value 0.99.
Create a number variable called coupling with initial value 0.5.
Create a number variable called stretch drive with initial value 5.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called phase difference history with initial value empty list.
While step is less than 40000, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the difference of voltage two and voltage one.
    Set coupling current to the product of coupling and that difference.
    Define a function called advance one cell that performs the following operations.
        Set sodium activation to the persistent sodium curve value for this voltage.
        Set sodium current to the product of 20 and the product of sodium activation and the difference of this voltage and 60.
        Set potassium current to the product of 10 and the product of this potassium and the difference of this voltage and -90.
        Set leak current to the product of 8 and the difference of this voltage and -80.
        Calculate the sum of stretch drive and this coupling current.
        Set net current to the difference of that sum and the sum of sodium current and the sum of potassium current and leak current.
        Set voltage change to the product of net current and 0.01.
        Calculate the sum of this voltage and voltage change.
        Return that sum to the calling function.
    End the function definition.
    Call the function named advance one cell.
    Set voltage one to the returned value.
    Call the function named advance one cell.
    Set voltage two to the returned value.
    Calculate the difference of voltage one and voltage two.
    Add that difference to the end of phase difference history.
    Calculate the sum of step and 1.
    Set step to that sum.
End the loop.
Display the last item in phase difference history to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the two coupled cells must be stepped forward together in lockstep, and a shared derivative function makes it obvious that both obey identical rules and differ only in their starting voltage.

# Two mesencephalic trigeminal somata joined by a gap junction. The persistent
# sodium current makes each one oscillate on its own; the junction then drags
# the pair into step. Conductances are in the normalised units of the standard
# persistent-sodium-plus-potassium model, not in millisiemens per square centimetre.
import math

DT = 0.01                                  # milliseconds per step
C, G_L, E_L = 1.0, 8.0, -80.0              # capacitance, leak, leak reversal
G_NA, E_NA = 20.0, 60.0                    # persistent sodium: never switches off
G_K, E_K, TAU_N = 10.0, -90.0, 1.0         # the potassium brake and its lag
G_GAP = 0.5                                # gap junction between the two somata

def m_inf(v):                              # sodium activation, effectively instant
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp((-20.0 - v) / 15.0))

def n_inf(v):                              # potassium activation, delayed
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp((-25.0 - v) / 5.0))

def derivative(v, n, i_ext, i_gap):
    """Rate of change of voltage for one Mes V soma, in millivolts per millisecond."""
    i_na = G_NA * m_inf(v) * (v - E_NA)
    i_k = G_K * n * (v - E_K)
    return (-G_L * (v - E_L) - i_na - i_k + i_ext + i_gap) / C

def run(ms=400.0, stretch=5.0):
    v1, v2 = -64.0, -50.0                  # start deliberately out of phase
    n1, n2 = n_inf(v1), n_inf(v2)
    crests, previous = 0, v1
    for _ in range(int(ms / DT)):
        gap = G_GAP * (v2 - v1)            # current flows from cell two into cell one
        new_v1 = v1 + DT * derivative(v1, n1, stretch, gap)
        new_v2 = v2 + DT * derivative(v2, n2, stretch, -gap)
        n1 += DT * (n_inf(v1) - n1) / TAU_N
        n2 += DT * (n_inf(v2) - n2) / TAU_N
        if v1 > previous and v1 > new_v1:
            crests += 1                    # count oscillation crests in cell one
        previous, v1, v2 = v1, new_v1, new_v2
    return crests, abs(v1 - v2)

crests, mismatch = run()
print("voltage crests in 400 ms: %d (about %.0f Hz)" % (crests, crests / 0.4))
print("voltage gap between the coupled pair at the end: %.4f mV" % mismatch)

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A very large round pale-nucleated cell sitting inside the midbrain that has no business being there, carrying no chemical synapses on its body, joined to its neighbours by direct electrical tunnels, wrapped in a private astrocyte jacket, oscillating at chewing rhythm all by itself, and belonging by every developmental measure to the peripheral nervous system it left behind.

CHAPTER 132. LOCUS COERULEUS NEURONS (Brain-Wide Alerting Neuron) (COG)

A patch of tissue the size of a grain of rice, visibly blue to the naked eye in a fresh brain, sends fibres to almost every corner of the nervous system, and when it fires you snap awake.

Locus coeruleus is Latin for blue spot, a description of pigment seen at autopsy, and it teaches nothing about function.

Brain-Wide names the astonishing reach of this cell, since a nucleus of only some tens of thousands of neurons per side sends fibres to nearly the whole neuraxis, from spinal cord to cortex to cerebellum, so a handful of cells can change the state of the entire brain at once.

Alerting names the work, because these are the brain's main source of noradrenaline and their firing rises with arousal, novelty and stress, falls in slow-wave sleep, and goes essentially silent during rapid eye movement sleep.

Neuron marks it as a cell that does not carry a message about the world but sets how strongly every other message is heard.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Locus coeruleus is Latin for blue spot, and the colour comes from neuromelanin, a dark pigment that these cells accumulate over a lifetime as a by-product of making their transmitter.

Locus coeruleus neurons are the brain's near-exclusive source of noradrenaline, also called norepinephrine, a chemical messenger that sets arousal, alertness, attention and the response to anything surprising.

They are marked cognitive because their firing pattern does not report a sensation or drive a muscle; it decides how much of the brain's processing capacity is switched on and where it points.

They wake you, they focus you, they consolidate memories, and they decide when to abandon a strategy that is not working.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Medium-sized fusiform or multipolar cells packed densely into a compact cluster with almost no glial partitioning between them.

Three to seven dendrites extend well beyond the borders of the nucleus into a surrounding shell called the pericoerulear zone, where they gather inputs the cell bodies never see.

The cytoplasm is loaded with coarse brown neuromelanin granules, which makes the nucleus visible without any stain at all.

Dendrites of neighbouring cells bundle together, which is central to how the nucleus behaves as a unit.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 20 to 45 micrometres across.

The nucleus itself is tiny: about 12 to 17 millimetres long, 2.5 millimetres wide and 2.0 millimetres high in the human.

The axonal tree is the opposite of tiny: a single locus coeruleus neuron can support an axonal arbour with a total length estimated in metres, branching to reach cortex, hippocampus, cerebellum, thalamus and spinal cord.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Not a uniform pool.

A dorsal compact core projects mainly to forebrain and cortex, while a ventral group projects to spinal cord, cerebellum and brainstem.

Recent work shows individual cells are far more target-specific than the old picture of one cell broadcasting everywhere, with modules that project to prefrontal cortex behaving differently from modules projecting to amygdala.

A distinct subset co-expresses galanin heavily, and another subset projects almost exclusively to the spinal dorsal horn to suppress pain.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The dorsal pons, in the floor of the fourth ventricle at the lateral edge of the periaqueductal grey, just beneath the ependymal lining, extending from the level of the inferior colliculus down towards the mid pons.

It sits at several interfaces at once.

It is immediately adjacent to the mesencephalic trigeminal nucleus, so the two exchange signals by diffusion.

Its dendrites extend into the pericoerulear shell, which is technically outside the nucleus and is where most inhibitory control arrives.

And it lies against the ventricle wall, so its transmitter can reach the cerebrospinal fluid directly.

NUMBER:

Counts of pigmented neurons in adult human males give 22,000 to 51,000 in total across both sides, with the most commonly cited figure being about 45,000 to 50,000 for both hemispheres combined.

Confidence is good because neuromelanin makes these cells unusually easy to count, though pigment loss in disease biases counts downward.

That is the entire locus coeruleus allocation of the brainstem's 200 million neurons, about 0.025 percent of the brainstem, and roughly one in 1.7 million of the nervous system's 86 billion neurons.

It is one of the highest ratios of influence to headcount anywhere in biology: one locus coeruleus neuron for every few hundred thousand cortical neurons it can reach.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Slow autonomous pacemakers, firing 1 to 3 spikes per second at rest without any input, driven by a persistent sodium current and a hyperpolarisation-activated current.

Spikes are wide, lasting 2 to 4 milliseconds, followed by a long calcium-activated potassium afterhyperpolarisation lasting several hundred milliseconds that enforces the slow rate.

They operate in two modes: a tonic mode of steady low firing that scales with arousal, and a phasic mode in which a salient event triggers a burst of two to five spikes in under 100 milliseconds followed by a silent period.

Firing falls almost to zero during rapid eye movement sleep, the only time in life the brain is essentially free of noradrenaline.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The main excitatory input is from the nucleus paragigantocellularis in the medulla, carrying autonomic urgency, plus inputs from the prefrontal cortex, the central amygdala, the hypothalamic orexin cells, and the nucleus of the solitary tract.

The main inhibitory input is from the nucleus prepositus hypoglossi, using GABA, and it arrives largely onto the dendrites in the pericoerulear shell.

Output is extraordinary in scale: locus coeruleus fibres innervate the entire cerebral cortex, hippocampus, amygdala, thalamus, cerebellum, all of the brainstem and the whole spinal cord, while notably sparing the basal ganglia.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Very thin, 0.2 to 1.0 micrometres, and slow, conducting at 0.5 to 2 metres per second.

The arbour is immense and highly branched, with hundreds of thousands of small swellings called varicosities strung along it like beads.

Most varicosities do not form a classical synapse at all; they simply release noradrenaline into the surrounding tissue.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Noradrenaline, made by the enzymes tyrosine hydroxylase and dopamine beta-hydroxylase, acting on alpha-1 receptors that excite, alpha-2 receptors that inhibit, and beta receptors that boost signal-to-noise ratio in cortex.

Galanin and neuropeptide Y are co-released during strong firing and produce prolonged inhibition of targets.

Critically, noradrenaline also acts back on the locus coeruleus itself through alpha-2 autoreceptors, a self-applied brake that couples the whole nucleus together.

Some cells co-release dopamine, the immediate chemical precursor of noradrenaline.

MYELINATION:

Almost entirely unmyelinated.

This is a defining feature: the axons are too fine to be worth insulating, so the noradrenaline signal spreads slowly, taking tens to hundreds of milliseconds to reach distant cortex.

The consequence is that noradrenaline sets a slowly changing background state rather than delivering fast point-to-point messages.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Punishing, and this is the key to their vulnerability.

The axonal arbour is so vast that a single cell must maintain an enormous membrane area on a low firing rate, and the constant pacemaker activity means calcium is always entering.

Making and breaking down noradrenaline generates reactive oxygen species and the toxic aldehyde DOPEGAL, and neuromelanin is the visible residue of that chemistry.

These cells sit permanently close to their metabolic limit, which is why they fail early.

PLASTICITY:

They are the instrument of plasticity elsewhere more than the site of it.

Noradrenaline released during a surprising or emotional event lowers the threshold for long-term potentiation in the hippocampus and cortex, which is why you remember startling moments vividly.

The nucleus itself shows adaptation of alpha-2 autoreceptor sensitivity over weeks, which is the accepted mechanism by which antidepressant drugs take a fortnight to work.

Chronic stress causes measurable dendritic remodelling here.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here are unusual in two ways.

First, they carry beta-adrenergic receptors densely, so noradrenaline from these very neurons switches astrocytes into a high-energy state, causing them to break down stored glycogen and hand lactate to nearby neurons; this is the cellular basis of the metabolic surge that accompanies alertness.

Second, they gate volume transmission by controlling reuptake and diffusion distance, effectively deciding how wide the noradrenaline signal spreads.

Microglia here are highly reactive and are increasingly implicated in the earliest stages of Alzheimer disease.

There is almost no myelin, so oligodendrocytes play a minimal role.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the locus coeruleus and the dorsal pons loses its ability to change the state of the entire brain.

The pons would still relay, still time, still breathe.

But nothing would be able to say to the cortex: something important just happened, pay attention, remember this.

Wakefulness would become shallow and fragile, attention would not sharpen under threat, emotional memories would not be stamped in, and the brain would lose its capacity to switch from exploiting a familiar strategy to exploring a new one.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell type is the textbook case for everything beyond integrate and fire.

Transmission is largely volume transmission, with noradrenaline diffusing tens of micrometres to reach extrasynaptic receptors on neurons, astrocytes, microglia and blood vessels.

The cells release noradrenaline from their dendrites as well as their axons, and that dendritic release acting on alpha-2 autoreceptors within the bundled dendrites synchronises the nucleus into a functional unit.

Locus coeruleus neurons are gap-junction coupled, especially in early life.

They control local blood flow directly by acting on the smooth muscle of cortical arterioles.

They instruct astrocyte metabolism, they modulate microglial surveillance and inflammatory tone, and they influence clearance of waste through the brain's fluid pathways during sleep.

Their own gene transcription shifts with sleep and stress, changing how much transmitter machinery they build.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: excitatory glutamate from nucleus paragigantocellularis and prefrontal cortex, 0 to 40 spikes per second; inhibitory GABA from nucleus prepositus hypoglossi; orexin peptide from the lateral hypothalamus setting the wake state; corticotropin-releasing factor during stress; ambient noradrenaline acting on alpha-2 autoreceptors.

• OUTPUTS: noradrenaline released from about 100,000 varicosities per neuron into cortex, hippocampus, amygdala, thalamus, cerebellum, brainstem and spinal cord, with extracellular concentration swinging between roughly 10 nanomolar at rest and 1 micromolar during a phasic burst.

• STATE: membrane voltage; calcium-activated potassium afterhyperpolarisation level; local noradrenaline pool acting on autoreceptors; phasic burst variable; sleep and wake mode flag.

• PARAMETERS: tonic rate 1 to 3 hertz awake, 0 to 0.5 hertz in slow-wave sleep, near 0 in rapid eye movement sleep; phasic burst 2 to 5 spikes; spike width 2 to 4 milliseconds; conduction velocity 0.5 to 2 metres per second; autoreceptor gain 0.3 to 0.8.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 60 to 120 milliseconds; afterhyperpolarisation 300 to 500 milliseconds; phasic burst decay about 150 milliseconds; autoreceptor noradrenaline pool 1 to 2 seconds; antidepressant autoreceptor desensitisation 10 to 21 days.

• FAILURE MODE: hyperphosphorylated tau appears in locus coeruleus neurons before anywhere else in the brain in Alzheimer disease, and the cells are lost by Braak stages three and four as memory symptoms begin; in Parkinson disease models these neurons become abnormally overactive through failure of calcium-activated potassium channels before they die, and their loss drives the depression, sleep disturbance and cognitive slowing that often precede tremor by years.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to reproduce the slow tonic pacemaking of a locus coeruleus neuron and the phasic burst it produces when something surprising happens.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -55.
Create a number variable called noradrenaline pool with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called afterhyperpolarisation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called phasic drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called tonic drive with initial value 0.62.
Create a list variable called spike times with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 20000, repeat the following steps.
    Check if step is equal to a salience event time.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set phasic drive to 3.
    End the conditional block.
    Set phasic decay to the quotient of phasic drive divided by 150.
    Calculate the difference of phasic drive and phasic decay.
    Set phasic drive to that difference.
    Set autoinhibition to the product of noradrenaline pool and 0.5.
    Calculate the sum of tonic drive and phasic drive.
    Set gross drive to that sum.
    Calculate the sum of autoinhibition and afterhyperpolarisation.
    Set net drive to the difference of gross drive and that sum.
    Set target voltage to the sum of -60 and the product of net drive and 45.
    Calculate the difference of target voltage and membrane voltage.
    Set voltage change to the product of that difference and 0.0111.
    Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and voltage change.
    Set membrane voltage to that sum.
    Set pool decay to the quotient of noradrenaline pool divided by 1200.
    Calculate the difference of noradrenaline pool and pool decay.
    Set noradrenaline pool to that difference.
    Set afterhyperpolarisation decay to the quotient of afterhyperpolarisation divided by 400.
    Calculate the difference of afterhyperpolarisation and afterhyperpolarisation decay.
    Set afterhyperpolarisation to that difference.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -40.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add step to the end of spike times.
        Set membrane voltage to -60.
        Calculate the sum of noradrenaline pool and 0.05.
        Set noradrenaline pool to that sum.
        Calculate the sum of afterhyperpolarisation and 0.25.
        Set afterhyperpolarisation to that sum.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of step and 1.
    Set step to that sum.
End the loop.
Display the total number of items in spike times to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the three interacting slow variables, the afterhyperpolarisation, the self-inhibiting noradrenaline pool and the phasic drive, are easiest to follow when each gets its own clearly named line inside a single loop.

DT = 0.001                                   # one millisecond per step, in seconds

def locus_coeruleus(seconds=20.0, salience_times=(5.0, 12.0),
                    tonic_drive=0.62, autoreceptor_gain=0.5):
    """Slow pacemaking, a phasic burst on salience, then self-applied silence."""
    triggers = set(int(round(t / DT)) for t in salience_times)
    v, na_pool, ahp, phasic, spikes = -55.0, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0, []
    v_th, v_reset, e_rest, gain = -40.0, -60.0, -60.0, 45.0
    tau_m, tau_na, tau_ahp, tau_phasic = 0.090, 1.200, 0.400, 0.150
    for k in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        if k in triggers:
            phasic = 3.0                     # a startling stimulus arrives
        phasic -= phasic * DT / tau_phasic
        drive = tonic_drive + phasic - autoreceptor_gain * na_pool - ahp
        v += DT * (e_rest + gain * drive - v) / tau_m
        na_pool -= na_pool * DT / tau_na     # released noradrenaline clears slowly
        ahp -= ahp * DT / tau_ahp            # the long potassium brake fades
        if v >= v_th:
            spikes.append(k * DT)
            v = v_reset
            na_pool += 0.05                  # every spike deepens self-inhibition
            ahp += 0.25                      # and deepens the afterhyperpolarisation
    return spikes

sp = locus_coeruleus()
before = [t for t in sp if t < 5.0]
burst = [t for t in sp if 5.0 <= t < 5.3]
after = [t for t in sp if 5.3 <= t < 6.3]
print("tonic rate before the stimulus: %.2f Hz" % (len(before) / 5.0))
print("spikes in the 300 ms burst window: %d" % len(burst))
print("spikes in the following second (post-burst silence): %d" % len(after))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A brown-pigmented, visibly blue-black cell in the floor of the fourth ventricle that ticks over at one to three spikes a second all your waking life and falls completely silent when you dream, whose unmyelinated axon spreads further than any other single neuron's in the human body, which sprays its transmitter into the tissue instead of aiming it at a partner, which inhibits itself and thereby its whole nucleus with the very chemical it releases, and which is the first place in the entire brain where the pathology of Alzheimer disease can be found.

CHAPTER 133. RAPHE NUCLEI NEURONS (Mood-Steadying Patience Neuron) (COG)

Down the exact midline of the brainstem runs a thin grey seam, and from it a few hundred thousand neurons send hair-thin fibres to almost every other neuron you own.

Raphe means seam, describing only where the cells sit along the midline of the brainstem.

Mood-Steadying names the faculty for which they are best known, since these nuclei hold most of the brain's serotonin cells and their loss of tone is the target of the commonest antidepressant drugs.

Patience names a second and better-measured job, because recordings from the dorsal raphe show these neurons firing steadily while an animal waits for a delayed reward, and silencing them makes the animal give up waiting sooner.

Neuron marks it as a cell that, like the alerting cells, is quiet in rapid eye movement sleep and steady in waking, which is why it sets a background mood rather than reporting an event.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Raphe is Latin for seam, and these cells sit in the seam where the two halves of the brainstem meet.

Most make serotonin, properly 5-hydroxytryptamine (5-HT), a signalling molecule that acts less like a telephone call and more like a change in the weather.

A raphe neuron carries no detail about a sound or a touch; it sets the operating conditions of everything else, including patience, pain, sleep, breathing drive and body temperature.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is spindle-shaped or many-sided and throws out only three to six dendrites, the branch-like input antennae of a neuron.

Those dendrites are long, straight and barely branched, a starburst rather than a bush, and in the dorsal raphe they fan across the midline so one cell listens to both halves of the brainstem.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are roughly 15 to 35 micrometres across, a micrometre being one thousandth of a millimetre, with dorsal raphe serotonin cells clustering at 20 to 30.

Dendrites spread 300 to 600 micrometres, but the axon is the astonishing part: one human raphe neuron is estimated to carry tens of centimetres of total axon and on the order of a hundred thousand release swellings.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The classical scheme labels nine clusters, B1 to B9, running from medulla to midbrain.

The ascending set, B5 to B9, is dominated by the dorsal raphe (B7) and median raphe (B8) and supplies the forebrain; the descending set, B1 to B3, is raphe pallidus, obscurus and magnus, and supplies medulla and spinal cord.

Cutting across that geography are chemical subtypes: serotonin cells that also package glutamate using vesicular glutamate transporter 3 (VGLUT3), cells co-releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA, the main inhibitory chemical), pure GABA interneurons, and a small dopamine group in the dorsal raphe.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Dorsal raphe nucleus in the midbrain tegmentum beneath the cerebral aqueduct, physically embedded inside the ventral periaqueductal grey, which makes it a genuine interface population living within another region.

Median raphe and caudal linear nucleus on the pontine and upper midbrain midline; raphe magnus in the rostral ventromedial medulla at the level of the facial nucleus; raphe obscurus and raphe pallidus on the medullary midline, with pallidus pressed against the ventral surface at the interface between brain tissue and the cerebrospinal fluid of the subarachnoid space.

Group B9 lies off midline above the medial lemniscus, at the border between the raphe system and the reticular formation.

NUMBER:

The human dorsal raphe contains about 235,000 neurons, of which about 165,000, roughly 70 percent, make serotonin, from stereological counts by Baker and colleagues; the median raphe holds the second largest pool, usually given as 20 to 25 percent of all serotonin neurons.

Summing all nine groups, a defensible figure is 400,000 to 600,000 raphe neurons, of which perhaps 300,000 to 400,000 are serotonergic, with moderate confidence for the dorsal raphe and low confidence for the medullary groups, which have never been counted carefully.

That is about 0.25 percent of the brainstem's roughly 200 million neurons and about six millionths of the roughly 86 billion neurons of the whole nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is near minus 65 to minus 70 millivolts with a high input resistance of 200 to 500 megaohms, so small currents move the cell a long way.

The action potential, the brief electrical pulse a neuron sends, is unusually broad at 1.5 to 3 milliseconds and is followed by a large afterhyperpolarization of 150 to 300 milliseconds carried by calcium-activated potassium channels, which is why the cell ticks at only 0.5 to 5 hertz.

The rhythm is state dependent: fastest in active waking, roughly halved in non-rapid-eye-movement sleep, and almost silent during rapid eye movement (REM) sleep.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from medial prefrontal cortex, the lateral habenula (a powerful disappointment signal), orexin neurons of the lateral hypothalamus, the locus coeruleus, the periaqueductal grey and dense local GABA interneurons.

Outputs reach almost everywhere: cortex, hippocampus, amygdala, striatum, thalamus, hypothalamus and cerebellum from the rostral groups, and spinal dorsal horn laminae I, II and V, motor neuron pools and sympathetic preganglionic columns from the caudal groups.

Crucially, many release sites face no postsynaptic partner at all.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axons are among the thinnest in the nervous system at 0.1 to 0.5 micrometres, conducting at only 0.5 to 1 metre per second.

Fine varicose axons come mainly from the dorsal raphe and thicker beaded ones from the median raphe, and in humans a population of supraependymal axons crawls over the ventricle walls and releases serotonin directly into cerebrospinal fluid.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Serotonin is built from dietary tryptophan by tryptophan hydroxylase 2 (TPH2), loaded by the vesicular monoamine transporter 2 (VMAT2), recaptured by the serotonin transporter (SERT) and destroyed by monoamine oxidase A (MAO-A).

Co-transmitters include glutamate, GABA, substance P, thyrotropin-releasing hormone (TRH), enkephalin and galanin, and fourteen receptor subtypes mean the same molecule excites one target and inhibits another.

MYELINATION:

Overwhelmingly unmyelinated, because myelin, the fatty insulating wrap made by oligodendrocytes, is too expensive to apply to a hundred thousand twigs.

A minority of thick descending medullary raphe axons carries a thin sheath.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Firing is slow, so the spike bill is small, but the axon bill is enormous, since proteins, vesicles and mitochondria must be shipped along tens of centimetres of cable.

The cell is hostage to diet, as a few hours of tryptophan depletion measurably lowers brain serotonin, and MAO-A breakdown generates hydrogen peroxide inside the neuron, a chronic oxidative burden.

PLASTICITY:

The most important plasticity here is slow desensitisation of the cell's own 5-HT1A autoreceptor.

A selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor (SSRI) raises serotonin within hours, which at first switches the raphe neuron off through that autoreceptor, and only after two to three weeks does the autoreceptor weaken and firing recover, which is the accepted explanation for why antidepressants take weeks to work.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes around raphe neurons carry 5-HT2B receptors and release the growth protein S100B in response to serotonin, so the glia here are listeners rather than bystanders.

Medullary raphe astrocytes are themselves acid sensitive and release adenosine triphosphate (ATP) when carbon dioxide rises, and ependymal cells lining the aqueduct provide the surface on which supraependymal serotonin axons travel, a glial relationship found nowhere else.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete these cells and the medulla still breathes, but breathing stops responding to rising carbon dioxide, temperature drifts and arousal from a suffocating environment fails, which is the leading model for sudden infant death syndrome.

Higher up, the forebrain loses its capacity to wait, taking small immediate rewards over larger delayed ones, and the emotional colour of experience flattens.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Most output is volume transmission, meaning serotonin is spilled into the extracellular space and diffuses to receptors on cells never wired to the raphe at all.

The neuron also releases serotonin from its own dendrites and soma, feeding back onto its 5-HT1A autoreceptors and opening potassium channels, a self-braking loop with no synapse involved.

Medullary raphe neurons are direct chemoreceptors, since falling pH closes background potassium channels of the TASK family and speeds the cell up, and raphe serotonin additionally acts as a developmental growth factor and tunes local blood vessels and microglia, the brain's immune cells.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from medial prefrontal cortex and lateral habenula (0 to 40 hertz); orexin arousal scalar (0 to 1); noradrenaline from locus coeruleus as a permissive gain term (0 to 1); local GABA (0 to 50 hertz); extracellular pH 7.6 down to 7.2 for medullary groups; extracellular serotonin at the soma (0 to 500 nanomolar).

• OUTPUTS: extracellular serotonin concentration as a diffuse cloud at each target region rather than a spike train; co-released glutamate as fast synaptic events at a subset of targets; peptide release only above about 8 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage; calcium driving the afterhyperpolarization; vesicular serotonin store; somatodendritic serotonin; autoreceptor sensitivity (0 to 1, slow); behavioural state flag of wake, non-REM or REM.

• PARAMETERS: resting minus 68 millivolts; threshold minus 45 millivolts; spike width 2 milliseconds; input resistance 300 megaohms; waking rate 2 hertz; conduction velocity 0.7 metres per second; about 100,000 release sites per neuron.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 25 to 50 milliseconds; afterhyperpolarization 200 milliseconds; serotonin clearance 0.5 to 3 seconds; autoreceptor desensitisation 14 to 21 days.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of medullary raphe cells removes the carbon dioxide arousal reflex, the pattern found in sudden infant death syndrome; reduced dorsal raphe signalling accompanies major depression and impulsivity; combined serotonergic drugs cause serotonin syndrome with fever, rigidity and clonus; early alpha-synuclein deposition here contributes to the depression that precedes Parkinson's disease by years.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate one serotonin raphe neuron as a slow pacemaker whose rate is set by behavioural state, by carbon dioxide, and by its own released serotonin acting back on itself.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -68.
Create a number variable called afterhyperpolarization with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called somatodendritic serotonin with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called autoreceptor sensitivity with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called state drive with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called carbon dioxide drive with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called release events with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 20000 times.
    Calculate the product of somatodendritic serotonin and autoreceptor sensitivity.
    Set self inhibition to that product.
    Calculate the difference of state drive and self inhibition.
    Calculate the sum of that difference and carbon dioxide drive.
    Set total drive to that sum.
    Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and total drive.
    Calculate the difference of that sum and afterhyperpolarization.
    Set membrane voltage to that difference.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than -45.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to -68.
        Set afterhyperpolarization to 12.
        Add one release event to the end of release events.
        Calculate the sum of somatodendritic serotonin and 0.05.
        Set somatodendritic serotonin to that sum.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of afterhyperpolarization and 0.995.
    Set afterhyperpolarization to that product.
    Calculate the product of somatodendritic serotonin and 0.999.
    Set somatodendritic serotonin to that product.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in release events.
Display that number to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the output of interest is a slowly varying chemical concentration rather than a fast waveform, so readable arithmetic matters more than speed.

import math

DT = 0.001  # seconds per step

def raphe_neuron(seconds=60.0, state="wake", pH=7.4, autoreceptor_gain=1.0):
    v, ahp, sd_5ht, spikes = -68.0, 0.0, 0.0, 0
    drive = {"wake": 1.0, "nrem": 0.7, "rem": 0.0}[state]  # permissive arousal drive
    co2 = max(0.0, 7.4 - pH) * 2.0                         # medullary chemoreception
    for _ in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        total = drive + co2 - sd_5ht * autoreceptor_gain   # 5-HT1A self-braking
        v += ((-68.0 + 50.0 * total) - v) * (DT / 0.035) - ahp * DT / 0.035
        if v > -45.0:
            v, ahp, spikes = -68.0, 18.0, spikes + 1       # large calcium-gated AHP
            sd_5ht += 0.08                                 # dendritic release
        ahp *= math.exp(-DT / 0.200)                       # 200 ms recovery
        sd_5ht *= math.exp(-DT / 1.500)                    # SERT uptake and diffusion
    return spikes / seconds

for st in ("wake", "nrem", "rem"):
    print(f"{st:5s} {raphe_neuron(state=st):5.2f} Hz")
print("hypercapnia   ", round(raphe_neuron(pH=7.25), 2), "Hz")
print("desensitised autoreceptor after weeks of an SSRI:",
      round(raphe_neuron(autoreceptor_gain=0.3), 2), "Hz")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A neuron on the exact midline with long unbranched dendrites, a broad spike, a huge slow afterhyperpolarization and a metronomic one-to-five-hertz beat.

No other cell in the body switches itself off completely for the duration of REM sleep and resumes its clock the moment you wake.

CHAPTER 134. PEDUNCULOPONTINE AND LATERODORSAL TEGMENTAL NUCLEUS NEURONS (Walk-Starting Arousal Neuron) (COG)

Two smudges of large cells at the junction of midbrain and pons act as the ignition switch of consciousness, and when they fire the sleeping thalamus stops idling and starts reporting the world.

The existing label is two coordinates in the tegmentum and names no faculty.

Walk-Starting records the best established motor fact, that this region is the core of the midbrain locomotor area, and optogenetic excitation of its glutamatergic cells reliably starts an animal walking, while damage here contributes to the freezing of gait seen late in Parkinson disease.

Arousal names its other half, because its acetylcholine cells drive the thalamus into the fast, low-voltage rhythm of waking and of rapid eye movement sleep, and electrical stimulation of the region during sleep produces rapid awakening.

Neuron marks it as a cell of a pairing that is honest rather than tidy, because the same small nucleus really does hold both a gait switch and a wakefulness switch.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The pedunculopontine nucleus (PPN) and the laterodorsal tegmental nucleus (LDT) are twin cholinergic outposts, meaning their famous cells release acetylcholine, the same chemical your nerves use to command muscle.

Inside the brain acetylcholine does something different: it lifts thalamic relay cells out of rhythmic bursting into a faithful transmission mode, so signals from eye, ear and skin actually reach the cortex.

The same cells help start walking, make midbrain dopamine neurons burst when something goes better than expected, and switch on rapid eye movement (REM) sleep.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cholinergic cells are large, deeply staining and multipolar, with three to seven long dendrites that ignore the boundaries of the nucleus.

They are intermingled with, not separated from, glutamate-releasing and GABA-releasing neurons, and their dendrites weave among the fibres of the superior cerebellar peduncle, the great cable leaving the cerebellum, which is why the nucleus has no crisp border.

SIZE:

Cholinergic somata are 20 to 45 micrometres across, among the largest in the brainstem tegmentum, with dendritic fields of 500 to 800 micrometres.

The human PPN occupies roughly 100 cubic millimetres per side, stretched about 10 millimetres along the brainstem axis.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Three intermingled chemical populations exist: cholinergic (labelled Ch5 in the PPN and Ch6 in the LDT), glutamatergic and GABAergic.

Careful counts in the rat make the cholinergic group the minority, roughly a quarter to a third of neurons, with glutamatergic cells the largest group.

Anatomically the PPN splits into a dense caudal pars compacta and a scattered rostral pars dissipata, and many cholinergic cells here also stain for nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate diaphorase (NADPH-diaphorase), the signature of the enzyme that makes nitric oxide gas.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The PPN sits at the midbrain and pons junction in the lateral pontomesencephalic tegmentum, wrapped through the superior cerebellar peduncle, bounded medially by the central tegmental tract and laterally by the medial lemniscus.

The LDT lies dorsomedial to it, in the floor of the fourth ventricle beneath the locus coeruleus, spreading into the central grey and towards the medial longitudinal fasciculus.

This is explicitly an interface population, straddling the borders between reticular formation, cerebellar output pathway, basal ganglia output territory and periventricular grey, and the caudal PPN overlaps the mesencephalic locomotor region, the patch whose stimulation makes an animal walk.

NUMBER:

Recent human stereology gives about 72,000 cholinergic PPN neurons across both sides using the classical Ch5 boundary, or about 36,000 per side using a stricter fibre-tract boundary, with roughly 47,000 in pars dissipata and 26,000 in pars compacta of that bilateral total.

The LDT has not been counted as rigorously in humans, and a reasonable bound is 20,000 to 40,000 cholinergic neurons bilaterally.

Adding the non-cholinergic neighbours, which outnumber them at least two to one, the whole complex plausibly holds 250,000 to 500,000 neurons, roughly 0.15 to 0.25 percent of the brainstem's 200 million; confidence is good for the PPN cholinergic count and low for everything else.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting near minus 60 millivolts, these cells fire spontaneously at 1 to 10 hertz with no input at all.

A hyperpolarization-activated cation current called Ih acts like a spring pushing the cell back up whenever it is pushed down, and a low-threshold calcium current fires a rebound burst when the spring releases, while an A-type potassium current delays the first spike after a pause.

High-threshold calcium channels let these neurons oscillate near 40 hertz when strongly driven, which is thought to be how they impose a fast rhythm on the waking cortex.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from the internal globus pallidus and substantia nigra pars reticulata (inhibitory basal ganglia output), the subthalamic nucleus, motor cortex, lateral hypothalamus, amygdala and spinal cord.

Outputs go to the thalamus, especially intralaminar and reticular nuclei, to dopamine neurons of substantia nigra pars compacta and ventral tegmental area, to basal forebrain, pontine reticular formation, locus coeruleus, raphe and spinal cord.

A burst of acetylcholine plus glutamate from the PPN is a principal trigger for the dopamine burst that signals a better-than-expected outcome.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are thin at 0.5 to 2 micrometres and branch early, so one neuron can contact thalamus and midbrain at once.

Conduction is 1 to 10 metres per second, fast enough for arousal and far too slow for reflexes, with ascending fibres running in the dorsal tegmental bundle to thalamus and the ventral tegmental bundle to basal forebrain.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine, made by choline acetyltransferase (ChAT) and destroyed in the cleft by acetylcholinesterase, acting on nicotinic receptors for fast excitation and muscarinic receptors for slow effects.

Neighbouring cells use glutamate or GABA, many cholinergic cells co-release glutamate, and nitric oxide, a gas that diffuses straight through membranes, is a genuine transmitter for this population.

MYELINATION:

Thinly myelinated, with sheaths on larger ascending axons and bare membrane on fine terminal branches.

The compromise gives enough speed to keep arousal signals coherent across the millimetres to thalamus without the cost of heavy insulation.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and continuous, since the cells never truly stop except in deep non-REM sleep.

Acetylcholine synthesis needs a steady supply of choline and of acetyl coenzyme A from mitochondria, tying transmitter output directly to energy supply, which is one reason cholinergic neurons are early casualties in degenerative disease.

PLASTICITY:

Nicotinic receptors on cholinergic targets upregulate with chronic nicotine, a direct substrate of tobacco addiction.

The nucleus also undergoes unwanted plasticity: roughly half its cholinergic neurons are gone in Parkinson's disease, and the loss tracks falls and freezing of gait rather than tremor.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing about the astrocytes is exotic, but their geometry matters, because nitric oxide made by these neurons passes through glia unimpeded and dilates local capillaries, so glial coverage sets the radius of that gas signal.

Oligodendrocytes give only thin sheaths, and microglia here are notably reactive in Parkinson's disease and progressive supranuclear palsy, tracking the ongoing cholinergic loss.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these neurons and the upper brainstem loses its ability to hand the cortex a clean signal.

The thalamus falls into slow rhythmic bursting, the electroencephalogram slows, attention fragments, REM sleep disorganises, and walking loses the automatic postural readiness that lets you take a first step without thinking about it.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Nitric oxide release makes this a volume-signalling nucleus, since the gas is not stored in vesicles and cannot be aimed.

Intrinsic Ih and low-threshold calcium currents make the neuron a rhythm generator in its own right, and dendritic high-threshold calcium channels generate gamma-band oscillation, shaping the frequency of output rather than merely its rate.

Acetylcholine arriving in cortex and thalamus also acts as a permission signal deciding whether a coincidence between two other signals is written into the circuit.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: GABAergic drive from substantia nigra pars reticulata and internal globus pallidus (0 to 80 hertz); glutamate from subthalamic nucleus and motor cortex (0 to 50 hertz); orexin wake scalar (0 to 1); ascending spinal and vestibular locomotor drive (0 to 1); local GABAergic inhibition.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine plus co-released glutamate onto thalamic relay and reticular neurons, midbrain dopamine neurons and pontine reticular formation; nitric oxide as a diffusing concentration with a radius near 100 micrometres.

• STATE: membrane voltage; Ih activation; low-threshold calcium inactivation; A-current inactivation; intracellular calcium; vesicular acetylcholine; behavioural state flag of wake, non-REM or REM.

• PARAMETERS: resting minus 60 millivolts; threshold minus 45 millivolts; waking rate 4 hertz; burst rate 20 to 80 hertz; conduction velocity 1 to 10 metres per second; about 36,000 cholinergic cells per side in the PPN.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 to 30 milliseconds; Ih activation 100 to 500 milliseconds; calcium de-inactivation about 100 milliseconds; nicotinic response 5 to 20 milliseconds; muscarinic response 100 to 1000 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: about 50 percent loss of PPN cholinergic neurons in Parkinson's disease produces freezing of gait, falls and attention deficits that levodopa does not fix; severe loss occurs in progressive supranuclear palsy; disordered PPN and LDT output underlies REM sleep behaviour disorder, in which a sleeper acts out dreams, one of the strongest early warnings of later Parkinson's disease.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate one pedunculopontine cholinergic neuron that fires tonically when awake, falls silent in deep sleep, and produces a rebound burst after release from basal ganglia inhibition.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -60.
Create a number variable called calcium availability with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called h current with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called pallidal inhibition with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called wake drive with initial value 1.
Create a list variable called spike times with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 5000 times.
    Calculate the difference of wake drive and pallidal inhibition.
    Set net drive to that difference.
    Check if membrane voltage is less than -70.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of h current and 0.02.
        Set h current to that sum.
        Calculate the sum of calcium availability and 0.01.
        Set calcium availability to that sum.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the product of h current and 0.98.
        Set h current to that product.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of net drive and h current.
    Calculate the sum of that sum and membrane voltage.
    Set membrane voltage to that sum.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than -45.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the current step number to the end of spike times.
        Calculate the difference of membrane voltage and 20.
        Set membrane voltage to that difference.
        Check if calcium availability is greater than 0.5.
        If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
            Calculate the difference of calcium availability and 0.15.
            Set calcium availability to that difference.
            Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and 14.
            Set membrane voltage to that sum.
        End the conditional block.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in spike times.
Display that number to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the interesting behaviour is a change of firing mode over hundreds of milliseconds, and a plain loop with named state variables shows the rebound burst clearly.

DT = 0.0005  # 0.5 ms steps

def ppn_neuron(seconds=2.0, wake=1.0, inhibition=0.0, release_at=None):
    v, ih, ca = -60.0, 0.0, 0.0
    spikes = []
    for i in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        t = i * DT
        inh = 0.0 if (release_at is not None and t > release_at) else inhibition
        target = -60.0 + 16.0 * (wake - inh)
        low = 1.0 if v < -70.0 else 0.0
        ih += (low - ih) * (DT / 0.250)   # Ih builds while held hyperpolarised
        ca += (low - ca) * (DT / 0.100)   # T-type calcium recovers from inactivation
        v += ((target - v) + 18.0 * ih) * (DT / 0.060)
        if v > -45.0:
            spikes.append(round(t, 4))
            v = -62.0
            if ca > 0.5:                  # rebound burst rides the calcium spike
                ca -= 0.20
                v += 14.0
    return spikes

print("tonic waking:", round(len(ppn_neuron(wake=1.0)) / 2.0, 1), "Hz")
print("deep sleep:  ", round(len(ppn_neuron(wake=0.1)) / 2.0, 1), "Hz")
burst = ppn_neuron(wake=1.0, inhibition=3.2, release_at=1.0)
print("spikes in 150 ms after pallidal release:", len([s for s in burst if 1.0 < s < 1.15]))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A very large, darkly staining neuron at the midbrain and pons junction, threaded through the superior cerebellar peduncle, positive for both choline acetyltransferase and nitric oxide synthase, firing steadily when you are awake and dreaming but quiet in deep sleep.

No other brainstem cell both wakes the thalamus with acetylcholine and tells midbrain dopamine neurons that the world just went better than expected.

CHAPTER 135. VENTROLATERAL MEDULLARY ADRENERGIC NEURONS (Pressure-Holding Emergency Neuron) (NON-COG)

A few thousand cells tucked into the side of the lowest part of the brainstem set the pressure in every artery in your body, and if they stop for eight seconds you faint.

The old name gives a location and a chemical class, neither of which says why the cell matters.

Pressure-Holding names the everyday duty, because these cells fire tonically in rhythm with the cardiac cycle, drive the sympathetic preganglionic neurons of the spinal cord, and are the main reason resting arterial pressure exists at all, since silencing them drops blood pressure toward collapse.

Emergency names their finest hour, since their firing climbs during compensated bleeding to defend perfusion, and the sudden fall of their activity marks the moment a haemorrhage decompensates into cardiovascular collapse.

Neuron marks it as a cell silenced from moment to moment by the arterial baroreceptors, the negative feedback loop that keeps the holding steady rather than runaway.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are the C1 neurons, named for the first adrenaline-making cell group found in the medulla.

They are the only neurons in the brain that finish the catecholamine assembly line and make adrenaline itself, also called epinephrine, using an enzyme called phenylethanolamine N-methyltransferase (PNMT) that converts noradrenaline into adrenaline.

Their day job is blunt and vital: they send a continuous excitatory command down the spinal cord to the sympathetic neurons that squeeze your arteries, and when you bleed, suffocate, run out of blood sugar or get an infection, they mount the emergency response.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Small to medium multipolar cells with three to five modestly branched dendrites, arranged as a thin sheet rather than a compact ball, which is why they were missed for so long.

The dendrites of the spinally projecting cells reach towards the ventral surface of the medulla, putting them within range of substances in cerebrospinal fluid and of surface oxygen levels, and they interleave with, but are chemically distinct from, the A1 noradrenaline neurons behind them.

SIZE:

Somata are 15 to 30 micrometres across the long axis, spindle-shaped or triangular, with dendritic fields of 200 to 400 micrometres.

The whole C1 column in a human is a ribbon perhaps 6 to 10 millimetres long and under 1 millimetre thick, running just beneath the nucleus ambiguus.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Three adrenergic groups exist in the medulla and their proportions have been measured: C1 holds about 72 percent of adrenaline-making medullary neurons, C2 in the solitary tract and dorsal vagal region about 13 percent, and C3 in the mediodorsal medulla about 15 percent.

Within C1 the functional split is by target, since bulbospinal C1 neurons descend to the spinal cord and control blood vessels while others ascend to hypothalamus, locus coeruleus and solitary tract nucleus to control hormones and alertness.

A third split is by fast transmitter, as most bulbospinal C1 cells also load glutamate using vesicular glutamate transporter 2 (VGLUT2).

WHERE IT LIVES:

The rostral ventrolateral medulla (RVLM), a patch of reticular formation below the nucleus ambiguus, medial to the spinal trigeminal tract, lateral to the inferior olive and dorsal to the pyramid, running from the caudal pole of the facial nucleus towards the obex.

The column continues caudally and interdigitates with the A1 noradrenergic group, a genuine chemical interface inside one anatomical strip, while C2 sits in the nucleus of the solitary tract and dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus and C3 near the dorsal midline.

The ventral dendrites reach the glia-lined ventral medullary surface, the interface between brain tissue and cerebrospinal fluid.

NUMBER:

No rigorous human stereological count of C1 neurons exists, and that must be said plainly rather than papered over with false precision.

In the rat there are roughly 700 to 1,100 C1 neurons per side, about 60 percent of which project to the spinal cord.

Scaling by medullary volume gives a defensible human bound of 5,000 to 25,000 per side, so 10,000 to 50,000 in total, well under one hundredth of one percent of the brainstem's 200 million neurons; confidence is high for the rat figures and low for the human ones.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting near minus 55 to minus 60 millivolts, these cells are slow tonic pacemakers firing at 0.5 to 5 hertz driven by a persistent sodium current rather than by synaptic input.

Their signature is barosensitivity: a rise in arterial pressure stretches receptors in the carotid arteries and aortic arch, exciting the solitary tract nucleus, which excites the caudal ventrolateral medulla, which delivers GABA to C1 and switches it off within about 100 milliseconds.

Firing is also locked to the breathing rhythm, so sympathetic traffic surges once per breath.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs: inhibitory GABA from the caudal ventrolateral medulla carrying the baroreflex; glutamate from the solitary tract nucleus carrying oxygen and inflammatory signals; the retrotrapezoid nucleus carrying carbon dioxide; the paraventricular hypothalamus; the periaqueductal grey; and dense local inhibition.

Outputs: the intermediolateral cell column of the thoracic and upper lumbar spinal cord and sacral autonomic preganglionic neurons, with ascending branches to paraventricular hypothalamus, locus coeruleus, raphe, periaqueductal grey and solitary tract nucleus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin and slow, conducting at roughly 0.5 to 1.5 metres per second, so a command leaving the medulla takes tens of milliseconds to reach the mid-thoracic cord.

Descending axons travel in the lateral funiculus and form dense baskets around preganglionic cells, though many adrenaline-containing swellings again make no classical synapse.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Adrenaline, made by adding a methyl group to noradrenaline with PNMT, which is the stain used to identify these cells.

Glutamate is the fast transmitter that actually drives the spinal target moment to moment while adrenaline acting on alpha-2 receptors is a slower modulator, and this two-speed arrangement is the most commonly misunderstood fact about C1.

Co-stored peptides include neuropeptide Y (NPY), which causes long-lasting vasoconstriction, substance P and pituitary adenylate cyclase-activating polypeptide (PACAP), and mu-opioid receptors on these cells explain why opioid drugs lower blood pressure.

MYELINATION:

Essentially unmyelinated, in the class physiologists call C fibres, which is exactly why conduction is around 1 metre per second.

Slowness is tolerable because blood pressure control works over hundreds of milliseconds, not microseconds.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest, in keeping with slow steady firing, but unusually oxygen sensitive.

These neurons and their astrocytes respond to a fall in oxygen within seconds, which is a design feature rather than a weakness, though it also means prolonged hypoxia damages them early.

PLASTICITY:

Chronic high blood pressure is accompanied by a lasting rise in RVLM excitability involving oxidative stress, angiotensin II signalling and microglial activation, so the set point itself drifts upward and stays there.

Repeated low blood sugar blunts the C1 response to the next episode, the mechanism behind hypoglycaemia unawareness in diabetes.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The astrocytes here are not ordinary: astrocytes at the ventral medullary surface are themselves oxygen sensors, and when oxygen falls they release adenosine triphosphate (ATP) that directly excites C1 neurons, so part of what looks like neuronal oxygen sensing is glial.

Microglia in the RVLM become activated in experimental hypertension and heart failure and release cytokines that raise C1 firing, making local immune cells part of the blood pressure circuit rather than spectators.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take these cells out and the ventrolateral medulla stops being a pressure regulator and becomes ordinary reticular formation.

Resting arterial pressure collapses by 40 to 50 millimetres of mercury, the baroreflex has nothing left to act on, standing up causes fainting, and the coordinated one-second recruitment of heart, vessels, adrenal gland and pituitary during bleeding never happens.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

C1 neurons are the clearest brainstem example of a cell that is sensor, integrator and effector at once.

They release adrenaline and neuropeptide Y by volume transmission, are metabolically coupled to oxygen-sensing astrocytes, and drive the adrenal medulla to dump adrenaline into the blood, so one cell type controls both the neural and the hormonal arms of the stress response.
