They also signal to the immune system, since activating them changes anti-inflammatory hormone release and alters the course of experimental sepsis and kidney injury, and their peptide co-release is frequency dependent, so the same neuron speaks a different chemical language at 1 hertz and at 15 hertz.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: GABAergic baroreflex signal proportional to mean arterial pressure over 60 to 180 millimetres of mercury; glutamate from solitary tract nucleus carrying carotid body oxygen (arterial oxygen 30 to 100 millimetres of mercury); carbon dioxide and pH drive from the retrotrapezoid nucleus; blood glucose 2 to 10 millimoles per litre; circulating inflammatory signal; descending drive from hypothalamus and periaqueductal grey; astrocytic ATP.

• OUTPUTS: spike train to spinal sympathetic preganglionic neurons, 0 to 20 hertz, carrying glutamate plus adrenaline plus neuropeptide Y; ascending spike train to hypothalamus and locus coeruleus; simulation-level output is sympathetic nerve activity from 0 to 1.

• STATE: membrane voltage; persistent sodium activation; vesicular adrenaline store; vesicular neuropeptide Y store; slow excitability set point representing chronic remodelling.

• PARAMETERS: resting minus 57 millivolts; threshold minus 45 millivolts; baseline 2 hertz; maximum about 20 hertz; conduction velocity 1 metre per second; baroreflex gain about 0.2 hertz lost per millimetre of mercury; 5,000 to 25,000 cells per side.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 15 to 30 milliseconds; baroreflex loop delay 100 to 200 milliseconds; respiratory modulation 3 to 5 seconds; spinal adrenaline clearance 1 to 5 seconds; hypertensive remodelling weeks to months.

• FAILURE MODE: degeneration of C1 neurons in multiple system atrophy causes severe orthostatic hypotension, in which standing drops blood pressure enough to cause collapse; C1 loss also occurs in Parkinson's disease; chronic RVLM overactivity is a leading mechanism of neurogenic high blood pressure and of sympathetic overdrive in heart failure; sudden failure during severe bleeding turns a compensated haemorrhage into cardiovascular collapse.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate one C1 adrenergic neuron that sets sympathetic outflow from arterial pressure, oxygen and blood glucose, and releases the peptide neuropeptide Y only when firing is high.
Create a number variable called arterial pressure with initial value 100.
Create a number variable called oxygen level with initial value 95.
Create a number variable called blood glucose with initial value 5.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 2.
Create a number variable called peptide released with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called sympathetic output with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 600 times.
    Calculate the difference of 100 and arterial pressure.
    Calculate the product of that difference and 0.2.
    Set baroreflex drive to that product.
    Calculate the difference of 95 and oxygen level.
    Calculate the product of that difference and 0.15.
    Set hypoxia drive to that product.
    Calculate the difference of 5 and blood glucose.
    Calculate the product of that difference and 0.4.
    Set glucose drive to that product.
    Calculate the sum of baroreflex drive and hypoxia drive.
    Calculate the sum of that sum and glucose drive.
    Calculate the sum of that sum and 2.
    Set firing rate to that sum.
    Check if firing rate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if firing rate is greater than 20.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 20.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if firing rate is greater than 8.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of firing rate and 8.
        Calculate the product of that difference and 0.02.
        Set peptide released to that product.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set peptide released to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Add firing rate to the end of sympathetic output.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in sympathetic output.
Display that number to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because this cell is best understood as a closed feedback loop with the circulation, and Python lets the neuron and the simulated arteries sit side by side in a few readable lines.

DT = 0.1  # seconds per step

def c1_neuron(pressure, oxygen=95.0, glucose=5.0, baseline=2.0):
    """Return firing rate in Hz plus adrenaline and neuropeptide Y release."""
    baro = 0.20 * (100.0 - pressure)              # 0.2 Hz lost per mmHg rise
    hypoxia = 0.15 * max(0.0, 95.0 - oxygen)
    hypoglyc = 0.40 * max(0.0, 5.0 - glucose)
    rate = max(0.0, min(20.0, baseline + baro + hypoxia + hypoglyc))
    return rate, 0.05 * rate, 0.02 * max(0.0, rate - 8.0)  # peptide needs high rates

def run(seconds=60.0, bleed_at=20.0, loss_rate=1.2):
    pressure, deficit = 100.0, 0.0
    for i in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        t = i * DT
        if t > bleed_at:
            deficit += loss_rate * DT             # steady haemorrhage
        rate, adr, npy = c1_neuron(pressure)
        target = 100.0 - 1.4 * deficit + 2.5 * rate + 40.0 * npy - 5.0
        pressure += (target - pressure) * (DT / 2.0)   # vascular lag of about 2 s
        if abs(t - round(t)) < 1e-9 and int(t) % 10 == 0:
            print(f"t={t:5.1f}s  MAP={pressure:6.1f} mmHg  C1={rate:5.2f} Hz")

run()

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small spindle-shaped neuron in the ventrolateral medulla that stains for PNMT, is silenced within a tenth of a second by a rise in blood pressure, surges within a second of blood loss, and speaks glutamate for speed and adrenaline for persistence from the same terminal.

It is the only neuron in the human brain whose final chemical product is adrenaline and whose failure makes standing upright impossible.

CHAPTER 136. PERIAQUEDUCTAL GREY NEURONS (Freeze-Or-Flee Selecting Neuron) (NON-COG)

In 1969 a rat lay quietly through abdominal surgery with no anaesthetic, because a wire in this grey collar around the midbrain's central channel was switched on.

Periaqueductal grey states only that the tissue is grey and lies around the cerebral aqueduct, an address with no function in it.

Freeze-Or-Flee names the choice this tissue makes, and the choice is anatomically real, since exciting the dorsolateral column drives escape and threat vocalisation while exciting the lateral and ventrolateral columns drives immobility, so a few millimetres of position decide whether the animal runs or holds still.

Selecting names the work, since the region does not execute the movement itself but picks which defensive programme the lower motor machinery will run.

Neuron marks it as a cell that also launches the descending pathway producing opioid analgesia, which is why an animal in mortal danger stops feeling its wounds.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The periaqueductal grey (PAG) is a sleeve of neurons wrapped around the cerebral aqueduct, the narrow channel of cerebrospinal fluid running through the midbrain.

It is the brain's survival command post: it does not analyse a threat, it selects and executes a whole-body response to one.

Freeze, flee, fight, fall silent, stop feeling pain, change your breathing, change your blood pressure or make a sound, each is a package assembled here and fired as a unit, and this is also the most powerful pain-suppressing site in the nervous system and where morphine does much of its work.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Most PAG neurons are small to medium spindle-shaped or triangular cells with three to five sparsely branched dendrites running parallel to the aqueduct rather than across it.

That orientation matters, because it keeps each longitudinal column functionally separate from its neighbour despite there being no anatomical wall between them.

SIZE:

Somata are typically 10 to 30 micrometres across, small by brainstem standards, with dendritic fields of 200 to 500 micrometres elongated along the brainstem axis.

The whole human PAG is a tube roughly 15 millimetres long with a wall 2 to 4 millimetres thick and a total volume commonly reported near 400 cubic millimetres for both sides together.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The functional taxonomy is columnar rather than cellular, with four longitudinal columns: dorsomedial, dorsolateral, lateral and ventrolateral.

Dorsolateral neurons produce flight, raised blood pressure and a non-opioid analgesia; lateral neurons produce fight with similar autonomic activation; ventrolateral neurons produce freezing, quiescence, falling blood pressure and heart rate and a powerful opioid-dependent analgesia.

By chemistry the population splits roughly between glutamatergic output neurons and GABAergic local interneurons, with peptide subsets containing enkephalin, substance P, cholecystokinin and neurotensin, and a dopamine group in the ventrolateral column.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The midbrain tegmentum, forming a collar around the cerebral aqueduct from the posterior commissure rostrally to the locus coeruleus and fourth ventricle caudally, where it becomes continuous with the periventricular grey of the pons.

Its neighbours are all interfaces that matter: the dorsal raphe nucleus is physically embedded inside the ventral PAG, the superior and inferior colliculi lie above, the cuneiform nucleus and mesencephalic locomotor region laterally, the oculomotor and trochlear nuclei ventromedially, and the ependymal lining of the aqueduct forms its inner surface.

Rostrally it grades into the hypothalamus without a sharp border, which is why it behaves like a downstream extension of hypothalamic defence circuits.

NUMBER:

There is no rigorous stereological count of human PAG neurons, and inventing one would be dishonest.

An arithmetic bound can be given transparently: a bilateral volume near 400 cubic millimetres multiplied by a midbrain grey neuronal density in the plausible range of 5,000 to 25,000 neurons per cubic millimetre gives 2 million to 10 million neurons.

Taking the middle, about 3 to 5 million, the PAG is on the order of 1.5 to 2.5 percent of the brainstem's 200 million neurons; confidence is low and the figure is an order of magnitude, not a measurement.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is around minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts, and most PAG output neurons are quiet at rest, not because they lack drive but because local interneurons hold them down with continuous GABA release.

Many carry a prominent A-type potassium current that delays the first spike, so a weak threat produces nothing and a sustained one produces a sudden switch, which suits a system that must commit to one behaviour rather than blend several.

Firing during a defensive response is a sustained 10 to 40 hertz that lasts as long as the behaviour, and the key computational fact is that the resting state is suppression, so the fastest way to activate the PAG is to remove inhibition rather than add excitation.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from medial prefrontal and anterior cingulate cortex, the central and medial amygdala, the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis, the hypothalamus, the superior colliculus, and directly from spinal cord and trigeminal nucleus through the spinomesencephalic tract carrying pain.

Ventrolateral outputs go to the rostral ventromedial medulla, especially raphe magnus, which projects to the spinal dorsal horn; other outputs reach nucleus retroambiguus for vocalisation, the pontine micturition centre for the bladder, the ventrolateral medulla for blood pressure, the parabrachial nucleus for breathing and the cuneiform nucleus for locomotion.

The most important local connection is GABAergic interneuron onto output neuron, because that is the switch morphine flips.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Descending output axons are thin to medium at 0.5 to 2 micrometres, conducting at 1 to 10 metres per second, fast enough for a behaviour that unfolds over hundreds of milliseconds.

They descend in the dorsolateral funiculus and the ventrolateral tegmentum, while local interneuron axons are short, unmyelinated and confined to one column.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate for output neurons and GABA for interneurons form the backbone, layered with enkephalin and other endogenous opioid peptides, substance P, neurotensin, cholecystokinin, corticotropin-releasing factor and ventrolateral dopamine.

Mu-opioid receptors are dense and sit chiefly on GABAergic terminals, so morphine works by silencing the inhibitor and thereby releasing the output neuron, a mechanism called disinhibition.

Endocannabinoids made on demand by output neurons travel backwards across the synapse and suppress transmitter release from selected inputs.

MYELINATION:

Mixed and generally light, with thin sheaths on descending projection axons and none on the dense local circuitry.

The PAG is grey matter in the strict sense, so its low myelin density is one way it is recognised on a stained section.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low at rest because the cells are largely silent, and briefly very high during a defence reaction.

Functional imaging shows a sharp localised rise in PAG blood flow during acute pain and during panic, consistent with a tissue that idles and then sprints.

PLASTICITY:

Chronic opioid exposure remodels this circuit, so mu-opioid receptors become less effective, GABA release rebounds, and the descending pathway can flip from suppressing pain to facilitating it, the accepted mechanism of opioid-induced hyperalgesia and a driver of dose escalation.

Persistent pain and repeated threat also shift the balance between the ventrolateral and dorsolateral columns, biasing a person towards freezing rather than escape, which is relevant to post-traumatic stress.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Microglia here are unusually consequential, because chronic morphine activates them and blocking that activation restores morphine potency in animals, so local immune cells are part of the analgesic circuit rather than housekeeping.

Astrocytes in the PAG express mu-opioid receptors and modulate glutamate clearance during withdrawal, and the ependymal cells lining the aqueduct give this region direct access to substances carried in cerebrospinal fluid.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove PAG neurons and the midbrain keeps its eye movement control, its sensory maps and its dopamine cells, but loses its capacity to commit.

There would be no freezing, no coordinated flight, no vocal cry, no stress-induced analgesia and no bladder control; the animal still sees the predator and still runs the sensory analysis, it simply does nothing about it.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The dominant computation is disinhibition, so a model with excitation only will get the drug pharmacology completely wrong.

Retrograde endocannabinoid signalling lets the output neuron talk backwards to its own inputs, selectively quieting the amygdala input, which is how cannabinoids and opioids produce analgesia by different routes, and dendritic peptide release adds slow modulation over tens of seconds.

The columnar layout supports a winner-take-all competition in which activity in one column suppresses its neighbours, so the outputs are mutually exclusive behaviours rather than a blend, and PAG neurons also directly control local blood flow.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamatergic threat signal from amygdala and hypothalamus (0 to 50 hertz); top-down control from medial prefrontal and cingulate cortex (0 to 30 hertz); ascending nociceptive drive from spinal lamina I (0 to 100 hertz); local GABAergic tone (0 to 40 hertz, tonically active at rest); mu-opioid receptor occupancy (0 to 1); threat distance as a scalar selecting the column.

• OUTPUTS: sustained spike train to rostral ventromedial medulla ON and OFF cells, expressed as descending analgesia gain from 0 to 1; freezing command to medullary reticular formation; flight command to cuneiform nucleus; autonomic set point change to the ventrolateral medulla, positive for the dorsolateral column and negative for the ventrolateral column.

• STATE: output neuron voltage; interneuron voltage; A-current inactivation; endocannabinoid concentration; opioid receptor desensitisation (slow); currently selected column.

• PARAMETERS: resting minus 65 millivolts; threshold minus 45 millivolts; tonic interneuron rate 12 hertz; defence output 10 to 40 hertz; conduction velocity 1 to 10 metres per second; roughly 3 to 5 million neurons bilaterally.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 to 40 milliseconds; A-current inactivation 30 to 80 milliseconds; GABA-A decay 5 to 20 milliseconds; endocannabinoid signalling 2 to 20 seconds; descending analgesia onset 1 to 5 seconds; opioid tolerance days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: weakened descending inhibition to the rostral ventromedial medulla is a central mechanism of chronic pain, fibromyalgia and medication-overuse headache; over-activation of the dorsolateral column reproduces a panic attack, terror and urge to flee included; PAG damage from stroke or tumour causes loss of vertical gaze, sleep disturbance and, ventrally, akinetic mutism; opioid tolerance is maladaptive plasticity of this exact synapse.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a ventrolateral periaqueductal grey output neuron held silent by local inhibition and released by opioid action, and to report the resulting strength of descending pain suppression.
Create a number variable called interneuron rate with initial value 12.
Create a number variable called opioid occupancy with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called nociceptive drive with initial value 20.
Create a number variable called output voltage with initial value -65.
Create a list variable called output spikes with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 4000 times.
    Calculate the difference of 1 and opioid occupancy.
    Calculate the product of interneuron rate and that difference.
    Set effective inhibition to that product.
    Calculate the product of nociceptive drive and 0.06.
    Set excitation to that product.
    Calculate the product of effective inhibition and 0.09.
    Set inhibition to that product.
    Calculate the difference of excitation and inhibition.
    Calculate the product of that difference and 3.
    Calculate the sum of -65 and that product.
    Set target voltage to that sum.
    Calculate the difference of target voltage and output voltage.
    Calculate the product of that difference and 0.05.
    Calculate the sum of output voltage and that product.
    Set output voltage to that sum.
    Check if output voltage is greater than -45.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the current step number to the end of output spikes.
        Set output voltage to -66.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in output spikes.
Display that number to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the point is a two-cell interaction, an inhibitor and an output cell, and Python makes the disinhibition arithmetic visible with no framework in the way.

DT = 0.001  # 1 ms steps

def vlpag_output(seconds=4.0, opioid=0.0, nociception=20.0, tonic_gaba=12.0):
    """opioid is mu-receptor occupancy from 0 to 1; returns rate and analgesia."""
    v, spikes = -65.0, 0
    gaba = tonic_gaba * (1.0 - opioid)     # opioids act on GABA terminals
    for _ in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        target = -65.0 + 2.0 * (1.2 * nociception - 1.5 * gaba)
        v += (target - v) * (DT / 0.020)   # 20 ms membrane time constant
        if v > -45.0:
            spikes += 1
            v = -66.0
    rate = spikes / seconds
    return rate, min(1.0, rate / 60.0)     # analgesia saturates near 60 Hz

for occ in (0.0, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0):
    r, a = vlpag_output(opioid=occ)
    print(f"occupancy {occ:4.2f} -> vlPAG {r:5.1f} Hz, analgesia {a:4.2f}")

r, a = vlpag_output(opioid=0.75, tonic_gaba=26.0)   # tolerance: GABA rebounds
print(f"tolerant, same dose -> {r:5.1f} Hz, analgesia {a:4.2f}")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small neuron in a grey sleeve around the midbrain aqueduct, silent at rest because it is held down rather than because it has nothing to say, organised into four longitudinal columns that each command a different survival package.

Stimulate it in the right column and a conscious human reports either overwhelming terror or the complete disappearance of pain, which no other single cell group can do.

CHAPTER 137. SPHERICAL BUSHY CELLS (COG)

You can tell which ear a sound reached first even when the gap is ten millionths of a second, and this neuron is the reason the clock is that good.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A spherical bushy cell is the first relay inside the brain for sound, one synapse downstream of the auditory nerve.

Its job is not to add anything but to preserve the exact timing of the signal, because the timing is the information.

Auditory nerve fibres fire at a preferred point on each cycle of a low-frequency sound wave, a behaviour called phase locking, and this cell takes that timing, cleans it, and hands it to the medial superior olive, the nucleus that compares the two ears and tells you where a sound came from.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is round, hence spherical, and carries only one or two short stubby dendrites ending in a tangle of fine branches resembling a small bush, hence bushy.

The remarkable part is what is stuck to it: one to four gigantic nerve terminals called endbulbs of Held, each a branching claw from a single auditory nerve fibre, together covering a large fraction of the cell body.

Because the contact is on the soma rather than out on the dendrites, the signal does not travel and lose sharpness before it acts, and the small dendritic synapses that do exist have been shown to improve timing rather than degrade it.

SIZE:

Somata are typically 20 to 35 micrometres across in humans, large for a sensory relay cell.

A single endbulb can spread across 20 to 30 micrometres of that surface and contains dozens to a couple of hundred separate release sites, while the dendritic tuft extends only 30 to 80 micrometres, so the whole cell is compact by design.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

In cats and rodents the population divides into large spherical bushy cells, tuned to low frequencies and projecting to both medial superior olives, and small ones projecting mainly to the ipsilateral lateral superior olive.

In humans that clean separation is not evident, and spherical bushy cells, globular bushy cells and stellate cells lie somewhat intermixed among the entering branches of the cochlear nerve, an important species difference for anyone modelling the human system.

A practical third axis is endbulb count, since a cell with one endbulb behaves as a faithful relay and a cell with four behaves partly as a coincidence detector.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The rostral part of the anteroventral cochlear nucleus (AVCN), on the lateral surface of the brainstem at the pontomedullary junction, immediately deep to where the cochlear nerve enters the brain and against the inferior cerebellar peduncle.

The cells are laid out tonotopically, in an orderly map from low to high sound frequency, following the branching of the entering nerve.

This is explicitly an interface structure, straddling the boundary between peripheral nerve and central nervous system, and the changeover from peripheral myelin made by Schwann cells to central myelin made by oligodendrocytes happens inside it, at the Obersteiner-Redlich zone.

NUMBER:

The human cochlear nucleus on each side contains on the order of 100,000 neurons in total.

Spherical bushy cells are a major but not dominant fraction of the anteroventral part, and a defensible estimate is 25,000 to 40,000 per side, so 50,000 to 80,000 for both ears; confidence is low, because human counts by cell type are scarce and human bushy cells are not cleanly segregated.

A useful cross-check is that each human ear has only about 30,000 to 40,000 auditory nerve fibres, each branching to contact several bushy cells, so the numbers are of the same order; this places the type at roughly 0.03 to 0.04 percent of the brainstem's 200 million neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

This is one of the fastest neurons in the human body, and the speed comes from deliberately making the cell leaky.

A large low-voltage-activated potassium conductance carried by Kv1 channels is already partly open at rest, so the membrane time constant, the time the cell takes to blur an input, is only about 0.3 to 1 millisecond rather than the 20 to 50 milliseconds of a cortical neuron; input resistance is a correspondingly low 20 to 70 megaohms, and a hyperpolarization-activated current called Ih, carried by HCN1 channels, pins the resting voltage near minus 60 millivolts.

The consequence is phasic firing: a slow ramp of current produces no spike at all, while a fast endbulb input produces exactly one spike with jitter of only tens of microseconds, and the cell fires at 100 to 300 hertz during a loud tone.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitation comes from one to four auditory nerve fibres through endbulbs of Held, using glutamate on exceptionally fast AMPA receptors built from GluA3 and GluA4 subunits in their flop form, which open and close in well under a millisecond.

Inhibition arrives as glycine and gamma-aminobutyric acid from D-stellate cells of the ventral cochlear nucleus, from tuberculoventral cells of the dorsal cochlear nucleus, and as descending feedback from the ventral nucleus of the trapezoid body.

Output goes bilaterally to the medial superior olive, which computes interaural time difference, and to the lateral superior olive and lateral nucleus of the trapezoid body.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thick for a small cell at 2 to 4 micrometres and heavily myelinated, conducting at 10 to 30 metres per second.

The axons to the medial superior olive act as tuned delay lines, with internode lengths, the spacing between gaps in the myelin, adjusted so arrival times from the two ears line up, a rare case of a wire whose insulation is engineered for delay rather than speed alone.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, released in unusually large quanta with a low probability of release per site, which is how a giant terminal fires hundreds of times per second without running out.

Modulation arrives as glycine and GABA for inhibition and as acetylcholine from the ventral nucleus of the trapezoid body acting through nicotinic and muscarinic receptors to adjust gain.

MYELINATION:

Heavy and highly specialised on both the incoming and outgoing sides.

Inside the nucleus the auditory nerve fibre changes from Schwann cell myelin to oligodendrocyte myelin, and the last stretch before the endbulb loses myelin altogether in a controlled way.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Extremely high, among the greatest anywhere in the brain.

The auditory brainstem shows some of the densest staining for cytochrome oxidase, the marker enzyme of mitochondrial energy production, in the whole nervous system, because these cells fire at hundreds of hertz for a lifetime, and endbulbs are packed with mitochondria.

PLASTICITY:

Endbulbs are strikingly sensitive to hearing experience: deafness makes them shrink, flatten and lose their shape within weeks, and early cochlear implant stimulation partially restores them, which is the cellular basis of the clinical rule that deaf children implanted early do far better than those implanted late.

In adults, sudden loss of input produces a rebound of spontaneous activity in the cochlear nucleus, one of the leading explanations for tinnitus.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here do something genuinely unusual: astrocytes wrap the endbulb tightly and express very high densities of the glutamate transporters GLAST and GLT-1, because a synapse firing hundreds of times a second would otherwise flood itself with its own transmitter and lose all timing precision.

Oligodendrocytes handle the peripheral-to-central myelin transition and set internode lengths that tune conduction delay, so they participate in the computation rather than merely insulating it, and microglia prune endbulb terminals during development and after deafness.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove spherical bushy cells and the cochlear nucleus still reports that a sound happened, how loud it was and what frequencies it held, because stellate and octopus cells survive.

What vanishes is microsecond timing, and with it the ability to know where a sound is, to pick one voice out of a noisy room, and to sense that the auditory world has a spatial layout at all; the region becomes a loudness meter rather than a spatial instrument.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The dominant non-classical feature is that this cell computes by being deliberately bad at integrating, since Kv1 channels make it forget the past almost instantly, so it reports coincidence rather than sum.

Glial glutamate uptake is part of the circuit because clearance speed sets the width of the synaptic event, and myelin geometry is used as a computational variable, with oligodendrocytes instructed to set internode length so axonal delays match.

The endbulb also shows very little depression in a living animal despite its size, implying release probability is kept low on purpose, a form of self-regulation a naive model will get wrong.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: one to four auditory nerve spike trains, each 0 to 300 hertz, with phase locking described by a vector strength of 0 to 0.9 below 1 kilohertz; glycinergic and GABAergic inhibition from D-stellate and tuberculoventral cells, 0 to 100 hertz; cholinergic gain signal (0 to 1).

• OUTPUTS: a single spike train, 0 to 300 hertz, with timing jitter of 20 to 100 microseconds, delivered to ipsilateral and contralateral medial superior olive and to lateral superior olive, with a tuned axonal delay.

• STATE: membrane voltage; synaptic conductance per endbulb; Kv1 activation; Ih activation; readily releasable vesicle pool per endbulb; refractory timer.

• PARAMETERS: capacitance about 12 picofarads; leak conductance about 3 nanosiemens; maximum Kv1 conductance 30 to 50 nanosiemens; Ih conductance 10 to 20 nanosiemens; endbulb peak conductance 20 to 60 nanosiemens; threshold minus 45 millivolts; resting minus 62 millivolts; conduction velocity 10 to 30 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 0.3 to 1 millisecond; AMPA decay 0.2 to 0.4 milliseconds; Kv1 activation about 0.5 milliseconds; refractory period 0.7 to 1 millisecond; glutamate clearance under 1 millisecond; endbulb remodelling after deafness 2 to 8 weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of timing precision with age produces difficulty understanding speech in noise despite a normal audiogram; auditory neuropathy, in which the nerve fires but not in time, destroys the input this cell depends on; deafferentation causes endbulb collapse and rebound hyperactivity linked to tinnitus; anything that slows the membrane, including demyelination, converts a microsecond timer into a millisecond one and abolishes sound localisation.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a spherical bushy cell that fires one precisely timed spike for a fast endbulb input but refuses to fire for a slowly rising input, because its low-threshold potassium channels have already opened.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -62.
Create a number variable called synaptic conductance with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called potassium activation with initial value 0.35.
Create a number variable called refractory timer with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called output spike times with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 4000 times.
    Check if an endbulb input arrives at this step.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of synaptic conductance and 50.
        Set synaptic conductance to that sum.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of synaptic conductance and 0.98.
    Set synaptic conductance to that product.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than -57.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of potassium activation and 0.02.
        Set potassium activation to that sum.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the product of potassium activation and 0.99.
        Set potassium activation to that product.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of potassium activation and 12.
    Set potassium leak to that product.
    Calculate the difference of synaptic conductance and potassium leak.
    Calculate the product of that difference and 0.05.
    Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and that product.
    Set membrane voltage to that sum.
    Check if both membrane voltage is greater than -45 and refractory timer is equal to 0 are true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the current step number to the end of output spike times.
        Set membrane voltage to -68.
        Set refractory timer to 140.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if refractory timer is greater than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of refractory timer and 1.
        Set refractory timer to that difference.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in output spike times.
Display that number to the console.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because a conductance-based model in real physical units, nanosiemens and picofarads, reads almost like the sentence describing it, and the point is to show a beginner that the low-threshold potassium channel is what creates the timing.

import math

DT = 0.005  # milliseconds

def spherical_bushy(inputs_ms=(), ramp_pa=0.0, gklt_max=40.0, dur=20.0):
    """Units: mV, nS, pF, ms, pA. w is Kv1 low-threshold potassium activation."""
    V, gsyn, w = -62.0, 0.0, 0.35
    C, gleak, EL, gh, Eh, EK = 12.0, 3.0, -65.0, 15.0, -43.0, -80.0
    spikes, refr, k = [], -10.0, 0
    for i in range(int(dur / DT)):
        t = i * DT
        while k < len(inputs_ms) and inputs_ms[k] <= t:
            gsyn += 50.0                 # one endbulb of Held, about 50 nS
            k += 1
        gsyn *= math.exp(-DT / 0.3)      # AMPA decay of 0.3 ms
        w += (1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp(-(V + 57.0) / 6.0)) - w) * (DT / 0.5)
        I = (gleak * (EL - V) + gh * (Eh - V) + gsyn * (0.0 - V)
             + gklt_max * w * (EK - V) + ramp_pa * (t / dur))
        if t - refr > 0.7:               # 0.7 ms refractory period
            V += I * DT / C
            if V > -45.0:
                spikes.append(round(t, 3))
                V, refr = -68.0, t
    return spikes

print("one endbulb at 2 ms ->", spherical_bushy([2.0]))
print("300 Hz endbulb train ->", len(spherical_bushy([2.0 + 3.333 * i for i in range(5)])), "spikes")
print("slow ramp, Kv1 on  ->", len(spherical_bushy(ramp_pa=800.0)), "spikes")
print("slow ramp, Kv1 off ->", len(spherical_bushy(ramp_pa=800.0, gklt_max=0.0)), "spikes")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A round cell with a tiny bush of dendrites, wearing one to four of the largest nerve terminals in the brain like a helmet, that answers a fast input with exactly one spike and a slow input with nothing at all.

Find a neuron whose membrane forgets in under a millisecond and whose spike times are accurate to tens of microseconds, and you have found a spherical bushy cell.

CHAPTER 138. GLOBULAR BUSHY CELLS (COG)

This neuron collects up to seventy trembling, imprecise inputs, produces from them a spike train more accurate than any one of them, and sends it across the brainstem to build the largest synapse in the mammalian brain.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A globular bushy cell is the second great timing cell of the cochlear nucleus, and it exists to make inhibition as fast and accurate as excitation.

Its axon crosses the midline and ends on a neuron in the medial nucleus of the trapezoid body as the calyx of Held, a hand-shaped terminal so large it is visible under an ordinary microscope and so reliable it essentially never fails.

That target cell converts the signal from excitation to inhibition, so the arrangement is a sign-inverting, delay-matched relay that lets one ear silence the other ear's channel with sub-millisecond accuracy, which is how you work out which side a loud sound came from.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is slightly elongated or pear-shaped, which is what globular means here, and like its spherical cousin it has one or two short dendrites ending in a small bushy tuft.

The critical difference is on the surface, because instead of one to four giant endbulbs it is peppered with many medium-sized terminals called modified endbulbs, spread over the soma and proximal dendrites.

SIZE:

Somata are typically 15 to 30 micrometres, slightly smaller and more angular than spherical bushy cells, and the axon is the thickest in the cochlear nucleus at up to 3 to 5 micrometres.

The calyx of Held it forms wraps a target cell body 15 to 25 micrometres across and contains on the order of 500 to 700 separate release sites in one terminal.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The most meaningful subdivision is by convergence, which has been measured directly and ranges from 9 to 69 auditory nerve inputs, with more than half of cells receiving between 15 and 23.

Cells at the low end behave more like relays and those at the high end more like averagers, so the population spans a continuum from temporal fidelity to rate coding.

A second axis is target, since most globular bushy cells form a calyx in the contralateral medial nucleus of the trapezoid body while collaterals also supply the lateral and ventral nuclei of the trapezoid body and the superior paraolivary nucleus.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The caudal anteroventral cochlear nucleus and the adjacent nerve root region, sometimes called the interstitial or peripheral nucleus, on the lateral surface of the pontomedullary junction.

Its axon runs medially through the ventral acoustic stria, better known as the trapezoid body, crosses the midline of the pons, and ends in the contralateral medial nucleus of the trapezoid body, which sits lateral to the medial lemniscus against the ventral pons.

This is another true interface cell, living where the cochlear nerve root meets the brainstem and projecting across the midline; note that a distinct human medial nucleus of the trapezoid body was denied for nearly fifty years and has only recently been documented in human post-mortem tissue, so a simulation should include it while flagging that the human evidence is thinner than the animal evidence.

NUMBER:

Globular bushy cells are less numerous than spherical bushy cells, and a defensible estimate is 8,000 to 15,000 per side within a cochlear nucleus of roughly 100,000 neurons per side.

That is about 16,000 to 30,000 in a whole human brain, roughly 0.01 percent of the brainstem's 200 million neurons.

Confidence is low, because the figure is anchored on the size of the medial nucleus of the trapezoid body and on the approximately one-to-one relationship between a globular bushy cell and a calyx of Held, not on a direct human count.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Like its spherical cousin it is deliberately leaky, with a large Kv1 low-threshold potassium conductance giving a membrane time constant under a millisecond, but it adds a very large Kv3 high-threshold conductance that repolarises the spike in under half a millisecond.

That pairing allows sustained firing at 300 to 800 hertz without the spikes broadening, and because individual auditory nerve inputs here are sub-threshold, several must coincide within a few hundred microseconds to fire the cell.

That coincidence requirement is what removes jitter, so phase locking measured as vector strength is higher at the output than at any single input, meaning the cell manufactures timing precision rather than merely conserving it; the classic recorded pattern is primary-like with a notch.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs: 9 to 69 auditory nerve fibres through modified endbulbs of Held, all glutamatergic; glycinergic inhibition from D-stellate and tuberculoventral cells; descending cholinergic and GABAergic feedback from the superior olivary complex.

Outputs: one calyx of Held onto a principal cell of the contralateral medial nucleus of the trapezoid body, plus collaterals to the ipsilateral lateral and ventral nuclei of the trapezoid body and the superior paraolivary nucleus.

The chain to remember is that this excitatory cell exists to drive an inhibitory cell, so its net influence on sound localisation is negative.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The thickest and fastest axon in the cochlear nucleus, heavily myelinated and conducting at 15 to 40 metres per second, so the extra journey across the midline and the extra synapse cost almost nothing in time.

Near the calyx the internodes shorten and the axon narrows into a specialised heminode that reliably converts an arriving action potential into a massive calcium influx, and the whole delay is tuned so inhibition reaches the superior olive essentially simultaneously with the excitation it must oppose.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, released from the calyx onto AMPA receptors rich in the fast GluA4 subunit, with a postsynaptic response decaying in a fraction of a millisecond.

The calyx carries presynaptic metabotropic glutamate and GABA-B receptors that turn down release during loud sustained sound, and the target cell then releases glycine, so the pathway converts glutamate into glycine with one synapse and about 0.4 milliseconds of delay.

MYELINATION:

Among the most heavily myelinated axons in the brainstem for their diameter, and the geometry is tuned rather than merely maximised.

Losing this myelin desynchronises the binaural comparison and is a plausible contributor to the poor sound localisation reported in multiple sclerosis and some auditory processing disorders.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Enormous, both at the cell body and spectacularly at the calyx, which contains dense clusters of mitochondria held beside the release machinery by structures called mitochondria-associated adherens complexes.

Oxygen deprivation silences this pathway within seconds, which is why the auditory brainstem response is used clinically to monitor brainstem function during surgery.

PLASTICITY:

The calyx matures dramatically after hearing onset, changing from a spoon-shaped cup into a fenestrated hand, and that maturation depends on sound experience.

In the adult, short-term plasticity dominates, since the synapse depresses within milliseconds at high firing rates and recovers over tens to hundreds of milliseconds, and this depression is itself part of the code, signalling changes in sound level; conductive hearing loss in early childhood permanently degrades this timing, one reason repeated glue ear in infancy has lasting effects on listening in noise.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here are specialised for extreme glutamate loads, forming a tight sleeve around the calyx with very high densities of the transporter GLT-1, and the fenestrations in the mature calyx exist partly to let those glial processes reach in.

NG2 glia, a distinct class of progenitor cells, also contact the calyx directly, and oligodendrocytes tune internode length along the crossing axon, so glia are again setting the delay of a computation.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take away the globular bushy cells and the cochlear nucleus loses its only fast pathway to the other side of the brain.

The medial nucleus of the trapezoid body would fall silent, the superior olive would receive excitation from both ears with no matching inhibition, loudness comparisons between the ears would collapse, high-frequency sound localisation would fail, and the auditory brainstem response used in every newborn hearing screen would lose its later waves.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The headline non-classical property is noise reduction by convergence, since the cell exploits the statistical independence of jitter across many inputs so its output is more precise than its inputs, which no simple relay can do.

Short-term depression at the calyx is itself a computation, converting sustained sound level into a slowly adapting signal, while presynaptic receptors implement automatic gain control at the terminal and axonal delay is a tuned free parameter instructed by oligodendrocytes.

The calyx also regulates its own mitochondria and calcium buffering in an activity-dependent way, a form of self-maintenance without which it would fail within minutes at physiological firing rates.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: 9 to 69 auditory nerve spike trains, each 0 to 300 hertz and each sub-threshold alone, with phase locking vector strength 0 to 0.9 below 1 kilohertz; glycinergic inhibition from D-stellate and tuberculoventral cells, 0 to 100 hertz; descending modulation from the superior olivary complex (0 to 1).

• OUTPUTS: one spike train, 0 to 800 hertz, with output vector strength exceeding input vector strength, through a single calyx of Held onto one contralateral medial nucleus of the trapezoid body principal cell, plus collateral output to lateral and ventral trapezoid body nuclei.

• STATE: membrane voltage; summed synaptic conductance; Kv1 activation; Kv3 activation; refractory timer; readily releasable vesicle pool at the calyx; residual presynaptic calcium.

• PARAMETERS: capacitance 10 to 15 picofarads; membrane time constant 0.2 to 0.5 milliseconds; single input conductance 1 to 4 nanosiemens, deliberately sub-threshold; convergence 9 to 69 with a median near 19; threshold minus 45 millivolts; conduction velocity 15 to 40 metres per second; 500 to 700 calyx release sites.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 0.2 to 0.5 milliseconds; AMPA decay 0.2 to 0.3 milliseconds; spike width under 0.4 milliseconds; refractory period 0.6 to 0.8 milliseconds; calyx depression 2 to 50 milliseconds with recovery over 100 to 500 milliseconds; glutamate-to-glycine conversion delay about 0.4 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: demyelination or axonal slowing breaks the delay match and abolishes localisation of high-frequency sound; abnormalities of the superior olivary complex including the medial nucleus of the trapezoid body have been reported in autism and proposed to contribute to difficulty hearing speech in noise; loss of these cells removes waves three and four of the auditory brainstem response, detectable in a routine clinical test; sustained overdrive can transiently exhaust the calyx.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a globular bushy cell that receives many weak, jittered, phase-locked inputs and fires only when enough of them coincide, producing an output with less timing jitter than any single input.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -63.
Create a number variable called synaptic conductance with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called refractory timer with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called next arrival times with initial value empty list.
Create a list variable called output spike times with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 20 times.
    Add a randomly chosen time within one stimulus cycle to the end of next arrival times.
End the loop.
Repeat the following steps exactly 80000 times.
    For each fibre in next arrival times, perform the following operations.
        Check if the current clock time is greater than or equal to the arrival time of that fibre.
        If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
            Calculate the sum of synaptic conductance and 0.9.
            Set synaptic conductance to that sum.
            Set the arrival time of that fibre to the current clock time plus one to three stimulus cycles plus a small random jitter.
        End the conditional block.
    End the loop.
    Calculate the product of synaptic conductance and 0.9876.
    Set synaptic conductance to that product.
    Calculate the product of synaptic conductance and 4.
    Calculate the difference of -63 and membrane voltage.
    Calculate the sum of that difference and that product.
    Calculate the product of that sum and 0.00714.
    Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and that product.
    Set membrane voltage to that sum.
    Check if both membrane voltage is greater than -45 and refractory timer is equal to 0 are true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the current clock time to the end of output spike times.
        Set membrane voltage to -70.
        Set refractory timer to 280.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if refractory timer is greater than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of refractory timer and 1.
        Set refractory timer to that difference.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in output spike times.
Display that number to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because this cell must be simulated with microsecond time steps over many converging inputs, and a plain C loop makes that cost cheap and obvious; the program prints vector strength, the standard measure of phase locking, for locked and unlocked input.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#define DT   2.5e-6   /* 2.5 microsecond steps */
#define NIN  36       /* modified endbulbs converging on one cell */
#define PI   3.14159265358979323846

static double r01(void) { return (double)rand() / ((double)RAND_MAX + 1.0); }

static void run(int locked, double f) {
    double v = -63.0, g = 0.0, next[NIN], si = 0.0, co = 0.0, last = -1.0;
    long s, steps = (long)(0.2 / DT);
    int i, spikes = 0;
    srand(7);
    for (i = 0; i < NIN; i++) next[i] = r01() / f;      /* stagger the start */
    for (s = 0; s < steps; s++) {
        double t = s * DT;
        for (i = 0; i < NIN; i++) {
            if (t >= next[i]) {
                double c = floor(t * f) + 1 + rand() % 3;   /* skip 1 to 3 cycles */
                g += 0.9;                               /* one sub-threshold EPSC */
                next[i] = locked ? c / f + (r01() - 0.5) * 1.0e-4  /* 100 us jitter */
                                 : c / f + r01() / f;   /* same rate, phase destroyed */
            }
        }
        g *= exp(-DT / 2.0e-4);                         /* 200 us EPSC decay */
        v += ((-63.0 - v) + 6.0 * g) * (DT / 3.5e-4);   /* 350 us, Kv1-shortened */
        if (v > -45.0 && t - last > 8.0e-4) {           /* Kv3 gives a fast reset */
            spikes++; v = -70.0; last = t;
            si += sin(2.0 * PI * f * t); co += cos(2.0 * PI * f * t);
        }
    }
    printf("%-14s %6.1f Hz   vector strength %.3f\n", locked ? "phase-locked:" : "unlocked:",
           spikes / 0.2, spikes ? sqrt(si * si + co * co) / spikes : 0.0);
}

int main(void) { run(1, 500.0); run(0, 500.0); return 0; }

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A slightly oval cell at the back of the anteroventral cochlear nucleus, wearing dozens of medium-sized terminals rather than a few giant ones, with the fattest axon in the nucleus and an output whose timing is sharper than any of its inputs.

Follow that axon across the midline and it ends in the calyx of Held, the largest synapse in the mammalian brain, and no other neuron in the human body does that.

CHAPTER 139. STELLATE OR MULTIPOLAR CELLS, T-STELLATE TYPE (Narrow-Band Spectrum-Reporting Neuron) (COG)

Strike a piano key and this cell answers not with a copy of the note but with a steady private drumbeat, chopping the sound into evenly spaced spikes at a rhythm it chooses for itself.

The letter T is laboratory shorthand for where the axon leaves, not for what the cell does, so the name is rebuilt on the one property that matters, the narrow slice of frequency this cell listens to.

Narrow-Band names the tuning, because the cell is sharply selective for a small span of pitch and ignores energy outside it.

Spectrum-Reporting names the work, because the population together carries the spectral shape of a sound up to the opposite inferior colliculus, converting how much energy sits at each pitch into a firing rate.

Neuron marks it as an excitatory projection cell rather than a local one.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Stellate means star shaped, and this cell earns the name by sending dendrites, the branching input fibres of a neuron, outward in several directions.

Its job is to measure how much sound energy sits at one pitch and to report that amount as a firing rate.

It does this by ignoring the rhythm of the sound entirely and chopping out spikes at a pace of its own, so that loudness is carried by rate rather than by timing.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is oval, with three to five thick smooth dendrites flattened into a single plane.

Those dendrites lie parallel with the incoming hearing nerve fibres, which is what confines the cell to a narrow slice of pitch.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 15 to 25 micrometres across, a micrometre being one thousandth of a millimetre.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Planar multipolar cells, the commonest form, with dendrites confined to one frequency lamina.

• Type I multipolar cells, an older name for the same population.

• Sustained chopper cells, the name used when the cell is classified by its firing pattern rather than its shape.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The ventral cochlear nucleus, the front part of the first hearing relay in the brainstem, across both its anteroventral and posteroventral divisions.

Axon collaterals cross into the deep layer of the dorsal cochlear nucleus, the interface at which the ventral and dorsal halves of the nucleus exchange information.

NUMBER:

About 15,000 per side, so roughly 30,000 in total, which is a little under a sixth of the roughly 200,000 neurons in the two human cochlear nuclei.

Confidence is moderate, because human figures are extrapolated from cat and mouse proportions.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Input resistance is about 100 to 200 megaohms and the membrane time constant several milliseconds, so voltage changes linger long enough to add up.

Given steady drive the cell fires regularly at 100 to 400 spikes per second, with a coefficient of variation of the gaps between spikes below about 0.35.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It collects many small hearing nerve boutons of nearly the same best frequency and adds excitation from other cells of its own kind in the same pitch slice.

It receives glycine from the broad-band damping cells, from L-stellate cells and from vertical cells.

Its axon reaches the central nucleus of the inferior colliculus on the opposite side, with branches to periolivary nuclei and the deep dorsal cochlear nucleus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The main axon leaves through the ventral acoustic stria, is one to two micrometres thick, and conducts at roughly 5 to 15 metres per second.

Thin local collaterals stay inside the same pitch slice.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases glutamate, the brain's main excitatory messenger.

Unusually for this nucleus its inputs drive slow NMDA receptors as well as fast AMPA receptors, which keeps it depolarised between spikes and is part of why the chopping is so regular.

MYELINATION:

The main axon is well myelinated, wrapped in the fatty insulation made by oligodendrocytes that speeds conduction, while local feedback collaterals are thinly myelinated or bare.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Firing steadily at hundreds of spikes per second is expensive, and the cell is rich in mitochondria and in the enzyme cytochrome oxidase, though less extreme than its octopus and bushy cell neighbours.

PLASTICITY:

Nitric oxide, a gas that diffuses freely through tissue, is made during activity and raises the gain of this cell's response to hearing nerve input.

After cochlear damage the cell loses glycinergic inhibition and fires faster in silence, a change tied to loudness intolerance and tinnitus.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here carry unusually dense glutamate transporters, because the hearing nerve fires continuously even in silence and spilled glutamate would otherwise poison the cell.

After cochlear damage, microglia and astrocytes of this nucleus strip out the dead nerve endings within days, a demolition speed unmatched in most of the brain.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and the nucleus still forwards exquisite timing but stops forwarding the shape of the sound spectrum.

Vowels would lose their formants, the energy peaks that separate ah from ee, and speech would arrive as rhythm without identity.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

NMDA receptors let the dendrites amplify coincident inputs beyond the sum of their parts.

Nitric oxide spreads gain changes through a small volume of tissue rather than one synapse.

Mutual excitation among cells of this type forms a recurrent network that turns brief phasic drive into steady tonic output, a computation belonging to the circuit rather than to any one cell.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: 10 to 60 hearing nerve fibres from a narrow frequency band at 0 to 300 spikes per second each, recurrent excitation from neighbours of the same type, and glycinergic inhibition at 0 to 200 spikes per second.

• OUTPUTS: 0 to 400 spikes per second to the contralateral inferior colliculus, periolivary nuclei, deep dorsal cochlear nucleus and local neighbours.

• STATE: membrane voltage, refractory countdown, AMPA conductance, NMDA conductance, glycine conductance, nitric oxide gain factor.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 100 to 200 megaohms, threshold 15 millivolts above rest, refractory period 1.0 to 1.5 milliseconds, excitation to inhibition ratio about 3 to 1.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 4 to 10 milliseconds, AMPA decay 0.3 to 1 millisecond, NMDA decay 20 to 50 milliseconds, glycine decay 2 to 5 milliseconds, nitric oxide gain minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of glycinergic inhibition after noise trauma raises the resting rate and flattens the code for spectral contrast, contributing to hyperacusis, poor speech understanding in noise, and tinnitus.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn an irregular stream of hearing nerve spikes into a regular chopping discharge whose rate reports sound level in one narrow frequency band.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value negative sixty five.
Create a number variable called refractory countdown with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called nitric oxide gain with initial value one.
Create a list variable called spike times with initial value empty list.
For each time step in the simulation clock, perform the following operations.
    Set excitatory drive to the product of nitric oxide gain and the count of hearing nerve spikes in this time step.
    Set net current to the difference of excitatory drive and the count of glycine spikes in this time step.
    If refractory countdown is greater than zero, then perform the following actions.
        Set refractory countdown to the difference of refractory countdown and the step duration.
        Set membrane voltage to negative seventy.
        Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
    End the conditional block.
    Set voltage change to the quotient of the sum of net current and the negative displacement from rest divided by the membrane time constant.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the product of voltage change and the step duration.
    If membrane voltage is greater than or equal to negative fifty, then perform the following actions.
        Add the current time to the end of spike times.
        Set membrane voltage to negative seventy.
        Set refractory countdown to one point two.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in spike times.
Display the total number of items in spike times to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because it reads almost like the English above and needs no libraries.
import random

DT, TAU = 0.02, 6.0                        # step and membrane time constant, milliseconds
V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = -65.0, -50.0, -70.0
R_IN, T_REF = 120.0, 1.2                   # megaohms, refractory milliseconds

def chopper(level_hz, dur_ms=200.0, seed=1):
    rnd = random.Random(seed)
    v, ref, spikes = V_REST, 0.0, []
    for i in range(int(dur_ms / DT)):
        drive = 0.13 + 0.0006 * level_hz + 0.02 * rnd.gauss(0.0, 1.0)   # nanoamps
        if ref > 0.0:
            ref, v = ref - DT, V_RESET
            continue
        v += (-(v - V_REST) + R_IN * drive) * DT / TAU
        if v >= V_TH:
            spikes.append(i * DT)
            v, ref = V_RESET, T_REF
    return spikes

for level in (50, 150, 300):
    gaps = [b - a for a, b in zip(chopper(level), chopper(level)[1:])]
    mean = sum(gaps) / len(gaps)
    sd = (sum((g - mean) ** 2 for g in gaps) / len(gaps)) ** 0.5
    print(level, "-> chop rate", round(1000.0 / mean, 1), "Hz, CV", round(sd / mean, 3))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only cell in the cochlear nucleus whose spike rhythm ignores the rhythm of the sound entirely.

Play a 400 hertz tone and a bushy cell locks to 400 hertz, while this cell keeps chopping at its own pace, so its rate reports loudness and its regularity survives even in noise.

CHAPTER 140. STELLATE OR MULTIPOLAR CELLS, D-STELLATE TYPE (Broad-Band Sound-Damping Neuron) (COG)

Every other cell leaving the first hearing relay carries a message onward; this one leaves in order to tell the next nucleus, and its own, to be quiet.

The letter D is laboratory shorthand for the dorsal route its axon takes, which says nothing about the work.

Broad-Band names the listening, because unlike its narrow-band neighbour this cell deliberately sums energy across several octaves at once.

Sound-Damping names the output, because it is the only inhibitory projection cell leaving the cochlear nucleus, releasing glycine onto the narrow-band cells in both its own and the opposite cochlear nucleus.

Neuron marks it as a projection cell, and the shared word Band keeps the family resemblance with the cell it opposes.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is the wide listener of the ventral cochlear nucleus.

Where its narrow-band neighbour measures energy at one pitch, this cell measures total energy across many pitches and uses the answer to turn its neighbours down.

The effect is contrast: a sound with a sharp spectral peak is passed on with that peak exaggerated, because the broad background is subtracted.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell is larger than its narrow-band counterpart and its dendrites deliberately cut across the frequency laminae rather than lying within one.

That single anatomical decision is what lets one cell hear several octaves at once.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 20 to 35 micrometres across, with dendritic fields spanning 200 to 500 micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Radiate multipolar cells, named for dendrites radiating across laminae.

• Type II multipolar cells, an older name for the same population.

• Onset chopper cells, the name used when the cell is classified by its firing pattern.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The ventral cochlear nucleus, concentrated in the posteroventral division.

Its axon crosses to the opposite cochlear nucleus and also enters the deep layer of the dorsal cochlear nucleus, so a single cell influences both sides of the first hearing relay.

NUMBER:

About 5,000 per side, so roughly 10,000 in total, making this a small minority of the stellate population and about one twentieth of the neurons in the two human cochlear nuclei.

Confidence is moderate, extrapolated from animal proportions.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Given steady drive the cell fires once or twice at sound onset and then falls quiet, which is the signature that gives it the onset chopper name.

Its wide dendritic reach and large surface area give it a lower input resistance than the narrow-band cell, so it needs many inputs arriving together to fire at all.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It collects hearing nerve boutons drawn from a wide span of best frequencies, which is the anatomical basis of its broad tuning.

Its output lands on narrow-band cells in its own nucleus, on narrow-band cells across the midline, and in the deep dorsal cochlear nucleus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The main axon leaves through the intermediate acoustic stria, is one to two micrometres thick, and conducts at roughly 5 to 15 metres per second.

Local collaterals spread widely within the nucleus rather than staying in one pitch slice.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases glycine, a small inhibitory amino acid, and some cells also carry gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA), the other main inhibitory messenger.

This is what makes it unique among the nucleus's output cells, all of which are otherwise excitatory.

MYELINATION:

The main axon is well myelinated, while the wide local collaterals are thinly myelinated or bare.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Lower than its narrow-band neighbour, because it fires only briefly at onset rather than continuously, so its mitochondrial density is correspondingly more modest.

PLASTICITY:

After cochlear damage this cell's inhibitory output falls, which is one of the direct causes of the raised resting firing seen in the narrow-band cells.

That loss of damping is a leading candidate mechanism for tinnitus and for loudness intolerance.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

It shares the nucleus's unusually dense astrocytic glutamate transporters, needed because the hearing nerve fires continuously even in silence.

Its glycinergic terminals are also served by glial glycine transporters that clear the transmitter fast enough for the damping to be brief and well timed.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and the first hearing relay loses its contrast control.

Every sound would be passed on with its broadband background intact, so a voice in a noisy room would arrive smeared into the noise rather than standing out from it.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Because one cell samples several octaves and projects across the midline, it performs a genuine across-frequency and across-ear computation before any of that information reaches the midbrain.

It is also one of the few cells whose main contribution is subtraction rather than transmission, which makes it a rare example of a projection neuron built to reduce the signal it carries.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: 30 to 200 hearing nerve fibres drawn from a wide frequency span at 0 to 300 spikes per second each.

• OUTPUTS: a brief onset burst of 1 to 3 spikes to narrow-band cells in both cochlear nuclei and to the deep dorsal cochlear nucleus.

• STATE: membrane voltage, refractory countdown, AMPA conductance, adaptation variable.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 40 to 80 megaohms, threshold 15 millivolts above rest, refractory period 1.0 to 1.5 milliseconds, adaptation strength high.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 2 to 5 milliseconds, AMPA decay 0.3 to 1 millisecond, adaptation 20 to 60 milliseconds, glycine decay at its targets 2 to 5 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of this cell after noise trauma removes wideband damping, raising resting rates in the narrow-band population and flattening spectral contrast, which contributes to tinnitus and to difficulty understanding speech in noise.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to fire a brief onset burst when total sound energy across a wide frequency span rises sharply, and then to fall silent through adaptation.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value negative sixty five.
Create a number variable called adaptation with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called refractory countdown with initial value zero.
Create a list variable called spike times with initial value empty list.
For each time step in the simulation clock, perform the following operations.
    Set wideband drive to the sum of hearing nerve spike counts across all frequency bands in this time step.
    Set net current to the difference of wideband drive and adaptation.
    If refractory countdown is greater than zero, then perform the following actions.
        Set refractory countdown to the difference of refractory countdown and the step duration.
        Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
    End the conditional block.
    Set voltage change to the quotient of the sum of net current and the negative displacement from rest divided by the membrane time constant.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the product of voltage change and the step duration.
    If membrane voltage is greater than or equal to negative fifty, then perform the following actions.
        Add the current time to the end of spike times.
        Set membrane voltage to negative seventy.
        Set refractory countdown to one point two.
        Set adaptation to the sum of adaptation and one point five.
    End the conditional block.
    Set adaptation to the difference of adaptation and the quotient of adaptation divided by the adaptation time constant.
End the loop.
Display the total number of items in spike times to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because adaptation is one subtraction per step, which reads clearly here.
import random

DT, TAU, TAU_A = 0.02, 3.0, 40.0           # milliseconds
V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = -65.0, -50.0, -70.0
R_IN, T_REF = 60.0, 1.2                    # megaohms, milliseconds

def onset_cell(level_hz, dur_ms=100.0, seed=2):
    rnd = random.Random(seed)
    v, a, ref, spikes = V_REST, 0.0, 0.0, []
    for i in range(int(dur_ms / DT)):
        wide = 0.30 + 0.0010 * level_hz + 0.03 * rnd.gauss(0.0, 1.0)    # nanoamps, many bands
        if ref > 0.0:
            ref -= DT
        else:
            v += (-(v - V_REST) + R_IN * (wide - a)) * DT / TAU
            if v >= V_TH:
                spikes.append(i * DT)
                v, ref, a = V_RESET, T_REF, a + 1.5
        a -= a * DT / TAU_A
    return spikes

for level in (50, 150, 300):
    s = onset_cell(level)
    print(level, "-> spikes", len(s), "first at", round(s[0], 2) if s else None, "ms")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only cell that leaves the cochlear nucleus carrying inhibition rather than excitation.

Play a long tone and it answers with one or two spikes at the very beginning and then nothing at all, however long the tone continues.

CHAPTER 141. OCTOPUS CELLS (COG)

A few thousand cells in your brainstem are so leaky that a signal fades inside them in two ten thousandths of a second, and that near total forgetfulness makes them the fastest coincidence detectors in the human body.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An octopus cell is named for its shape, because all of its thick dendrites stream from one side of the cell body like the arms of an octopus caught in a current.

Its job is to detect synchrony, meaning the arrival of many hearing nerve spikes within a fraction of a millisecond of each other, which is what broadband events such as a consonant, a click or a footstep produce.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is large and round, with six to eight stout unbranched dendrites all leaving one pole and running across, not along, the bundle of incoming hearing nerve fibres.

Because those fibres enter in order of pitch, a dendrite crossing them samples the whole hearing range in sequence, from low to high.

SIZE:

The cell body is about 25 to 35 micrometres across, among the largest in the cochlear nucleus, and the dendritic tuft extends 150 to 300 micrometres, enough to sweep several octaves of the frequency map.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There are no accepted subtypes, and the octopus cell area contains essentially only this one principal cell, which is why the region is named after it.

Cells differ only in whether their tuft leans toward high or low frequencies, and that bias is what makes an individual cell prefer upward or downward frequency sweeps.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Octopus cells occupy the octopus cell area, a compact cap in the caudal and dorsal corner of the posteroventral cochlear nucleus where the hearing nerve fibre bundle turns.

That site is itself an interface, lying against the entering nerve root and against the border with both the dorsal cochlear nucleus and the granule cell shell that wraps the whole nucleus.

NUMBER:

In the mouse roughly 200 octopus cells sample about 12,000 hearing nerve fibres, and scaling to the human nerve of about 30,000 fibres gives an estimate of 1,000 to 2,000 per side, so 2,000 to 4,000 in total.

That is one to two percent of the cochlear nuclei and about four millionths of one percent of the nervous system, with low confidence because no direct human count exists.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Input resistance is under 10 megaohms, often 4 to 6, because of an enormous low voltage activated potassium conductance of roughly 500 nanosiemens, about sixty percent of it carried by channels containing the Kv1.1 subunit, plus about 150 nanosiemens of a hyperpolarisation activated conductance built from HCN1 subunits.

The result is a membrane time constant near 0.2 milliseconds, synaptic potentials of a fraction of a millivolt, small action potentials, and an integration window of about one millisecond.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Each cell receives more than sixty hearing nerve fibres as small boutons spread along the dendrites, and no single one can make it fire.

Inhibition is sparse, which is unusual, so the cell is close to a pure detector of excitatory coincidence.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is among the thickest in the auditory brainstem, leaves through the intermediate acoustic stria, crosses the midline, and ends in cup shaped calyx like terminals on cells of the ventral nucleus of the lateral lemniscus.

It also supplies the main excitatory drive to the superior paraolivary nucleus, and it conducts at tens of metres per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Octopus cells release glutamate onto very fast AMPA receptors whose currents last under a millisecond, with little NMDA receptor contribution in the mature cell, because a slow receptor would smear the timing this cell exists to preserve.

MYELINATION:

The axon is heavily myelinated with widely spaced nodes, matched to a demand for minimum delay, and even the proximal dendrites carry unusual glial investment thought to lower capacitance near the cell body.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Holding a huge potassium leak open at rest means the sodium potassium pump must run continuously even in silence, so these mitochondria rich cells burn energy simply to stay ready and are correspondingly vulnerable to oxygen shortage.

PLASTICITY:

Octopus cells show very little classical synaptic plasticity, and that stability is the point, because a detector of microsecond synchrony cannot afford drifting weights.

Their properties are set during development by sound driven activity, and after deafness the low voltage potassium conductance falls and temporal precision is lost.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The octopus cell area holds an unusually dense extracellular matrix forming perineuronal nets, which are lattices of sugar and protein that clamp channel distribution in place and support fast firing.

Astrocytes here press tightly against the cell body with high potassium buffering capacity, because a cell with this much potassium flux would otherwise flood its own surroundings.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove octopus cells and the brainstem loses its stopwatch for acoustic events.

Speech would keep its pitch and loudness but lose the crisp punctuation of consonant onsets, and the brain would lose the precise timing signal used to suppress echoes and to hear gaps in noise.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The cell does not integrate voltage, it differentiates it, firing when the rate of depolarisation exceeds a threshold rather than when voltage does.

Its dendrites perform sequence detection, so an upward sweep that reaches the tuft tip first can fire the cell while the downward sweep fails, and its hyperpolarisation activated current is a standing inward leak, a form of active self maintenance paid for in every second of life.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: more than 60 hearing nerve fibres spanning several octaves, each 0 to 300 spikes per second and each worth 0.3 to 1 millivolt at the soma, with dendritic position adding an arrival delay of 0 to 0.4 milliseconds.

• OUTPUTS: precisely timed spikes at 0 to 800 per second to the contralateral ventral nucleus of the lateral lemniscus and the superior paraolivary nucleus.

• STATE: membrane voltage, previous membrane voltage, low voltage potassium activation, hyperpolarisation activated current activation, refractory countdown.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 4 to 10 megaohms, potassium conductance about 500 nanosiemens, HCN conductance about 150 nanosiemens, rate of rise threshold 100 to 300 millivolts per millisecond.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane about 0.2 milliseconds, AMPA decay 0.2 to 0.5 milliseconds, integration window about 1 millisecond, refractory period about 0.8 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: when ageing or noise damage makes hearing nerve timing jitter exceed the coincidence window, the cell stops responding to speech transients, producing the pattern of a normal hearing test with poor speech in noise seen in hidden hearing loss and auditory neuropathy.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to fire a spike only when enough hearing nerve inputs arrive close enough together to make the membrane voltage rise faster than a fixed rate.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called previous voltage with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called refractory countdown with initial value zero.
Create a list variable called spike times with initial value empty list.
