Olivary neurons also show a remarkable pathological plasticity called hypertrophic olivary degeneration, in which loss of the inhibitory input from the red nucleus makes the neurons swell and enlarge instead of dying, producing a rhythmic palatal tremor at two to three beats per second.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Excitatory glutamatergic error and sensory input from spinal cord, trigeminal nuclei, superior colliculus and mesodiencephalic junction, zero to fifty events per second.

◦ Inhibitory GABAergic input from cerebellar nuclei onto the glomerulus, which both hyperpolarises and reduces gap junction coupling.

◦ Electrical current from coupled neighbours through connexin 36 gap junctions.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ One climbing fibre spike or short burst to five to ten Purkinje cells, plus collaterals to cerebellar nuclei and Golgi cells.

◦ Mean output rate about one hertz, rising briefly to about ten hertz.

• STATE.

◦ Oscillation phase in radians.

◦ Membrane potential and dendritic calcium level.

◦ Effective coupling conductance to each neighbour.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Oscillation frequency one to ten hertz, typically six hertz.

◦ Oscillation amplitude three to ten millivolts.

◦ Coupling conductance per pair on the order of a few hundred picosiemens, adjustable to near zero.

◦ Climbing fibre divergence five to ten Purkinje cells, about three hundred contacts each.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Oscillation period about one hundred and sixty milliseconds.

◦ Membrane time constant about ten to twenty milliseconds.

◦ Calcium clearance hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Coupling modulation by inhibition within tens of milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Interrupting the central tegmental tract removes inhibition and produces hypertrophic olivary degeneration with palatal tremor and oculopalatal tremor.

◦ Excessive synchrony, as produced experimentally by the drug harmaline, produces a violent whole-body tremor.

◦ Loss of olivary neurons abolishes cerebellar motor learning and eye-blink conditioning.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether an olivary cell fires a climbing fibre spike, given its ongoing oscillation, the pull of its electrically coupled neighbours, and an arriving error signal.
Create a number variable called phase with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step_size with initial value 0.0377.
Create a number variable called neighbour_pull with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike_count with initial value 0.
For each millisecond in the trial, perform the following operations.
    Set neighbour_pull to the mean sine of the phase differences reported by the coupled neighbours.
    Calculate the sum of step_size and neighbour_pull.
    Set advance to the result.
    Calculate the sum of phase and advance.
    Set phase to the result.
    Set excitability to the cosine of phase.
    Set drive to the strength of the error signal arriving this millisecond.
    Calculate the product of drive and excitability.
    Set effective_drive to the result.
    Calculate the difference of effective_drive and the inhibition from the cerebellar nuclei.
    Set net_drive to the result.
    Check if net_drive is greater than 0.6.
    Set will_spike to the result.
    If will_spike is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of spike_count and 1.
        Set spike_count to the result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spike_count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python was chosen because coupled phase oscillators need only trigonometry and a loop, and the code then reads almost exactly like the biology.

import math

DT = 0.001                 # one millisecond steps
F_HZ = 6.0                 # subthreshold oscillation frequency
K_GAP = 4.0                # gap junction coupling strength, per second
TWO_PI = 2.0 * math.pi
phases = [0.0, 1.6, 3.1, 4.7]      # four cells starting out of step

def advance(phases, k_gap):
    updated = []
    n = len(phases)
    for i, own in enumerate(phases):
        pull = sum(math.sin(other - own) for j, other in enumerate(phases) if j != i)
        pull = k_gap * pull / (n - 1)          # electrical pull from neighbours
        updated.append((own + DT * (TWO_PI * F_HZ + pull)) % TWO_PI)
    return updated

def fires(phase, drive, nucleo_olivary_inhibition):
    gate = math.cos(phase)                     # +1 at the peak of the oscillation
    return (drive * (0.5 + 0.5 * gate) - nucleo_olivary_inhibition) > 0.6

spikes = 0
for t in range(2000):                          # two seconds
    phases = advance(phases, K_GAP)
    drive = 1.0 if t % 250 == 0 else 0.0       # an error signal every 250 ms
    if fires(phases[0], drive, 0.2):
        spikes += 1

sx = sum(math.cos(p) for p in phases) / len(phases)
sy = sum(math.sin(p) for p in phases) / len(phases)
print("climbing fibre spikes in 2 s:", spikes)
print("synchrony index, 1.0 means locked:", round(math.hypot(sx, sy), 3))
# prints 3 spikes from 8 error signals, and a synchrony index of 0.998

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other neuron in the human body makes a synapse that never fails.

No other nucleus wraps its dendritic spines and its gap junctions inside astrocyte-sealed glomeruli.

If you see a spherical cell with dendrites that curl back on themselves, oscillating gently at six hertz, sitting in a crumpled ribbon on the front of the medulla, it is an inferior olive neuron.

CHAPTER 149. PONTINE NUCLEI NEURONS (Plan-Forwarding Cerebellum-Feeding Neuron) (COG)

The bulge on the front of your brainstem that surgeons call the belly of the pons is a switchboard, and it is almost entirely made of twenty million cells whose only job is to tell the cerebellum what the cerebral cortex has just decided to do.

Pontine nuclei names a bulge in the brainstem, not a job, and the job here is the largest single act of copying in the brain.

Plan-Forwarding names the input, because these cells receive a massive projection from layer five of almost the entire cerebral cortex on the same side, so what arrives is a copy of the command the cortex has just issued.

Cerebellum-Feeding names the output, because their axons cross the midline and end as mossy fibres, supplying more than half of all mossy fibre input to the cerebellum, each axon giving rise to roughly one hundred to two hundred terminals and each terminal contacting thirty to forty granule cells.

Neuron marks it as the cell that ensures the cerebellum is told what the cortex intends at the same moment the body is told to do it.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The cerebral cortex plans; the cerebellum checks and refines.

They are not directly connected, so almost everything the cortex sends to the cerebellum passes through one intermediate stop, the pontine nuclei.

A pontine neuron collects axons from layer 5 of the cerebral cortex, sums them, and sends the answer across the midline into the opposite half of the cerebellum as a mossy fibre.

The traffic is enormous and one-way: roughly twenty million corticopontine axons descend into the pons, about twenty times the number of fibres in the pyramidal tract that carries voluntary movement commands to the spinal cord.

In other words, the brain spends twenty times more wire telling the cerebellum about the plan than it spends issuing the plan itself.

The functional consequence is that the cerebellum receives an efference copy, a duplicate of every outgoing instruction, in time to predict what should happen next.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The typical pontine neuron is a medium multipolar cell with a round or oval cell body and four to seven sparsely branched dendrites.

The dendrites are long and straight rather than bushy, and they do something unusual: their fields respect the invisible borders between the territories of different cortical areas, so a cell inside a motor cortex territory keeps its dendrites inside it.

This is the anatomical basis of the modular map of the pons, in which each cortical area paints its own patch.

The pontine nuclei are not one nucleus but grey islands scattered between the descending bundles of the corticospinal tract, so the region looks in section like gravel embedded in cables.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are fifteen to twenty-five micrometres across.

Dendrites extend up to about five hundred micrometres, which is long relative to the cell body and is what lets one cell sample a whole cortical territory.

The axon runs across the midline and out through the middle cerebellar peduncle, a distance of one to three centimetres, ending in mossy fibre rosettes about five to ten micrometres wide.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Almost all pontine neurons are projection neurons and they are essentially all excitatory, so the classical subtypes are defined by connection rather than by shape.

Dorsal and medial pontine neurons tend to be larger with more dendrites than ventral ones.

A small population of local inhibitory interneurons exists but is sparse compared with most brain regions, which is one reason the pons behaves as a relay rather than a processor.

A separate cell group in the same region, the nucleus reticularis tegmenti pontis, is developmentally a sibling of the pontine nuclei and projects to cerebellum as well, but it also receives cerebellar feedback and so forms a loop rather than a one-way relay.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Every one of these cells lives in the basilar pons, also called the basis pontis, the swollen front half of the pons.

They fill the space between the descending corticospinal, corticobulbar and corticopontine fibre bundles, on both sides of the midline, from the level of the midbrain junction down to the pontomedullary junction.

Anatomists divide the region into median, paramedian, peduncular, ventral, lateral and dorsolateral nuclei, and each is dominated by a different cortical source.

The main interface is with white matter on all sides: dorsally the pontine tegmentum, ventrally the surface of the brainstem and the basilar artery, and laterally the middle cerebellar peduncle into which the axons pour.

Developmentally these neurons were born far away, at the rhombic lip on the upper edge of the fourth ventricle, and migrated forward and around the brainstem in the anterior extramural stream to reach the belly of the pons.

NUMBER:

The pontine nuclei contain about twenty million neurons in the human, and the corticopontocerebellar pathway carries about twenty million axons, figures quoted consistently across the anatomical literature, so confidence is moderate.

That is about ten percent of the roughly two hundred million brainstem neurons and about zero point zero two three percent of all human neurons.

They are by a wide margin the largest single neuron population in the brainstem, which is why the human pons is so much bigger than the pons of animals with smaller cerebral cortices.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Pontine neurons are electrically plain, which is appropriate for a relay.

They rest quietly, with low spontaneous rates of a few spikes per second, and fire regular trains up to fifty to one hundred spikes per second when the cortex is active.

They show little bursting, little adaptation and no pacemaking, and their spikes are short with brief afterhyperpolarisations.

The interesting computation is not in the membrane but in the wiring: convergence of several cortical areas onto one cell, then divergence of that cell onto many granule cells.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The dominant input is from layer 5 pyramidal cells of almost the whole cerebral cortex, including primary motor, premotor, somatosensory, posterior parietal, visual and prefrontal areas.

Additional input arrives from the superior colliculus, the spinal cord by the spinopontine tract, the mamillary body and the cerebellar nuclei.

The output is a mossy fibre that ends in one or more rosettes, each rosette forming the core of a cerebellar glomerulus and contacting the claw-shaped dendrites of roughly fifteen to twenty granule cells at once.

Collaterals also excite the cerebellar nuclei directly before the main axon reaches the cortex.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Pontocerebellar axons are one to three micrometres in diameter, cross the midline within the pons, and gather into the middle cerebellar peduncle, the largest white matter bundle in the brainstem.

Individual axons branch several times within cerebellar cortex, so one pontine neuron may reach several separate lobules.

The crossing is why a stroke in one half of the pons produces clumsiness on the opposite side of the cerebellum.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The projection neurons are glutamatergic, using glutamate loaded by the vesicular glutamate transporter VGLUT1, with a smaller VGLUT2 population.

Their mossy fibre synapses activate fast AMPA receptors and slower NMDA receptors on granule cell dendrites, and glutamate spilling out of the glomerulus also reaches metabotropic receptors on Golgi cells.

The sparse local interneurons use GABA.

Corticopontine terminals are themselves modulated by acetylcholine and by serotonin from the raphe.

MYELINATION:

Pontocerebellar axons are well myelinated by oligodendrocytes, and the middle cerebellar peduncle is one of the most heavily myelinated tracts in the brain.

This makes the region highly visible on magnetic resonance imaging and also unusually vulnerable to demyelinating injury.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The pons has a high resting glucose uptake because of the sheer density of synapses and the amount of myelin to maintain.

Individual neurons are not extravagant, but the population is, since twenty million cells each supporting a long myelinated axon and dozens of large rosettes adds up.

The rosette itself is packed with mitochondria, because a single glomerulus must sustain transmission onto twenty postsynaptic partners at high rates.

PLASTICITY:

Corticopontine synapses show ordinary long-term potentiation and depression, so the weighting of cortical areas onto a pontine cell can change with training.

Mossy fibre rosettes are remarkably stable in adults, showing almost no turnover even when sensory input is manipulated for weeks, which suggests that the pons deliberately supplies a fixed, reliable channel and leaves the learning to the cerebellum.

During development the migration and target selection of these neurons is highly plastic and depends on guidance molecules including Netrin and its receptor.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The distinctive glial feature of the basilar pons is the density and importance of oligodendrocytes, packed in rows between the fibre bundles.

Those oligodendrocytes are unusually sensitive to rapid changes in blood sodium, which is why correcting low sodium too quickly destroys myelin here first, producing central pontine myelinolysis.

Astrocytes in the pons are ordinary, but in the cerebellum the Bergmann-like glia and velate astrocytes that ensheath the glomerulus perform a special job for this cell type, sealing the rosette so that glutamate builds up inside the glomerulus and reaches all the granule cell claws together.

Pontine microglia are also the resident population that reacts in pontine glioma, a childhood tumour that arises in exactly this territory.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete the pontine neurons and the pons becomes a bundle of passing cables with nothing of its own.

Movement commands would still reach the spinal cord, and the face would still work, but the cerebellum would go deaf to the cortex.

The consequence is a very specific loss: the ability to make a movement smooth by predicting it in advance.

The person would still be able to move, but every action would become a slow, corrected, visually guided approximation, and skilled fast movement would be gone.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The great computational trick here is not inside the cell but in the fan-out: about twenty million pontine neurons feed roughly fifty billion cerebellar granule cells, an expansion of more than two thousand to one that turns a small set of signals into a vast sparse code.

Individual mossy fibre rosettes are also presynaptically regulated, with terminals carrying receptors that let Golgi cells and ambient glutamate control release before any spike arrives.

Pontine neurons help instruct their own myelination, since axonal activity drives oligodendrocyte precursor differentiation, and the middle cerebellar peduncle myelinates in proportion to use during childhood.

The dendritic boundary respecting behaviour is itself a form of structural computation, because the wiring diagram is built by dendrites refusing to cross an invisible line.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Glutamatergic corticopontine input from layer 5 of multiple cortical areas, zero to one hundred spikes per second per source, typically two to five sources per cell.

◦ Weaker input from superior colliculus, spinal cord and cerebellar nuclei.

◦ Neuromodulatory acetylcholine and serotonin acting as a gain term from zero point eight to one point two.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Mossy fibre spikes to contralateral cerebellar granule cells, fifteen to twenty granule cells per rosette, several rosettes per axon.

◦ Collateral excitation of cerebellar nuclei.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential.

◦ Short-term synaptic depression level at each rosette.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Threshold on mean weighted cortical drive, about zero point two of maximum.

◦ Input output gain about two, in spikes per second per unit drive.

◦ Convergence two to five cortical areas per cell, divergence about five rosettes per axon.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane time constant about fifteen milliseconds.

◦ Synaptic rise about one millisecond, decay about five milliseconds for AMPA.

◦ Short-term depression recovery about two hundred milliseconds.

◦ Conduction delay pons to cerebellar cortex about three to five milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Central pontine myelinolysis after rapid correction of low blood sodium, causing locked-in syndrome in severe cases.

◦ Pontocerebellar hypoplasia, most often from TSEN54 mutations, in which these neurons fail to develop and the pons is visibly tiny.

◦ Loss in multiple system atrophy of the cerebellar type, which produces the hot cross bun sign on magnetic resonance imaging as the transverse fibres degenerate.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to combine cortical commands arriving from several different cortical areas onto one pontine cell and broadcast the result to a scattered set of cerebellar granule cells.
Create a number variable called running_total with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called granule_deliveries with initial value empty list.
For each cortical_input in the arriving_cortical_inputs, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the product of the rate of cortical_input and the weight of cortical_input.
    Set weighted to the result.
    Calculate the sum of running_total and weighted.
    Set running_total to the result.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in arriving_cortical_inputs.
Set input_count to the result.
Calculate the quotient of running_total divided by input_count.
Set mean_drive to the result.
Calculate the difference of mean_drive and 0.2.
Set above_threshold to the result.
Check if above_threshold is greater than 0.
Set is_firing to the result.
If is_firing is true, then perform the following actions.
    Calculate the product of above_threshold and 2.
    Set firing_rate to the result.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set firing_rate to 0.
End the conditional block.
For each target in the mossy_fibre_targets, perform the following operations.
    Add firing_rate to the end of granule_deliveries.
End the loop.
Display firing_rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python was chosen because dictionaries make the many-cortical-areas-to-one-pontine-cell mapping self-documenting, which is the whole point of this cell type.

import random
random.seed(7)

CORTICAL_AREAS = ["M1", "S1", "premotor", "posterior_parietal", "visual"]
N_PONTINE = 8
N_GRANULE = 20
THRESHOLD = 0.20      # mean weighted drive needed before the cell fires
GAIN = 2.0            # spikes per second per unit of drive above threshold

# each pontine cell listens to only two cortical areas, and reaches five granule cells
weights = {p: {a: random.random() for a in random.sample(CORTICAL_AREAS, 2)}
           for p in range(N_PONTINE)}
mossy_targets = {p: random.sample(range(N_GRANULE), 5) for p in range(N_PONTINE)}

def pontine_rate(cortical_activity, listening_to):
    total = sum(w * cortical_activity[area] for area, w in listening_to.items())
    mean_drive = total / len(listening_to)
    return GAIN * (mean_drive - THRESHOLD) if mean_drive > THRESHOLD else 0.0

activity = {"M1": 0.9, "S1": 0.6, "premotor": 0.8,
            "posterior_parietal": 0.3, "visual": 0.1}

granule_input = [0.0] * N_GRANULE
firing_cells = 0
for cell in range(N_PONTINE):
    rate = pontine_rate(activity, weights[cell])
    if rate > 0.0:
        firing_cells += 1
    for target in mossy_targets[cell]:
        granule_input[target] += rate

reached = sum(1 for g in granule_input if g > 0.0)
print("pontine cells firing:", firing_cells, "of", N_PONTINE)
print("granule cells reached:", reached, "of", N_GRANULE)
# prints 4 of 8 pontine cells firing, reaching 12 of 20 granule cells

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other neuron population in the brainstem is so large, so uniform and so single-minded.

No other cell has dendrites that stop dead at the invisible border of a cortical territory.

If you find a medium multipolar glutamatergic cell sitting in an island of grey matter between corticospinal bundles in the belly of the pons, sending one axon across the midline into the middle cerebellar peduncle, it is a pontine nucleus neuron.

CHAPTER 150. RETICULAR FORMATION NEURONS (Whole-Body Readiness Neuron) (COG)

One of these cells can touch your spinal cord and your thalamus with the very same action potential, which is why a single lesion the size of a pea in the middle of the brainstem can end consciousness.

Reticular formation means little more than net-like tissue, a nineteenth-century description of how the cells look under a microscope when no clear boundaries can be drawn between nuclei.

Whole-Body names the reach, because a single one of these cells can carry sensory news from skin, muscle, eyes, ears and balance organs and send branches as far apart as the spinal cord, the thalamus and the hypothalamus.

Readiness names the shared purpose behind its otherwise scattered duties, since the same core sets the level of wakefulness that everything else depends on, holds muscle tone and posture through the reticulospinal tract, and starts and sustains walking.

Neuron marks it as a cell whose duties are all ways of preparing the body to act rather than actions themselves.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The reticular formation is the ancient core that runs the whole length of the brainstem, from the top of the spinal cord to the edge of the thalamus.

It is not a nucleus with a neat border; it is a net, and the word reticular simply means net-like.

Its neurons do the jobs that require the whole body to change state at once: waking up, falling asleep, holding posture, breathing, coughing, vomiting, turning the eyes and head, dampening or amplifying pain, and generating the startle jump when a door slams.

The key design feature is that one neuron talks to a great many places.

A single reticular cell can send one branch of its axon down to the spinal cord and another up to the thalamus, so its decision arrives at the muscles and at the cortex simultaneously.

That is why this system is described as an integrative matrix rather than a set of relays.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

These are the classic isodendritic neurons, a term meaning that the dendrites are all much the same: long, straight, sparsely branched and radiating outward with no obvious specialisation.

The dendritic field is usually flattened into a single plane lying across the brainstem, like a hand held palm-forward, so each cell intercepts every fibre bundle running up and down past it.

Cell bodies range from tiny to enormous, and the giant cells of the gigantocellular nucleus are among the largest neurons in the brainstem.

The overall impression under the microscope is of scattered cells of wildly different sizes sitting in a dense, tangled feltwork of fibres, with no layers and no columns.

SIZE:

Small parvocellular neurons are ten to twenty micrometres across.

Medium cells are twenty to forty micrometres.

The giant cells of nucleus gigantocellularis and nucleus reticularis pontis caudalis reach fifty to seventy micrometres, big enough to see with a hand lens in a stained section.

Dendritic fields can span one to two millimetres, covering roughly half the width of the brainstem, and single axons can be twenty to sixty centimetres long if they run from midbrain to lumbar spinal cord.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The reticular formation is traditionally divided into three long columns.

The median column is the raphe, which sits on the midline and is largely serotonergic.

The medial column, also called magnocellular or effector, contains the large cells that give rise to the reticulospinal tracts, including nucleus reticularis pontis oralis and caudalis, gigantocellularis and magnocellularis.

The lateral column, also called parvocellular or associative, contains small cells that receive sensory input and coordinate the cranial nerve motor nuclei, and includes the parvicellular and intermediate reticular nuclei.

Modern molecular work adds genetic subtypes on top of this, notably the Chx10-expressing V2a neurons of the gigantocellular nucleus, which stop locomotion when activated, and glycinergic ventromedial medullary neurons that paralyse the body during dreaming sleep.

The lateral reticular nucleus in the medulla is a distinct precerebellar member of the family, sending mossy fibres to the cerebellum.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The reticular formation occupies the tegmentum, the central core, of the entire brainstem.

In the midbrain it comprises the cuneiform and subcuneiform nuclei and the surrounding midbrain reticular formation, adjacent to the pedunculopontine tegmental nucleus.

In the pons it comprises nucleus reticularis pontis oralis and nucleus reticularis pontis caudalis.

In the medulla it comprises nucleus gigantocellularis, nucleus magnocellularis, the lateral and dorsal paragigantocellular nuclei, the parvicellular reticular nucleus, the intermediate reticular nucleus, the dorsal and ventral medullary reticular nuclei and the lateral reticular nucleus.

It sits at an interface in every direction: dorsally against the cranial nerve nuclei and the floor of the fourth ventricle, ventrally against the pontine nuclei and the pyramids, medially against the raphe, and laterally against the spinal trigeminal nucleus and the ascending sensory tracts, all of which it samples.

Rostrally it merges without a boundary into the intralaminar thalamic nuclei and the posterior hypothalamus, and caudally into the reticular intermediate grey of the spinal cord.

NUMBER:

There is no complete stereological census of the human reticular formation, and honesty requires saying so.

A defensible working figure is tens of millions of neurons across midbrain, pons and medulla, and a reasonable simulation estimate is about twenty-five million with a plausible range of ten to fifty million.

Confidence is low, and the bound comes from subtraction: the brainstem holds about two hundred million neurons in total, the pontine nuclei take twenty million and the inferior olive one million, and the reticular core is the largest of the remaining tissue by volume.

On the working figure it is about twelve percent of brainstem neurons and about zero point zero three percent of all human neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

There is no single reticular firing pattern, and that variety is the point.

Many cells fire tonically at five to twenty spikes per second and change rate slowly with behavioural state.

Many carry a strong hyperpolarisation-activated current, Ih, and a low-threshold calcium current, which together give a rebound burst when the cell is released from inhibition, useful for producing a sudden jump.

Giant cells of nucleus reticularis pontis caudalis are almost silent until a startling stimulus arrives, then fire a single high-frequency burst with a latency of only three to eight milliseconds, driving the whole-body startle response.

Most reticular neurons show strong state dependence, changing gain by a factor of two or more between waking, non-rapid-eye-movement sleep and rapid-eye-movement sleep.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs converge from almost everywhere: spinothalamic and spinoreticular pain fibres, trigeminal touch and pain, vestibular nuclei, auditory pathway, superior colliculus, cerebellar nuclei, hypothalamus, amygdala and layer 5 of the motor cortex.

Neuromodulatory inputs arrive from the raphe, locus coeruleus, orexin neurons of the hypothalamus and cholinergic cells of the pedunculopontine and laterodorsal tegmental nuclei.

Outputs go to the spinal cord by the medial and lateral reticulospinal tracts, to every cranial nerve motor nucleus, to the intralaminar and midline thalamus, to the basal forebrain, to the hypothalamus, to the cerebellum and back to the other reticular columns.

The signature is convergence at the input and radical divergence at the output.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are typically two to five micrometres in diameter and conduct fast, at twenty to eighty metres per second in the reticulospinal tracts.

The classic feature, described by the Scheibels in Golgi material, is that a single axon runs the length of the brainstem and cord giving off collateral branches at right angles at intervals along the way, so one cell contacts dozens of nuclei.

Pontine reticulospinal axons descend uncrossed in the ventral funiculus and excite antigravity extensor muscles.

Medullary reticulospinal axons from gigantocellularis descend in the ventrolateral funiculus, facilitate flexors and inhibit extensors, and carry the glycinergic drive that paralyses the body during dreaming.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

There is no single transmitter, which is another reason the reticular formation resists tidy description.

About sixty percent of neurons projecting down to the spinal cord are glutamatergic.

Neurons projecting up to the midbrain more often carry GABAergic markers, and many carry both GABAergic and glutamatergic markers together.

Glycine is the key inhibitory transmitter of the ventromedial medullary neurons that produce muscle atonia in dreaming sleep, and it works alongside GABA.

Peptides are abundant, including enkephalin, substance P, neurotensin and somatostatin, and the region is also threaded by serotonergic raphe cells and cholinergic tegmental cells that are described in their own entries.

MYELINATION:

The long reticulospinal and reticulothalamic axons are thickly myelinated, which is essential because postural corrections and startle must be fast.

Within the reticular core itself the local collaterals are thin and often unmyelinated, which is part of why the tissue looks like felt.

Oligodendrocytes here must myelinate axons that branch repeatedly, so myelin segments are interrupted at every collateral branch point.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Because they never stop, reticular neurons are metabolically expensive in aggregate, and the brainstem tegmentum has a high resting glucose consumption that barely falls in sleep.

The giant cells are especially costly, since a seventy micrometre cell body must supply an axon that may exceed half a metre with dozens of branch points.

Their large size means a low surface-to-volume ratio, which helps them hold a stable resting potential, but they pay for it with enormous sodium pump activity.

PLASTICITY:

Startle responses habituate strongly and rapidly, and the site of that habituation is the synapse onto the giant pontine reticular neurons, which is one of the best-studied examples of simple learning in a mammal.

Startle is also potentiated by fear through input from the amygdala, which is the basis of the fear-potentiated startle test.

After spinal cord injury, reticulospinal axons sprout new collaterals and can partly take over lost corticospinal function, making this system a major target for rehabilitation research.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes in the reticular formation are unusually large and have unusually long, straight processes that run alongside the dendrites rather than forming the tight non-overlapping domains seen in cortex.

The practical effect is that a single astrocyte can couple metabolically to many reticular neurons at once, supporting the wide dendritic fields.

The reticular core also contains a dense microglial population that is quick to react, and inflammatory activation here, as in sepsis, produces confusion and reduced consciousness out of proportion to the damage.

Circumventricular organs near the medullary reticular formation, including the area postrema, let blood-borne signals reach these glia and neurons directly, which is why illness makes you sleepy.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Everything else in the brainstem is a specialist: this nucleus moves the eyes, that one chews, that one hears.

The reticular formation is what makes the brainstem a single organ rather than a stack of specialists.

Remove it and each surviving nucleus would still perform its reflex, but nothing would set the overall level: no waking, no sleeping, no posture, no startle, no coordinated turn of head and eyes together, no linking of breathing to effort.

The region would lose its tone of voice entirely, and the body would become a set of reflexes with nobody at home.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Massive axonal collateralisation is the defining non-classical feature, since a single spike is delivered as a broadcast rather than a message.

Many reticular neurons are electrically coupled by gap junctions, and coupled clusters can synchronise to produce the all-or-none quality of startle and of the swallow.

Volume transmission matters here more than almost anywhere else outside the hypothalamus, because peptides released from dense-core vesicles diffuse through the loose neuropil to act on cells with no synaptic connection at all.

Reticular neurons act as gain controllers rather than message carriers, multiplying the effectiveness of other pathways by changing the background membrane potential of whole populations.

They also drive local blood flow directly, since activation of the medullary reticular formation changes cerebral perfusion, and they interact with the immune system through vagal and circumventricular routes.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Wide convergence from spinoreticular, trigeminal, vestibular, auditory, collicular, cerebellar, hypothalamic, amygdalar and cortical sources, zero to one hundred spikes per second each, with signed weights.

◦ State signals from orexin, noradrenaline, serotonin and acetylcholine, applied as a multiplicative gain.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Reticulospinal drive to spinal motor circuits at named segmental levels.

◦ Ascending drive to intralaminar thalamus and basal forebrain.

◦ Parallel drive to cranial motor nuclei, delivered on the same spike through separate collaterals.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential and adaptation level.

◦ Current behavioural state, one of wake, non-rapid-eye-movement sleep, rapid-eye-movement sleep.

◦ Habituation level of the startle synapse.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ State gain: about one point six in wake, zero point eight in non-rapid-eye-movement sleep, zero point four in rapid-eye-movement sleep.

◦ Output threshold about twelve spikes per second.

◦ Collateral count six to sixty targets per neuron.

◦ Conduction velocity twenty to eighty metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane time constant ten to thirty milliseconds.

◦ Startle latency three to eight milliseconds from stimulus to giant cell spike.

◦ State transitions over seconds to minutes.

◦ Habituation over tens of trials.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Bilateral damage to the paramedian midbrain and upper pontine tegmentum produces coma, because the ascending arousal broadcast is cut.

◦ Loss of the glycinergic and GABAergic ventromedial medullary neurons removes dream paralysis and produces rapid-eye-movement sleep behaviour disorder, in which people act out their dreams, often years before Parkinson disease appears.

◦ Damage to the descending reticulospinal tracts with the corticospinal tract spared produces the abnormal extensor posturing seen in severe brain injury.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to let one reticular neuron sum inputs from many unrelated sources, scale the result by the current arousal state, and deliver the same spike to every one of its widely separated targets.
Create a number variable called membrane_drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called arousal_gain with initial value 1.
Create a list variable called delivered_targets with initial value empty list.
For each input_source in the converging_inputs, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the product of the rate of input_source and the weight of input_source.
    Set contribution to the result.
    Calculate the sum of membrane_drive and contribution.
    Set membrane_drive to the result.
End the loop.
Check if the current_state is equal to wakefulness.
Set is_awake to the result.
Check if the current_state is equal to rapid eye movement sleep.
Set is_dreaming to the result.
If is_awake is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set arousal_gain to 1.6.
Else if is_dreaming is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set arousal_gain to 0.4.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set arousal_gain to 0.8.
End the conditional block.
Calculate the product of membrane_drive and arousal_gain.
Set gated_drive to the result.
Calculate the difference of gated_drive and 12.
Set output_rate to the result.
For each target in the collateral_targets, perform the following operations.
    Add target to the end of delivered_targets.
End the loop.
Display output_rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python was chosen because the essential fact about this neuron is a one-to-many mapping with per-target delays, and a dictionary is the clearest possible way to write that down.

STATE_GAIN = {"wake": 1.6, "nrem": 0.8, "rem": 0.4}
THRESHOLD_HZ = 12.0

# one axon, many collaterals, each with its own conduction delay in milliseconds
COLLATERALS = {"spinal_cord_C5": 4.0, "spinal_cord_L2": 9.0,
               "thalamus_intralaminar": 6.0, "hypoglossal_nucleus": 2.0,
               "facial_nucleus": 2.5, "lateral_reticular_nucleus": 3.0}

# each input is a firing rate in spikes per second and a signed synaptic weight
INPUTS = {"trigeminal_touch": (40.0, 0.25), "spinothalamic_pain": (15.0, 0.60),
          "cortex_M1": (30.0, 0.35), "vestibular": (10.0, 0.20),
          "raphe_serotonin": (2.0, -0.50)}

def reticular_output(inputs, state):
    drive = sum(rate * weight for rate, weight in inputs.values())
    return max(0.0, drive * STATE_GAIN[state] - THRESHOLD_HZ)

def broadcast(rate, collaterals):
    # the same spike train arrives everywhere, only the timing differs
    return {target: (round(rate, 1), delay) for target, delay in collaterals.items()}

for state in ("wake", "nrem", "rem"):
    rate = reticular_output(INPUTS, state)
    packets = broadcast(rate, COLLATERALS)
    print(state, "output", round(rate, 1), "Hz to", len(packets), "targets")
# prints wake 36.8 Hz, nrem 12.4 Hz, rem 0.2 Hz, each to 6 targets

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other neuron in the human body has a dendritic field flattened into a single plane that spans half the brainstem.

No other cell delivers the same action potential to the lumbar spinal cord and the thalamus.

If you see a giant, sparsely branched, undecorated cell sitting in a tangled feltwork in the core of the brainstem, with an axon that gives off right-angled collaterals every few millimetres for half a metre, you are looking at a reticular formation neuron.

CHAPTER 151. PRE-BOTZINGER COMPLEX NEURONS (Breath-Rhythm Generating Neuron) (NON-COG)

Somewhere around a thousand cells on each side of your medulla have started every breath you have ever taken, and they will start the last one too.

The existing chapter label is a coordinate on a brainstem map and teaches a student nothing about breathing.

Breath names the single output these cells own, because this small knot of interneurons in the upper medulla sets the moment each inward breath begins, and animals lacking it die at birth of asphyxia.

Rhythm names the work, which is not the muscular effort of breathing but the decision about when the next effort starts, since brief silencing during an outward breath delays the following inward breath and brief stimulation brings it forward.

Generating records that the beat is built inside the group rather than delivered to it.

Neuron marks it as one of an astonishingly small and fragile population, since destroying roughly fifteen percent of the rhythm-generating cells in a neonatal mouse slice stops the rhythm irreversibly.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Breathing has to happen about twenty thousand times a day whether you are thinking about it or not, and something has to decide when each breath begins.

That decision is made in a patch of tissue no bigger than a grain of rice on each side of the lower brainstem, called the pre-Botzinger complex.

Cut it out of a slice of brainstem in a dish and the slice stops producing the inspiratory rhythm; leave the slice with the pre-Botzinger complex intact and it keeps gasping rhythmically in a bath of salt solution for hours, with no lungs, no blood and no brain attached.

That experiment, first done in 1991, is why this cluster is called the kernel of breathing.

Its neurons build a rhythm out of nothing but their own membranes and their connections to each other, then hand the timing signal to the motor neurons that pull the diaphragm down.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

These are small, unremarkable-looking neurons, which is part of why the region went unrecognised for so long.

They are mostly fusiform or small multipolar cells with three to five modestly branched dendrites, scattered loosely through the surrounding reticular tissue rather than packed into a visible nucleus.

In the human brainstem they are notable for being heavily loaded with lipofuscin, the yellow-brown wear pigment of long-lived cells.

You cannot recognise them by shape at all, only by their molecules, which is why the human complex was only properly identified in 2011 using antibody staining.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are small, roughly twelve to twenty-five micrometres across.

Dendritic fields are modest, spanning about two hundred to three hundred micrometres, and stay largely within the complex, which is what allows the population to behave as a tight local network.

Axons are short for the ones that stay inside the complex and up to a centimetre or two for the ones that reach the phrenic and hypoglossal premotor pools.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The core rhythm generators are derived during development from progenitor cells expressing the gene Dbx1, and they are glutamatergic.

Within that core, one subgroup expresses the peptide somatostatin, and a separate, non-overlapping subgroup expresses the adhesion molecule cadherin 9.

Roughly seventeen percent of the Dbx1 population carries somatostatin and about fifty-six percent carries cadherin 9.

The cadherin 9 subgroup projects to the locus coeruleus and links breathing to arousal, so a small subset of these cells is the reason that slow, deliberate breathing calms you down.

Nearly all of the rhythm-critical cells carry the neurokinin 1 receptor, which responds to substance P, and a large fraction also carry the mu opioid receptor.

A separate population of inhibitory glycinergic and GABAergic interneurons is intermingled and shapes the burst.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The pre-Botzinger complex sits in the ventrolateral medulla oblongata, in the reticular formation just beneath and slightly medial to the semi-compact division of the nucleus ambiguus.

Along the front-to-back axis it lies between the Botzinger complex above it and the rostral ventral respiratory group below it, at roughly the level where the hypoglossal nerve rootlets and the rostral pole of the lateral reticular nucleus appear.

It is dorsal to the inferior olive and lateral to the raphe obscurus.

It sits at a functional interface between three systems: the chemosensory system that measures blood gases, the reticular arousal system, and the somatic motor system that drives the muscles of breathing.

In humans it has been mapped as a discrete region containing neurons that stain for both the neurokinin 1 receptor and somatostatin while staining negative for the markers of monoamine neurons and motor neurons.

NUMBER:

In the mouse the rhythm-generating Dbx1 core is small and well bounded: killing about eighty-five cells with a laser stops the rhythm, and that is roughly fifteen percent of the population, implying about five hundred to six hundred cells per side.

No equivalent count exists for the human, and it would be dishonest to pretend otherwise.

The human region is physically larger, so a defensible working estimate is two thousand to four thousand neurons per side, giving perhaps four to eight thousand in total, and confidence in that figure is low.

Even at the top of that range it is about four thousandths of one percent of the two hundred million brainstem neurons and about seven millionths of one percent of all human neurons.

This is the most consequential handful of cells in the body: no other population of a few thousand neurons kills you within minutes when it fails.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A minority of these neurons are true pacemakers, meaning they burst rhythmically even when all synaptic input is blocked.

The current responsible is the persistent sodium current, a small sodium leak that stays open instead of shutting off, which slowly drives the cell up to threshold and then slowly inactivates, forcing a pause.

A second current, the calcium-activated non-selective cation current carried largely by the TRPM4 channel, sets the size of the burst rather than its timing.

Most cells are not pacemakers on their own but are recruited into the burst by their neighbours, so the rhythm is a group property, sometimes described as a group pacemaker.

Careful recording shows that each full breath is preceded by a small, low-amplitude pre-inspiratory ripple called a burstlet, and only some burstlets grow into full bursts, which is why breathing amplitude and breathing rate can be controlled separately.

At rest the population bursts about twelve to eighteen times a minute, which is zero point two to zero point three hertz.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory input arrives from the retrotrapezoid nucleus carrying carbon dioxide information, from the nucleus of the solitary tract carrying signals from lung stretch receptors and the carotid body, from the raphe carrying serotonin, and from the hypothalamus carrying orexin.

Inhibitory input arrives from the Botzinger complex, which enforces the silent expiratory phase, and from local glycinergic interneurons.

The critical output goes to the rostral ventral respiratory group, which drives phrenic motor neurons in spinal segments C3, C4 and C5 to contract the diaphragm, and to hypoglossal premotor neurons that open the airway a fraction of a second before the diaphragm pulls.

Ascending outputs reach the locus coeruleus, the parabrachial nucleus and the hypothalamus, linking each breath to arousal and emotion.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are thin, roughly half a micrometre to one micrometre, and mostly lightly myelinated or unmyelinated.

Many are purely local, arborising densely within the complex itself, which is what builds the recurrent excitation the rhythm depends on.

The projection axons cross the midline or run ipsilaterally to the premotor pools, and the two sides of the complex are strongly cross-coupled so that both halves of the diaphragm contract together.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The rhythm-generating cells release glutamate, packaged by the vesicular glutamate transporter VGLUT2, and act on AMPA and NMDA receptors.

Substance P, acting on the neurokinin 1 receptor, powerfully speeds and stabilises the rhythm.

Somatostatin, released by a subset, slows and depresses it.

Opioid drugs bind mu opioid receptors on these very cells and slow the rhythm, and at high doses stop it entirely, which is the mechanism of death in an opioid overdose.

Local glycine and GABA sculpt the off phase, and serotonin, noradrenaline and adenosine all set the background excitability.

MYELINATION:

Local axons within the complex are largely unmyelinated, consistent with a slow, densely interconnected local network.

The longer projection axons to the phrenic and hypoglossal premotor pools are thinly myelinated by oligodendrocytes, giving conduction fast enough that both sides of the diaphragm stay synchronised to within a few milliseconds.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

These cells never rest for more than a few seconds in an entire lifetime, so their cumulative workload is extreme, which is the likely reason for the heavy lipofuscin loading seen in human tissue.

They are also unusually sensitive to a fall in oxygen, and their response to hypoxia is biphasic: a brief increase in rate followed by depression and then gasping.

Adenosine, which accumulates when energy runs low, directly inhibits them, coupling the breathing rhythm to the energy state of the tissue.

PLASTICITY:

Repeated brief exposures to low oxygen produce long-term facilitation of breathing, a serotonin-dependent strengthening that lasts hours and is now being trialled as a treatment after spinal cord injury.

Chronic opioid exposure produces tolerance at these synapses, though tolerance to the respiratory effect develops more slowly than tolerance to pain relief, which is exactly why overdose remains lethal in long-term users.

The network also reorganises after partial damage, so a substantial fraction of the cells can be lost before breathing fails, which is a mercy and also a reason that decline is often silent until it is sudden.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes in the pre-Botzinger complex are not passive.

They are themselves chemosensitive, releasing adenosine triphosphate when carbon dioxide rises or oxygen falls, and that released adenosine triphosphate directly excites the neurons, so part of the drive to breathe is glial rather than neuronal.

They also clear glutamate from the dense local network fast enough to keep the recurrent excitation from running away into a seizure-like discharge.

Microglia in this region are implicated in the failure of breathing during severe inflammation, and inflammatory mediators from them depress the rhythm.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

The ventrolateral medulla is a crowded neighbourhood full of cells that swallow, cough, vomit, control blood pressure and vocalise.

Take out the pre-Botzinger complex and every one of those functions still exists, but nothing starts.

The region loses its downbeat, the tick that every other respiratory-related behaviour hangs on, because swallowing, coughing and speaking are all timed against the breath.

Practically, the animal or person simply stops breathing, while all the machinery of breathing remains intact and ready.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Rhythm here is an emergent network property, not a property of any one cell, and that is the central lesson: you cannot find the breath by looking at a single neuron.

Recurrent excitation causes percolation, in which activity spreads through the network like a rumour, and the burstlet-to-burst transition is a threshold in that percolation process.

Astrocytic gliotransmission adds a second, chemical rhythm layer beneath the electrical one.

Gap junction coupling exists in the immature complex and helps synchronise the network before chemical synapses mature.

The complex also performs state-dependent self-modification, changing its own excitability across sleep and wakefulness, which is why central sleep apnoea happens in sleep and not in waking.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Tonic excitatory drive from the retrotrapezoid nucleus and raphe, modelled as a conductance of zero point three to zero point five nanosiemens.

◦ Phasic input from the nucleus of the solitary tract carrying lung stretch and carotid body signals.

◦ Inhibitory input from the Botzinger complex during expiration.

◦ Adenosine triphosphate released by local astrocytes as a chemosensory boost.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Inspiratory burst to the rostral ventral respiratory group and thence to phrenic motor neurons at spinal levels C3 to C5.

◦ Pre-inspiratory drive to hypoglossal premotor neurons, leading the diaphragm by about one hundred milliseconds.

◦ Ascending copy of each breath to the locus coeruleus, parabrachial nucleus and hypothalamus.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential.

◦ Availability of the persistent sodium current, a slow inactivation variable from zero to one.

◦ Calcium concentration setting burst amplitude.

◦ Recruitment level of the surrounding network.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Persistent sodium conductance about two point eight nanosiemens, leak about two point eight nanosiemens.

◦ Membrane capacitance about twenty-one picofarads.

◦ Tonic excitatory conductance zero point three to zero point five nanosiemens, which sets the rate.

◦ Burst threshold about minus forty millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane time constant about seven milliseconds.

◦ Persistent sodium inactivation and recovery, seconds, which is what sets the breath period.

◦ Breath period about four to five seconds at rest.

◦ Burst duration about zero point five to one second.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Opioid drugs acting on mu opioid receptors slow and then stop the rhythm, the direct cause of death in opioid overdose.

◦ Loss of neurokinin 1 receptor neurons in this region in multiple system atrophy produces central sleep apnoea, inspiratory stridor and sudden death in sleep.

◦ Degeneration in other conditions produces Ondine curse-like failure of automatic breathing while voluntary breathing is preserved.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to produce the rhythmic inspiratory burst of a pre-Botzinger complex neuron by letting a persistent sodium current depolarise the cell and then slowly inactivate.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value -60.
Create a number variable called availability with initial value 1.
Create a boolean variable called in_burst with initial value false.
For each millisecond in the recording, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the product of 12 and availability.
    Set persistent_sodium_drive to the result.
    Calculate the difference of persistent_sodium_drive and 8.
    Set net_drive to the result.
    Calculate the sum of voltage and net_drive.
    Set voltage to the result.
    Check if voltage is greater than -40.
    Set is_above_threshold to the result.
    If is_above_threshold is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set in_burst to true.
    End the conditional block.
    If in_burst is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of availability and 0.02.
        Set availability to the result.
        Check if availability is less than 0.05.
        Set is_exhausted to the result.
        If is_exhausted is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set in_burst to false.
        End the conditional block.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of availability and 0.0006.
        Set availability to the result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python was chosen because the accepted model of this cell is two coupled differential equations, and a plain Euler loop in Python shows the mechanism without hiding it inside a library.

import math

C = 21.0            # membrane capacitance, picofarads
G_NAP = 2.8         # persistent sodium conductance, nanosiemens
E_NA = 50.0         # sodium reversal potential, millivolts
G_LEAK = 2.8        # leak conductance, nanosiemens
E_LEAK = -65.0      # leak reversal potential, millivolts
G_TONIC = 0.45      # drive from retrotrapezoid nucleus and raphe, nanosiemens
E_SYN = 0.0         # reversal potential of that drive, millivolts
TAU_H_BAR = 10000.0 # scale of the slow inactivation, milliseconds
DT = 0.5            # integration step, milliseconds

def m_inf(v):       # fast activation of the persistent sodium current
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp((v + 40.0) / -6.0))

def h_inf(v):       # availability falls as the cell depolarises
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp((v + 48.0) / 6.0))

def tau_h(v):       # availability recovers over seconds, setting the breath period
    return TAU_H_BAR / math.cosh((v + 48.0) / 12.0)

v, h = -60.0, 0.6
onsets = []
previous_v = v
for step in range(int(60000 / DT)):        # sixty seconds of simulated breathing
    i_nap = G_NAP * m_inf(v) * h * (v - E_NA)
    i_leak = G_LEAK * (v - E_LEAK)
    i_drive = G_TONIC * (v - E_SYN)
    v += DT * (-i_nap - i_leak - i_drive) / C
    h += DT * (h_inf(v) - h) / tau_h(v)
    if previous_v <= -40.0 < v:            # crossing threshold starts a breath
        onsets.append(step * DT)
    previous_v = v

periods = [onsets[k + 1] - onsets[k] for k in range(len(onsets) - 1)]
mean_period = sum(periods) / len(periods)
print("inspiratory bursts:", len(onsets))
print("breath period:", round(mean_period / 1000.0, 2), "seconds")
print("rate:", round(60000.0 / mean_period, 1), "breaths per minute")
# prints 13 bursts, a period of 4.96 seconds, and 12.1 breaths per minute

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other neuron population, when removed from a slab of tissue in a dish, takes the rhythm of life with it.

No other few thousand cells in the human body can be silenced by a single dose of a common painkiller and kill the person within four minutes.

If you find small, lipofuscin-laden, glutamatergic cells that carry both the neurokinin 1 receptor and somatostatin, scattered just beneath the nucleus ambiguus in the ventrolateral medulla, bursting slowly and together, you have found the pre-Botzinger complex.

CHAPTER 152. RETROTRAPEZOID NUCLEUS NEURONS (Acid-Sensing Air-Hunger Neuron) (NON-COG)

You cannot hold your breath until you die, and the reason is a thin sheet of a few thousand cells lying against the front surface of your medulla that can taste the acid in your blood.

The old name is a piece of surveying, telling you only that the cells sit behind a fibre bundle.

Acid-Sensing names what the cell actually measures, because it does not detect oxygen at all but the acidity produced when carbon dioxide dissolves in the fluid around it, using pH sensors such as the potassium channel called Task2 and the proton-sensing receptor called Gpr4.

Air-Hunger names the sensation and the behaviour that follow, since these cells drive the urgent deepening and quickening of breathing during high carbon dioxide while leaving breathing in ordinary room air and even in low oxygen largely untouched.

Neuron marks it as a cell of striking specificity, since silencing its master gene in rats leaves normal-air and low-oxygen breathing intact but sharply blunts the response to high carbon dioxide, and in humans mutations in that same gene cause a condition in which people stop breathing adequately in sleep.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Breathing exists to get rid of carbon dioxide, and carbon dioxide is dangerous because it turns into acid the moment it meets water.

Something has to measure that acid and demand more breathing when it rises.

The retrotrapezoid nucleus (RTN) is the main place where that measurement is made inside the brain.

Its neurons are almost silent when your blood carbon dioxide is normal, and they fire faster and faster as it climbs, pushing the pre-Botzinger complex to breathe harder and more often.

They are the reason that a breath-hold ends in an overwhelming urge to inhale, an urge you cannot override for long, and the reason people whose RTN fails simply stop breathing when they fall asleep.

Roughly two thirds of the whole central response to carbon dioxide is attributed to this one small group.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

RTN neurons are medium-sized cells with two to four long, smooth dendrites.

Their most striking feature is orientation: many of them send a dendrite straight to the outer surface of the brainstem, into the thin marginal layer that is bathed in cerebrospinal fluid and lined with blood vessels.

They are, in effect, dipping a finger into the fluid to sample it.

Cell bodies are packed close to the ventral surface, forming a thin sheet only a few cells deep rather than a rounded nucleus.

They cannot be identified by shape and are recognised instead by the transcription factor Phox2b in the nucleus together with the absence of the enzymes that mark adrenaline and acetylcholine cells.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are roughly fifteen to twenty-five micrometres across.

Dendrites reach one hundred to three hundred micrometres, which is far enough to cross from the cell body to the surface of the brainstem.

Axons run one to two centimetres to the respiratory nuclei nearby.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The defining chemical signature is a Phox2b-positive, tyrosine hydroxylase-negative, choline acetyltransferase-negative, glutamatergic neuron, born from an Atoh1-dependent lineage that also required the gene Egr2 during development.

Most also express the peptide neuromedin B, and this marker is now used to distinguish the true chemoreceptor population from neighbouring cells.

A closely related and partly overlapping group, the lateral parafacial region, drives active breathing out during exercise rather than breathing in, and is often treated as a sister population.

Individual RTN cells differ in how much of their carbon dioxide sensitivity is intrinsic and how much is relayed from astrocytes, so a useful simulation subdivides them by that ratio.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The retrotrapezoid nucleus lies at the very front surface of the rostral medulla oblongata, immediately beneath and behind the facial motor nucleus, in the region anatomists call parafacial.

It extends from the caudal pole of the facial nucleus backwards along the marginal layer, sitting ventral to the superior olivary complex and ventromedial to the spinal trigeminal tract.

It is named for its position behind the trapezoid body, the great sheet of crossing auditory fibres.

Its defining interface is with the outside world of the brain: it sits directly against the pial surface, the cerebrospinal fluid and the branches of the basilar and vertebral arteries, which is exactly where a blood gas sensor should be.

It also interfaces with the ventral respiratory column just behind it, so the measurement and the response are only a millimetre or two apart.

NUMBER:

In the rat the nucleus contains about two thousand neurons and in the mouse about eight hundred.

A 2025 study of adult human brainstems counted approximately five thousand Phox2b-positive, tyrosine hydroxylase-negative neurons in the putative human RTN, counting both sides together, which is substantially more than the roughly nine hundred and eighty reported earlier in human infants.

Confidence is moderate, since the adult figure comes from a single recent study, and the infant and adult figures may partly reflect different counting criteria rather than real growth.

Five thousand neurons is about two and a half thousandths of one percent of the two hundred million brainstem neurons, and about six millionths of one percent of all human neurons.

Together with the other five types in this batch the total comes to roughly forty-six million cells, comfortably inside the two hundred million brainstem budget.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

RTN neurons are silent or nearly silent below an arterial carbon dioxide pressure of about thirty-five to thirty-eight millimetres of mercury, the level called the apnoeic threshold.

Above that they fire with a strikingly linear relationship to carbon dioxide, climbing to about ten to thirty spikes per second as carbon dioxide rises into the fifties.

They adapt very little, which is essential, because a sensor that adapts would let carbon dioxide drift.

They detect acid through two intrinsic molecular sensors: the potassium channel TASK-2, which closes when protons build up outside the cell, and the proton-activated receptor GPR4, which switches on.

Closing a potassium leak and opening an excitatory receptor both depolarise the cell, so acid literally turns them on.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from the carotid bodies by way of the nucleus of the solitary tract, so oxygen information and carbon dioxide information are added together here.

Further inputs come from the hypothalamus during exercise, from the raphe carrying serotonin, from orexin neurons carrying arousal state, and from local astrocytes releasing adenosine triphosphate onto purinergic receptors.

Outputs go to the pre-Botzinger complex, to the rostral and caudal ventral respiratory groups, to hypoglossal motor neurons that keep the airway open, and to the parafacial lateral region for active expiration.

The connection to the pre-Botzinger complex is direct and excitatory, which is why raising carbon dioxide immediately raises breathing rate.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are thin, under one micrometre, and travel only short distances within the medulla and lower pons.

They branch to reach several respiratory nuclei at once, so a single RTN neuron can raise rate, depth and airway patency together.

Conduction delays are short, on the order of a few milliseconds, so the loop from acid detection to changed breathing is fast compared with the seconds it takes blood to circulate.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

RTN neurons are glutamatergic, using the vesicular glutamate transporter VGLUT2.

Most also release the peptide neuromedin B, which prolongs the excitatory effect.

They are excited by adenosine triphosphate released from neighbouring astrocytes, by serotonin, by substance P and by orexin, and inhibited by GABA and glycine from the surrounding respiratory network.

Their own transmitter action on the pre-Botzinger complex is fast excitation through AMPA and NMDA receptors.

MYELINATION:

The short local axons are thinly myelinated or unmyelinated, which is unproblematic because the distances are so short.

Myelination is not a limiting factor for this cell type, and there is no specialised myelin arrangement, unlike the long tracts elsewhere in the brainstem.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Energy use is modest at rest, since firing rates are low, but rises steeply during exercise or lung disease when these cells fire continuously for hours.

Their position against the pial surface gives them privileged access to the densest capillary bed in the ventral medulla, and blood flow to this exact patch of tissue increases when carbon dioxide rises, a local coupling that also amplifies the signal.

Because carbon dioxide dilates blood vessels, the sensor and its blood supply are physically entangled, and disentangling the vascular from the neuronal contribution has taken decades.

PLASTICITY:

Chronic exposure to high carbon dioxide, as in advanced lung disease, blunts the response of these neurons over weeks, which is why people with severe chronic obstructive pulmonary disease stop responding normally to carbon dioxide and rely instead on low oxygen to drive their breathing.

Repeated exposure to low oxygen produces long-lasting facilitation of the chemoreflex, which contributes to the high blood pressure seen in obstructive sleep apnoea.

The population is also plastic across development, expanding its influence in the first months of life, which is one reason infants are especially vulnerable to failures of this system.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The astrocytes here are genuinely different from astrocytes elsewhere, and that difference is the interesting part of the story.

Ventral surface astrocytes in this region are themselves chemosensitive: when carbon dioxide rises they acidify internally, which triggers release of adenosine triphosphate through connexin hemichannels.

That adenosine triphosphate acts on purinergic receptors on the RTN neurons and adds roughly a fifth to a third of the total response.

These astrocytes also send processes to the surrounding arterioles and dilate them when carbon dioxide rises, so the same glial cell both boosts the neural signal and increases the local blood flow that generates it.

Nowhere else in the brain do astrocytes function so clearly as a primary sensory element in their own right.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

The ventral medullary surface is otherwise unremarkable tissue, a thin rind over the exit of the facial nerve.

Remove the RTN and that rind becomes just a boundary rather than a sense organ.

Breathing would continue, driven by the pre-Botzinger complex and by oxygen sensors in the neck, but it would no longer be steered by carbon dioxide.

The person would breathe adequately while awake, because wakefulness itself provides drive, and would hypoventilate dangerously the moment they fell asleep.

That is precisely the clinical picture of congenital central hypoventilation syndrome, and it is why the region gives the brainstem its quality of vigilance about blood chemistry.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The cell is a chemical sensor before it is a computing element, and its input is not a synapse at all but the proton concentration of the fluid around it.

Sensing is distributed between the neuron and the astrocyte, so the true sensory unit is a neuron-glia pair, not a neuron.

The nucleus controls local blood flow, since RTN activity and astrocytic signalling dilate nearby arterioles, meaning this population regulates its own perfusion.

Volume transmission is important, since adenosine triphosphate and neuromedin B act over a diffusion distance rather than a cleft.

The whole assembly acts as a negative feedback controller with a very long loop delay imposed by the circulation time of blood, and much of its behaviour, including the instability called periodic breathing, is a property of that delay rather than of the cells.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Arterial carbon dioxide pressure, thirty to sixty millimetres of mercury, converted to local pH.

◦ Carotid body signal relayed by the nucleus of the solitary tract, adding an oxygen term.

◦ Adenosine triphosphate from local astrocytes, contributing about twenty to thirty percent of total drive.

◦ Serotonin, orexin and hypothalamic exercise signals as multiplicative gain.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Excitatory glutamate and neuromedin B to the pre-Botzinger complex, rostral ventral respiratory group and hypoglossal motor neurons.

◦ Firing rate zero to about thirty spikes per second.

• STATE.

◦ Current firing rate.

◦ Local extracellular pH.

◦ Astrocytic adenosine triphosphate level.

◦ Long-term adaptation level for chronic carbon dioxide exposure.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Apnoeic threshold about thirty-eight millimetres of mercury of carbon dioxide.

◦ Sensitivity about two to three spikes per second per millimetre of mercury.

◦ Maximum rate about thirty spikes per second.

◦ Relationship of carbon dioxide to pH about minus zero point zero zero eight pH units per millimetre of mercury.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Intrinsic proton sensing within one to two seconds.

◦ Astrocytic adenosine triphosphate release over several seconds.

◦ Circulatory loop delay from lung to brainstem, ten to twenty seconds, which sets the stability of the whole system.

◦ Chronic adaptation over days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Congenital central hypoventilation syndrome, caused by polyalanine repeat expansion mutations in the PHOX2B gene, in which these neurons fail to form and the carbon dioxide response is absent, so the child stops breathing adequately in sleep and needs lifelong ventilation.

◦ The condition affects roughly one in one hundred and fifty thousand to one in two hundred thousand births.

◦ Acquired loss contributes to central sleep apnoea, to the blunted carbon dioxide response of advanced lung disease, and to periodic breathing when the loop delay grows in heart failure.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert the carbon dioxide level of the blood into a firing rate that drives the breathing rhythm generator, and then to let the resulting ventilation lower the carbon dioxide again.
Create a number variable called carbon_dioxide_pressure with initial value 40.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called ventilation with initial value 6.
Repeat the following steps exactly 600 times.
    Calculate the difference of carbon_dioxide_pressure and 38.
    Set excess_carbon_dioxide to the result.
    Check if excess_carbon_dioxide is greater than 0.
    Set is_above_apnoeic_threshold to the result.
    If is_above_apnoeic_threshold is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the product of excess_carbon_dioxide and 2.5.
        Set firing_rate to the result.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set firing_rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of firing_rate and 0.9.
    Set ventilation to the result.
    Calculate the sum of ventilation and 4.
    Set ventilation to the result.
    Calculate the quotient of 240 divided by ventilation.
    Set target_carbon_dioxide to the result.
    Calculate the difference of target_carbon_dioxide and carbon_dioxide_pressure.
    Set gap to the result.
    Calculate the quotient of gap divided by 30.
    Set adjustment to the result.
    Calculate the sum of carbon_dioxide_pressure and adjustment.
    Set carbon_dioxide_pressure to the result.
End the loop.
Display carbon_dioxide_pressure to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python was chosen because this cell is a sigmoid sensor inside a negative feedback loop, and Python lets the sensor, the plant and the loop each be one short readable block.

import math

PCO2_TO_PH_SLOPE = -0.008   # pH units per millimetre of mercury of carbon dioxide
PH_REST = 7.40              # arterial pH at 40 mmHg
HALF_ACTIVATION_PH = 7.36   # pH at which RTN firing is half maximal
STEEPNESS = 0.02            # pH units for an e-fold change in rate
MAX_RATE_HZ = 30.0
ASTROCYTE_ATP_GAIN = 0.25   # extra drive relayed by chemosensitive astrocytes

def ph_from_pco2(pco2):
    return PH_REST + PCO2_TO_PH_SLOPE * (pco2 - 40.0)

def rtn_rate(pco2):
    ph = ph_from_pco2(pco2)
    neuronal = MAX_RATE_HZ / (1.0 + math.exp((ph - HALF_ACTIVATION_PH) / STEEPNESS))
    return neuronal * (1.0 + ASTROCYTE_ATP_GAIN)

def ventilation(pco2):
    # litres per minute: a fixed background plus the drive from the RTN
    return 4.0 + 0.9 * rtn_rate(pco2) * 0.4

pco2 = 40.0
for second in range(600):
    target = 240.0 / ventilation(pco2)   # steady state CO2 for this ventilation
    pco2 += (target - pco2) / 30.0       # blood and tissue stores lag behind

print("resting arterial CO2 settles at", round(pco2, 2), "mmHg")
print("RTN firing at 40 mmHg:", round(rtn_rate(40.0), 1), "Hz")
print("RTN firing at 50 mmHg:", round(rtn_rate(50.0), 1), "Hz")
# prints 40.51 mmHg, 4.5 Hz at rest, and 33.0 Hz when CO2 rises by ten

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other neuron in the human body sends a dendrite to the outer surface of the brain to taste the fluid there.

No other cell type is defined clinically by a single gene whose mutation makes a newborn forget to breathe in its sleep.

If you find a Phox2b-positive, glutamatergic, neuromedin B-expressing cell pressed against the ventral surface of the medulla just behind the facial nucleus, silent at normal carbon dioxide and firing linearly above it, you have found a retrotrapezoid nucleus neuron.

CHAPTER 153. RED NUCLEUS NEURONS (Limb-Path Correcting Neuron) (NON-COG)

Cut a fresh human midbrain across and two pink discs look back at you, stained by their own iron, which is how the red nucleus got its name.

Red nucleus is a colour seen in a fresh specimen, an address that names a pigment rather than a purpose.

Limb-Path names the domain, since every input and output of this midbrain group belongs to the machinery of limb and hand action.

Correcting names the specific signal and the purpose it serves, because the great majority of the human structure is the small-celled division, which does not reach the spinal cord at all but feeds the inferior olive, the source of the climbing fibres that report mismatches between intended and achieved movement to the cerebellum, so the loop adjusts the next attempt rather than commanding the present one.

Neuron marks it honestly, since the large-celled, spinal-projecting division is heavily reduced in bipedal primates including humans, so the direct commanding role seen in cats is not the human story and the name deliberately records the loop rather than the tract.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The red nucleus is a paired ball of grey matter, one on each side of the midbrain, and its neurons are the junction box where the cerebellum hands its finished movement commands onward.

In a cat or a rat a large part of this nucleus drives the limbs directly, through a cable called the rubrospinal tract, which runs from the midbrain down the spinal cord.

In a human that limb-driving part has almost disappeared, and the bulk of the nucleus instead sends a running copy of movement commands downward to the inferior olive, the small medullary structure that teaches the cerebellum what went wrong.

So the human red nucleus is less a commander of muscles and more a clerk inside the machinery of motor learning, and recent human imaging work argues it has been repurposed toward goal-directed action rather than reflex.
