STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The nucleus is an oval mass with two clearly different cell families living in it.

The magnocellular part, meaning the large-celled part, sits at the tail end and holds enormous multipolar neurons, each with four to eight thick dendrites, which are the branching input antennae of a nerve cell.

The parvocellular part, meaning the small-celled part, fills the front and most of the human nucleus, and its cells are small, round or spindle shaped, and tightly packed.

Cerebellar fibres land as big blobs on the cell body and the nearest dendrites, while cortical fibres land far out on the dendritic tips, so the cerebellum shouts and the cortex whispers.

SIZE:

The giant magnocellular neurons have cell bodies 40 to 90 micrometres across, which puts them among the largest neurons in the human brainstem.

Parvocellular neurons measure 10 to 25 micrometres.

The nucleus itself is about 5 millimetres wide, roughly the size of a peppercorn, and occupies on the order of 60 to 120 cubic millimetres on each side.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Magnocellular projection neuron, huge, exclusively glutamatergic, meaning it releases the excitatory chemical glutamate, and sending its axon to the brainstem and the top three segments of the neck spinal cord.

• Parvocellular projection neuron, small, glutamatergic, sending its axon down to the principal inferior olive.

• Parvocellular inhibitory interneuron, using gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main calming chemical, and staying entirely inside the nucleus.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It sits in the tegmentum, the core, of the midbrain at the level of the superior colliculus, above and behind the substantia nigra and in front of the medial lemniscus.

Two interfaces define it precisely.

It is embedded inside the crossing fibres of the superior cerebellar peduncle, the cerebellum's main exit cable, so cerebellar axons pass through the nucleus on their way to the thalamus.

It is also pierced by the rootlets of the third cranial nerve, the oculomotor nerve, which thread straight through the nucleus on their way out of the brain.

NUMBER:

The magnocellular part of the human red nucleus contains only about 150 to 200 giant neurons per side, a count published by Nathan and Smith in 1982 and repeated ever since, and confidence in it is good.

No unbiased stereological count of the whole human nucleus exists, so the parvocellular figure is an estimate: at the neuron packing density measured in the neighbouring human inferior colliculus, roughly 19,000 neurons per cubic millimetre, and allowing for looser packing, the likely range is 300,000 to 1.5 million per side, with low confidence.

That makes the pair perhaps 0.6 to 3 million neurons, up to about 1.5 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons of the whole brainstem, and about 0.002 percent of the 86 billion neurons of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These cells fire steadily at 5 to 50 spikes per second at rest and burst up to about 200 per second at the start of a movement.

The giant cells have a low input resistance near 20 to 40 megaohms, meaning it takes a lot of current to move their voltage, and a membrane time constant of 5 to 15 milliseconds, meaning they forget an input in about a hundredth of a second.

They carry a slow inward current called Ih, the hyperpolarisation-activated current, which switches on when the cell is pushed downward in voltage and then fires the cell back up in a rebound burst when the push is released.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from the opposite cerebellum, chiefly the interposed nucleus, from the same-side motor and premotor cortex, from the pedunculopontine nucleus, and from local GABA cells.

Outputs run three ways: the rubrospinal tract to the opposite spinal cord, rubrobulbar branches to the facial nucleus and the lateral reticular nucleus, and the large rubro-olivary bundle from the small cells down the central tegmental tract to the same-side principal olive.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Magnocellular axons cross the midline immediately, in the ventral tegmental decussation, then run down heavily insulated, 10 to 15 micrometres thick, conducting at 60 to 100 metres per second.

In humans these fibres are few and usually cannot be traced below the third neck segment, so the tract that dominates a cat's forelimb barely reaches a human elbow.

Parvocellular axons are thin, 1 to 3 micrometres, and end in the olive.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate is the output chemical of nearly every projecting cell, packaged by the transporter VGLUT2.

The cells receive GABA and glycine, both calming chemicals, and are modulated by acetylcholine from the pedunculopontine nucleus, a chemical that raises alertness.

MYELINATION:

Myelin is the fatty insulation that speeds up nerve fibres.

The magnocellular axons are among the most heavily insulated in the brainstem, with sheaths so thick that the tract stands out in myelin-stained sections; the parvocellular axons are lightly insulated by comparison.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The red nucleus concentrates iron, which is why it glows on iron-sensitive magnetic resonance imaging and why it looks pink in fresh tissue.

Iron is needed for the enzymes of energy production, but it also drives oxidative damage, so these cells run a high-output, high-risk metabolism and accumulate the ageing pigment lipofuscin early.

PLASTICITY:

Rubrospinal neurons are the textbook example of sprouting after injury: when the corticospinal tract is damaged, their axons grow new branches and take over part of its job.

The connection from motor cortex onto red nucleus neurons can be strengthened by pairing, and the nucleus is a required output stage in classical eyeblink conditioning.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The astrocytes here are unusual in being loaded with ferritin, the iron-storage protein, because they must buffer the nucleus's high iron content and keep it out of trouble.

Microglia, the brain's immune cells, in this nucleus also carry visible iron, and oligodendrocytes here build some of the thickest sheaths in the brainstem.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell type and the midbrain loses its return path from cerebellum to olive.

The cerebellum could still act, but it could no longer send a copy of its own output back to the teacher that grades it, so motor learning would lose its thermostat and skills would drift.

The midbrain would also lose its emergency flexor bias, the reflex that pulls a limb inward when balance fails.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Rebound bursting means these cells answer the end of inhibition, not only the arrival of excitation, so silence itself is a signal.

Their terminals in the olive enter glomeruli, small enclosed knots of tissue that also contain the electrical gap junctions coupling olivary cells together, so red nucleus activity changes how strongly the olive synchronises rather than merely how fast it fires.

Dendrites of the giant cells support local calcium spikes, small self-generated electrical events that let one branch decide on its own, and the cells depend on growth factors sent backward from their spinal targets to stay alive.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Cerebellar interposed nucleus, glutamate, 0 to 300 spikes per second per fibre, onto soma and proximal dendrites, weight about 1.4.

◦ Motor and premotor cortex, glutamate, 0 to 100 spikes per second, onto distal dendrites, weight about 0.6.

◦ Local GABA interneurons, 0 to 60 spikes per second, subtractive.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Spike train to contralateral spinal laminae five to seven, cervical segments one to three.

◦ Efference copy spike train to the ipsilateral principal inferior olive.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage in millivolts, Ih activation between 0 and 1, recent spike history, calcium level in dendrites.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting voltage -65 millivolts, threshold -45 millivolts, reset -70 millivolts, input resistance 30 megaohms, maximum rebound conductance 0.6 arbitrary units.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 5 to 15 milliseconds, Ih 100 to 200 milliseconds, spinal conduction 3 to 6 milliseconds, olivary conduction 8 to 15 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Damage to this nucleus or to the cerebellar fibres passing through it produces Holmes tremor, a slow shaking of about 2 to 5 cycles per second that is present at rest, worse on holding a posture and worst on reaching, appearing weeks to months after the injury.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn a cerebellar movement command into a limb command while sending a copy of that command down to the inferior olive.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -65.
Create a number variable called rebound activation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called total drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called olive copy with initial value empty list.
For each time step in the movement, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the product of the cerebellar input at this time step and 1.4.
    Set total drive to the result.
    Calculate the difference of total drive and the inhibitory input at this time step.
    Set total drive to the result.
    Check if membrane voltage is less than -70.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of rebound activation and 0.02.
        Set rebound activation to the result.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the product of rebound activation and 0.99.
        Set rebound activation to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of total drive and rebound activation.
    Set total drive to the result.
    Calculate the product of total drive and 3.
    Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and the result.
    Set membrane voltage to the result.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -45.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to -70.
        Calculate the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set spike count to the result.
        Add 1 to the end of olive copy.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Add 0 to the end of olive copy.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python, chosen because the rebound arithmetic is short and a simulation agent
# can drop this straight into a larger model without a build step.
DT = 0.001            # time step in seconds
TAU_M = 0.010         # membrane time constant, 10 ms
TAU_H = 0.150         # rebound current time constant, 150 ms
V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = -65.0, -45.0, -70.0   # millivolts
R_IN = 30.0           # input resistance in megaohms
G_H_MAX = 0.6         # maximum rebound drive in nanoamps

def red_nucleus_neuron(cortical, cerebellar, inhibition, steps):
    """Currents in nanoamps. Returns spike times and the olivary copy."""
    v = V_REST
    h = 0.0                      # rebound current activation, 0 to 1
    spikes = []
    olive_copy = []
    for t in range(steps):
        drive = 0.6 * cortical[t] + 1.4 * cerebellar[t] - inhibition[t]
        h_target = 1.0 if v < -70.0 else 0.0   # sag builds only when pushed down
        h += (h_target - h) * DT / TAU_H
        v += (-(v - V_REST) + R_IN * (drive + G_H_MAX * h)) * DT / TAU_M
        fired = v >= V_TH
        if fired:
            v = V_RESET
            spikes.append(t)
        olive_copy.append(1.0 if fired else 0.0)
    return spikes, olive_copy

if __name__ == "__main__":
    n = 400
    cortex = [0.5] * n
    cerebellum = [0.0] * 100 + [1.2] * 60 + [0.0] * 240
    inhibit = [0.0] * 200 + [2.0] * 80 + [0.0] * 120
    fired, copy = red_nucleus_neuron(cortex, cerebellum, inhibit, n)
    print("spikes:", len(fired), "olive copy length:", len(copy))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other nucleus in the human brain is pink from its own iron, embedded in the crossing fibres of the cerebellum's exit cable, and pierced by the rootlets of a cranial nerve.

And no other human cell class shrinks so dramatically from its animal version: 150 to 200 giant cells, where a cat has tens of thousands.

CHAPTER 154. SUPERIOR COLLICULUS NEURONS (Gaze-Shifting Orienting Neuron) (COG)

Something flickers at the edge of your vision and your eyes are already on it, before you know you have seen anything at all.

The chapter label names a bump on the brainstem and hides everything the tissue does.

Gaze-Shifting names the output, because the deep layers of this midbrain roof drive the fast flicking eye movements called saccades, sending commands to the pontine machinery for sideways movement and the midbrain machinery for up and down movement.

Orienting names the broader faculty, since the same map turns the head and attention toward whatever is suddenly interesting, and the superficial layers hold two complementary detector cells, broad-armed ones that notice a small object anywhere in a large patch of the visual field and narrow-armed ones that report its exact position.

Neuron marks it as a cell of a structure whose upper layers ask what is there and whose lower layers decide whether to look, with build-up cells ramping their firing from the moment a target appears until the eye actually moves.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The superior colliculus is a pair of layered mounds on the roof of the midbrain, and it is the oldest map of space in the vertebrate brain.

Its top layers hold a picture of the visual world, laid out in order, and its bottom layers hold a map of where to point the eyes and head, laid out in exactly the same order and stacked directly underneath.

Its job is to notice that something happened somewhere, decide which somewhere wins, and launch the eyes there in about 200 milliseconds.

It is also the route that supports blindsight, the eerie condition in which a person with a destroyed visual cortex can point at objects they swear they cannot see.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is built in seven sheets, named from the surface downward: stratum zonale, stratum griseum superficiale, stratum opticum, stratum griseum intermediale, stratum album intermediale, stratum griseum profundum and stratum album profundum.

Grey sheets hold cell bodies and white sheets hold passing fibres.

Superficial cells come in four shapes: wide-field vertical cells whose dendrites spread sideways like an umbrella, narrow-field vertical cells with a tight vertical brush, horizontal cells lying flat, and stellate cells with dendrites in all directions.

Deep-layer output cells are large and T-shaped, with one dendritic tuft reaching up into the visual sheets, which is how a sensory map and a motor map are physically stitched together.

SIZE:

Cell bodies run from 8 micrometres for small interneurons to 40 micrometres for the biggest deep output cells.

A wide-field vertical cell spreads its dendrites 300 to 500 micrometres across, wide enough to cover a large patch of the visual field on its own.

Each colliculus is about 10 millimetres across and measures roughly 200 cubic millimetres by magnetic resonance imaging, with reported means of 195 to 218 cubic millimetres per side.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Wide-field vertical cell, superficial, which reports looming, the rapid expansion of an approaching object, and projects to the pulvinar of the thalamus.

• Narrow-field vertical cell, superficial, which reports small moving features and projects to the lateral geniculate nucleus, the thalamic relay for vision.

• Horizontal and stellate cells, superficial, mostly inhibitory, which sharpen the visual map.

• Saccade burst neuron, intermediate layers, silent until a movement is committed and then firing furiously.

• Buildup neuron, intermediate and deep layers, whose firing climbs slowly from the moment a target appears until the eyes move.

• Fixation neuron, at the front tip of the map, firing steadily while the eyes hold still and pausing during a movement.

• Tectospinal and tectoreticular projection neurons, deep, which turn the head.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It occupies the upper half of the tectum, the roof of the midbrain, directly above the cerebral aqueduct and immediately in front of the inferior colliculus.

Its interfaces are important: it borders the pretectum in front, which handles pupil reflexes, the periaqueductal grey below, which handles defensive behaviour, and it receives retinal fibres through the brachium of the superior colliculus, a surface bundle arriving from the optic tract.

NUMBER:

There is no published unbiased count of human superior colliculus neurons, which is a real gap.

Multiplying the measured volume of roughly 200 cubic millimetres per side by the neuron density measured in the neighbouring human inferior colliculus, about 19,000 per cubic millimetre, gives an order-of-magnitude estimate of 2 to 4 million neurons per side, with low confidence.

That makes 4 to 8 million for the pair, which is 2 to 4 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons of the brainstem and about 0.008 percent of the 86 billion neurons of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Superficial visual cells answer a flash after 40 to 70 milliseconds with a brief burst.

Saccade burst neurons in the deep layers reach the highest firing rates in the midbrain: peaks near 750 spikes per second for small movements, falling to about 550 spikes per second for large ones, because bigger movements are coded by spreading the burst over more cells for longer rather than by firing harder.

Fixation neurons hold a steady 50 to 100 spikes per second and fall silent for the duration of a movement.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from the retina, with roughly one in ten of the million or so output fibres of each eye branching here, from layer five of the primary visual cortex, from the frontal eye field and parietal cortex, from the auditory and somatosensory systems into the deep layers, and, crucially, from the substantia nigra pars reticulata, which pours steady GABA onto the deep layers and holds the whole map switched off until it is allowed to fire.

Outputs go to the pulvinar and the lateral geniculate nucleus of the thalamus, to the paramedian pontine reticular formation for sideways eye movements, to the rostral interstitial nucleus for up and down eye movements, to the cervical spinal cord through the tectospinal tract, and to the opposite colliculus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Output axons are 1 to 4 micrometres thick and insulated, conducting at 10 to 40 metres per second.

Tectospinal axons cross the midline in the dorsal tegmental decussation immediately below the nucleus, so a target in the left visual field is turned toward by muscles driven from the right side of the brainstem.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Projection neurons release glutamate.

Roughly a third of collicular neurons are inhibitory and release GABA, and the deep layers additionally receive powerful GABA from the substantia nigra.

Acetylcholine from the parabigeminal and pedunculopontine nuclei arrives in patches in the intermediate layers and turns steady input into an explosive burst, and nitric oxide, a gas that drifts between cells, is made here in quantity.

MYELINATION:

The two white sheets, stratum opticum and the album layers, are made of insulated fibres running horizontally, which is exactly why they appear pale.

Local connections inside a grey sheet are largely bare, because they only travel a fraction of a millimetre.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The superficial layers are among the most metabolically active tissue in the midbrain, marked by dense cytochrome oxidase, an enzyme of energy production, because the retina never stops sending.

Deep layers are cheap at rest and extremely expensive for the 50 milliseconds of a burst.

PLASTICITY:

The alignment of the visual, auditory and touch maps onto one another is learned during development and can be re-learned; barn owls fitted with prisms famously shift their auditory map to match the displaced visual one.

In adults, saccade adaptation retunes the map within a few hundred trials when the eyes consistently land short of a target.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes in the superficial layers are arranged in vertical palisades that run in the same direction as the retinal input columns, so each little patch of visual space has its own glial compartment, which limits the sideways spread of glutamate and keeps neighbouring points of the map from blurring into each other.

This columnar glial arrangement is not seen in most brainstem nuclei, where astrocytes are arranged without a preferred direction.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the midbrain roof stops being a decision-maker and becomes a wire.

You would lose express saccades, the very fast eye movements made in under 100 milliseconds, lose the automatic flinch and freeze to a looming shadow, and lose blindsight entirely.

Vision would still work, but nothing would ever grab you.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The wide-field vertical cell performs a genuine dendritic computation: because its umbrella of dendrites is excited by many small motion detectors at once, an expanding shadow sums across the whole arbor while a small moving dot does not, so looming is detected by the shape of the dendritic tree rather than by any synaptic weight.

Recurrent excitation among neighbouring deep-layer cells, combined with long-range inhibition, makes the map a winner-take-all device that physically cannot represent two saccade targets at once.

Superficial cells are coupled by gap junctions, direct electrical pores between cells, during development; nitric oxide acts as a diffusing retrograde messenger; and the nigral GABA input works as a brake that is released rather than a signal that is added.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Retinal ganglion cell input to superficial layers, glutamate, 0 to 200 spikes per second, retinotopically mapped.

◦ Cortical input from primary visual cortex and frontal eye field, glutamate, 0 to 100 spikes per second.

◦ Nigral brake, GABA, tonic 40 to 100 spikes per second, falling to near zero 60 to 100 milliseconds before a movement.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Burst command to the paramedian pontine reticular formation and the rostral interstitial nucleus, encoded as position on the map, not as rate.

◦ Ascending copy to pulvinar and lateral geniculate nucleus.

• STATE.

◦ Activity value at each of roughly 40 by 40 map sites, brake level, elapsed time since target onset, refractory flag after a movement.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Self-excitation 1.6, near-neighbour excitation 0.9, long-range inhibition -0.35 divided by map size, burst threshold 0.8 normalised units.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 10 milliseconds, visual latency 40 to 70 milliseconds, brake release 60 to 100 milliseconds, burst duration 20 to 100 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In progressive supranuclear palsy the collicular and midbrain saccade machinery degenerates, and up and down eye movements slow and then fail while the eyes can still be moved passively, which is why the disorder is called supranuclear.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to pick the single strongest target on the collicular map and to release a saccade burst once the basal ganglia brake is lifted.
Create a list variable called map activity with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called strongest with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called winning site with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called burst released with initial value false.
For each site in the visual scene, perform the following operations.
    Add the sensory strength of that site to the end of map activity.
End the loop.
For each time step in the trial, perform the following operations.
    Set strongest to 0.
    For each site in map activity, perform the following operations.
        Calculate the product of the activity at that site and 1.6.
        Calculate the difference of the result and the nigral brake at this time step.
        Set the activity at that site to the result.
        Check if the activity at that site is less than 0.
        If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set the activity at that site to 0.
        End the conditional block.
        Check if the activity at that site is greater than strongest.
        If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set strongest to the activity at that site.
            Set winning site to the position of that site.
        End the conditional block.
    End the loop.
    Check if strongest is greater than 0.8.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set burst released to true.
        Display winning site to the console.
        Break out of the current loop immediately.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python, chosen because the whole point is a map competing with itself, and a
# plain list plus one loop shows that competition without hiding it in a library.
N = 40                  # sites along one axis of the motor map
DT = 0.001              # 1 ms time step
TAU = 0.010             # 10 ms membrane time constant
SELF, NEAR, FAR = 1.6, 0.9, -0.35
THRESH = 0.8            # burst threshold in normalised activity units

def step(act, visual, brake):
    new = list(act)
    for i in range(N):
        recurrent = SELF * act[i]
        if i > 0:
            recurrent += NEAR * act[i - 1]
        if i < N - 1:
            recurrent += NEAR * act[i + 1]
        for j in range(N):
            if abs(i - j) > 3:              # distant sites suppress each other
                recurrent += FAR * act[j] / N
        drive = visual[i] + recurrent - brake
        if drive < 0.0:
            drive = 0.0
        new[i] = act[i] + (drive - act[i]) * DT / TAU
    return new

def run(visual, brake_profile, steps):
    act = [0.0] * N
    for t in range(steps):
        act = step(act, visual, brake_profile[t])
        peak = max(act)
        if peak > THRESH:
            return act.index(peak), t * DT   # winning site and saccade latency
    return None, None

if __name__ == "__main__":
    vis = [0.0] * N
    vis[12] = 0.9                            # the target
    vis[27] = 0.7                            # a competing distractor
    brake = [1.0] * 60 + [0.15] * 240        # nigra releases the map at 60 ms
    print(run(vis, brake, 300))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

This is the only place in the human brain where a map of the sensory world and a map of the movement that answers it are stacked in exact register, one directly above the other, joined by single cells whose dendrites reach from one map into the other.

Nowhere else does a neuron fire at 750 spikes per second and then say nothing for a second.

CHAPTER 155. INFERIOR COLLICULUS NEURONS (Sound-Gathering Relay Neuron) (COG)

Almost every sound that ever reaches your awareness passes through this one nucleus, and almost no ascending hearing fibre is allowed to skip it.

Inferior colliculus is an address naming a small hill on the roof of the midbrain.

Sound names the sense served, because almost every ascending pathway from the ear passes through this station on its way to the thalamus and cortex, making it the obligatory crossroads of hearing.

Gathering names the work, which is bringing together streams that were computed separately lower down, such as timing and loudness differences between the two ears, so that the location and identity of a sound can be represented in one place.

Relay names the honest limit of what these cells do, since they pass the result upward rather than terminating the computation.

Neuron marks it as a cell of a structure whose internal division of labour is visible in the shapes, about seventy-five percent of those in the central core being flat, disc-shaped cells whose branches lie within a single frequency sheet while star-shaped cells spread across sheets and so combine information across pitches.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The inferior colliculus is the pair of mounds on the lower half of the midbrain roof, sitting just behind the superior colliculus, and it is the compulsory tollgate of hearing.

Eight or more separate brainstem hearing nuclei, each of which has already computed something different about a sound, send their answers here, and these neurons combine them.

They compute where a sound is, by comparing the microsecond-scale difference in arrival time between the two ears, how a sound is changing, by tuning to the rhythm of its loudness fluctuations, and how long it lasted, by cells that fire only for sounds of one particular duration.

The result is passed upward to the thalamus and cortex, and sideways to the superior colliculus so that the eyes can turn toward a noise.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The core is the central nucleus, and it is built like a stack of pages.

Each page, called a fibrodendritic lamina, is a flat sheet made of incoming insulated fibres running side by side with the flattened dendrites of the resident cells, and every sheet is tuned to one sound frequency, with low notes at the back and high notes at the front.

Disc-shaped neurons keep their dendrites inside one page, so they hear one frequency purely.

Stellate neurons spread their dendrites across several pages, so they hear across frequencies and can detect a chord or a voice.

SIZE:

Cell bodies measure 10 to 30 micrometres.

A disc-shaped cell's dendritic field is a flattened ellipse, only 50 to 70 micrometres thick but 300 to 500 micrometres wide, which is what makes the laminar stack visible under a microscope.

The human central nucleus occupies about 22.4 cubic millimetres per side.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Disc-shaped glutamatergic principal neuron, the majority cell of the central nucleus, projecting to the thalamus.

• Disc-shaped GABAergic neuron, inhibitory, sharpening frequency tuning within a page.

• Stellate glutamatergic neuron, integrating across pages.

• Stellate GABAergic neuron containing neuropeptide Y, a peptide that damps excitability; between 38 and 50 percent of the inhibitory cells of the central nucleus are of this across-page kind.

• Large GABAergic neuron wrapped in a dense coat of excitatory terminals and a perineuronal net, built for speed.

• Neurons of the dorsal cortex and lateral cortex, which handle descending cortical feedback and touch input respectively.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It sits in the caudal tectum of the midbrain, below and behind the superior colliculus, wrapped around the cerebral aqueduct, with the superior cerebellar peduncle passing underneath.

Two interfaces matter.

The brachium of the inferior colliculus leaves its surface to reach the medial geniculate body of the thalamus, and the lateral cortex of the nucleus is a genuine hearing-and-touch interface, receiving fibres from the dorsal column nuclei and the trigeminal system, which is why clenching your jaw or pressing your neck can change the pitch of tinnitus.

NUMBER:

The human central nucleus holds more than 420,000 neurons per side, measured by unbiased stereology, and confidence in that figure is good.

Adding the dorsal and lateral cortices, which together are several times the central nucleus in volume but less densely packed, the whole inferior colliculus plausibly holds 800,000 to 1.2 million neurons per side, with moderate confidence.

The pair therefore holds roughly 2 million neurons, about 1 percent of the brainstem's 200 million, and about 0.002 percent of the 86 billion neurons of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These cells show a whole catalogue of firing patterns, and the pattern is the computation: onset cells fire once at the start of a sound, sustained cells fire throughout, pauser cells fire, stop and restart, buildup cells ramp, and rebound cells fire only when inhibition ends.

Rebound depends on the T-type calcium current, a channel that only opens after the cell has been held down.

Firing rates range from silence to about 300 spikes per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory input arrives from the opposite cochlear nucleus, from both superior olivary nuclei, and, in a very large descending projection, from the auditory cortex.

Inhibitory input arrives as glycine from the medial nucleus of the trapezoid body and the lateral superior olive, and as GABA from the dorsal nucleus of the lateral lemniscus, one of the biggest single inhibitory inputs in the brain.

Outputs run to the medial geniculate body, to the deep superior colliculus, to the opposite inferior colliculus through its own commissure, to the pontine nuclei, and to the periaqueductal grey, which is how a sudden loud noise can make you freeze.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Ascending axons are 1 to 3 micrometres thick, insulated, conducting at 5 to 20 metres per second, and they collect into the brachium on the surface of the midbrain.

Local axons stay inside one lamina, so inhibition is delivered within a frequency page rather than across pages.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate for the projecting cells.

About 30 percent of the cells of the central nucleus are GABAergic.

Glycine is received rather than made here, acetylcholine arrives from the pedunculopontine and laterodorsal tegmental nuclei, and serotonin from the raphe nuclei changes how strongly the nucleus responds depending on behavioural state.

MYELINATION:

Myelin defines the anatomy here, because the laminae are made of bundles of insulated afferent fibres interleaved with dendrites, so a myelin stain reveals the frequency map directly.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

In rats measured with radioactive glucose, the inferior colliculus has the highest resting glucose consumption of any grey structure in the brain, higher than cortex, because its cells fire constantly and precisely even in silence.

That appetite makes it one of the first structures damaged when oxygen or thiamine runs short.

PLASTICITY:

When hearing is lost at the ear, this nucleus turns up its own gain: GABA levels and inhibitory strength fall over weeks, excitatory terminals lengthen, and the tissue becomes hyperexcitable.

That compensation is currently the leading explanation for tinnitus, a phantom ringing, and hyperacusis, in which ordinary sounds become painful.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here carry an unusually high density of glutamate transporters, the pumps that clear the transmitter from the synapse, because a cell that must resolve arrival-time differences of tens of microseconds cannot tolerate transmitter lingering.

Many large inhibitory neurons are wrapped in perineuronal nets, lattices of sugar-protein made by glia that stiffen the cell surface, speed up its electrical behaviour and lock its connections in place.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take these cells away and the midbrain still passes sound upward, but the two ears never meet.

You would hear a bang and have no idea where it came from, no startle, no automatic turn of the eyes and head, and no sense of rhythm in speech, because the tuning to loudness fluctuation is built here.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Duration tuning is a real computation performed by timing rather than weighting: inhibition arrives with the sound, a slow excitation arrives at a fixed delay after the sound starts, and a fast excitation arrives when the sound stops, so only a sound of the right length makes the two excitations collide.

Stellate cells integrate across laminae, so their dendrites act as a chord detector.

The descending projection from cortex is not feedback in the engineering sense but a gain and map controller that can shift frequency tuning within minutes, serotonin and nitric oxide act by volume transmission, spreading through the tissue rather than crossing single synapses, and developing collicular cells are electrically coupled by gap junctions.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Contralateral cochlear nucleus, glutamate, 0 to 400 spikes per second, frequency-labelled.

◦ Medial and lateral superior olive, carrying interaural time differences of 0 to 700 microseconds and level differences of 0 to 30 decibels.

◦ Glycinergic and GABAergic inhibition from the trapezoid body and the lateral lemniscus, leading the excitation by 0.5 to 2 milliseconds.

◦ Descending auditory cortex, glutamate, slow gain control.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Spike trains to the medial geniculate body, the deep superior colliculus and the periaqueductal grey.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, T-type calcium channel availability, inhibitory conductance, adaptation level, time since sound onset and offset.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Best frequency 20 hertz to 20 kilohertz, best duration 2 to 40 milliseconds, onset excitation delay about 10 milliseconds, coincidence window about 3 milliseconds, threshold 2.5 arbitrary units.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 1 to 3 milliseconds, inhibition 3 to 10 milliseconds, rebound 20 to 50 milliseconds, gain change hours to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss of inhibition after cochlear damage produces runaway central gain, and the result is tinnitus and hyperacusis; in some inherited conditions the same hyperexcitability produces sound-triggered seizures.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to make an auditory midbrain neuron fire only for a sound of about ten milliseconds, by testing whether a slow onset excitation and a fast offset excitation arrive together.
Create a number variable called onset time with initial value 50.
Create a number variable called duration with initial value 10.
Create a number variable called offset time with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called slow excitation time with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called fast excitation time with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called gap with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called cell fires with initial value false.
Calculate the sum of onset time and duration.
Set offset time to the result.
Calculate the sum of onset time and 10.
Set slow excitation time to the result.
Calculate the sum of offset time and 1.
Set fast excitation time to the result.
Calculate the difference of slow excitation time and fast excitation time.
Set gap to the result.
Check if gap is less than 0.
If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
    Calculate the product of gap and -1.
    Set gap to the result.
End the conditional block.
Check if gap is less than 3.
If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set cell fires to true.
End the conditional block.
Check if offset time is greater than slow excitation time.
If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set cell fires to false.
End the conditional block.
Display cell fires to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python, chosen because this cell is about milliseconds rather than megaflops,
# and an explicit time loop makes the coincidence visible line by line.
DT = 0.0001           # 0.1 ms steps, because the answer depends on timing
ONSET_DELAY = 0.010   # slow excitation arrives 10 ms after sound onset
WIDTH = 0.003         # each excitation lasts 3 ms
TAU = 0.002           # 2 ms membrane time constant
GAIN = 3.0
THRESHOLD = 2.5       # set above what one excitation alone can reach

def duration_tuned(sound_on, sound_off, total=0.120):
    v = 0.0
    fired_at = None
    for k in range(int(total / DT)):
        t = k * DT
        exc, inh = 0.0, 0.0
        if sound_on + 0.001 <= t <= sound_off + 0.001:
            inh = 1.0                          # glycine, present with the sound
        if sound_on + ONSET_DELAY <= t <= sound_on + ONSET_DELAY + WIDTH:
            exc += 0.7                         # slow onset excitation
        if sound_off + 0.001 <= t <= sound_off + 0.001 + WIDTH:
            exc += 0.7                         # fast offset excitation
        v += (-v + GAIN * (exc - inh)) * DT / TAU
        if v > THRESHOLD and fired_at is None:
            fired_at = t
    return fired_at

if __name__ == "__main__":
    for ms in (2, 5, 8, 10, 12, 20, 40):
        out = duration_tuned(0.050, 0.050 + ms / 1000.0)
        print(ms, "ms sound ->", "spike" if out else "silent")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Nowhere else in the brain is a sense laid out as a physical stack of pages you can see in a myelin stain, one frequency per page.

And no other nucleus is quite so obligatory: destroy it on both sides and a person with perfectly healthy ears becomes deaf.

CHAPTER 156. VESTIBULAR NUCLEI NEURONS (Head-Motion Balance Neuron) (COG)

Lie perfectly still and these cells are still firing about thirty times a second, because in this system stillness is not silence, it is a number.

The old chapter label is a plural address on an anatomical chart and says nothing about why losing these cells makes the world appear to swing.

Head-Motion names the input, because these medullary and pontine cells receive the nerve from the inner ear organs that report rotation and tilt of the skull, and they encode the speed at which the head is turning.

Balance names the two faculties they support, keeping the eyes still on the world while the head moves, and keeping the body upright by sending commands down the spinal cord to neck and trunk muscles.

Neuron marks it as part of the fastest reflex arc in the brain, since the eye arm of this system uses only three cells in series and compensatory eye movements lag head movements by about five to six milliseconds in primates.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The vestibular nuclei are four grey masses on each side of the upper medulla and lower pons, and they are the brain's balance exchange.

They receive the raw signals from the inner ear organs that sense head rotation and head tilt, and turn them into commands for the eyes, the neck, the spine, the gut and the cortex.

Their most famous job is the vestibulo-ocular reflex, which rotates your eyes exactly opposite to your head so the world stays still, in a circuit of only three cells and 5 to 7 milliseconds.

They also tell you which way is down, and they tell the stomach when the eyes and the inner ear disagree, which is where motion sickness begins.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The four nuclei are the superior nucleus of Bechterew, the lateral nucleus of Deiters, the medial nucleus of Schwalbe, and the inferior or descending nucleus.

Deiters' cells are giant multipolar neurons with thick radiating dendrites, among the largest in the brainstem, and their axons run all the way to the lumbar spinal cord.

Medial nucleus cells are medium, multipolar and densely packed, and they specialise in the eye reflex.

The whole complex lies immediately under the floor of the fourth ventricle, so a thin sheet of ependymal cells is all that separates these neurons from cerebrospinal fluid.

SIZE:

Deiters' giant cells have somas 50 to 80 micrometres across.

Medial and superior nucleus cells are 15 to 35 micrometres.

The complex as a whole is roughly 10 millimetres long from top to bottom on each side.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Type A neuron, with a broad action potential and a single deep after-hyperpolarisation, carrying an A-type potassium current that makes it slow to speed up.

• Type B neuron, with a narrow spike and a double after-hyperpolarisation, faster, more sensitive and more easily driven into bursts.

• Secondary vestibulo-ocular neuron of the medial and superior nuclei, the middle cell of the three-cell reflex arc.

• Vestibulospinal neuron of Deiters' nucleus, driving the anti-gravity muscles of the trunk and legs.

• Commissural inhibitory neuron, GABAergic or glycinergic, crossing to silence the opposite side.

• Vestibular-only neuron, which reports head motion but falls silent when you move your own head, because it subtracts an internal prediction.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The complex occupies the floor of the fourth ventricle in the rostral medulla and caudal pons, forming the vestibular area you can see on the surface of the brainstem.

It is a triple interface: the eighth cranial nerve enters it from the ear, the cerebellum reaches it directly through the juxtarestiform body, and it merges without a sharp border into the pontomedullary reticular formation.

Immediately next to it lies the nucleus prepositus hypoglossi, the eye-position integrator, and the two work as one functional unit.

NUMBER:

The medial vestibular nucleus, the largest of the four, holds about 151,000 neurons per side, measured by unbiased stereology in human brainstems, and confidence is good.

That count falls by roughly 3 percent per decade from age 40, an 18 percent loss across a normal lifespan, and loss occurs in the descending, medial and lateral nuclei but not in the superior nucleus.

Adding the other three nuclei gives a defensible range of 300,000 to 450,000 neurons per side, so roughly 0.6 to 0.9 million for the pair, about 0.4 percent of the brainstem's 200 million and about 0.001 percent of the nervous system's 86 billion.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These are pacemakers: cut off every input and they still fire regularly, at 5 to 35 spikes per second in a slice and faster in a living animal, driven by a persistent sodium current and the Ih current.

That standing rate is the zero point of the system, because a head turn one way speeds the cell up and a turn the other way slows it down, with a sensitivity near 0.4 to 1.0 extra spikes per second for each degree per second of head rotation.

Because the rate cannot fall below zero, a very fast head turn saturates the silent side, and the brain must rely on the excited side.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from the vestibular nerve, whose cell bodies sit in Scarpa's ganglion in the temporal bone and number on the order of 20,000 fibres per side, from Purkinje cells of the cerebellar flocculus, nodulus, uvula and anterior vermis, which are inhibitory, from neck muscle and joint receptors, from vision through the accessory optic system, and from the opposite vestibular nuclei.

Outputs go through the medial longitudinal fasciculus to the abducens, trochlear and oculomotor nuclei, down the lateral and medial vestibulospinal tracts, up to the thalamus and the parieto-insular vestibular cortex, and into the nucleus of the solitary tract and the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus, the autonomic route that turns a rough sea into nausea.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Vestibulo-ocular axons are 3 to 8 micrometres thick and heavily insulated, conducting at 30 to 70 metres per second, because the reflex must beat the head.

Lateral vestibulospinal axons are the longest of the group, reaching the lower back, and they run uncrossed.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate for the excitatory projection cells.

GABA and glycine for commissural cells and for cerebellar input.

Histamine from the tuberomammillary nucleus of the hypothalamus and acetylcholine both modulate the nuclei strongly, which is the reason antihistamines such as cinnarizine and the muscarinic blocker scopolamine are the standard drugs for vertigo and seasickness.

MYELINATION:

The medial longitudinal fasciculus is one of the first tracts in the human brain to be insulated, beginning before birth, because a newborn needs to stabilise gaze immediately.

Sheaths here are thick and internodes long.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

A neuron that never stops firing never stops spending, and these cells run a continuous energy bill.

That is why they are among the first to fail in thiamine deficiency, in which the earliest sign of Wernicke encephalopathy is often nystagmus, an involuntary jerking of the eyes.

PLASTICITY:

Vestibular compensation is one of the most striking recoveries in neurology: after one inner ear is destroyed, the deafferented nuclei are silent and the patient has violent vertigo, yet within two to three days the resting firing rate returns almost to normal and within weeks the person can walk normally.

Reflex gain is also continuously recalibrated by the cerebellum, which is why new spectacles feel wrong for a day and then feel right.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here are active participants in recovery rather than bystanders: after the nerve on one side is cut they change their glutamate and GABA transporter levels within hours, which is one of the mechanisms that restores the resting firing rate, and blocking astrocyte metabolism delays compensation.

Microglia proliferate in the deafferented nucleus within a day, and the tissue briefly becomes a small controlled inflammatory site.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the medulla loses its gravity reference.

The world would jump with every footstep, a symptom called oscillopsia, reading a sign while walking would be impossible, standing would require constant vision, and the brainstem would lose the automatic link between head motion and blood pressure that keeps you from fainting when you stand.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Three unusual things happen here.

First, endogenous pacemaking means the cell computes with a bidirectional rate code around a standing baseline, so inhibition carries as much information as excitation.

Second, the vestibular-only cells perform an explicit subtraction: a copy of the motor command for a self-generated head turn is subtracted from the sensed head turn, so these cells report only what the world did to you and not what you did to yourself.

Third, the two sides are wired in push-pull through commissural inhibition, so the pair behaves as a single differential amplifier, and this is exactly why losing one ear produces a violent spinning sensation rather than a simple gap.

Add to that histaminergic volume transmission, gap-junction coupling in young animals, and a direct line into autonomic control.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Vestibular nerve afferents, glutamate, resting rate about 90 spikes per second, modulated by head angular velocity in degrees per second.

◦ Purkinje cell inhibition from the cerebellum, GABA, 20 to 100 spikes per second.

◦ Commissural inhibition from the opposite complex, GABA and glycine.

◦ Efference copy of self-generated head movement, subtractive.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Firing rate to the abducens nucleus and other eye muscle nuclei, 0 to 300 spikes per second.

◦ Firing rate to spinal motor neurons and to the solitary nucleus for autonomic effects.

• STATE.

◦ Canal adaptation state, current firing rate, gain of the efference copy subtraction, compensation level after injury.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting rate about 30 spikes per second, sensitivity 0.6 spikes per second per degree per second, ceiling about 300 spikes per second, floor 0.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Reflex latency 5 to 7 milliseconds, semicircular canal cupula time constant about 5.7 seconds, velocity storage 15 to 20 seconds, compensation 2 days to several weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Sudden loss of input on one side, as in vestibular neuritis or a Wallenberg stroke, unbalances the push-pull pair and produces spinning vertigo, one-directional nystagmus, vomiting and a fall toward the damaged side.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn a head rotation into an eye command while ignoring the part of the rotation the person produced themselves.
Create a number variable called resting rate with initial value 30.
Create a number variable called sensitivity with initial value 0.6.
Create a number variable called canal state with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called sensed velocity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called predicted velocity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 30.
For each time step in the head movement, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of the head velocity at this time step and canal state.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.000175.
    Calculate the sum of canal state and the result.
    Set canal state to the result.
    Calculate the difference of the head velocity at this time step and canal state.
    Set sensed velocity to the result.
    Calculate the product of the motor command at this time step and 0.9.
    Set predicted velocity to the result.
    Calculate the difference of sensed velocity and predicted velocity.
    Calculate the product of the result and sensitivity.
    Calculate the sum of resting rate and the result.
    Set firing rate to the result.
    Check if firing rate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if firing rate is greater than 300.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 300.
    End the conditional block.
    Display firing rate to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C, chosen because a vestibulo-ocular reflex is a hard real time controller and
   this is the language such controllers are actually written in. */
#include <stdio.h>

#define DT 0.001            /* one millisecond time step */
#define TAU_CUPULA 5.7      /* semicircular canal leak, seconds */
#define REST_RATE 30.0      /* spikes per second with the head still */
#define SENSITIVITY 0.6     /* spikes per second per degree per second */
#define MAX_RATE 300.0

double vestibular_neuron(double head_velocity, double motor_copy,
                         double *leak_state, double copy_gain)
{
    double sensed, drive, rate;
    /* the canal is a high pass filter: a steady turn fades away in seconds */
    *leak_state += (head_velocity - *leak_state) * DT / TAU_CUPULA;
    sensed = head_velocity - *leak_state;
    /* subtract the brain's own prediction of a self generated head turn */
    drive = sensed - motor_copy * copy_gain;
    rate = REST_RATE + SENSITIVITY * drive;
    if (rate < 0.0) rate = 0.0;
    if (rate > MAX_RATE) rate = MAX_RATE;
    return rate;
}

int main(void)
{
    double leak = 0.0;
    int k;
    for (k = 0; k < 400; k++) {
        double t = k * DT;
        double pushed = (t > 0.05 && t < 0.25) ? 100.0 : 0.0;  /* shoved by someone */
        double self_turn = 0.0;                                 /* not self generated */
        double rate = vestibular_neuron(pushed, self_turn, &leak, 0.9);
        if (k % 50 == 0) printf("t=%.3f s  rate=%.1f spikes/s\n", t, rate);
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

This is the neuron whose resting state is not zero but thirty spikes a second, and whose two sides are wired as opposites, so that the difference between them, not the value of either, is the signal.

No other cell in the human brainstem is simultaneously a pacemaker, a subtractor of your own movements, and a direct line to the vomiting circuit.

CHAPTER 157. BODY-ALARM BROADCAST NEURON (COG) (PUC)

A wasp sting, a mouthful of bad oyster, a swelling throat and a lungful of stale air are four completely different disasters, and one small knot of cells in the upper pons shouts the same word about all of them.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is a previously unnamed construct, and the name is given here for the cell known in the laboratory literature as the calcitonin gene-related peptide neuron of the external lateral parabrachial nucleus.

Calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) is a small chain of amino acids used as a slow chemical messenger, and these cells are the only population in the brainstem that uses it as a general alarm.

Their job is not to say what is wrong or where, but that something is wrong enough to stop everything, and they broadcast that message to the amygdala, the brain's threat centre, and to its neighbours.

They are the cell that makes pain feel urgent rather than merely located, and they are necessary for learning to hate a food that once made you ill.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

They are small fusiform and multipolar neurons packed into a thin shell of tissue that caps the superior cerebellar peduncle, the cerebellum's exit cable, where it passes through the upper pons.

Their dendrites stay short and local, which matters, because it means these cells sample a small, tightly curated set of inputs rather than listening to the whole brainstem.

Their axons are thin and enormously branched at the far end, so a handful of cells can flood a large forebrain territory.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 10 to 18 micrometres across, which is small.

The external lateral subnucleus that holds them is a shell a fraction of a millimetre thick and a few millimetres long in a human brainstem, so the entire alarm system occupies less tissue than a grain of rice.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Outer external lateral cell, driven mainly by tissue injury signals arriving from the spinal cord.

• Inner external lateral cell, driven mainly by rising carbon dioxide and by signals from the gut.

• Amygdala-projecting cell, the largest group, ending in the capsular part of the central nucleus of the amygdala.

• Bed nucleus projecting cell, driving sustained anxiety rather than acute fear.

• Cells co-expressing substance P and pituitary adenylate cyclase-activating polypeptide, two further slow messengers, which prolong the alarm.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the dorsolateral pons at the junction with the midbrain, in the external lateral subnucleus of the parabrachial complex, wrapped around the superior cerebellar peduncle.

This is a true interface: the parabrachial complex is the single relay where the body's internal reports, carried up from the nucleus of the solitary tract, meet the pain reports carried up from lamina one of the spinal cord and the trigeminal system, and are handed together to the forebrain.

Its immediate neighbours are the locus coeruleus and the Koelliker-Fuse nucleus, which is why alarm, arousal and breathing change together.

NUMBER:

No one has counted these cells in a human brain, and that should be said plainly.

In a mouse the population is on the order of several hundred to about two thousand per side.

Scaling by brainstem size and allowing generously for uncertainty, a defensible human bound is 5,000 to 20,000 per side, certainly fewer than 50,000, so under 0.02 percent of the brainstem's 200 million neurons and around 0.00003 percent of the nervous system's 86 billion.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

At rest they are nearly silent, firing 0.5 to 3 spikes per second.

When a threat arrives they switch into long bursts of 10 to 40 spikes per second lasting seconds to tens of seconds, and they encode how bad the threat is in the length and height of that burst rather than in which cell fires.

This matters for simulation: a peptide is only released when firing exceeds roughly 5 to 10 spikes per second, so low-level activity is invisible to the downstream target and the cell behaves as a thresholded broadcaster.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from lamina one of the spinal dorsal horn, the thin surface sheet that carries pain, itch, temperature and tissue damage, from the spinal trigeminal nucleus carrying face and dura pain, from the nucleus of the solitary tract carrying taste, gut and airway signals, from the area postrema carrying blood-borne poison signals, and from central chemoreceptors reporting carbon dioxide.

Descending inputs from the insular cortex and the central amygdala allow context to raise or lower the alarm.

Outputs go to the capsular central amygdala, the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis, the paraventricular thalamus, the substantia innominata and the parvicellular ventral posterior thalamus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are thin, 0.5 to 1.5 micrometres, largely bare of insulation, conducting at only 0.5 to 2 metres per second, and they ascend in the ventral amygdalofugal pathway.

The alarm therefore arrives late, tens of milliseconds after the fast pain pathway to cortex, but it lasts far longer, which is exactly the right design for a message that means keep away from this for the rest of your life.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate carries the fast signal.

CGRP is the slow signal, released from dense-core vesicles only during bursts, acting on a two-part receptor made of calcitonin receptor-like receptor and receptor activity-modifying protein one, which raises cyclic adenosine monophosphate inside the target cell for seconds to minutes.

Substance P and pituitary adenylate cyclase-activating polypeptide are co-released, and mu-opioid receptors on these cells are the reason morphine takes the horror out of pain while leaving the sensation.

MYELINATION:

Insulation is sparse to absent on the ascending axons.

That is not a defect: a peptide broadcast does not need speed, it needs reach and duration, and thin bare fibres are cheap to build and easy to branch.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The expensive part is not firing but manufacturing.

Peptides cannot be made at the terminal; they must be transcribed and packaged in the cell body and carried several centimetres to the axon endings, so a sustained alarm depletes the store and the cell needs hours of protein synthesis to recharge.

PLASTICITY:

The synapse from these cells onto the central amygdala strengthens after nerve injury or repeated migraine, so the same input produces a bigger alarm, which is one mechanism by which acute pain becomes chronic pain.

CGRP itself is now a proven drug target: antibodies and small molecules against CGRP and its receptor prevent migraine in humans, which is the strongest clinical evidence that this signalling system sets the volume of head pain.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

There is something genuinely special about the glia here, and it follows from chemistry.

Glutamate is removed from a synapse by astrocyte pumps in under a millisecond, but there is no reuptake pump for CGRP at all, so the peptide is cleared only by drifting away and by being cut up by enzymes sitting in the extracellular space and on glial membranes.

Astrocyte peptidase activity and the density of the surrounding matrix therefore set how far and how long the alarm spreads, making the glia here part of the signal rather than merely its housekeepers.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Silence these cells and the pons still relays taste, breathing and blood pressure perfectly well.

What the region loses is its exclamation mark: pain becomes information rather than emergency, a poisonous food is eaten again the next day because no aversion is learned, and a rising carbon dioxide level produces no panic.

The brainstem would keep the body alive and lose the reason to protect it.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Almost everything interesting about this cell is outside the classical model.

It uses volume transmission: CGRP leaves the synapse and diffuses tens of micrometres, so one cell influences a small cloud of amygdala neurons rather than a single partner.

Its release is frequency-dependent, which turns the cell into a switch rather than a dial, and because the receptor works through cyclic adenosine monophosphate the downstream effect outlasts the spikes by minutes.

It also participates in immune signalling, since CGRP modulates immune cells and inflammatory tone, and its terminals drive long-lasting synaptic strengthening in the amygdala, so a single episode can leave a permanent trace.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Spinal lamina one nociceptive channel, glutamate, 0 to 50 spikes per second, weight about 1.0.

◦ Gut and taste channel from the solitary nucleus, weight about 1.2.

◦ Carbon dioxide and airway channel, weight about 0.9, itch about 0.5, noxious cold about 0.4.

◦ Mu-opioid tone, 0 to 1, multiplicative suppression up to 90 percent.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Spike train plus a CGRP concentration signal to the capsular central amygdala and the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis.

• STATE.

◦ Accumulated threat evidence, current firing rate, peptide store, released peptide concentration in the target tissue.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Burst release threshold 8 spikes per second, maximum rate 40 spikes per second, release 0.02 peptide units per spike.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Evidence accumulation about 400 milliseconds, burst duration 1 to 30 seconds, peptide clearance about 30 seconds, receptor effect 1 to 5 minutes, store recovery hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Chronic over-activity of this pathway is implicated in migraine, in the affective misery of chronic pain, and in the persistent hypervigilance of post-traumatic stress; complete loss would remove learned food aversion and blunt fear learning.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to combine several kinds of bad news into one alarm and to release a peptide only when the alarm is loud enough.
Create a number variable called evidence with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called peptide level with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called raw signal with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called threat channels with initial value empty list.
Add the skin pain channel to the end of threat channels.
Add the gut toxin channel to the end of threat channels.
Add the airway carbon dioxide channel to the end of threat channels.
For each time step in the episode, perform the following operations.
    Set raw signal to 0.
    For each channel in threat channels, perform the following operations.
        Calculate the product of the value of that channel at this time step and the weight of that channel.
        Calculate the sum of raw signal and the result.
        Set raw signal to the result.
    End the loop.
    Calculate the difference of 1 and the opioid tone at this time step.
    Calculate the product of raw signal and the result.
    Set raw signal to the result.
    Calculate the difference of raw signal and evidence.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.025.
    Calculate the sum of evidence and the result.
    Set evidence to the result.
    Calculate the product of evidence and 12.
    Calculate the difference of the result and 1.
    Set firing rate to the result.
    Check if firing rate is greater than 8.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the product of firing rate and 0.0002.
        Calculate the sum of peptide level and the result.
        Set peptide level to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of peptide level and 0.99967.
    Set peptide level to the result.
End the loop.
Display peptide level to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python, chosen because this cell is bookkeeping rather than number crunching:
# weighted evidence in, a thresholded peptide broadcast out.
DT = 0.010                 # 10 ms steps; this cell works on the scale of seconds
TAU_ACC = 0.400            # threat evidence accumulates over about 400 ms
TAU_PEPTIDE = 30.0         # CGRP clears from the tissue over about 30 s
BURST_THRESHOLD = 8.0      # spikes per second needed to release peptide
MAX_RATE = 40.0
RELEASE_PER_SPIKE = 0.02

WEIGHTS = {"skin_pain": 1.0, "gut_toxin": 1.2, "airway_co2": 0.9,
           "itch": 0.5, "noxious_cold": 0.4}

def alarm_neuron(channels, opioid_tone, steps):
    evidence, peptide = 0.0, 0.0
    rates, peptides = [], []
    for t in range(steps):
        raw = sum(w * channels[name][t] for name, w in WEIGHTS.items())
        raw *= (1.0 - min(opioid_tone[t], 0.9))     # morphine clamps the alarm
        evidence += (raw - evidence) * DT / TAU_ACC
        rate = min(MAX_RATE, max(0.0, 12.0 * evidence - 1.0))
        if rate > BURST_THRESHOLD:                  # peptide needs a real burst
            peptide += RELEASE_PER_SPIKE * rate * DT
        peptide -= peptide * DT / TAU_PEPTIDE
        rates.append(rate)
        peptides.append(peptide)
    return rates, peptides

if __name__ == "__main__":
    n = 600                                          # six seconds
    traces = {name: [0.0] * n for name in WEIGHTS}
    for t in range(100, 160):                        # a toxin hits the gut
        traces["gut_toxin"][t] = 1.0
    quiet = [0.0] * n
    r, p = alarm_neuron(traces, quiet, n)
    print("peak rate %.1f Hz, peak CGRP %.3f units" % (max(r), max(p)))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

This is the only cell in the human nervous system that treats a burn, a poison, a blocked airway and an itch as literally the same message, and answers all four with the same chemical word.

Recognise it by the shell of tiny cells hugging the cerebellar peduncle, by the peptide it makes, and by the fact that blocking that peptide in the bloodstream stops migraines.

CHAPTER 158. POISON-WATCH NAUSEA NEURON (NON-COG) (PUC)

There is exactly one small patch of your brain where the blood is let in unfiltered, and it exists so that a mouthful of poison can be sent back the way it came.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is a previously unnamed construct, and the name is given here for the chemosensitive neuron of the area postrema, the structure clinicians call the chemoreceptor trigger zone.

Everywhere else in the brain, a dense wall of cells called the blood-brain barrier keeps circulating chemicals out.

Here the wall is deliberately left open, and these neurons sit bathed in whatever is in the blood, tasting it directly.

When they detect a substance that resembles a poison, they trigger nausea, then vomiting, then a durable dislike of whatever food you last ate, which is one of the fastest and most powerful forms of learning a human being can do.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The area postrema is a pair of small ridges bulging into the lower end of the fourth ventricle, just above the point where the ventricle narrows into the central canal.

Its neurons are small and simple, with short dendrites that reach into the spaces around leaky blood vessels rather than toward other neurons.

Around them sit sinusoidal fenestrated capillaries, meaning blood vessels with actual windows in their walls, together with tanycytes, long specialised glial cells whose tips carry brushes of microvilli into the cerebrospinal fluid.

So the cell is arranged like a taste bud rather than like a relay: it faces the fluid, not the wiring.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 8 to 15 micrometres across, among the smallest neurons in the brainstem.

Each ridge is roughly 2 millimetres long and well under a millimetre thick, so the entire poison-detecting organ of a human being is smaller than a match head.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Glucagon-like peptide one receptor neuron, excitatory, the cell activated by the modern weight-loss and diabetes drugs and by blood-borne bacterial products.

• Calcitonin receptor neuron, excitatory, overlapping with cells carrying GFRAL, the receptor for the stress hormone growth differentiation factor fifteen.

• GFRAL-carrying neuron, excitatory, the cell whose removal abolishes avoidance learning after several different visceral poisons.

• Inhibitory GABA neuron, the third population found by single-cell gene profiling, which appears to hold the nausea circuit in check.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It sits in the dorsomedial medulla at the caudal floor of the fourth ventricle, just in front of the obex, separated from the vagal triangle by a thin ridge called the funiculus separans.

Its interfaces are the whole point of it.

Above and outside it is blood, with no barrier; below it lies the nucleus of the solitary tract, especially the medial and commissural parts, and the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus; the barrier that the rest of the brain places at the capillary is instead placed at the boundary between the area postrema and the solitary nucleus.

NUMBER:

There is no unbiased count of human area postrema neurons, and the honest answer is that nobody knows.

From the organ's dimensions, well under one cubic millimetre of neural tissue per side, and typical small-neuron packing, a defensible bound is 3,000 to 20,000 neurons per side, with low confidence.

That is around 0.01 percent of the brainstem's 200 million neurons and roughly 0.00003 percent of the nervous system's 86 billion, making this one of the smallest populations in the entire book and one of the most consequential.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These cells fire slowly and steadily, about 0.5 to 5 spikes per second, and they are unusually easy to move.

Because they are small they have a high input resistance, on the order of 200 to 800 megaohms, so a current of a few trillionths of an amp, produced by a handful of receptor molecules binding a hormone, is enough to change the firing rate measurably.

They are also directly sensitive to blood glucose and to the saltiness of the blood, so the same cell reports several kinds of internal trouble.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The most important input is not a synapse at all: it is the concentration of chemicals in the blood, read by receptors on the cell surface.

Conventional synaptic input arrives from vagal afferents carrying reports from the stomach and intestine and from the solitary nucleus.

Outputs run a very short distance to the nucleus of the solitary tract and the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus, and a longer distance to the external lateral parabrachial nucleus, where they excite the body-alarm broadcast neuron described in the previous entry, which is how a chemical in the blood becomes a feeling of dread and a lifelong aversion.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are short, thin, 0.2 to 0.8 micrometres, and mostly uninsulated, travelling only a millimetre or two to the neighbouring solitary nucleus.

Conduction speed is under 1 metre per second and it does not matter, because poisoning is measured in minutes, not milliseconds.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Most projecting cells release glutamate, and a minority release GABA.

The receptors are the real story, and they are a pharmacology textbook in one cell: dopamine D2, serotonin 5-HT3, neurokinin one for substance P, glucagon-like peptide one, calcitonin, GFRAL and angiotensin two.

Every one of those is a drug target: ondansetron blocks 5-HT3, aprepitant blocks neurokinin one, metoclopramide and domperidone block D2, and semaglutide deliberately activates the glucagon-like peptide one receptor, which is precisely why it makes people feel sick.

MYELINATION:

Essentially none.

These axons are short and slow by design, and building insulation for a two-millimetre journey would be a waste of energy.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Firing is cheap, but exposure is expensive.

Living outside the blood-brain barrier means constant contact with circulating toxins, so these cells and their glia run a heavy load of efflux pumps and detoxifying enzymes, and they turn over surface receptors continuously.

PLASTICITY:

The clearest human example is tolerance: the nausea caused by a glucagon-like peptide one drug is worst in the first weeks and then fades, as receptor sensitivity is turned down, even though the dose goes up.

In the other direction, sensitisation of this pathway is thought to underlie anticipatory nausea, in which a chemotherapy patient begins to feel sick in the hospital car park.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here are unlike glia anywhere else, because their job is inverted.

Elsewhere astrocyte end-feet help build the blood-brain barrier at the capillary; here the capillary is deliberately leaky and the astrocytes instead form a tight boundary at the far edge of the organ, keeping blood-borne signals from flooding the rest of the medulla.

Tanycytes relay signals from the cerebrospinal fluid, and the astrocytes carry an exceptionally high density of aquaporin four, a water channel; that is why the autoimmune disease neuromyelitis optica, in which antibodies attack aquaporin four, so often begins with weeks of intractable vomiting and hiccups long before any eye or spinal symptom appears.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take these cells out and the medulla still swallows, breathes and controls the heart.

What it loses is its chemical sentry.

Chemotherapy would no longer cause vomiting, which sounds like a gift, but so would a plate of poisonous mushrooms, and the person would never learn to avoid them.

The region would lose the only sense organ the brain keeps inside itself.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell barely fits the classical model at all.

Its principal input is hormonal rather than synaptic, so the correct simulation primitive is receptor occupancy against blood concentration, not synaptic weight against presynaptic spikes.

It works by volume transmission in both directions, since local capillaries connect it to neighbouring tissue and its own signals spread through the extracellular space; it integrates over minutes and hours rather than milliseconds; it senses immune molecules such as interleukin six and bacterial cell-wall fragments, making it a genuine neuro-immune interface; and it reads the cerebrospinal fluid through tanycytes as a second sampling channel.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Blood concentrations in nanomolar of dopamine agonists, serotonin, substance P, glucagon-like peptide one and growth differentiation factor fifteen, converted to receptor occupancy.

◦ Vagal afferent spike trains from stomach and intestine, 0 to 30 spikes per second.

◦ Blood glucose and osmolarity as slow modulators.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Spike train to the solitary nucleus, the dorsal motor vagus and the external lateral parabrachial nucleus.

◦ A scalar nausea signal, defined as firing rate above threshold.

• STATE.

◦ Per-receptor sensitivity between 0 and 1, current firing rate, cumulative exposure, learned aversion flag.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Baseline 1.5 spikes per second, nausea threshold 6 spikes per second, half-maximal concentrations 0.5 to 60 nanomolar depending on receptor, drive weights 1.5 to 3.0.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Receptor binding seconds, firing change tens of seconds, nausea build-up minutes, desensitisation about 30 minutes to weeks, aversion learning permanent after one trial.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Autoimmune destruction produces area postrema syndrome, weeks of unstoppable nausea, vomiting and hiccups, which opens 7 to 10 percent of cases of aquaporin-four positive neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder; over-activation is the dose-limiting side effect of chemotherapy and of glucagon-like peptide one drugs.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:
