The purpose of this procedure is to read the concentration of several substances in the blood and decide whether the body should feel sick.
Create a number variable called baseline rate with initial value 1.5.
Create a number variable called nausea threshold with initial value 6.
Create a number variable called total drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called occupancy with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 1.5.
Create a boolean variable called nausea is present with initial value false.
Create a list variable called receptors with initial value empty list.
Add the dopamine receptor to the end of receptors.
Add the serotonin receptor to the end of receptors.
Add the glucagon-like peptide one receptor to the end of receptors.
For each minute in the observation period, perform the following operations.
    Set total drive to 0.
    For each receptor in receptors, perform the following operations.
        Calculate the sum of the blood concentration for that receptor and the half maximal concentration for that receptor.
        Calculate the quotient of the blood concentration for that receptor divided by the result.
        Set occupancy to the result.
        Calculate the product of occupancy and the weight of that receptor.
        Calculate the product of the result and the sensitivity of that receptor.
        Calculate the sum of total drive and the result.
        Set total drive to the result.
        Calculate the product of occupancy and 0.0004.
        Calculate the difference of the sensitivity of that receptor and the result.
        Set the sensitivity of that receptor to the result.
    End the loop.
    Calculate the product of total drive and 4.
    Calculate the sum of baseline rate and the result.
    Set firing rate to the result.
    Check if firing rate is greater than nausea threshold.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set nausea is present to true.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set nausea is present to false.
    End the conditional block.
    Display nausea is present to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python, chosen because the cell is a weighted sum of receptor occupancies and a
# dictionary keeps the pharmacology readable to a clinician and a coder alike.
DT = 1.0                    # one second steps; nausea builds over minutes
BASELINE_HZ = 1.5
NAUSEA_THRESHOLD_HZ = 6.0
TAU_DESENS = 1800.0         # receptors blunt over about 30 minutes

# receptor name: (half maximal concentration in nanomolar, drive weight)
RECEPTORS = {"D2_dopamine": (30.0, 1.8), "5HT3_serotonin": (60.0, 2.2),
             "NK1_substance_P": (20.0, 1.5), "GLP1R": (5.0, 2.6),
             "GFRAL_GDF15": (0.5, 3.0)}

def area_postrema_neuron(blood, blocked, minutes):
    sensitivity = {name: 1.0 for name in RECEPTORS}
    log = []
    for step in range(int(minutes * 60 / DT)):
        drive = 0.0
        for name, (half_nm, weight) in RECEPTORS.items():
            if name in blocked:
                continue                       # an antiemetic occupies this site
            conc = blood[name][step]
            occ = conc / (conc + half_nm)      # simple binding curve
            drive += weight * occ * sensitivity[name]
            target = 1.0 - 0.7 * occ           # steady exposure blunts the cell
            sensitivity[name] += (target - sensitivity[name]) * DT / TAU_DESENS
        rate = BASELINE_HZ + 4.0 * drive
        log.append((rate, rate > NAUSEA_THRESHOLD_HZ))
    return log

if __name__ == "__main__":
    mins = 60
    n = int(mins * 60)
    blood = {name: [0.0] * n for name in RECEPTORS}
    for t in range(n):
        blood["GLP1R"][t] = 12.0               # a steady dose of a GLP-1 drug
    out = area_postrema_neuron(blood, blocked=set(), minutes=mins)
    print("after 10 s: %.2f Hz, nausea=%s" % out[10])
    print("after 60 min: %.2f Hz, nausea=%s" % out[-1])

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

This is the only neuron in the human brain that tastes your blood.

It is the one cell type identified by an absence, the missing blood-brain barrier, and by a clinical signature nothing else produces: weeks of vomiting and hiccups from a lesion two millimetres across.

CHAPTER 159. DREAM-SLEEP STILLNESS NEURON (NON-COG) (PUC)

Every night a few tens of thousands of cells in your upper brainstem issue one life-saving order: while you dream of running, do not run.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This small excitatory cell in the pons, the bulge at the middle of the brainstem, switches on during dreaming sleep and paralyses the body from the neck down.

It works by a sign flip, exciting inhibitory cells downstream so that its excitement becomes your stillness.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is a plain multipolar cell of the reticular formation, the loose mesh of neurons filling the brainstem core.

Three to six long, sparsely branched dendrites, the fibres that gather signals, spread as a flat sheet across the pons, and one thin axon descends to medulla and spinal cord.

SIZE:

The cell body measures 15 to 25 micrometres, a fifth the width of a human hair, against 27 to 30 micrometres for its rat cholinergic and noradrenergic neighbours and about 34 micrometres for their human counterparts.

Dendrites reach 400 to 600 micrometres, and the axon may run 45 centimetres to the lumbar cord.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Medulla-projecting cells, carrying the main paralysis command.

• Spinally-projecting cells, reaching inhibitory interneurons of the spinal ventral horn directly.

• Forward-lying cells tied to generating the dreaming brain state rather than the paralysis.

• A resident population releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main "stop" chemical, which suppresses wakefulness instead.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It fills the sublaterodorsal tegmental nucleus (SLD) of the dorsolateral pontine tegmentum, beneath the locus coeruleus, medial to the parabrachial nucleus, above the oral pontine reticular nucleus, caudal to the laterodorsal tegmental nucleus, a territory human atlases call nucleus subcoeruleus, peri-locus coeruleus alpha, or the pontine inhibitory area.

Its address is an interface, where the arousal machinery of the locus coeruleus meets the motor machinery of the pontine reticular formation, with the REM-off ventrolateral periaqueductal grey (vlPAG) and lateral pontine tegmentum (LPT) along its upper border.

NUMBER:

Rodents hold a few thousand glutamate-releasing SLD neurons per side, and the nearest human landmark is a count of 6,260 noradrenergic subcoeruleus cells against 53,900 in the locus coeruleus.

A defensible human estimate is 20,000 to 60,000, held with low confidence because nobody has counted them in a human brain, which is 0.01 to 0.03 percent of the brainstem's 200 million neurons and under one millionth of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

It is a REM-on cell, under one spike per second in waking and dreamless sleep, then firing steadily through rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, the stage of dreaming and darting eyes.

Slices give a resting voltage near minus 60 millivolts, a voltage sag from the current called Ih, and a low-threshold calcium spike, so release from inhibition fires a rebound burst.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are dominated by GABA fibres from the vlPAG and LPT that clamp it silent all day, joined by acetylcholine from the laterodorsal and pedunculopontine tegmental nuclei, noradrenaline from the locus coeruleus, serotonin from the dorsal raphe, and orexin and melanin-concentrating hormone from the lateral hypothalamus.

Outputs reach glycine- and GABA-releasing premotor neurons of the ventral gigantocellular and magnocellular reticular nuclei, so this commander never touches a muscle itself.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The thin axon, under about two micrometres, descends in the ventrolateral funiculus with other reticulospinal fibres, branches richly in the ventral medulla, and reaches cervical, thoracic and lumbar levels at a few metres per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases glutamate, the brain's main "go" chemical, packaged by vesicular glutamate transporter 2 and acting on fast ionotropic and slower N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptors downstream.

It carries receptors for GABA, acetylcholine, noradrenaline, serotonin, orexin and melanin-concentrating hormone, and no peptide co-transmitter is established.

MYELINATION:

Its fibres run in lightly myelinated company, myelin being the fatty insulation that speeds conduction, but whether these particular axons carry a sheath has never been measured, a genuine gap rather than an omission.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Averaged over a day it is cheap, silent nine tenths of the time, then firing without pause for five to thirty minutes inside a brain whose energy use in REM sleep matches or exceeds waking.

PLASTICITY:

It stores no memories, but its drive is homeostatic, so REM sleep lost one night is repaid the next, and drugs blocking serotonin or noradrenaline reuptake weaken the paralysis and can unmask dream enactment within weeks.

Newborns spend half of sleep in REM with imperfect paralysis, adults a fifth with near-complete paralysis, so the switch matures.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing uniquely specialised has been shown for the glia here, and ordinary astrocytes simply clear released glutamate through excitatory amino acid transporter 2, preventing spillover into the arousal cells above.

The striking regional fact is vulnerability, not support, because the misfolded protein alpha-synuclein gathers in neurons and astrocytes of this exact zone very early in Parkinson-type disease.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this one cell type and the dorsolateral pons still wakes the brain, steers breathing and cycles through sleep stages, but it loses its disconnect switch, so the dreaming forebrain stays wired to the muscles and every imagined action becomes a real one.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Subcoeruleus neurons show electrical coupling through gap junctions, direct pores passing current with no chemical step, so the population can flip as a bloc.

Its low-threshold calcium spike turns inhibition itself into output, and at network level it forms a bistable flip-flop with the vlPAG and LPT showing hysteresis, meaning the switch resists flipping back once thrown, which is why REM sleep arrives in discrete episodes instead of fading in.

Noradrenaline reaches it by volume transmission, seeping through tissue from locus coeruleus dendrites overhead rather than crossing a synapse.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: GABA from vlPAG and LPT at 0 to 20 spikes per second, plus acetylcholine, noradrenaline, serotonin, orexin and melanin-concentrating hormone from the sources named above, plus a homeostatic REM pressure signal scaled 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate at 0 to 15 spikes per second onto ventral medullary glycine and GABA premotor cells and spinal ventral horn interneurons.

• STATE: firing rate, membrane voltage, calcium channel inactivation, accumulated REM pressure, episode flag.

• PARAMETERS: resting voltage near minus 60 millivolts, peak rate 12 to 15 spikes per second, mutual inhibition weight against the REM-off group, atonia gain.

• TIME CONSTANTS: 10 to 30 milliseconds for integration, 20 to 60 seconds for state transitions, 5 to 30 minutes per episode, about 90 minutes between episodes.

• FAILURE MODE: partial loss gives REM sleep behaviour disorder, sleepers punching, kicking and leaping from bed, seen in roughly 1 percent of adults on sleep studies and converting to Parkinson's disease, dementia with Lewy bodies or multiple system atrophy at about 6 percent a year, reaching 75 to 80 percent within fifteen years, while firing wrongly in waking gives the collapse of cataplexy.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether the stillness neuron fires and how limp the sleeper becomes.
Create a number variable called rem_pressure with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called threshold with initial value 0.875.
Create a number variable called stillness_rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called muscle_tone with initial value 1.
Repeat the following steps exactly 144000 times.
    Check if rem_pressure is greater than threshold.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set stillness_rate to 12.
        Set threshold to 0.125.
        Calculate the difference of rem_pressure and 0.0000833.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set stillness_rate to 0.
        Set threshold to 0.875.
        Calculate the sum of rem_pressure and 0.0000167.
    End the conditional block.
    Set rem_pressure to the result.
    Calculate the quotient of stillness_rate divided by 12.
    Calculate the difference of 1 and the result.
    Set muscle_tone to the result.
End the loop.
Display muscle_tone to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: the cell is two coupled rate equations, and Python reads like the specification.
import math
DT, PEAK, TAU, W, K = 0.05, 12.0, 20.0, 3.0, 1.1   # s, Hz, s, inhibition, atonia gain
sig = lambda x: 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp(-x))
on, off, press, rem_s, low = 0.2, 12.0, 0.0, 0.0, 1.0   # on = stillness cell, off = REM-off group
for _ in range(int(7200 / DT)):                 # two simulated hours
    press = min(1.0, press + DT / 3000.0)       # REM pressure builds outside REM
    d_on = 4.0 * press - W * (off / PEAK) - 0.5
    d_off = 0.6 - W * (on / PEAK) + 0.5         # 0.6 is the orexin drive
    on += DT * (-on + PEAK * sig(4.0 * d_on)) / TAU
    off += DT * (-off + PEAK * sig(4.0 * d_off)) / TAU
    low = min(low, max(0.0, 1.0 - K * (on / PEAK)))   # 1.0 is tone, 0.0 is atonia
    if on > 0.5 * PEAK:
        rem_s += DT
        press = max(0.0, press - DT / 400.0)    # the episode discharges the pressure
print("REM %.0f s, lowest tone %.2f, final rate %.2f Hz" % (rem_s, low, on))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other neuron causes paralysis by getting excited, and none is silent twenty-three hours a day then holds a whole skeleton limp for the twenty-fourth.

A glutamate cell under the locus coeruleus firing only in REM sleep, speaking to glycine cells in the ventral medulla, whose quiet loss makes an elderly patient act out dreams years before any tremor, is this one.


PART TEN: SPINAL CORD

CHAPTER 160. ALPHA MOTOR NEURONS (NON-COG)

Every word you have spoken, every step you have taken and every breath you have drawn left your nervous system through this one kind of cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The alpha motor neuron is the last neuron in every chain of command that ends in movement, which is why Charles Sherrington called it the final common path.

It commands extrafusal muscle fibres, the ordinary force-producing fibres of a skeletal muscle, and one such neuron plus its fibres is a motor unit, the true atom of movement.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is multipolar, with eight to twenty thick primary dendrites, the branching input antennae of a neuron, radiating like spokes from a broken wheel.

The cell body is packed with Nissl substance, dense stacks of protein-making machinery, and a single axon leaves it, drops a few collateral branches inside the cord, then quits the central nervous system altogether.

SIZE:

The cell body is 40 to 80 micrometres across, reaching about 100 micrometres in human lamina IX, making it the largest neuron in the spinal cord; a micrometre is one thousandth of a millimetre.

The dendritic tree spans 1 to 3 millimetres and the axon can run a full metre to a foot muscle, so well over 99 percent of the cell's cytoplasm lies outside the cell body.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Physiologically there are three, each given its own entry below: fast fatigable, fast fatigue-resistant, and slow.

Anatomically they also divide by column, into the medial motor column for trunk muscles and the lateral motor column for limbs, with the rare beta motor neuron supplying ordinary and sensory muscle fibres at once.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Rexed lamina IX, the outermost cell layer of the ventral horn of the spinal grey matter, at every segment from C1 to S5, gathered into long clusters called motor pools, one per muscle, mapped so that trunk pools lie medially, hand and foot pools laterally, and flexor pools dorsal to extensor pools.

Named groups include the phrenic nucleus at C3 to C5, the spinal accessory nucleus at C1 to C6 and Onuf's nucleus at S2 to S4, and its axon crosses two interfaces: the ventral rootlet, where central myelin from oligodendrocytes hands over to peripheral myelin from Schwann cells, and the neuromuscular junction, where nervous system meets muscle.

NUMBER:

About 200 thousand in the whole human spinal cord, both sides, all segments.

Direct serial-section counts of the human lumbosacral cord give 52 thousand to 62 thousand limb motor neurons and carry high confidence, while the whole-cord total is an extrapolation of only moderate confidence; the figure is about 0.1 percent of the roughly 200 million spinal cord neurons and 0.0002 percent of the roughly 86 billion in the nervous system, and the three subtypes below divide this same 200 thousand rather than adding to it.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Rest is near minus 70 millivolts, threshold 10 to 15 millivolts above that, input resistance 0.5 to 4 megohms and rheobase, the smallest steady current that will make it fire, 3 to 20 nanoamps.

Each impulse is followed by an afterhyperpolarisation (AHP), a voltage dip of 30 to 150 milliseconds made by calcium-activated potassium channels that sets the firing rate at 5 to 50 impulses per second, and a persistent inward current (PIC), a self-reinforcing sodium and L-type calcium current in the dendrites, amplifies input several-fold and can sustain firing after the input stops.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Tens of thousands of endings cover it: monosynaptic group Ia fibres from muscle spindles that produce the knee-jerk, direct corticospinal fibres in primates, and reticulospinal, vestibulospinal and rubrospinal drive.

Inhibition comes from glycine-releasing Ia inhibitory interneurons and from Renshaw cells excited by its own axon collaterals, while cholinergic C-boutons from Pitx2-positive V0c interneurons cap the cell body and raise its gain.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

An A-alpha fibre, 13 to 20 micrometres across with its myelin and conducting at 80 to 120 metres per second, so a command reaches the calf in about 10 milliseconds.

It leaves by the ventral root and branches into 10 endings in an eye muscle or 2000 in the calf, each a pretzel-shaped endplate 30 to 50 micrometres wide.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine, made by choline acetyltransferase and loaded by the vesicular acetylcholine transporter, the two markers used to identify this cell, acting on nicotinic receptors at muscle and on Renshaw cells inside the cord.

The peptide calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) is co-released and helps the muscle maintain its receptors.

MYELINATION:

Among the most heavily myelinated cells in the body, oligodendrocyte myelin inside the cord and Schwann cell myelin beyond the ventral root, with internodes 1 to 1.5 millimetres long and an axon-to-fibre diameter ratio near 0.6 to 0.7.

The axon sets its own insulation thickness by displaying neuregulin-1 type III for the Schwann cell to measure.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

One of the most expensive cells in the body, supplying a metre of axon by fast transport at 200 to 400 millimetres per day, so a mitochondrion made today reaches the foot in three days.

Sodium pumping at 30 impulses per second and replacement of every junctional acetylcholine receptor within one to two weeks run continuously.

PLASTICITY:

It does not learn like a cortical cell, but serotonin and noradrenaline reset its gain minute by minute through the persistent inward current, so identical commands make different force depending on arousal.

Presynaptic inhibition adjusts its sensory input, reflex conditioning shifts its membrane properties over weeks, and after partial denervation surviving axons sprout to adopt orphaned muscle fibres.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

An aggrecan-rich perineuronal net, a lattice of sugar-coated proteins, encircles roughly nine in ten alpha motor neurons and anchors their large synapses; few other spinal cells are so armoured.

Astrocytes cap each C-bouton and supply lactate, microglia strip synapses off the cell body after nerve injury, terminal Schwann cells lid each neuromuscular junction and guide regrowth, and in motor neuron disease these same glia turn actively toxic to this cell type.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and the ventral horn becomes a computer with no printer, which is exactly what polio does: flaccid paralysis, absent reflexes, muscle wasted within weeks.

The region also loses its main source of acetylcholine, the Renshaw circuit falls silent for want of excitation, and the whole spindle loop has nothing left to act upon.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The amplification happens in the dendrites, where the plateau current can latch on so that a brief input yields sustained firing and a brief inhibition switches it off.

Its axon collaterals make it an interneuron as well as an output cell; it dictates its muscle's fibre type, as cross-innervation experiments proved; it instructs its own myelination through neuregulin-1; it depends on survival factors sent back from the muscle; and after injury it releases nitric oxide, a gas that diffuses to its neighbours.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: group Ia and II spindle afferents at 0 to 100 impulses per second; descending drive as 0 to 30 nanoamps; glycinergic inhibition 0 to minus 15 nanoamps; monoamine level 0 to 1 scaling the plateau current.

• OUTPUTS: impulse train to muscle at 0 to 50 hertz; the same train to Renshaw cells; force in millinewtons.

• STATE: membrane voltage; afterhyperpolarisation level; plateau latched or not; intracellular calcium; fatigue factor 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 0.5 to 4 megohms; threshold 10 to 15 millivolts above rest; plateau current 2 to 6 nanoamps; conduction velocity 80 to 120 metres per second; innervation ratio 10 to 2000.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 5 to 10 milliseconds; afterhyperpolarisation 30 to 150 milliseconds; twitch 20 to 100 milliseconds; transport days; receptor turnover 8 to 14 days.

• FAILURE MODE: it dies in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) and in spinal muscular atrophy; after spinal cord injury its plateau current runs uncontrolled and produces spasticity.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to run one alpha motor neuron for one second of simulated time and count the impulses it sends to its muscle.
Create a number variable called excitation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called afterhyperpolarisation with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called plateau is on with initial value false.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 1000 times.
    Set excitation to the synaptic input for this millisecond in millivolts above rest.
    If excitation is greater than 10, then perform the following actions.
        Set plateau is on to true.
    Else if excitation is less than 5, then perform the following actions.
        Set plateau is on to false.
    End the conditional block.
    If plateau is on is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the product of excitation and 2.5.
        Set excitation to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of excitation and afterhyperpolarisation.
    Set excitation to the result.
    If excitation is greater than or equal to 15, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set spike count to the result.
        Calculate the sum of afterhyperpolarisation and 9.
        Set afterhyperpolarisation to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of afterhyperpolarisation and 0.975.
    Set afterhyperpolarisation to the result.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the model is a few lines of arithmetic and any reader can run it with no libraries and no setup.

DT, TAU_M, R_IN = 0.1, 6.0, 1.5     # ms per step, membrane time constant ms, megohms
THRESHOLD = 15.0                    # millivolts above rest
PIC_ON, PIC_OFF, PIC_CURRENT = 7.0, 3.0, 6.0   # plateau latch points, and its current in nA
AHP_STEP, TAU_AHP = 9.0, 40.0       # millivolts added per impulse, and its decay in ms
def run(drive_na, duration_ms=1000.0):
    v, ahp, pic, spikes = 0.0, 0.0, False, 0    # v is millivolts above rest
    for _ in range(int(duration_ms / DT)):
        if v > PIC_ON:
            pic = True
        elif v < PIC_OFF:
            pic = False
        current = drive_na + (PIC_CURRENT if pic else 0.0)
        v += DT * (R_IN * current - v - ahp) / TAU_M   # leaky membrane
        ahp -= DT * ahp / TAU_AHP                      # the dip fades
        if v >= THRESHOLD:
            spikes, v = spikes + 1, 0.0                # fire and reset
            ahp += AHP_STEP
    return spikes
for drive in (8.0, 10.0, 12.0):
    print("drive %4.1f nA -> %3d impulses per second" % (drive, run(drive)))

The printed rates fall in the physiological band of roughly ten to forty impulses per second, and raising the drive by half raises the rate by much more than half because the plateau current amplifies it.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the giant of the cord: the largest cell body in lamina IX, dense with Nissl substance, wrapped in a thick perineuronal net and positive for choline acetyltransferase.

It is the only neuron whose firing you can feel, because its impulses are your movements, and the only cell here whose axon leaves the central nervous system and never comes back.

CHAPTER 161. FAST-FATIGABLE (FF) ALPHA MOTOR NEURONS (Seconds-Long Power Motor Neuron) (NON-COG)

This is the cell you call on when a car is rolling towards a child, and it will give you everything it has for about thirty seconds.

The old label is an abbreviation of a laboratory fatigue test rather than a description of the cell.

Seconds-Long names the honest limit of this cell, since its muscle fibres run on glycolytic metabolism, meaning they burn sugar without oxygen, and they lose most of their force within roughly a minute of repeated use.

Power names what it buys with that short window, because these are the largest motor units and produce the highest force of the three types.

Motor Neuron places it in the family that commands muscle, and together the name teaches the trade this cell embodies, maximum force purchased with almost no endurance.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The fast-fatigable alpha motor neuron is the largest and strongest of the family and the one held in reserve, recruited last and used briefly.

It commands type IIx muscle fibres, pale fibres full of the fast form of the contractile protein myosin and of the enzymes for burning sugar without oxygen, so its speciality is maximum force in minimum time.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It has the biggest cell body and the widest dendritic tree of any motor neuron, which is exactly why it is hard to recruit: a large membrane leaks current, so a given input moves its voltage least.

That simple physics is the mechanical basis of Henneman's size principle, the rule that motor units are always recruited from smallest to largest.

SIZE:

Cell body 60 to 100 micrometres in humans, at the top of the alpha range, with the thickest axon in the pool.

Its motor unit is the largest, 1000 to 2000 muscle fibres in a calf muscle, producing on the order of 30 to 130 millinewtons, ten to a hundred times the force of a slow unit in the same muscle.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No anatomical subdivision is recognised inside the class, but a borderline group called fast intermediate sits between this type and the fatigue-resistant type on every measure, showing that the three classes are peaks on a continuum rather than separate species.

They also differ by muscle, dominating fast limb muscles such as gastrocnemius and being nearly absent from a postural muscle such as soleus.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Rexed lamina IX of the ventral horn, intermingled with the other alpha types inside the same motor pool, commonest in the lateral motor column of the cervical enlargement at C4 to T1 and of the lumbosacral enlargement at L1 to S3.

Within a pool they sit slightly more dorsally and laterally than slow cells, a statistical rather than an anatomical separation, and their axons cross the same central to peripheral interface at the ventral rootlet.

NUMBER:

Roughly 60 thousand in the human spinal cord, about 30 percent of the 200 thousand alpha motor neurons, which is about 0.03 percent of the 200 million spinal neurons and 0.00007 percent of the whole nervous system.

Confidence is low, because the proportion is inferred from cat and rat motor unit surveys and human muscle fibre typing rather than counted directly, and it ranges from near zero in soleus to about half in fast limb muscles.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Lowest input resistance of the family at 0.5 to 1 megohm and the highest rheobase at 10 to 20 nanoamps, so it needs a large input before it will fire at all.

Its afterhyperpolarisation is the briefest, with a half-decay of 10 to 20 milliseconds, permitting brief bursts at 40 to 60 impulses per second, and its persistent inward current is weaker and harder to latch, giving it a crisp on-and-off character.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It gets the same catalogue of inputs as any alpha motor neuron with a different balance: monosynaptic group Ia excitation produces the smallest voltage change here of any motor neuron.

Corticospinal and reticulospinal drive, the voluntary and startle pathways, weigh more heavily on it than the stretch reflex does, and Renshaw inhibition onto it is comparatively weak, so once recruited it is not easily reined in.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The fastest motor axon, at the top of the 80 to 120 metres per second range, branching into more terminals than any other motor neuron.

Its neuromuscular junctions are large and complex with high transmitter release, and those same junctions are the first to break down in ageing and disease, since denervation begins at the terminal, not the cell body.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine with calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) as peptide co-transmitter, as in all motor neurons.

Quantal content, the number of transmitter packets released per impulse, is the highest of the three types when fresh, and its safety margin is the first to collapse under repetitive firing, which is the component of fatigue that happens in the nerve rather than the muscle.

MYELINATION:

Thickest myelin of the family, matched to the thickest axon, with an axon-to-fibre ratio near 0.6 and internodes above 1 millimetre.

The axon buys its own insulation by presenting neuregulin-1 type III to its Schwann cells, so the biggest axon gets the thickest sheath.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Enormous but intermittent: it must maintain a huge axon and hundreds of large junctions, while its glycolytic muscle fibres burn stored sugar without oxygen and accumulate acid.

Its mitochondrial reserve is proportionally the lowest of the three types, and that thin energetic buffer is a leading explanation for why these cells die first.

PLASTICITY:

Highly convertible: endurance training shifts its muscle fibres from type IIx towards the more oxidative type IIa, turning a fast-fatigable unit into something close to a fatigue-resistant one, and disuse reverses it.

These units are the first lost in sarcopenia, the muscle wasting of old age, and their orphaned fibres are adopted by slow units, which is why aged muscle shows large blocks of one fibre type.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Its perineuronal net is the thickest in the spinal cord, holding the C-bouton and Renshaw synapses in place.

At the muscle, the terminal Schwann cells over its large junctions are unusually reactive and retract before the nerve terminal is lost, while in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) microglia and astrocytes attack this subtype before any other, and these vulnerable cells specifically express the enzyme matrix metalloproteinase-9 that resistant cells lack.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and the ventral horn keeps every reflex, every posture and every ordinary movement, and loses the ceiling.

A person could stand, walk and write, but could not jump, sprint or produce a sudden protective force; muscles would lose their pale fibres, turn uniformly red, and peak power would fall by more than half while endurance improved.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

It dictates the identity of its targets, since the pattern of impulses it delivers switches the fast myosin gene on and the slow one off in the muscle fibre.

Its vulnerability is partly a consequence of its own activity, because the calcium load and metabolic cost of high-frequency firing in a very large cell leave no margin, and its classic laboratory signature, a sag in force during an unfused contraction, comes from the muscle it has shaped rather than from the neuron itself.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: corticospinal and reticulospinal drive up to 40 nanoamps; weak group Ia excitation, under 100 microvolts per afferent volley; weak Renshaw inhibition.

• OUTPUTS: burst trains at 40 to 60 hertz; 30 to 130 millinewtons per unit when fresh, scaled by the fatigue factor.

• STATE: membrane voltage; afterhyperpolarisation level; fatigue factor falling from 1 to below 0.25; recruited flag.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 0.5 to 1 megohm; rheobase 10 to 20 nanoamps; recruitment threshold near 80 percent of maximum effort; innervation ratio 300 to 2000.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 4 to 6 milliseconds; afterhyperpolarisation half-decay 10 to 20 milliseconds; twitch 15 to 30 milliseconds; fatigue over 20 to 60 seconds of activity; recovery over many minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: first to denervate in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis and first lost in ageing, so power and speed disappear long before endurance does.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to drive one fast fatigable motor unit through the standard two minute fatigue test and record how quickly its force runs out.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called recruitment threshold with initial value 80.
Create a number variable called fatigue factor with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called peak force with initial value 100.
Create a number variable called force with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called recovery with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called force record with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 120 times.
    Set drive to the percentage of maximum voluntary effort requested this second.
    If drive is greater than recruitment threshold, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the product of peak force and fatigue factor.
        Set force to the result.
        Calculate the product of fatigue factor and 0.96.
        Set fatigue factor to the result.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set force to 0.
        Calculate the difference of 1 and fatigue factor.
        Set recovery to the result.
        Calculate the product of recovery and 0.004.
        Set recovery to the result.
        Calculate the sum of fatigue factor and recovery.
        Set fatigue factor to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Add force to the end of force record.
End the loop.
Display force to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because this is the kind of tight numeric loop a real-time muscle controller runs, and C makes every arithmetic step and every unit explicit.

#include <stdio.h>
int main(void) {
    const double dt = 0.001;           /* seconds per step */
    const double tau_rise = 0.010;     /* fast twitch rise, 10 ms */
    const double tau_fall = 0.030;     /* fast twitch relaxation, 30 ms */
    const double fatigue_tau = 20.0;   /* seconds of activity to run the unit down */
    const double recovery_tau = 400.0; /* seconds of rest to build it back */
    double f1 = 0.0, f2 = 0.0, fatigue = 1.0;
    for (int ms = 0; ms < 121000; ms++) {       /* the two minute Burke test */
        int active = (ms % 1000) < 330;         /* 330 ms train once per second */
        double drive = active ? 100.0 : 0.0;    /* millinewtons when fresh */
        f1 += dt * (drive - f1) / tau_rise;     /* two stage filter makes the twitch train */
        f2 += dt * (f1 - f2) / tau_fall;
        if (active) fatigue -= dt * fatigue / fatigue_tau;
        else        fatigue += dt * (1.0 - fatigue) / recovery_tau;
        if (ms % 20000 == 300)
            printf("t=%3d s  force=%6.2f mN  fatigue factor=%.3f\n",
                   ms / 1000, f2 * fatigue, fatigue);
    }
    return 0;
}

The printed fatigue factor falls to roughly one fifth of fresh by 120 seconds, which is the defining criterion for this type, a fatigue index below 0.25.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the last cell recruited and the first cell lost.

Nothing else combines the largest cell body, the fastest axon, the shortest afterhyperpolarisation, the biggest motor unit and a force that collapses to a fifth within two minutes.

CHAPTER 162. FAST FATIGUE-RESISTANT (FR) ALPHA MOTOR NEURONS (Minutes-Long Effort Motor Neuron) (NON-COG)

This is the cell that carries you through the last mile of a run, quick enough to sprint for a bus and stubborn enough to keep going after the sprinters have quit.

The old label is again a fatigue-test abbreviation and hides where the cell sits on the scale.

Minutes-Long places this cell exactly midway on the endurance scale that defines the three motor unit types, since its type IIa muscle fibres hold force for many minutes rather than seconds.

Effort rather than power records that it contracts fast but at moderate force, the workhorse for walking and repeated lifting.

Motor Neuron keeps it in the family, and its fibres carry higher mitochondrial volume density and oxidative capacity, meaning more oxygen-burning machinery, than the fastest fibres, which is precisely why the fatigue clock runs longer.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The fast fatigue-resistant alpha motor neuron is the compromise candidate of the motor system, and it is a good compromise.

It commands type IIa muscle fibres, which contract with fast myosin, the quick form of the contractile protein, yet are crowded with mitochondria, the oxygen-burning power plants of a cell, so the unit makes moderate force quickly and repeats it for minutes; it is the workhorse of running, cycling and stair climbing.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Intermediate in every structural measure between the fast-fatigable and the slow cell, with a middling cell body and a dendritic tree that is dense rather than wide.

Its axon collaterals to Renshaw cells are well developed, so every impulse it sends to muscle is also fed into the local inhibitory circuit.

SIZE:

Cell body 50 to 70 micrometres, axon 14 to 17 micrometres including myelin.

Innervation ratio is intermediate at roughly 300 to 800 muscle fibres in a large limb muscle, and unit force measured in the cat calf muscle was about 10 to 40 millinewtons.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No formal subdivisions exist inside this class.

It shades continuously into the fast-fatigable class through the borderline group called fast intermediate and towards the slow class in endurance-trained muscle, so a simulation should treat fatigue resistance as a continuous parameter with three peaks rather than a three-valued switch.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Rexed lamina IX of the ventral horn, mixed into the same motor pools as the other two alpha types.

They are abundant in the lateral motor column of the cervical enlargement at C4 to T1 and of the lumbosacral enlargement at L1 to S3, dominate mixed muscles such as quadriceps, tibialis anterior and medial gastrocnemius, and are numerous in the phrenic nucleus at C3 to C5, where the diaphragm must be quick and untiring for a lifetime; their axons cross the central to peripheral interface at the ventral rootlet.

NUMBER:

Roughly 60 thousand in the human spinal cord, about 30 percent of the 200 thousand alpha motor neurons, which is about 0.03 percent of the 200 million spinal neurons and 0.00007 percent of the whole nervous system.

Confidence is low, since the proportion comes from animal motor unit surveys and human muscle fibre typing rather than counted human cells, and it changes with training and age.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Input resistance 1 to 2 megohms and rheobase 5 to 12 nanoamps, squarely between the slow and fast-fatigable values, with an afterhyperpolarisation half-decay near 20 to 30 milliseconds that supports steady firing in the twenties of impulses per second.

Its persistent inward current is well developed and readily facilitated by serotonin, so effort and arousal noticeably raise its gain, and it is recruited at moderate effort, between about 25 and 60 percent of maximum.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Group Ia and group II spindle afferents make solid monosynaptic contact, so this unit participates fully in the stretch reflex, and reticulospinal and vestibulospinal drive for locomotion and balance are strongly represented.

Renshaw inhibition is moderate and continuously useful for a cell that fires for minutes, and cholinergic C-boutons from V0c interneurons are strongly recruited during locomotion, turning its gain up exactly when the animal is running.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Conduction velocity 85 to 110 metres per second with intermediate branching and moderately sized neuromuscular junctions.

Unlike the fast-fatigable unit it holds its transmission safety margin through a minute of hard work, so when this unit fades, the fading is in the muscle and not in the nerve.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine at every terminal with calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) alongside it.

Serotonin from the raphe nuclei and noradrenaline from the locus coeruleus reach it by volume transmission, meaning the chemicals diffuse through tissue rather than crossing a single synaptic gap, and both raise its persistent inward current.

MYELINATION:

Well myelinated, axon-to-fibre diameter ratio near 0.65, internodes around 1 millimetre.

Its steady firing works the nodes of Ranvier hard, and node-associated mitochondria are correspondingly dense.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High and sustained, which is a harder engineering problem than high and brief.

Its oxidative muscle fibres need continuous oxygen, which is why these units are the main reason blood flow through a running muscle can rise more than twentyfold, and the neuron carries a good mitochondrial reserve with more of the protective protein osteopontin than its fast-fatigable neighbour.

PLASTICITY:

The most trainable motor neuron in the body, in both directions.

Endurance training recruits it constantly and drives its fibres to build mitochondria and capillaries, pulling neighbouring fast-fatigable units towards this phenotype, while bed rest, detraining and spinal injury reverse the change within weeks; its recruitment threshold also shifts with practice, which is part of what people mean by an efficient movement.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The astrocytes around this cell are notable for their lactate shuttle: during sustained locomotion they break down glycogen and hand lactate to the neuron as fuel, a service that matters far more here than at a cell firing in short bursts.

Perineuronal nets are of intermediate density, capillary density around its muscle fibres is roughly double that around fast-fatigable fibres, and its terminal Schwann cells are stable rather than reactive.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and the ventral horn keeps posture and keeps the emergency sprint but loses everything in between.

Walking would survive on slow units and a single jump on fast-fatigable ones, but running for a bus would end within seconds and rhythmic work such as cycling or climbing would be impossible, because the spinal locomotor circuitry would have lost the population it normally drives.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its persistent inward current does real work during locomotion, amplifying the rhythmic drive from spinal pattern-generating circuits so that a modest oscillating input yields full-amplitude output.

It couples metabolically to astrocytes more than other motor neurons do, so its firing is part-funded by neighbouring glia, and its sustained moderate-frequency trains switch on the genetic programme for mitochondrial building inside the muscle fibre, which is the cellular basis of aerobic fitness.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: reticulospinal and corticospinal drive 0 to 25 nanoamps; group Ia and II afferents 0 to 100 impulses per second; rhythmic locomotor drive at 0.5 to 3 hertz; serotonin level 0 to 1 scaling the plateau current.

• OUTPUTS: sustained trains at 15 to 35 hertz; 10 to 40 millinewtons per unit; collateral output to Renshaw cells.

• STATE: membrane voltage; afterhyperpolarisation level; aerobic reserve 0 to 1; accumulated activity history for training effects.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 1 to 2 megohms; rheobase 5 to 12 nanoamps; recruitment threshold 25 to 60 percent of maximum effort; innervation ratio 300 to 800; aerobic ceiling near 30 percent of peak demand.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 5 to 7 milliseconds; afterhyperpolarisation half-decay 20 to 30 milliseconds; twitch 20 to 40 milliseconds; fatigue over 2 to 6 minutes; training adaptation over 2 to 6 weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) these cells degenerate after the fast-fatigable population and before the slow one; in chronic heart or lung disease their fibres lose oxidative capacity and the unit behaves like a fast-fatigable one, which is much of what exercise intolerance is.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to run one fast fatigue-resistant motor unit through four minutes of repeated contraction and track the aerobic reserve that keeps its force up.
Create a number variable called demand with initial value 0.33.
Create a number variable called aerobic ceiling with initial value 0.30.
Create a number variable called reserve with initial value 12.
Create a number variable called shortfall with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called peak force with initial value 45.
Create a number variable called force with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called reserve fraction with initial value 1.
Repeat the following steps exactly 240 times.
    If demand is greater than aerobic ceiling, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of demand and aerobic ceiling.
        Set shortfall to the result.
        Calculate the difference of reserve and shortfall.
        Set reserve to the result.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of reserve and 0.05.
        Set reserve to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the quotient of reserve divided by 12.
    Set reserve fraction to the result.
    Calculate the product of reserve fraction and 0.65.
    Set reserve fraction to the result.
    Calculate the sum of reserve fraction and 0.35.
    Set reserve fraction to the result.
    Calculate the product of peak force and reserve fraction.
    Set force to the result.
End the loop.
Display force to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because this is slow metabolic bookkeeping rather than fast spiking, so readability matters more than execution speed.

DT = 0.01                 # seconds per step
PEAK_FORCE_MN = 45.0      # force of one fresh fatigue-resistant unit, millinewtons
DUTY = 0.33               # the standard test: 330 ms of firing every second
AEROBIC_CEILING = 0.30    # fraction of peak demand the mitochondria cover indefinitely
STORE_MAX = 12.0          # anaerobic reserve, arbitrary energy units
reserve = STORE_MAX
for step in range(int(240 / DT)):                     # four minutes
    demand = DUTY
    shortfall = max(demand - AEROBIC_CEILING, 0.0)    # only the excess drains the store
    reserve = max(reserve - shortfall * DT, 0.0)
    if demand < AEROBIC_CEILING:
        reserve = min(reserve + 0.05 * DT, STORE_MAX) # refill only when demand is low
    force = PEAK_FORCE_MN * (0.35 + 0.65 * reserve / STORE_MAX)
    if step % int(60 / DT) == 0:
        print("t=%3d s  force=%5.1f mN  (%3.0f percent of fresh)  reserve=%5.2f"
              % (int(step * DT), force, 100.0 * force / PEAK_FORCE_MN, reserve))

The printed force is still about 80 percent of fresh at two minutes, which is the criterion that defines a fatigue-resistant unit, a fatigue index above 0.75, and it has sagged to about 70 percent by three minutes.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only motor neuron that is fast and stubborn at once, with a twitch of 20 to 40 milliseconds and a force still three quarters of fresh after two minutes of repeated contraction.

Under the microscope its muscle fibres give the giveaway pairing of fast myosin staining with heavy oxidative enzyme staining, which no other motor unit shows.

CHAPTER 163. SLOW (S) ALPHA MOTOR NEURONS (Hours-Long Posture Motor Neuron) (NON-COG)

Right now, as you read this, a few thousand of these cells are firing about ten times a second to stop your head falling forward, and they will not tire before you sleep.

Slow names a speed and leaves out the duty the cell actually holds.

Hours-Long completes the endurance series and is not an exaggeration, because these highly oxidative units are effectively unfatiguable under normal use.

Posture names the job they hold, keeping you upright against gravity all day, and it reflects that these are the smallest, lowest-force, first-recruited units under Henneman's size principle.

Motor Neuron keeps it in the same family as its two faster siblings so that the single dimension separating all three, how long the force can be held, is visible in the names alone.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The slow alpha motor neuron is the smallest, most excitable and most persistent member of the family, commanding type I muscle fibres, deep red with myoglobin, the oxygen-storing pigment, and dense with mitochondria.

Its job is not power but permanence: posture, muscle tone and the low-force background of every movement, and because it is the smallest cell in the pool it is always recruited first and released last.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The compact end of the alpha range, with a smaller cell body, fewer and shorter primary dendrites and a tighter dendritic field, so less current leaks away and a modest input produces a large voltage change.

Its dendrites nonetheless carry the densest group Ia sensory input of any motor neuron, tying it tightly to the stretch reflex that holds a joint steady.

SIZE:

Cell body 40 to 55 micrometres, axon 12 to 15 micrometres including myelin, the thinnest of the alpha fibres.

Its motor unit is the smallest at 50 to 300 thin muscle fibres, producing roughly 2 to 10 millinewtons in the cat calf muscle.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No formal subdivisions are recognised, but there is real diversity across muscles: the slow cells of soleus fire nearly continuously all day, while those of a hand muscle fire only when the hand is used.

There is also a strong family resemblance between these cells and the disease-resistant motor neurons of Onuf's nucleus and the eye muscle nuclei, which share the same protective molecular profile.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Rexed lamina IX of the ventral horn in the same motor pools as the other alpha types, at every spinal segment.

They dominate the medial motor column, which serves the deep postural muscles of trunk and neck along the whole cord, the pools of antigravity muscles such as soleus at S1 and S2, and Onuf's nucleus at S2 to S4 controlling the pelvic floor; their axons cross the central to peripheral interface at the ventral rootlet.

NUMBER:

Roughly 80 thousand in the human spinal cord, about 40 percent of the 200 thousand alpha motor neurons, which is about 0.04 percent of the 200 million spinal neurons and 0.00009 percent of the whole nervous system.

Confidence is low to moderate, inferred from human muscle fibre typing, which averages close to half type I fibres, adjusted downwards because slow units contain fewer fibres each; their share rises with age, not because more are born but because the fast ones are lost.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The highest input resistance of the family at 2 to 4 megohms and the lowest rheobase at 3 to 6 nanoamps, with the longest afterhyperpolarisation of any motor neuron, a half-decay of 30 to 50 milliseconds and a total duration over 100 milliseconds that caps firing at 8 to 25 impulses per second.

That long dip is a design feature rather than a limit, because it matches the slow twitch of the muscle and produces smooth fused force at low rates, and this cell also has the strongest and most easily latched persistent inward current of the three types.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The heaviest monosynaptic group Ia input of any motor neuron, which makes this the cell that dominates the tendon-tap reflex, with strong vestibulospinal and reticulospinal drive for balance and posture.

Renshaw inhibition onto it is the strongest of the three types, giving fine control of a cell that is otherwise almost always on, and cholinergic C-boutons modulate its gain during sustained effort.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Conduction velocity 70 to 95 metres per second, the slowest of the alpha fibres and still among the fastest axons in the body.

It branches into relatively few terminals, and its small, simple neuromuscular junctions are remarkably stable, the last to be lost in ageing and disease and the ones that sprout to adopt orphaned muscle fibres.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine with calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) as co-transmitter.

Serotonin from the raphe nuclei matters more to this cell than to any other motor neuron because it facilitates the persistent inward current behind sustained postural firing, which is one reason muscle tone collapses during rapid eye movement sleep, when raphe output falls almost silent.

MYELINATION:

Myelinated with an axon-to-fibre diameter ratio near 0.7 and internodes slightly shorter than those of fast axons.

Because it fires continuously it carries the largest lifetime sodium load of any motor axon, so its nodes of Ranvier are unusually rich in sodium pumps and mitochondria.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate in power but enormous in total, because the cell never really stops: over a waking day it may fire several hundred thousand times.

Its muscle fibres are the most capillary-rich and mitochondria-rich in the body, and the cell has the best energetic buffer of the three types, with high osteopontin, a protective protein, and low matrix metalloproteinase-9, a damaging enzyme.

PLASTICITY:

Its properties shift measurably within weeks of altered use, since bed rest, limb casting and spaceflight all reduce its recruitment and push its fibres towards faster types, which is why disuse produces weakness and poor balance together.

It is the great compensator, sprouting new terminals to adopt fibres abandoned by dying fast units, preserving strength in old age at the cost of coarser force gradation, and reflex conditioning studies show even the size of its afterhyperpolarisation changes with training.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here must supply fuel continuously rather than in bursts, and they carry high densities of the transporter that clears glutamate from its unusually dense sensory synapses.

Its perineuronal net is thinner than the fast-fatigable cell's but still stabilises the heavy Ia input, its terminal Schwann cells actively extend guiding processes to reinnervate nearby denervated fibres, making glia the agents of the compensation above, and microglia leave these neurons largely alone even in advanced motor neuron disease.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and a person would collapse into a chair and stay there, because posture, muscle tone and the tendon reflexes all depend on this cell.

The ventral horn would lose its steady background hum and retain only brief tiring bursts, muscles would lose the red colour that lives in the fibres this neuron commands, and holding a cup without crushing it would become impossible because the smallest available increment of force would suddenly be large.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its plateau behaviour is its defining computation: dendritic calcium channels latch on and sustain firing, so posture is held by a self-sustaining membrane state rather than by continuous instruction from the brain, which saves the brain enormous work.

Serotonin switches that plateau on and inhibition switches it off, the cell dictates the slow identity of its muscle fibres as the classic cross-innervation experiments showed, and its resistance to disease is active, arising from a distinct set of genes rather than merely from working less hard.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: strong group Ia excitation of 200 to 500 microvolts per afferent volley; vestibulospinal and reticulospinal drive 0 to 15 nanoamps; Renshaw and Ia inhibition 0 to minus 20 nanoamps; serotonin level 0 to 1 gating the plateau.

• OUTPUTS: near-continuous trains at 8 to 25 hertz; 2 to 10 millinewtons per unit; collateral output to Renshaw cells.

• STATE: membrane voltage; afterhyperpolarisation level; plateau latched or unlatched; a fatigue factor that stays above 0.95 for hours.

• PARAMETERS: input resistance 2 to 4 megohms; rheobase 3 to 6 nanoamps; recruitment threshold under 20 percent of maximum effort; innervation ratio 50 to 300; conduction velocity 70 to 95 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 8 to 12 milliseconds; afterhyperpolarisation half-decay 30 to 50 milliseconds; twitch 60 to 100 milliseconds; plateau onset within 200 milliseconds; fatigue negligible over an hour.

• FAILURE MODE: being the most disease-resistant motor neuron, it is left overloaded in late amyotrophic lateral sclerosis and finally fails too; after spinal cord injury its plateau current runs uncontrolled and produces the involuntary sustained contractions of spasticity.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to hold one slow motor unit at the steady low rate that keeps a standing person upright, and to check how little it tires over an hour.
Create a number variable called excitation with initial value 15.
Create a number variable called afterhyperpolarisation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called net drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called fatigue factor with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called mean rate with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 3600000 times.
    Calculate the difference of excitation and afterhyperpolarisation.
    Set net drive to the result.
    If net drive is greater than or equal to 14, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set spike count to the result.
        Calculate the sum of afterhyperpolarisation and 6.
        Set afterhyperpolarisation to the result.
        Calculate the product of fatigue factor and 0.9999998.
        Set fatigue factor to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of afterhyperpolarisation and 0.978.
    Set afterhyperpolarisation to the result.
End the loop.
Calculate the quotient of spike count divided by 3600.
Set mean rate to the result.
Display mean rate to the console.
Display fatigue factor to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

JavaScript on Node is chosen because this is an hour of millisecond-by-millisecond bookkeeping, and a plain loop in a plain runtime shows honestly how many events that is.

const EXCITATION = 15.0;                   // millivolts of steady postural drive above rest
const THRESHOLD = 14.0;                    // millivolts
const AHP_STEP = 6.0;                      // millivolts added by each impulse
const AHP_DECAY = Math.exp(-1 / 45);       // long 45 ms afterhyperpolarisation, per ms
const TWITCH_DECAY = Math.exp(-1 / 90);    // slow 90 ms twitch, per ms
const TWITCH_ADD = 2.8;                    // millinewtons contributed by each impulse
let ahp = 0, force = 0, spikes = 0, fatigue = 1.0;
for (let ms = 0; ms < 3600000; ms++) {     // one hour of quiet standing
  if (EXCITATION - ahp >= THRESHOLD) {     // the long dip sets the rate, not the drive
    spikes++;
    ahp += AHP_STEP;
    force += TWITCH_ADD * fatigue;
    fatigue *= 0.9999998;                  // a slow unit barely fatigues at all
  }
  ahp *= AHP_DECAY;
  force *= TWITCH_DECAY;
  if (ms > 0 && ms % 600000 === 0) {
    console.log("t=" + (ms / 60000) + " min  force=" + force.toFixed(2) +
                " mN  rate=" + (spikes / (ms / 1000)).toFixed(1) + " Hz");
  }
}
console.log("hour total " + spikes + " impulses, force left " +
            (100 * fatigue).toFixed(2) + " percent of fresh");

The rate settles near 11 impulses per second because the afterhyperpolarisation, not the drive, decides when the next impulse may occur, and after roughly forty thousand impulses the unit has lost under one percent of its force.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the smallest alpha motor neuron, the first recruited, the last to die, and the only one still firing steadily an hour into a task.

An afterhyperpolarisation longer than 100 milliseconds, a twitch slower than 60 milliseconds and a force that has not measurably faded after an hour identify it beyond doubt.

CHAPTER 164. GAMMA MOTOR NEURONS, ALSO CALLED FUSIMOTOR NEURONS (Spindle-Tightening Motor Neuron) (NON-COG)

This little cell moves no bones and lifts no weight; it exists solely to stop your body's stretch sensors going blind at the moment you most need them.

Gamma is a Greek letter and fusimotor is jargon, so neither teaches.

Spindle names the target, the muscle spindle, which is the small stretch sensor of one to four millimetres holding six to fourteen fibres inside a capsule.

Tightening names the work, because contracting those internal fibres takes up slack so the sensor keeps reporting even while the whole muscle shortens.

Motor Neuron places it in the commanding family, and these cells make up about thirty percent of the motor neurons in the ventral horn, so this is a large and deliberate investment in keeping the body's stretch sensors honest.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The gamma motor neuron is a motor neuron whose muscle is only a sense organ: it commands intrafusal muscle fibres, the tiny specialised fibres inside a muscle spindle, which is the capsule-shaped stretch sensor buried among the working fibres of every skeletal muscle.

Those fibres add essentially nothing to the force of the muscle; pulling on their ends simply stretches the sensory middle of the spindle, and that solves an elegant problem, because a shortening muscle leaves its spindle slack and silent exactly when the brain most needs to know where the limb is.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small compact neuron with a modest spiny dendritic tree, sitting shoulder to shoulder with alpha motor neurons two or three times its diameter.

Its dendrites are strikingly bare of the large sensory terminals that coat alpha cells, and it is identified in the laboratory by that absence together with high levels of the nuclear receptors ERR3 and ERR2 and of the signalling protein Wnt7a, and low levels of the neuronal marker NeuN.

SIZE:

The cell body is 25 to 35 micrometres across, about half the diameter and roughly one eighth the volume of a typical alpha motor neuron.

Its axon is an A-gamma fibre of 3 to 8 micrometres including myelin, and its targets are minute, usually under 25 micrometres wide and a few millimetres long, packed about eight to a spindle.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two functional subtypes are firmly established, and a related cell provides a hard-wired alternative.

• Dynamic gamma motor neurons end on the dynamic nuclear bag fibre and sharpen the spindle's report of how fast the muscle is being stretched.

• Static gamma motor neurons end on the static nuclear bag fibre and the nuclear chain fibres and raise the spindle's steady report of muscle length, and they are the subject of the next entry.

• Beta motor neurons, which are not gamma neurons, supply ordinary and intrafusal fibres together and so couple force and sensor bias without any separate command.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Rexed lamina IX of the ventral horn, scattered among the alpha motor neurons of the very same motor pool serving the same muscle, at every segment from C1 to S5.

They cluster in the pools of spindle-rich muscles such as the deep neck muscles, the small muscles of the hand and the postural muscles of the back, and their critical interface is inside the muscle spindle capsule itself, where the smallest motor endings in the body sit within a millimetre of the sensory endings they adjust; their axons also cross the central to peripheral boundary at the ventral rootlet.

NUMBER:

About 60 thousand in the human spinal cord, which is about 0.03 percent of the roughly 200 million spinal neurons and 0.00007 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons of the whole nervous system.

Confidence is moderate and rests on a repeatedly confirmed observation that gamma cells are roughly 30 percent of ventral horn motor neurons, so 200 thousand alpha cells implies 60 to 80 thousand gamma cells; no direct human count exists and the true figure could be half again either way.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

High input resistance and low rheobase because the cell is small, yet it is not recruited before alpha cells in normal movement, because what it receives is a different set of inputs rather than a smaller share of the same ones.

It fires tonically at 10 to 30 impulses per second at rest and above 50 during movement, sustaining higher rates than a comparable alpha cell because its afterhyperpolarisation is briefer, and its discharge is smooth rather than bursty, suiting a cell that sets a bias rather than delivering a blow.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The defining negative is that it receives no monosynaptic group Ia input, so the stretch reflex cannot close a loop directly through it, which prevents a runaway cycle of sensor tightening.

Excitation comes from reticulospinal, vestibulospinal and rubrospinal pathways and from interneurons carrying skin and joint information, cortical drive reaches it only indirectly, which is why its output tracks intended effort rather than actual length, and Renshaw cells inhibit it alongside its alpha neighbours.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Conduction velocity 15 to 40 metres per second, fast for the body at large and slow for a motor nerve, so gamma commands arrive a few milliseconds behind alpha commands.

Gamma axons are around a third of all myelinated fibres in a ventral root, which is why counting root fibres overestimates the number of force-producing neurons, and each axon supplies several spindles, ending in small plate or trail endings rather than the large pretzel endplates of alpha cells.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine on nicotinic acetylcholine receptors of the intrafusal fibres.

Gamma cells carry the same choline acetyltransferase and vesicular acetylcholine transporter markers as alpha cells, which is exactly why they were mistaken for small alpha cells for decades and why molecular markers such as ERR3 were needed to separate them.

MYELINATION:

Thinly but genuinely myelinated, oligodendrocyte myelin inside the cord and Schwann cell myelin outside it.

Its thin axon presents little neuregulin-1 type III, so its Schwann cells build few wraps, and that is not a deficiency, because a slower cheaper cable is adequate for setting a slowly changing bias.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Perhaps an order of magnitude lower than an alpha motor neuron, since the cell is small, the axon thin and the targets microscopic.

Its firing is nonetheless continuous, so its daily impulse count can exceed that of a fast alpha cell, and its modest budget is one credible reason it survives diseases that kill its large neighbours.

PLASTICITY:

The fusimotor system is set task by task rather than learned synapse by synapse: people raise gamma drive when a movement is unfamiliar, difficult or unpredictable and lower it during smooth familiar movement.

Its development is instructed from the periphery, since gamma neurons never acquire their identity in animals lacking proprioceptive sensory neurons, meaning the sensor helps build the cell that will later adjust it, and after nerve injury mismatched reinnervation of spindles is one cause of the clumsiness that outlasts recovered strength.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Gamma cells are reported to lack the thick aggrecan perineuronal net that encircles about nine in ten alpha motor neurons, and that difference is one way anatomists tell the two apart in tissue.

Inside the spindle its endings sit in a fluid-filled capsule of flattened perineurial cells, a blood-free micro-environment found nowhere else in muscle, whose capsule cells and terminal Schwann cells supply the trophic factor GDNF that the developing gamma neuron requires; microglia largely spare these cells even in advanced motor neuron disease.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and nothing looks wrong until the person moves.

Strength, resting reflexes and standing posture would all be normal, but every active movement would blind the limb's own sensors, so the person could walk while watching their feet and would stumble badly in the dark, a milder version of the disabling clumsiness of someone who has lost position sense entirely; the ventral horn would keep its output and lose the ability to keep its input meaningful during action.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its whole purpose lies outside classical integrate-and-fire, because it contributes nothing to the network's output and instead sets the gain of an input.

Alpha-gamma coactivation splits one descending command into a force order and a sensor-bias order, so action and sensory prediction are generated by a single act, an arrangement engineers later rediscovered as feed-forward compensation; by tightening the spindle it sets the loop gain of the stretch reflex, and because its targets are so small, a handful of impulses can change what the whole nervous system believes about where a limb is.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: reticulospinal, vestibulospinal and rubrospinal drive 0 to 20 nanoamps; skin and joint interneuron input; Renshaw inhibition 0 to minus 10 nanoamps; explicitly no monosynaptic group Ia input.

• OUTPUTS: a tonic train of 0 to 80 hertz to intrafusal fibres, which changes the firing of group Ia and group II afferents rather than producing force.

• STATE: membrane voltage; afterhyperpolarisation level; fusimotor set level, a slow variable holding the current task-dependent bias.

• PARAMETERS: cell body 25 to 35 micrometres; conduction velocity 15 to 40 metres per second; equatorial bias about 0.003 strain units per hertz of drive; population share 30 percent of ventral horn motor neurons.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 8 to 15 milliseconds; intrafusal contraction 50 to 200 milliseconds, far slower than ordinary muscle; fusimotor set changes over seconds as the task changes.

• FAILURE MODE: too little drive gives sensory silence during movement and clumsiness; too much gives an over-sensitive stretch reflex and contributes to spasticity and some dystonias; in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) these cells survive while alpha cells die, and their continued drive on a failing system appears to make the disease worse.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to keep a muscle spindle from falling silent while its muscle shortens, by driving the spindle poles in step with the alpha motor neurons.
Create a number variable called muscle length with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called alpha drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called gamma drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called pole bias with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called equator strain with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called afferent rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called silent milliseconds with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 2000 times.
    Set alpha drive to the fraction of maximum effort commanded this millisecond.
    Set muscle length to the measured length of the muscle this millisecond.
    Calculate the product of alpha drive and 60.
    Set gamma drive to the result.
    Calculate the product of gamma drive and 0.003.
    Set pole bias to the result.
    Calculate the difference of muscle length and 1.
    Set equator strain to the result.
    Calculate the sum of equator strain and pole bias.
    Set equator strain to the result.
    Calculate the product of equator strain and 90.
    Set afferent rate to the result.
    If afferent rate is less than or equal to 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set afferent rate to 0.
        Calculate the sum of silent milliseconds and 1.
        Set silent milliseconds to the result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display silent milliseconds to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the whole point is a side-by-side comparison of two runs, one with fusimotor drive and one without, and Python keeps that comparison in plain sight.

DT = 0.001                  # seconds per step
STATIC_GAIN = 90.0          # afferent impulses per second per unit of equatorial strain
BIAS_PER_HZ = 0.003         # strain added to the sensory equator per hertz of gamma drive
def muscle_length(t):
    if t < 0.5:
        return 1.00                          # holding still
    if t < 1.5:
        return 1.00 - 0.15 * (t - 0.5)       # shortening by 15 percent over one second
    return 0.85                              # holding at the short length
def run(gamma_hz):
    rates = []
    for step in range(2000):                 # two seconds, one millisecond per step
        t = step * DT
        strain = (muscle_length(t) - 1.0) + BIAS_PER_HZ * gamma_hz
        rates.append(max(STATIC_GAIN * strain, 0.0))   # a sensor cannot fire below zero
    return rates
for gamma_hz in (0.0, 60.0):
    r = run(gamma_hz)
    silent = sum(1 for x in r if x == 0.0)
    print("gamma %4.0f Hz: rest %5.1f Hz, mid-shortening %5.1f Hz, silent for %4d ms"
          % (gamma_hz, r[250], r[1000], silent))

With no gamma drive the spindle is silent for the whole two seconds; with 60 impulses per second of drive it reports about 16 impulses per second at rest and is still reporting about 9 in the middle of the shortening, which is the entire reason this cell exists.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only motor neuron that makes no movement.

Look for a small cholinergic cell in lamina IX with no group Ia contacts on its dendrites, a thin slow axon, the ERR3 marker in its nucleus, and a target you need a microscope to find.

CHAPTER 165. STATIC GAMMA MOTOR NEURONS (Length-Sense Sharpening Motor Neuron) (NON-COG)

This is the cell that decides whether your muscle sensors behave like a ruler or like a speedometer, and it chooses the ruler.

Static is a physics word applied to a cell and tells a reader nothing about what is being preserved.

Length-Sense names what this cell protects, the spindle's steady report of how long the muscle currently is.

Sharpening names the action, since by contracting the nuclear bag2 and nuclear chain fibres it raises the sensor's steady-state output.

Motor Neuron keeps the family, and the anatomy is specific rather than vague, since the static axon innervates the chain and bag2 fibres and it is those fibres that mediate length sensitivity to stretch.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The static gamma motor neuron is the subtype of fusimotor neuron that biases the muscle spindle towards reporting position rather than speed.

It ends on the static nuclear bag fibre, usually called the bag2 fibre, and on the nuclear chain fibres, and driving them raises the ongoing background firing of the spindle's sensory endings while blunting their response to the velocity of a stretch, giving a sensor that keeps talking clearly at every length, which is what posture and slow deliberate movement need.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small motor neuron indistinguishable from a dynamic gamma neuron under a routine stain, identified by where its axon ends rather than by how its cell body looks.

Its terminals are trail endings, long diffuse strings of small contacts running along the intrafusal fibre, unlike the compact plate endings that dynamic gamma axons make, and that spread-out arrangement produces a slow well-fused contraction rather than a localised twitch.

SIZE:

Cell body 25 to 35 micrometres, within the gamma range and about half the diameter of an alpha motor neuron.

Axon 3 to 6 micrometres including myelin, in most surveys slightly thinner and slower than a dynamic gamma axon, and its targets are the smallest muscle fibres in the body, with chain fibres under 15 micrometres across, about five times thinner than a human hair.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two working subdivisions are recognised, based on which intrafusal fibre an axon prefers, and many axons supply both, so this is a spectrum rather than a clean pair.

• Chain-preferring static axons act almost entirely on nuclear chain fibres and produce a strong fast-following rise in the firing of the spindle's secondary endings with almost no time lag.

• Bag2-preferring static axons act on the static bag fibre and bias the sensory endings more weakly and with a marked lag.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Rexed lamina IX of the ventral horn, intermingled with alpha and dynamic gamma cells in the motor pool of the same muscle, at all levels from C1 to S5.
