They are relatively concentrated in the pools of spindle-rich postural and axial muscles, including the deep neck muscles, the small muscles of the back and the leg extensors, and their functional interface is the equatorial region of the spindle, the narrow middle zone where the sensory endings wrap the intrafusal fibres and feel this cell's pull; the axon crosses the central to peripheral boundary at the ventral rootlet like any motor axon.

NUMBER:

Roughly 35 to 40 thousand in the human spinal cord, the larger share of the 60 thousand gamma motor neurons, which is about 0.02 percent of the roughly 200 million spinal neurons and 0.00005 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons of the whole nervous system.

Confidence is low, because the split rests on cat physiology, where static fusimotor axons outnumber dynamic ones about two to one, with no direct human count; a simulation should treat static and dynamic gamma cells as dividing one 60 thousand population, not as two extra populations.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A small cell with high input resistance, low rheobase and a short afterhyperpolarisation that permits high sustained rates.

Many static gamma neurons are spontaneously active at 10 to 30 impulses per second even in a relaxed limb, which is why a resting spindle has any background discharge at all, and their rates climb with the effort and the slowness of a movement, tracking the size of the intended posture change rather than its speed.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Like all gamma neurons it receives no monosynaptic group Ia input, which keeps the fusimotor loop from closing on itself.

Its main excitation is reticulospinal and vestibulospinal, so it is driven hardest by the systems that manage posture and balance, skin input and flexor reflex interneurons can raise its output sharply, which is part of why standing on an unstable surface changes how the whole limb feels, and Renshaw cells inhibit it alongside its alpha neighbours.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Conduction velocity 15 to 35 metres per second, at the slower end of the fusimotor range.

One axon usually supplies several spindles in the same muscle and branches within each capsule to several chain fibres, so a single cell biases a whole neighbourhood of sensors, and its trail endings spread along the intrafusal fibre rather than concentrating at one point, which is the anatomical reason its contractions are smooth.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine at nicotinic acetylcholine receptors on the intrafusal fibres, with the same synthetic machinery as any motor neuron.

Because trail endings are diffuse and the target fibres are tiny, transmission produces a sustained well-fused contraction at quite low firing rates, unlike the twitch-by-twitch behaviour of ordinary muscle.

MYELINATION:

Thinly myelinated, oligodendrocyte myelin inside the cord and Schwann cell myelin outside, with few wraps because the axon presents little neuregulin-1 type III.

Internodes are short, on the order of a few hundred micrometres, matching the thin axon.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Among the lowest of any motor neuron, with a small cell body, a thin axon and microscopic targets.

Its continuous background firing means it is never fully idle, so its cost is small but uninterrupted, more like a pilot light than a furnace, and the intrafusal fibres it drives are themselves rich in mitochondria for their size because they too must contract for hours.

PLASTICITY:

Its output is set by task rather than by long-term change, rising during steady posture, slow deliberate movement and precision holding, and falling when a movement becomes fast or ballistic.

Recordings in awake animals show static and dynamic drive can be adjusted independently, so the nervous system genuinely chooses its sensor characteristics moment to moment, while long-term change is mostly indirect, through the health of the spindle and the age-related loss of intrafusal fibres that contributes to poor balance in older people.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The specialised support here is the muscle spindle capsule itself, an enclosed fluid-filled compartment maintained by flattened perineurial cells that shields the endings from the mechanical chaos of the surrounding muscle.

Within it the terminal Schwann cells covering the trail endings are unusually elongated, matching the diffuse ending pattern, while in the spinal cord itself nothing special is known about this cell's astrocytes or microglia, which treat it much as they treat any small ventral horn neuron.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and the spindle becomes a speedometer with no odometer.

The stretch reflex would still answer a tap or a sudden pull, but the steady background report of how long a muscle currently is would fade, so people would still catch a falling object yet drift out of a held posture without noticing and sway when they closed their eyes; the ventral horn would be left reacting to changes rather than knowing states.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell performs an unusual computation, changing the transfer function of a sensor rather than adding to a signal, so its influence is multiplicative rather than additive.

By raising the tonic firing of group Ia and group II afferents it also sets the gain of every spinal circuit those afferents feed, including reciprocal inhibition and the locomotor networks; the fibres it drives are mechanically coupled inside the capsule, so contracting a chain fibre changes the tension felt by endings on neighbouring fibres, a purely mechanical crosstalk between sensory channels with no synapse involved, and it couples the group Ia and group II endings of one spindle so that both report together.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: reticulospinal and vestibulospinal drive 0 to 20 nanoamps; skin and flexor reflex interneuron input; Renshaw inhibition; a task-level static fusimotor set command.

• OUTPUTS: a tonic train of 0 to 80 hertz to bag2 and chain fibres, which raises the static gain and lowers the velocity gain of group Ia afferents and raises the firing of group II afferents.

• STATE: membrane voltage; afterhyperpolarisation level; the equatorial bias currently imposed; intrafusal contraction level, which lags the impulse train.

• PARAMETERS: equatorial bias about 0.003 strain units per hertz; static gain rising from about 90 to about 123 impulses per second per strain unit at 60 hertz of drive; velocity gain falling from about 200 to about 116 at the same drive; population share roughly two thirds of gamma neurons.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 8 to 15 milliseconds; chain fibre contraction 50 to 100 milliseconds; bag2 fibre contraction 150 to 300 milliseconds; task-level changes over seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: excessive static drive makes a spindle report high tension at every length and contributes to rigidity and to the exaggerated tendon reflexes seen after stroke and spinal injury; loss of static drive, and loss of spindles with age, degrades position sense and balance while leaving strength intact.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to show how a static gamma motor neuron firing at sixty impulses per second changes what a muscle spindle reports during a slow stretch.
Create a number variable called bias with initial value 0.18.
Create a number variable called static gain with initial value 123.
Create a number variable called dynamic gain with initial value 116.
Create a number variable called strain with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called velocity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called working value with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called afferent rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called resting rate with initial value 0.
Calculate the product of bias and static gain.
Set resting rate to the result.
Display resting rate to the console.
Repeat the following steps exactly 2000 times.
    Set velocity to the stretch velocity of the muscle in length fractions per second.
    Calculate the quotient of velocity divided by 1000.
    Set working value to the result.
    Calculate the sum of strain and working value.
    Set strain to the result.
    Calculate the sum of strain and bias.
    Set working value to the result.
    Calculate the product of working value and static gain.
    Set afferent rate to the result.
    Calculate the product of velocity and dynamic gain.
    Set working value to the result.
    Calculate the sum of afferent rate and working value.
    Set afferent rate to the result.
End the loop.
Display afferent rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because the useful result is a table of three conditions compared on identical arithmetic, and C forces every gain and every unit to be written down explicitly.

#include <stdio.h>
static double ia_rate(double strain, double velocity,
                      double static_hz, double dynamic_hz) {
    double bias   = 0.0030 * static_hz + 0.0008 * dynamic_hz;    /* pull on the equator */
    double stat_g = 90.0 + 0.55 * static_hz;                     /* static drive lifts tonic gain */
    double dyn_g  = 200.0 + 6.0 * dynamic_hz - 1.4 * static_hz;  /* and damps velocity gain */
    if (dyn_g < 0.0) dyn_g = 0.0;
    double r = stat_g * (strain + bias) + dyn_g * (velocity > 0.0 ? velocity : 0.0);
    return r > 0.0 ? r : 0.0;                                    /* a sensor cannot fire below zero */
}
int main(void) {
    const double dt = 0.001, ramp_speed = 0.10;   /* stretch at 10 percent of length per second */
    double conditions[3][2] = { {0, 0}, {60, 0}, {0, 60} };      /* static Hz, dynamic Hz */
    const char *label[3] = {"no fusimotor drive", "static gamma 60 Hz", "dynamic gamma 60 Hz"};
    for (int c = 0; c < 3; c++) {
        double strain = 0.0, peak = 0.0, plateau = 0.0;
        double resting = ia_rate(0.0, 0.0, conditions[c][0], conditions[c][1]);
        for (int step = 0; step < 2000; step++) {                /* two seconds */
            double t = step * dt;
            double velocity = (t >= 0.5 && t < 1.5) ? ramp_speed : 0.0;
            strain += velocity * dt;
            double r = ia_rate(strain, velocity, conditions[c][0], conditions[c][1]);
            if (r > peak) peak = r;
            if (step == 1900) plateau = r;                       /* held at the new length */
        }
        printf("%-22s rest %6.1f Hz  peak %6.1f Hz  hold %6.1f Hz\n",
               label[c], resting, peak, plateau);
    }
    return 0;
}

The table makes the division of labour plain: static drive lifts the resting rate from 0 to about 22 impulses per second and the held rate from 9 to about 34 while shrinking the extra firing during the stretch, whereas dynamic drive leaves resting and held rates almost untouched and more than doubles the peak.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Stimulate a single fusimotor axon during a stretch, and if the spindle's steady firing rises while its response to the speed of stretch falls, the axon is static.

No other cell in the body raises a sensor's baseline and lowers its sensitivity to change at the same time, and none other ends in the trail endings that wrap the nuclear chain fibres.

CHAPTER 166. DYNAMIC GAMMA MOTOR NEURONS (Speed-Sense Sharpening Motor Neuron) (NON-COG)

There is a muscle inside your muscle, and this tiny cell is the only thing that tunes it for speed.

Dynamic is the matching physics word and is equally uninformative on its own.

Speed-Sense names the other half of what a spindle reports, not how long the muscle is but how fast it is being stretched.

Sharpening is shared with its partner cell so the pair reads as one set differing on a single axis, sensitivity to position against sensitivity to velocity.

Motor Neuron keeps the family, and the claim is anatomically grounded, since the dynamic axon is essentially restricted to the nuclear bag1 fibre and it is the bag1 fibre that mediates the spindle's velocity sensitivity.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Its output wire, the axon, runs out to a muscle but never touches the fibres that pull, gripping instead one fibre inside a muscle spindle, the capsule of modified fibres wrapped in sensory endings that is the muscle's own stretch sensor.

Squeezing the ends of that nuclear bag one fibre makes the spindle report speed of stretch far more strongly, so this cell is a gain knob for velocity that moves nothing.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small round cell body with a sparse tree of input branches, called dendrites, sitting among far larger alpha motor neurons yet bare of the big sensory swellings that stud them, and marked by the transcription factor Err3.

SIZE:

The cell body is fifteen to thirty micrometres across against fifty to one hundred for an alpha motor neuron, and its axon is two to eight micrometres thick, conducting at four to twenty-four metres per second.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Classical anatomy recognises trail endings and two plate endings, and this cell forms the plate two ending on the bag one poles, serving one to three spindles.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in lamina nine, the motor cell layer of the ventral horn, wherever muscle carries spindles: cervical one to eight, all twelve thoracic segments, lumbar one to five and sacral one to two, densest in the cervical enlargement from the fifth cervical to the first thoracic segment and the lumbosacral enlargement from the second lumbar to the second sacral segment.

It straddles two interfaces, the grey matter boundary at the ventral root exit zone, and the functional boundary between motor and sensory systems.

NUMBER:

The human cord holds about sixty thousand gamma motor neurons, of which the dynamic class is the minority, best estimated at twenty thousand, range fifteen to thirty thousand, at low to moderate confidence because the figure is inferred from animals.

That is one hundredth of one percent of the two hundred million spinal neurons, and it fits the rough total of fifty thousand human spindles, each with one bag one fibre.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Small size gives high input resistance, meaning a tiny current swings its voltage far, so little drive reaches threshold near minus fifty millivolts, and it fires tonically at ten to fifty spikes per second, held steady during walking rather than pulsed like its static cousins.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitation descends from the reticulospinal, vestibulospinal and rubrospinal tracts, corticospinal influence arrives through interneurons, and Renshaw cells inhibit it.

It receives almost no direct spindle sensory contacts, shown by absent vesicular glutamate transporter one boutons, and its only output is the neuromuscular junction on the bag one pole.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

It leaves by the ventral root and runs the whole peripheral nerve without one branch to force-producing muscle, taking forty to fifty milliseconds to reach a foot.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine on nicotinic receptors of the intrafusal fibre is the sole fast transmitter, and these cells carry far less of the trophic co-transmitter calcitonin gene-related peptide than alpha motor neurons.

MYELINATION:

Thinly myelinated, by Schwann cells outside the cord and oligodendrocytes within, with short sheath segments giving the modest speed above.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low, perhaps a fiftieth of an alpha motor neuron's budget, but relentless, since fusimotor tone never switches off in waking life.

PLASTICITY:

The fusimotor set is reset within seconds by task, posture, fatigue and anxiety, and in development these cells die without a sustaining signal from the spindle.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Glia inside the cord are ordinary with one exception: the dense perineuronal net encasing alpha motor neurons is largely absent here, leaving this surface open.

At the far end the terminal sits within the spindle's inner capsule, a sleeve of flattened cells making a private fluid space shared with the sensory ending.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete it and the ventral horn still moves the limb, but the cord goes velocity-blind.

The snap of a tendon jerk, the catch when a tray is loaded and the early correction when a foot lands on a slope all fade.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its output is not force but a change in another cell's measuring instrument, and because the bag one fibre contracts slowly and without propagating, that change builds over about one hundred and fifty milliseconds.

Motor neurons here are coupled by connexin thirty-six gap junctions early in life, spilled acetylcholine reaches muscarinic receptors away from the release site inside the sealed capsule, and lifelong dependence on retrograde spindle signals means damage to the sensor eventually kills its controller.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Descending fusimotor command, reticulospinal and vestibulospinal, zero to one hundred spikes per second, and Renshaw inhibition, subtractive.

◦ No group Ia sensory input, modelled as an explicit absence.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Bag one stiffness fraction, zero to one, and thereby the velocity gain of that spindle's group Ia afferent.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, firing rate, bag one stiffness.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Input resistance one hundred to three hundred megohms, threshold minus fifty millivolts, conduction four to twenty-four metres per second, maximum velocity gain about sixty afferent spikes per second per unit stretch velocity.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane eight to fifteen milliseconds, bag one stiffening one hundred and fifty milliseconds, fusimotor set seconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ These cells survive in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis while alpha motor neurons die, so the gamma loop keeps driving a shrinking pool.

◦ Too little drive gives hypotonia, too much contributes to rigidity and clonus.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute how one dynamic gamma motor neuron sets the velocity sensitivity of a muscle spindle primary ending.
Create a number variable called gamma firing rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called bag stiffness with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called stretch velocity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive fraction with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called afferent rate with initial value 0.
Set gamma firing rate to the descending fusimotor command in spikes per second.
Repeat the following steps exactly 600 times.
    Set stretch velocity to the muscle lengthening speed at this step.
    Set drive fraction to the quotient of gamma firing rate divided by 100.
    Set bag stiffness to the sum of bag stiffness and the product of the difference of drive fraction and bag stiffness and 0.00667.
    Set afferent rate to the sum of 20 and the product of bag stiffness and the product of 60 and stretch velocity.
End the loop.
Display afferent rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the arithmetic is plain and needs no libraries.

# Dynamic fusimotor gain: gamma neuron, bag1 fibre, Ia ending.
DT = 0.001            # seconds per step
TAU_BAG = 0.150       # bag1 stiffening time constant
K_LENGTH = 20.0       # Ia spikes/s per unit length
K_VEL_MAX = 60.0      # extra Ia spikes/s per unit velocity, full drive

def ramp(gamma_rate):
    stiff, length, peak, plateau = 0.0, 1.0, 0.0, 0.0
    for step in range(600):
        t = step * DT
        vel = 0.5 if 0.100 <= t < 0.300 else 0.0   # a 200 ms ramp stretch
        length += vel * DT
        stiff += (min(gamma_rate / 100.0, 1.0) - stiff) * (DT / TAU_BAG)
        rate = K_LENGTH * length + K_VEL_MAX * stiff * vel
        peak = max(peak, rate) if t < 0.300 else peak
        plateau = rate if t > 0.500 else plateau
    return peak, plateau

for drive in (0.0, 50.0, 100.0):
    peak, plateau = ramp(drive)
    print("gamma %5.1f Hz -> dynamic index %6.2f Hz" % (drive, peak - plateau))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Stimulate it and a stretch sensor's velocity signal climbs steeply while a force transducer on the tendon reads exactly zero.

CHAPTER 167. BETA MOTOR NEURONS, ALSO CALLED SKELETOFUSIMOTOR NEURONS (Move-And-Monitor Motor Neuron) (NON-COG)

Evolution's cheapest trick lives here: one axon that pulls the rope and adjusts the instrument measuring the rope.

Beta is a Greek letter and skeletofusimotor is a compound of two Latin roots, so both hide the one genuinely striking fact about this cell, which is that a single axon branches to both ordinary muscle fibres and the sensor fibres inside a spindle.

Move names the first branch and Monitor the second, so one command shortens the muscle and simultaneously keeps its stretch sensor loaded.

Motor Neuron keeps the family, and this is not a rare curiosity, since perhaps twenty to thirty percent of spindles receive a branch from a large efferent axon bound for ordinary muscle.

Beyond this dual innervation and a conduction velocity higher than either alpha or gamma cells, little about these cells is firmly established, and the name deliberately claims no more.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Its single axon splits near the muscle, some branches ending on ordinary force-producing fibres, called extrafusal because they lie outside the spindle capsule, and others on the modified fibres inside a muscle spindle, the stretch sensor.

So every command that makes the muscle pull also, in the same instant and in proportion, tightens the sensor, keeping it reporting while the muscle shortens.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It looks exactly like a large alpha motor neuron and can be identified only by tracing the axon and finding one fibre serving both target types, which is why it is still under-counted.

SIZE:

The cell body is forty to eighty micrometres across in humans, and the axon is thickly myelinated at eight to sixteen micrometres, conducting at fifty to one hundred metres per second.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Dynamic beta neurons end on the nuclear bag one fibre as plate one endings and boost velocity sensitivity, while static beta neurons end on nuclear chain fibres and boost length sensitivity.

In cat peroneus brevis twenty-nine of thirty-four beta axons were dynamic and five static, while in peroneus tertius the split was thirteen to twelve, so the mix is muscle-specific.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives inside ordinary motor pools of lamina nine of the ventral horn, mixed among alpha and gamma motor neurons through every cervical, thoracic, lumbar and sacral segment supplying skeletal muscle, and cat data show the trait is commoner distally, where over seventy percent of spindles receive a beta axon, than proximally, where the figure is forty-one to forty-seven percent.

Its interface is internal, since this one cell is where the skeletomotor and fusimotor systems meet.

NUMBER:

Human beta motor neurons have never been counted, and they are not extra but a subset already inside the roughly two hundred thousand large motor neurons.

The cat finding that eighteen percent of extrafusal-supplying axons in one muscle were beta gives a bracket of ten to forty thousand, half of one hundredth of one percent of the two hundred million spinal neurons, at low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Dynamic beta neurons serve slow, fatigue-resistant units and share that signature: low rheobase, meaning little current fires them, an afterhyperpolarisation up to one hundred milliseconds capping their rate, steady discharge at five to thirty spikes per second, and persistent inward currents that turn a brief input into a long plateau.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Its inputs are the full alpha set: monosynaptic group Ia fibres from spindles, group two fibres, corticospinal, reticulospinal and vestibulospinal tracts, Renshaw cells and Ia inhibitory interneurons.

Its outputs are two kinds of neuromuscular junction, endplates on force fibres and intrafusal endings inside the capsule, driven by one indivisible spike train.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A single fast myelinated axon leaves the ventral root and branches deep in the muscle, and because both branch classes carry identical spikes the cell cannot separate force from sensor bias.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine at every terminal on nicotinic receptors of both fibre types, with calcitonin gene-related peptide as a slower trophic co-transmitter maintaining the endplate.

MYELINATION:

Heavy, with thick sheaths and internodes of one to one and a half millimetres, so force and sensor bias arrive together.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High, matching an alpha motor neuron, because a long thick axon and a large terminal field make such cells among the most expensive in the body.

PLASTICITY:

Beta innervation is not fixed, and after nerve injury regenerating motor axons frequently stray into spindles and create skeletofusimotor connections that were never there before.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Inside the cord the glia match those of alpha motor neurons, with a dense perineuronal net and astrocytes shuttling lactate.

At the periphery it is unique in having two terminal Schwann cell environments at once, one at the endplate in open muscle and one inside the sealed fluid compartment of the spindle capsule.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and the motor pool loses its free, zero-latency insurance policy.

Every time a muscle shortened its spindles would go slack and silent unless the brain had predicted exactly the right separate gamma drive, so movement would turn sensorless at the moment it mattered.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The computation is at the axonal branch point, where one spike train becomes force in one place and a change of measurement gain in the other.

That closes a positive feedback loop, since contraction drives the sensor, the sensor excites the motor neuron monosynaptically, and the motor neuron drives more contraction, a loop that must be damped in simulation or it runs away.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Monosynaptic group Ia excitation, zero to one hundred spikes per second per afferent, plus descending drive, zero to fifty spikes per second.

◦ Renshaw and Ia inhibitory interneuron inhibition, subtractive.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Extrafusal force, zero to one in units of peak tetanic force, and intrafusal bias on bag one or chain fibres, zero to one.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, firing rate, extrafusal activation, intrafusal stiffness.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Rheobase about five nanoamperes, afterhyperpolarisation one hundred milliseconds, conduction fifty to one hundred metres per second, extrafusal to intrafusal branch ratio about ten to one.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane five to ten milliseconds, twitch fusion forty milliseconds, intrafusal stiffening one hundred and fifty milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In amyotrophic lateral sclerosis these cells die with the alpha population, so spindle feedback collapses faster than motor unit loss alone predicts.

◦ Aberrant beta connections after regeneration create sensor signals uncoupled from real length.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn one beta motor neuron spike train into simultaneous extrafusal force and intrafusal sensor bias.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called activation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called extrafusal force with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called intrafusal stiffness with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spindle output with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 500 times.
    Set firing rate to the descending motor command for this step.
    Set activation to the quotient of firing rate divided by the sum of firing rate and 20.
    Set extrafusal force to the sum of extrafusal force and the product of the difference of activation and extrafusal force and 0.025.
    Set intrafusal stiffness to the sum of intrafusal stiffness and the product of the difference of activation and intrafusal stiffness and 0.00667.
    Set spindle output to the sum of 20 and the product of intrafusal stiffness and 40.
    Check if extrafusal force is greater than 0.95.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Display spindle output to the console.
        Break out of the current loop immediately.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display extrafusal force to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because this is the tight fixed-step loop a real-time muscle plant runs.

/* Beta motor neuron: one spike train, two targets. */
#include <stdio.h>

#define DT        0.001    /* seconds per step */
#define TAU_FORCE 0.040    /* extrafusal twitch fusion time constant, seconds */
#define TAU_BAG   0.150    /* intrafusal stiffening time constant, seconds */
#define HALF_RATE 20.0     /* rate giving half maximal activation, Hz */

int main(void) {
    double force = 0.0, stiff = 0.0;
    int step;
    for (step = 0; step < 500; step++) {
        double t = step * DT;
        double rate = (t >= 0.100 && t < 0.400) ? 30.0 : 0.0;
        double activation = rate / (rate + HALF_RATE);
        force += (activation - force) * (DT / TAU_FORCE);
        stiff += (activation - stiff) * (DT / TAU_BAG);
        if (step % 100 == 0)
            printf("t=%.3f s  rate=%5.1f Hz  force=%.3f  bag1=%.3f\n",
                   t, rate, force, stiff);
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Block ordinary muscle endplates with a drug, stimulate the axon, and the spindle keeps being driven although the muscle no longer twitches, which no alpha or gamma cell can do.

CHAPTER 168. SYMPATHETIC PREGANGLIONIC NEURONS (NON-COG)

Every time you stand up and do not faint, a slim column of small cells hidden in the side wing of your spinal cord has already done the arithmetic.

This chapter merges the two entries that previously described this cell, one filed with the spinal cord and one with the autonomic ganglia, since the cell body lies in the spinal cord and only its axon reaches the ganglion.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It is the spinal output cell of the sympathetic nervous system, the automatic branch that prepares the body for effort and emergency, and it hands its message to a second neuron in a ganglion, a cluster of nerve cells outside the cord.

Through that relay it sets the calibre of every artery, the rate and force of the heart, sweating, pupil size and the release of adrenaline.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An oval multipolar cell whose long dendrites run head to tail along the column, binding neighbours into bundles that sample the same descending fibres and behave as one unit.

SIZE:

In human cord the cell bodies have a median diameter near fourteen micrometres and a mean cross-sectional area of about four hundred square micrometres, measured as four hundred point seven at the third thoracic segment and four hundred and nine point nine at the seventh.

The axon is a thin myelinated group B fibre under three micrometres, conducting at three to fifteen metres per second.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Four anatomical groupings exist: the principal intermediolateral nucleus, the funicular part out in white matter, the intercalated nucleus, and the central autonomic nucleus by the central canal.

Functionally they split into muscle vasoconstrictor, skin vasoconstrictor, sudomotor, pilomotor, cardiac accelerator, visceromotor and sympathoadrenal cells.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It occupies lamina seven in the lateral horn from the first thoracic to the second lumbar segment, sometimes reaching the third, in rounded clusters of up to thirty neurons stacked along the cord.

It straddles two interfaces, the grey and white matter border, since the funicular subgroup sits inside the lateral white matter, and the boundary between central nervous system and peripheral ganglia, which it alone crosses.

NUMBER:

There is no reliable stereological count in humans, so only a bound is honest: clusters of thirty repeated along about twenty segments on both sides, and a divergence ratio of ten to two hundred ganglion cells per preganglionic cell against several million ganglion neurons.

Both routes point to one hundred thousand to five hundred thousand, roughly five hundredths to one quarter of one percent of the two hundred million spinal neurons, at low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting near minus sixty millivolts with input resistance in the hundreds of megohms, it fires slowly and irregularly under five spikes per second, locked to the heartbeat and modulated by breathing, giving the two to six hertz rhythm recordable from a sympathetic nerve.

A transient potassium current delays its first spike, making it an integrator rather than an instant repeater.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitation descends from the rostral ventrolateral medulla, the brainstem region setting resting blood pressure, with A5 noradrenergic cells, raphe serotonergic cells, the paraventricular hypothalamic nucleus and orexin cells, plus lumbar V3 interneurons of the walking network.

Its output is a fast nicotinic synapse onto chain ganglia, prevertebral ganglia such as the coeliac and mesenteric, and adrenal chromaffin cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

It leaves in the ventral root, turns aside as the white ramus communicans, may travel many segments along the chain, and diverges onto ten to two hundred ganglion cells, so it is an amplifier rather than a relay.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine on nicotinic receptors carries the fast signal with a slower muscarinic component, and enkephalin, neurotensin, substance P and nitric oxide appear as co-transmitters in defined subsets.

MYELINATION:

Thinly myelinated, which is why the white ramus looks white while the grey ramus of unmyelinated fibres looks grey, and the sheath is lost at the ganglion.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest per cell, but the population never rests, sustaining output through sleep, anaesthesia and starvation.

PLASTICITY:

After spinal cord injury above the sixth thoracic segment these cells lose brainstem drive and their human cell bodies shrink, then over weeks turn hyperexcitable as afferents sprout onto them, producing autonomic dysreflexia in which a full bladder drives blood pressure to dangerous heights.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here ensheath whole head-to-tail dendritic bundles as single units rather than wrapping individual synapses, supporting coordinated behaviour across a cluster.

The funicular subgroup is also one of the few places where neuronal cell bodies live surrounded by myelinated tracts and oligodendrocytes.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take these cells out and the thoracic cord becomes a purely somatic machine for moving ribs and trunk.

The region loses its slow cardiac-locked hum, and the body loses blood pressure control on standing, sweating and the adrenaline surge, so a patient would faint on sitting up.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Connexin thirty-six gap junctions couple these cells electrically, letting a cluster synchronise and generate the bursting rhythm rather than firing independently.

They respond directly to circulating angiotensin two and aldosterone with no synapse involved, receive descending noradrenaline and serotonin partly by diffusion through the extracellular space, and through the splenic nerve they join the cholinergic anti-inflammatory pathway that restrains the immune system.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Rostral ventrolateral medulla glutamatergic drive, zero to twenty spikes per second, and baroreceptor error in millimetres of mercury.

◦ Respiratory phase, zero point one to zero point five hertz, multiplicative, plus hormonal angiotensin two.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Nicotinic excitation of ten to two hundred postganglionic neurons per axon, and adrenal chromaffin secretion in the sympathoadrenal subset.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, potassium channel availability, firing rate, coupled cluster phase.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential minus sixty millivolts, input resistance one hundred to five hundred megohms, baseline rate zero point five to five spikes per second, soma area about four hundred and ten square micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane twenty to forty milliseconds, baroreflex loop about one second, hormonal modulation minutes.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Multiple system atrophy destroys more than half of these lateral horn cells, producing severe orthostatic hypotension.

◦ Spinal cord injury above the sixth thoracic segment leaves them disconnected and hyperexcitable, causing autonomic dysreflexia.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the firing rate of one sympathetic preganglionic neuron under baroreflex and respiratory control.
Create a number variable called set point pressure with initial value 100.
Create a number variable called arterial pressure with initial value 100.
Create a number variable called pressure error with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called reflex gain with initial value 0.25.
Create a number variable called respiratory factor with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 400 times.
    Set arterial pressure to the mean arterial pressure measured at this step.
    Set pressure error to the difference of set point pressure and arterial pressure.
    Set firing rate to the sum of 2 and the product of reflex gain and pressure error.
    Set respiratory factor to the inspiratory gating value between 0.4 and 1.6.
    Set firing rate to the product of firing rate and respiratory factor.
    Check if firing rate is less than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if firing rate is greater than 12.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 12.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display firing rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the baroreflex is a short control law that reads like the textbook sentence it encodes.

# One sympathetic preganglionic neuron under baroreflex and respiratory control.
import math

DT = 0.010         # seconds per step
SET_POINT = 100.0  # target mean arterial pressure, mmHg
GAIN = 0.25        # spikes/s per mmHg of error
BASELINE = 2.0     # tonic firing with no error, spikes/s
MAX_RATE = 12.0    # ceiling on firing, spikes/s
RESP_DEPTH = 0.6   # depth of respiratory modulation

def rate_of(pressure, phase):
    drive = BASELINE + GAIN * (SET_POINT - pressure)   # low pressure excites
    drive *= 1.0 + RESP_DEPTH * math.sin(phase)        # inspiratory gating
    return max(0.0, min(drive, MAX_RATE))

pressure = 100.0
for step in range(400):
    t = step * DT
    if t >= 1.0:
        pressure = 70.0                    # head up tilt at one second
    rate = rate_of(pressure, 2.0 * math.pi * 0.25 * t)
    if step % 50 == 0:
        print("t=%4.2f s  %5.1f mmHg  %5.2f Hz" % (t, pressure, rate))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron whose body lies in the lateral horn between the first thoracic and second lumbar segments, makes acetylcholine, leaves by the white ramus communicans, and fires in time with the heartbeat.

CHAPTER 169. SACRAL PARASYMPATHETIC PREGANGLIONIC NEURONS (NON-COG)

The reason you can decide when to empty your bladder, rather than having your bladder decide for you, is a short column of cells at the very bottom of your spinal cord.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It is the spinal output cell of the sacral parasympathetic system, the rest-and-restore branch serving the pelvic organs, and its axon leaves in the pelvic splanchnic nerves to reach ganglia in the pelvic plexus and in the organ walls themselves.

Through that relay it empties the bladder, drives the last of the bowel and produces the vessel dilation of erection.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small multipolar cell whose dendrites fan three ways, laterally into the white matter of the lateral funiculus, dorsally toward lamina one where bladder sensory fibres arrive, and medially to the central canal.

In the cat its axon often springs from a dendrite rather than the cell body, and the insulating sheath starts only some distance along.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are fifteen to thirty micrometres across, and the axon is a thin myelinated group B fibre of one to three micrometres conducting at three to fifteen metres per second, so the command to void takes tens of milliseconds to arrive.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Three functional groups are set by target, bladder, colorectal and erectile tissue, and rat reconstructions show the bladder and penile groups occupy separate territories inside the same nucleus.

Intracellular filling in the cat found distinct morphological classes, but no molecular scheme is agreed.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the sacral parasympathetic nucleus, a lateral band of intermediate grey in lamina seven, spanning the second to fourth sacral segments in humans and the sixth lumbar to first sacral segments in the rat, with dendrites crossing into lamina one and the lateral funiculus.

Its two named neighbours matter: the dorsal commissural nucleus, which relays bladder sensation, and Onuf's nucleus below, whose cells are voluntary motor neurons for the external sphincter, and the interface with Onuf's nucleus is the anatomical heart of continence.

NUMBER:

No human count exists, so only a bound is honest.

Retrograde labelling in rats recovers a few thousand cells, and scaling by cord and organ size gives a human figure of the order of ten thousand, most plausibly five to twenty thousand across both sides of the second to fourth sacral segments, well under one hundredth of one percent of the two hundred million spinal neurons, at low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

At rest these cells are held below threshold, which is why a healthy bladder fills for hours without leaking, and during voiding they switch to a sustained burst of ten to thirty spikes per second.

The switch shows hysteresis, turning on at high volume and off only far below it, a transient potassium current delays the first spike, and mu opioid receptors on these cells are why morphine causes urinary retention.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The commanding input descends from Barrington's nucleus in the pons, the pontine micturition centre, using glutamate with corticotropin-releasing factor, and this is the wire the forebrain uses to grant or withhold permission to void.

Segmental input comes from dorsal commissural interneurons carrying bladder stretch, from raphe serotonergic fibres and from rostral ventrolateral medulla C1 cells, and the output is a nicotinic synapse onto pelvic and intramural ganglion neurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

It exits by the second to fourth sacral ventral roots into the pelvic splanchnic nerves and ends in the inferior hypogastric plexus and organ walls, so the last stage of the pathway is embedded in the tissue it controls.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine on nicotinic receptors at the ganglion, after which second-order neurons use acetylcholine on muscarinic M three and M two receptors, adenosine triphosphate as a fast purinergic co-transmitter, and nitric oxide to relax the urethra and fill erectile tissue.

MYELINATION:

Thin, group B, and in the cat the sheath famously begins only after the axon has travelled some way, leaving a long bare initial stretch.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low at baseline because these cells are silent most of the day, with sharp peaks during voiding, so the cost profile is bursty.

PLASTICITY:

After spinal cord injury the pontine command is cut and bladder and sphincter contract against each other in detrusor sphincter dyssynergia, while a new reflex driven by unmyelinated C fibre afferents emerges and nitric oxide synthase expression rises sharply in the nucleus.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here must maintain a synaptic environment around dendrites running hundreds of micrometres inside white matter, among myelinated tracts.

Microglia, the resident immune cells, are distinctively reactive in this nucleus, activating during bladder inflammation and actively driving the overactivity that follows.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell type and the sacral cord keeps sensation, voluntary sphincter control and leg reflexes, but loses the ability to empty anything.

The region turns from a two-way organ controller into a one-way holding device, so the bladder distends silently and the bowel stalls.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is one of the clearest biological bistable switches, with two stable states and a jump between them, and it must be simulated as such rather than as a graded response.

Its loop is spinobulbospinal, running to the pons and back with a cat loop time of fifty to seventy milliseconds, corticotropin-releasing factor acts slowly and partly by diffusion, opioids gate it on both sides of the synapse, and it must be coordinated with Onuf's nucleus through interneurons rather than direct contact.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Bladder stretch afferent rate from dorsal commissural interneurons, zero to twenty spikes per second, proportional to volume in millilitres.

◦ Descending permission gate from Barrington's nucleus, open or closed, plus opioid and serotonergic modulation.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Nicotinic drive to pelvic and intramural ganglia, zero or ten to thirty spikes per second, and detrusor pressure in centimetres of water.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, switch state as a boolean, burst rate, bladder volume.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Voiding threshold about three hundred millilitres, switch off volume about thirty millilitres, burst rate ten to thirty spikes per second, conduction three to fifteen metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane twenty to forty milliseconds, spinobulbospinal loop fifty to seventy milliseconds, filling minutes to hours, voiding ten to thirty seconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Spinal cord injury separates them from the pons and gives detrusor sphincter dyssynergia with dangerous back pressure on the kidneys.

◦ Multiple system atrophy and diabetic autonomic neuropathy weaken them and give a large, poorly emptying bladder.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to model the sacral parasympathetic preganglionic neuron as the bistable switch that fills and empties a bladder.
Create a number variable called bladder volume with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called afferent rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called preganglionic rate with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called voiding with initial value false.
Create a boolean variable called cortical permission with initial value true.
Repeat the following steps exactly 6000 times.
    Set afferent rate to the product of bladder volume and 0.06.
    Check if both bladder volume is greater than 300 and cortical permission is true.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set voiding to true.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if bladder volume is less than 30.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set voiding to false.
    End the conditional block.
    If voiding is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set preganglionic rate to 20.
        Set bladder volume to the difference of bladder volume and 2.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set preganglionic rate to 0.
        Set bladder volume to the sum of bladder volume and 0.1.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display bladder volume to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

JavaScript is chosen because its plain style suits a reflex that waits a long time and then fires all at once, and it runs anywhere with no toolchain.

// Sacral parasympathetic preganglionic neuron as a bistable voiding switch.
const DT = 0.1;            // seconds per step
const FILL_RATE = 1.0;     // millilitres per second
const ON_ML = 300;         // volume that triggers voiding
const OFF_ML = 30;         // volume at which voiding stops
const VOID_RATE = 20.0;    // millilitres emptied per second
const BURST_HZ = 20.0;     // preganglionic firing while voiding

let volume = 0, voiding = false;
const permission = true;   // the pontine gate held open by the forebrain

for (let step = 0; step < 6000; step++) {
  const t = step * DT;
  const afferentHz = 0.06 * volume;        // bladder wall stretch afferents
  if (!voiding && volume > ON_ML && permission) voiding = true;
  if (voiding && volume < OFF_ML) voiding = false;
  volume += voiding ? -VOID_RATE * DT : FILL_RATE * DT;
  if (volume < 0) volume = 0;
  if (step % 500 === 0) {
    console.log("t=" + t.toFixed(0) + "s vol=" + volume.toFixed(0) +
                "ml aff=" + afferentHz.toFixed(1) +
                "Hz pre=" + (voiding ? BURST_HZ : 0) + "Hz");
  }
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Stimulate it and bladder pressure rises without the slightest twitch of the pelvic floor, which instantly separates it from its neighbour in Onuf's nucleus.

CHAPTER 170. RENSHAW CELLS (NON-COG)

Birdsey Renshaw found a cell that motor neurons use to shout themselves down, and when it answers it can fire at fifteen hundred spikes a second.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Every motor neuron axon, leaving the cord, gives off a short branch called a recurrent collateral that turns back into the grey matter and excites this small inhibitory cell.

The Renshaw cell then inhibits the very motor neurons that excited it and their close partners, a loop called recurrent inhibition that acts as automatic volume control on muscle force.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small oval cell whose dendrites run head to tail along the cord, carrying an unusually clear signature: the calcium-binding protein calbindin, the glycine transporter two, and huge clusters of gephyrin, the scaffold that anchors inhibitory receptors.

It comes from the V1 class of embryonic interneurons marked by engrailed one, in the subgroup defined by MafA and MafB.

SIZE:

The cell body is ten to twenty micrometres across with a dendritic field of a few hundred micrometres, and its axon is short and thin, staying within its own and a few neighbouring segments.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No formal scheme is agreed, but thoracic Renshaw cells serve respiratory motor pools, fire in time with inspiration and project further along the cord than the classical limb form from which nearly all measurements come.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the most ventral strip of lamina seven, so consistently that anatomists call that strip the Renshaw area, immediately below and medial to the motor nucleus of lamina nine, throughout cervical, thoracic, lumbar and sacral cord and densest in the two enlargements.

In that thin strip it makes up as much as eighty percent of all glycine-using neurons, and its interface is the boundary between motor nucleus and intermediate grey, exactly where motor axons pass to the ventral white matter.

NUMBER:

In mouse lumbar cord Renshaw cells are two to three percent of ventral interneurons, with about one Renshaw cell per five motor neurons.

Applied to the roughly two hundred thousand human alpha motor neurons that gives about forty thousand, range twenty to eighty thousand, some two to four hundredths of one percent of the two hundred million spinal neurons, at low confidence since no human count exists.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A single motor volley evokes a long-lasting response opening at an instantaneous frequency near fifteen hundred spikes per second and decaying over thirty to fifty milliseconds.

Its input resistance is high, its cholinergic excitation slow and strong, and its own cell body is blanketed in inhibition, since glycine and gamma-aminobutyric acid synapses form seventy-five to eighty percent of all synapses on its soma.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are recurrent collaterals from alpha motor neurons, releasing acetylcholine onto nicotinic and muscarinic receptors and co-releasing glutamate, plus other Renshaw cells, Ia inhibitory interneurons and descending fibres.

Outputs are unusually varied for so small a cell: alpha motor neurons of the same and synergist muscles, gamma motor neurons, Ia inhibitory interneurons, other Renshaw cells, and ventral spinocerebellar tract neurons, and because it inhibits Ia inhibitory interneurons, driving a pool also releases the antagonist and permits deliberate co-contraction.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Short and locally branching, mostly within its own segment, so inhibition reaches the motor neuron one to two milliseconds after that motor neuron's own spike.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases glycine and gamma-aminobutyric acid, abbreviated GABA, from the same terminals, glycine giving a fast component and GABA a slower one, which is why recurrent inhibition outlasts reciprocal inhibition.

MYELINATION:

Unmyelinated or only thinly myelinated, because over a millimetre speed costs nothing to give up.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Transiently enormous, since firing at fifteen hundred spikes per second demands dense mitochondria and heavy sodium pump work, while between bursts the cell is cheap.

PLASTICITY:

The size of its gephyrin clusters, and so the inhibition it receives, is set by how much excitation it gets from motor axons, so the cell tunes its own brake to its own drive, and in mouse amyotrophic lateral sclerosis this circuit is disrupted before motor neurons visibly die.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here are largely conventional and it would be wrong to invent a speciality.

The genuine local difference is chemical, since the Renshaw area is exceptionally rich in acetylcholinesterase, the enzyme destroying acetylcholine, so the space is swept clean between bursts, and unlike motor neurons micrometres away these cells wear no perineuronal net.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete Renshaw cells and the ventral horn loses its automatic gain control.

Firing rates would climb with no internal brake, recruitment would lose some of its orderly progression from weak to strong units, and the cord would lose its built-in way of releasing an antagonist when a movement needs stiffness.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The fifteen hundred hertz opening burst is itself a computation, turning a brief synchronous volley into a graded decaying inhibition whose strength encodes how much of the pool just fired.

Two transmitters of different speed from one terminal give a two-timescale output, neonatal Renshaw cells are electrically coupled so the population acts as a unit, and strychnine blocking its glycine receptors and tetanus toxin blocking its release are why both poisonings produce rigid spasm.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Recurrent collateral excitation proportional to summed motor pool rate, zero to fifty spikes per second per motor neuron.

◦ Mutual inhibition from other Renshaw cells and Ia inhibitory interneurons, subtractive, and descending gain of zero point five to two.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Mixed glycine and GABA inhibition of alpha and gamma motor neurons, Ia inhibitory interneurons and other Renshaw cells.

◦ A copy of ventral horn activity to ventral spinocerebellar tract neurons.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, instantaneous rate, short-term depression, inhibitory conductance in the pool.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Peak rate about fifteen hundred spikes per second, burst thirty to fifty milliseconds, Renshaw to motor neuron ratio one to five, inhibitory share of somatic synapses seventy-five to eighty percent.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Nicotinic excitation five milliseconds, burst decay thirty milliseconds, glycine ten milliseconds, GABA thirty milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Strychnine blocks the glycine receptor and tetanus toxin blocks release, both producing runaway motor firing and rigid spasm.

◦ In amyotrophic lateral sclerosis models the circuit degrades early, removing a brake from stressed motor neurons.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute recurrent inhibition, in which a motor pool drives a Renshaw cell that then inhibits the pool.
Create a number variable called descending command with initial value 40.
Create a number variable called motoneuron rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called renshaw rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called inhibition with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called collateral drive with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 1000 times.
    Set motoneuron rate to the difference of descending command and inhibition.
    Check if motoneuron rate is less than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set motoneuron rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set collateral drive to the product of motoneuron rate and 18.
    Set renshaw rate to the sum of renshaw rate and the product of the difference of collateral drive and renshaw rate and 0.0167.
    Check if renshaw rate is greater than 1500.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set renshaw rate to 1500.
    End the conditional block.
    Set inhibition to the sum of inhibition and the product of the difference of the product of renshaw rate and 0.02 and inhibition and 0.02.
End the loop.
Display motoneuron rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because the circuit is three state variables on a fixed half-millisecond step, what an embedded spinal simulator would run.

/* Recurrent inhibition: motor pool drives Renshaw cell, which inhibits the pool. */
#include <stdio.h>

#define DT        0.0005   /* half millisecond step */
#define TAU_RC    0.030    /* Renshaw burst decay, seconds */
#define TAU_IPSP  0.025    /* inhibitory decay in the motoneuron, seconds */
#define RC_GAIN   18.0     /* Renshaw spikes/s per motoneuron spike/s */
#define RC_MAX    1500.0   /* peak instantaneous Renshaw rate */
#define IPSP_GAIN 0.020    /* motoneuron spikes/s lost per Renshaw spike/s */

int main(void) {
    double renshaw = 0.0, inhib = 0.0, command = 40.0;
    int step;
    for (step = 0; step < 1000; step++) {
        double mn = command - inhib;
        if (mn < 0.0) mn = 0.0;
        renshaw += (RC_GAIN * mn - renshaw) * (DT / TAU_RC);
        if (renshaw > RC_MAX) renshaw = RC_MAX;
        inhib += (IPSP_GAIN * renshaw - inhib) * (DT / TAU_IPSP);
        if (step % 200 == 0)
            printf("t=%.4f  mn=%6.2f Hz  RC=%7.2f Hz  inhib=%5.2f\n",
                   step * DT, mn, renshaw, inhib);
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron excited by acetylcholine released from a motor neuron's own axon inside the spinal cord, and the only one answering with a burst near fifteen hundred spikes per second.

CHAPTER 171. IA INHIBITORY INTERNEURONS (Counter-Muscle Silencing Neuron) (NON-COG)

You cannot bend and straighten your elbow at the same time, and one modest cell in the spinal cord is the reason why.

The old label names a nerve fibre class, group Ia, which is a barcode rather than a description.

Counter-Muscle names the target, because this cell is driven by stretch sensors inside one muscle and then inhibits the motor neurons of the muscle that pulls the opposite way across the same joint, so a bending muscle and a straightening muscle never fight.

Silencing names the work and its chemistry, since the cell releases glycine, an inhibitory transmitter, in a two-synapse path that takes roughly one extra millisecond compared with the direct stretch reflex.

Neuron marks it as a spinal interneuron switched on by descending fibres from the motor cortex at the moment a voluntary movement begins, so that the brain commands the mover and the relaxer with one instruction.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It takes a direct connection from the fastest sensory fibres in the body, the group Ia fibres carrying stretch and stretch velocity from muscle spindles, and inhibits the motor neurons of the opposing muscle, a pattern called reciprocal inhibition.

Its second job is to carry a copy of the brain's command, since corticospinal, vestibulospinal, reticulospinal and rubrospinal fibres all converge on it, so one descending order both contracts a muscle and relaxes its antagonist.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A medium multipolar neuron whose dendrites catch incoming sensory fibres and whose axon branches densely in the neighbouring motor nucleus, using glycine and marked by the glycine transporter two.

Most belong to the Foxp2 subgroup of V1 interneurons, born of the same engrailed one lineage as Renshaw cells but later, and with them supply most inhibitory synapses on motor neuron cell bodies.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are twenty to forty micrometres across, larger than a Renshaw cell and smaller than a motor neuron, with an axon of a millimetre or two spreading over the antagonist pool.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The population divides by target into flexor-related cells that inhibit extensors and extensor-related cells that inhibit flexors, and these mirror groups inhibit each other.

A smaller contribution comes from V2b lineage interneurons, so this is a functional class assembled from more than one developmental origin.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in lamina seven of the ventral horn, just dorsal and medial to the motor nucleus of lamina nine, in the region sometimes called the intermediate nucleus, at every segment with a motor pool and most abundantly in the cervical enlargement from the fifth cervical to the first thoracic segment and the lumbosacral enlargement from the second lumbar to the second sacral segment.

Its interface is the crossing point between sensory intermediate grey above and motor nucleus below, which is why a sensory signal reaches a motor neuron with only one extra cell in the way.

NUMBER:

No human count exists for this class, and any single figure would be an invention.

The Foxp2 subgroup is among the largest V1 families and these cells are thought to outnumber Renshaw cells substantially, so a defensible bracket is one hundred thousand to three hundred thousand, five to fifteen hundredths of one percent of the two hundred million spinal neurons, at low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A fast phasic cell with a short membrane time constant, it turns a synchronous sensory volley into one to three spikes with very little delay, quiet at rest and tonically active once movement begins.

Its inhibition arrives at disynaptic latency, about one millisecond after the direct monosynaptic excitation of the agonist, and that timing is the signature by which human neurophysiologists identify the pathway in living subjects.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are monosynaptic group Ia afferents, recognised by vesicular glutamate transporter one boutons, plus corticospinal, rubrospinal, vestibulospinal and reticulospinal fibres, Renshaw inhibition, and mutual inhibition from the opposing Ia inhibitory interneuron.

Outputs are the antagonist's alpha motor neurons, contacted on soma and proximal dendrites where inhibition bites hardest, that antagonist's gamma motor neurons, the opposing Ia inhibitory interneuron, and Renshaw cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

It stays inside the ventral horn, usually within its own segment, and never leaves the cord, and its brevity is the point, because the pathway's value is being only one synapse slower than the fastest reflex in the body.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glycine on receptors built from alpha one and beta subunits, essentially purely glycinergic in the adult, a useful contrast with the Renshaw cell that explains why reciprocal inhibition is brief and sharply timed.

MYELINATION:

Unmyelinated or very thinly myelinated, since a journey of a millimetre or two gains nothing from insulation.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and tracking movement, cheap at rest and continuously active through every second of walking, reaching and standing.

PLASTICITY:

Reciprocal inhibition is modulated with task, gait phase and posture and can be deliberately increased by therapeutic electrical stimulation, and after stroke it increased in patients who recovered well with mild spasticity while remaining unchanged or reduced in those who recovered poorly with marked extensor spasticity.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here are structurally ordinary and it would mislead to claim otherwise.

The one function worth specifying is that surrounding astrocytes carry the glycine transporter one, clearing glycine from the cleft and therefore setting how quickly each inhibitory event ends, making astrocytic uptake a direct parameter of reciprocal inhibition timing.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell type and the ventral horn keeps its motor neurons, sensory input and recurrent brake, but loses its push-pull logic.

Every voluntary movement would fight itself, joints stiffening, limbs moving slowly and expensively, and the crisp alternation of walking dissolving into a rigid shuffle.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This neuron performs a subtraction, taking excitation from one muscle's sensors and from the brain and delivering a negative quantity to the opposite muscle, so it is a sign-inverting adder rather than a relay.

Because flexor and extensor versions inhibit each other the pair behaves like a flip-flop, one of the building blocks of the alternating rhythm that produces stepping, and its inputs are gated before arrival, since gamma-aminobutyric acid released onto the group Ia terminals turns the sensory signal down without touching the interneuron.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Monosynaptic group Ia excitation from the agonist spindle, zero to one hundred spikes per second, and descending drive from four tracts, zero to fifty spikes per second.

◦ Renshaw and opposing interneuron inhibition, subtractive, plus presynaptic gating of the Ia terminals, multiplicative, zero point two to one.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Glycinergic inhibition of antagonist alpha and gamma motor neurons, of the opposing interneuron and of Renshaw cells.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, firing rate, inhibitory conductance in the antagonist pool, presynaptic gating level.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Disynaptic latency about one millisecond beyond the monosynaptic reflex, membrane time constant about five milliseconds, inhibitory gain zero point three to zero point four.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Glycinergic decay five to ten milliseconds, task modulation hundreds of milliseconds, training days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Reduced reciprocal inhibition after stroke, in cerebral palsy and in dystonia produces co-contraction and spasticity.

◦ Loss of glycine receptor function, as in hyperekplexia or strychnine poisoning, removes the inhibition and produces exaggerated startle and rigidity.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute reciprocal inhibition between a flexor and an extensor motor pool using two mirror image Ia inhibitory interneurons.
Create a number variable called flexor command with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called extensor stretch with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called flexor interneuron rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called extensor interneuron rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called flexor motoneuron rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called extensor motoneuron rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called target rate with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 300 times.
    Set flexor command to the cortical drive to the flexor pool at this step.
    Set extensor stretch to the lengthening signal from the extensor spindles at this step.
    Set flexor motoneuron rate to the difference of flexor command and the product of extensor interneuron rate and 0.35.
    Set extensor motoneuron rate to the difference of 5 and the product of flexor interneuron rate and 0.35.
    Check if extensor motoneuron rate is less than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set extensor motoneuron rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set target rate to the product of 0.4 and flexor command.
    Set flexor interneuron rate to the sum of flexor interneuron rate and the product of the difference of target rate and flexor interneuron rate and 0.125.
    Set target rate to the sum of the product of 0.8 and extensor stretch and 2.
    Set extensor interneuron rate to the sum of extensor interneuron rate and the product of the difference of target rate and extensor interneuron rate and 0.125.
End the loop.
Display flexor motoneuron rate to the console.
Display extensor motoneuron rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the two halves of the circuit are identical, so one function is called twice, exactly as the spinal cord builds it.

# Reciprocal Ia inhibition between a flexor and an extensor motor pool.
DT = 0.001          # seconds per step
TAU_IPSP = 0.008    # glycinergic inhibitory time constant, seconds
IA_GAIN = 0.8       # interneuron spikes/s per unit of drive
INHIB_GAIN = 0.35   # motoneuron spikes/s lost per interneuron spike/s
SHARE = 0.5         # share of the cortical command sent to the Ia interneuron

def half(command, opposing_ia, own_stretch):
    mn = max(command - INHIB_GAIN * opposing_ia, 0.0)
    return mn, IA_GAIN * (own_stretch + SHARE * command)

flex_ia, ext_ia = 0.0, 0.0
for k in range(300):
    moving = k * DT >= 0.050                 # the cortex says bend the elbow
    flex_cmd = 40.0 if moving else 0.0
    ext_stretch = 12.0 if moving else 0.0    # the extensor is being lengthened
    flex_mn, flex_t = half(flex_cmd, ext_ia, 0.0)
    ext_mn, ext_t = half(5.0, flex_ia, ext_stretch)
    flex_ia += (flex_t - flex_ia) * (DT / TAU_IPSP)
    ext_ia += (ext_t - ext_ia) * (DT / TAU_IPSP)
    if k % 60 == 0:
        print("t=%.3f s  flexor=%5.2f Hz  extensor=%5.2f Hz" % (k * DT, flex_mn, ext_mn))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only spinal neuron that takes a direct connection from the group Ia fibres of one muscle and delivers pure glycine inhibition to the motor neurons of that muscle's exact opposite, one synapse and about one millisecond later.

CHAPTER 172. IB INHIBITORY INTERNEURONS (Tendon-Load Restraining Neuron) (NON-COG)

Lift something heavier than you should, and a cell the width of a grain of pollen quietly decides how much force your muscle is allowed to make.

Group Ib is again a fibre-class barcode and teaches nothing.

Tendon-Load names the signal it reads, because its input comes from Golgi tendon organs, sensors sitting in series with the tendon that report the actual force a muscle is producing, each one typically sampling only about ten to twenty muscle fibres.

Restraining names the job, since the cell feeds that force reading back as inhibition onto the motor neurons of the very same muscle, a negative feedback loop that trims output when tension climbs too high.

Neuron marks it as the cell called non-reciprocal in the older literature, because unlike the counter-muscle pathway it acts on the muscle that generated the force itself.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The Ib inhibitory interneuron is a small nerve cell buried in the middle layers of the spinal cord's grey matter that listens to force sensors in your tendons and turns down the very muscle that produced the force.

Its input comes from the Golgi tendon organ, a small braid of collagen fibres woven into the join between muscle and tendon, wrapped by a nerve ending that fires faster the harder that braid is pulled.

The nerve fibre carrying that report is called a Group Ib afferent, where afferent simply means a fibre carrying signals inward toward the cord, and the interneuron takes its name from that fibre.

An interneuron is a nerve cell whose whole life is spent between other nerve cells, never touching muscle or skin itself.

The textbook story is a safety valve that stops you tearing your own tendon.

The measured truth is better: this cell is a force comparator that continuously scales muscle output to the load actually being carried, and it is the reason you can lift an empty cup and a full kettle with the same smoothness.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is a small multipolar neuron, meaning it has several branches leaving the cell body in several directions.

Four to eight primary dendrites, the branches that collect incoming signals, spread mostly sideways across the cord rather than up and down it.

That flat, fan-shaped dendritic field is a design choice: it lets one cell sample Ib fibres arriving from several different muscles at the same spinal level.

Its axon, the single output cable, dives toward the motor neuron pools and breaks into a dense local bush rather than travelling far.

SIZE:

The cell body is 15 to 30 micrometres across, where a micrometre is one thousandth of a millimetre.

The dendritic fan spans roughly 300 to 600 micrometres mediolaterally and only 100 to 250 micrometres along the length of the cord.

The whole cell would sit comfortably inside the full stop at the end of this sentence.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Pure Ib cells, driven almost entirely by tendon organ afferents from one muscle, are the minority and the classical case.

• Convergent group I cells receive both Ib force signals and Ia length signals from muscle spindles, so they report a blend of force and stretch.

• Cutaneous-dominant cells receive heavy skin input and act as load-and-contact detectors for the foot or palm.

• Excitatory partners exist alongside them: the same Ib afferents drive excitatory interneurons that lift the antagonist muscle, so the pathway is a push-pull pair, not a lone brake.

• By developmental lineage most inhibitory members are thought to arise from the V1 and V2b classes described later in this section, though the assignment in humans is not settled.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in laminae V, VI and VII of the spinal grey matter, the layered scheme of Rexed that divides the cord's butterfly-shaped core into ten bands.

Laminae VI and VII together form the zona intermedia, the intermediate zone that sits between the sensory dorsal horn above and the motor ventral horn below.

That is the important point about its address: this cell occupies the sensorimotor interface itself, the seam where incoming body reports are converted into outgoing movement commands.

It is present at every segment that serves a limb, and is densest in the cervical enlargement from C5 to T1, which serves the arm, and the lumbosacral enlargement from L2 to S2, which serves the leg.

Smaller numbers sit in thoracic segments serving trunk and intercostal muscles.

NUMBER:

There is no direct human count, and any book that gives you a precise one is guessing.

A defensible range is 100 thousand to 1 million cells across the whole cord, reasoned from the roughly 200 thousand alpha motor neurons in the human spinal cord and the convergence ratios measured in cat.

That is under one half of one percent of the roughly 200 million neurons in the human spinal cord.

Confidence is low, and the honest bound is this: they are certainly more numerous than motor neurons and certainly a tiny minority of interneurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting membrane voltage sits near minus 65 millivolts, the electrical charge difference the cell maintains across its skin while idle.

Input resistance is high for a spinal cell, around 100 to 300 megaohms, which means a small current makes a large voltage change and the cell is easy to recruit.

It fires tonically, meaning steadily rather than in bursts, at rates that climb smoothly with tendon force up to 100 hertz or more.

The whole reflex is disynaptic, that is, two synapses long, and in human experiments the central delay measured by conditioning the soleus H reflex from the medial gastrocnemius nerve is roughly 2.5 milliseconds, with inhibition appearing at a condition-test interval near 6 milliseconds.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from Group Ib afferents conducting at 72 to 120 metres per second, from Group Ia spindle afferents, from Group II afferents, from skin and joint afferents, and from descending corticospinal, rubrospinal, vestibulospinal and reticulospinal fibres.

That descending convergence is why the brain can decide, moment by moment, how loud the force feedback should be.

Its own inputs are gated by presynaptic inhibition, in which a GABA-releasing cell synapses onto the incoming Ib terminal itself and turns the message down before it is ever heard.

Outputs go to alpha motor neurons of the muscle that generated the force and its close synergists, to gamma motor neurons that set spindle sensitivity, and to neighbouring interneurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, 1 to 3 micrometres across, and stays local, branching within its own segment and one to three segments above and below.

A single cell forms several hundred to a few thousand synaptic boutons, the small swellings where transmitter is released.

Conduction along it runs at roughly 3 to 15 metres per second, slow by afferent standards but irrelevant over such short distances.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The main transmitter is glycine, the smallest amino acid, which opens chloride channels and makes the target harder to fire.

A substantial subset co-releases gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) from the same vesicles, giving a fast glycine component and a slower GABA component in one package.

Postsynaptic receptors are the glycine receptor built from alpha1 and beta subunits and the GABA type A receptor.

MYELINATION:

The axon carries a thin myelin sheath, myelin being the fatty insulation wrapped by support cells that speeds conduction.

Internodes, the insulated stretches between bare gaps, are short at roughly 100 to 300 micrometres, matching the small axon calibre.

Some of the finest terminal branches lose myelin entirely before they reach their boutons.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Absolute energy use is modest because the cell is small, but the duty cycle is punishing: during standing, carrying or gripping it fires continuously for minutes at a time.

Mitochondria, the cell's power plants, cluster in the boutons rather than the cell body, because the expensive job is refilling vesicles, not making spikes.

PLASTICITY:
