This is one of the few reflex pathways in mammals proven to be trainable: monkeys and humans can be operantly conditioned to increase or decrease group I reflex gain over weeks, and the change persists.

Its strength is also state-dependent within a single stride, and after spinal cord injury the pathway reorganises, contributing to the abnormal stiffness of spasticity.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes in the intermediate zone are unusual in expressing high levels of glycine transporter 1, the pump that clears glycine from the synaptic gap.

That matters here more than almost anywhere else in the nervous system, because glycine is this cell's currency, and the same astrocytes also supply the serine that neurons need to make glycine in the first place.

Chloride handling is a second speciality: the potassium-chloride cotransporter KCC2 in the target motor neuron keeps internal chloride low, and if astrocyte and neuronal support of KCC2 fails, glycine stops inhibiting and starts exciting.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and the intermediate zone keeps its wiring diagram but loses its sense of weight.

Movements would still start and stop, but force would no longer be graded against load: the cord would command a muscle to contract without ever asking what it costs.

The flavour lost is negative feedback on effort, the thing that lets a hand close on an egg and a crowbar with the same confident economy.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The headline non-linear trick is reflex reversal.

During quiet standing, Ib input inhibits the extensor muscles that hold you up, but during the stance phase of walking the same input is routed instead through excitatory partners, so heavier loading now increases extensor drive and stops the leg collapsing.

The sign of a reflex is therefore not a property of the wire but of the network state, and any simulation that hard-codes the sign will be wrong for half the gait cycle.

Beyond that: presynaptic inhibition edits its inputs before integration; neonatal cells are electrically coupled through gap junctions, which are direct pores between cells, and uncouple with maturity; serotonin and noradrenaline released by volume transmission, meaning diffuse spillover rather than point-to-point contact, reset its excitability over seconds; and its own chloride reversal potential is a slow variable set by transporter expression rather than by voltage.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: Group Ib afferent firing rate from homonymous muscle, 0 to 200 hertz, roughly proportional to tendon force in newtons; Group Ib from synergists, 0 to 200 hertz; Group Ia, 0 to 150 hertz; cutaneous afferents, 0 to 100 hertz; descending gain command, dimensionless 0 to 2; presynaptic inhibition of the Ib terminal, dimensionless 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: inhibitory synaptic conductance onto homonymous and synergist alpha motor neurons, 0 to 5 nanosiemens per cell; smaller inhibitory drive onto gamma motor neurons; excitatory drive is not emitted by this cell but by its paired excitatory interneuron.

• STATE: membrane voltage in millivolts; refractory countdown in seconds; short-term synaptic depression variable, 0 to 1; locomotor phase flag, stance or swing.

• PARAMETERS: membrane time constant 8 to 15 milliseconds; spike threshold near minus 50 millivolts; input resistance 100 to 300 megaohms; peak inhibitory conductance per bouton 0.1 to 0.5 nanosiemens; glycine to GABA co-release ratio, typically 0.7 to 1.0 glycine.

• TIME CONSTANTS: synaptic delay 0.5 to 1 millisecond per synapse; total reflex central delay 2 to 3 milliseconds; glycine current decay 5 to 10 milliseconds; GABA type A decay 20 to 50 milliseconds; task-dependent gain changes over 100 milliseconds to seconds; conditioning-induced change over days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: loss or silencing produces unopposed extensor drive under load, which appears clinically as the stiff, poorly graded contraction of spasticity after spinal cord injury or stroke; in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS), a disease that kills motor neurons, the surrounding inhibitory interneuron input is stripped away early, removing force feedback before the motor neuron itself dies; loss of glycine receptor function, as in hereditary hyperekplexia caused by mutations in the GLRA1 gene, makes the target motor neuron deaf to this cell entirely and produces violent startle and neonatal rigidity.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert tendon force reports into a graded inhibitory command for the muscle that produced the force.
Create a number variable called homonymous_hertz with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called synergist_hertz with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called descending_gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called presynaptic_gate with initial value 1.
Create a boolean variable called stance_phase with initial value false.
Create a number variable called membrane with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called threshold with initial value 15.
Create a number variable called output_sign with initial value 0.
Set homonymous_hertz to the current firing rate of the muscle's own tendon organ afferents.
Set synergist_hertz to the current firing rate of the synergist tendon organ afferents.
Calculate the sum of homonymous_hertz and synergist_hertz.
Set membrane to that result.
Calculate the product of membrane and presynaptic_gate.
Set membrane to that result.
Calculate the product of membrane and descending_gain.
Set membrane to that result.
Check if stance_phase is true.
If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set output_sign to 1.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set output_sign to negative 1.
End the conditional block.
Check if membrane is greater than threshold.
If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
    Calculate the product of output_sign and membrane.
    Display that result to the console.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Display 0 to the console.
End the conditional block.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the arithmetic is transparent and a simulation agent can lift the class straight into a larger network without a build step.

# One Ib inhibitory interneuron: a force comparator whose sign flips with gait phase.
DT = 0.001  # seconds per simulation step

class IbInterneuron:
    def __init__(self):
        self.v = -65.0        # membrane voltage, millivolts
        self.rest = -65.0
        self.thresh = -50.0   # spike threshold, millivolts
        self.tau = 0.012      # membrane time constant, seconds
        self.refrac = 0.0     # refractory countdown, seconds

    def step(self, ib_hz, cutaneous_hz, descending_gain, stance):
        # Force feedback is pooled from the muscle's own tendon organs plus skin load signals.
        drive = (0.06 * ib_hz + 0.02 * cutaneous_hz) * descending_gain
        # In stance the group I pathway is routed through excitatory partners: the sign flips.
        sign = 1.0 if stance else -1.0
        self.v += DT * (-(self.v - self.rest) + 2.0 * drive) / self.tau
        fired = False
        if self.refrac > 0.0:
            self.refrac -= DT
            self.v = self.rest
        elif self.v >= self.thresh:
            fired = True
            self.v = self.rest
            self.refrac = 0.004
        # Output is current delivered to the homonymous motor pool, negative meaning inhibitory.
        return fired, (sign * 0.8 if fired else 0.0)

cell = IbInterneuron()
spikes, net = 0, 0.0
for i in range(2000):                      # two seconds of heavy loading
    fired, out = cell.step(180.0, 10.0, 1.0, stance=(i >= 1000))
    spikes += 1 if fired else 0
    net += out
print("firing rate:", spikes / 2.0, "Hz")  # prints 55.5 Hz
print("net drive to motor pool:", round(net, 2))  # near zero: braked, then assisted

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only spinal cell whose input is measured in newtons rather than millimetres, and the only common reflex interneuron whose output changes sign halfway through a footstep.

Find a cell in laminae V to VII that is silent at rest, fires in proportion to tendon tension, inhibits the muscle that is pulling on that tendon at a two-synapse delay of about 2.5 milliseconds, and releases glycine, and you have found it.

CHAPTER 173. PROPRIOSPINAL INTERNEURONS (Spine-Spanning Relay Neuron) (NON-COG)

When a stroke destroys the direct line from brain to hand, the hand sometimes learns to move again, and it does so by routing the order through these cells.

The old term is Latin for belonging to the spine itself, an address rather than a function.

Spine-Spanning names the anatomy that defines the cell, because its axon never leaves the spinal cord but runs from the segment where the cell body sits to other segments, over one or two segments in the short variety and from neck to lower back in the long variety.

Relay names the work, since these cells carry both descending commands from the brain and local reflex signals between segments, letting an arm and a leg, or a shoulder and a hand, be timed together.

Neuron marks it as a cell that matters clinically, because after a spinal cord injury surviving spine-spanning relays can form detour circuits around the damaged stretch and support partial recovery of stepping.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A propriospinal interneuron is a nerve cell whose body and whose entire axon stay inside the spinal cord, connecting one spinal segment to another.

The name is literal: proprio means own, so these are the cord's own private wires, as opposed to fibres that come down from the brain or go out to muscle.

Their job is to knit the cord into one organ instead of thirty-one independent slices.

They carry a command that arrives at the neck down to the arm, they tell the legs what the arms are doing, they let a single descending order reach several motor pools at once, and they hold the timing that keeps a limb's joints in sequence.

They are also the cord's spare parts department: after injury they sprout around the damage and rebuild a route the brain can use again.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Most are multipolar cells with a dendritic tree, the branching input structure, that spreads across several Rexed laminae, the ten grey matter layers of the cord.

The defining feature is not the cell body but the axon: it leaves the grey matter, enters the ring of white matter immediately surrounding it called the fasciculus proprius or propriospinal tract, runs for a distance, then dives back into grey matter and branches.

A single long propriospinal axon typically branches at three or more separate spinal levels, dropping a terminal bush at each.

Many also send an ascending collateral, a side branch travelling the opposite way, so the same cell reports its own output back toward the brainstem.

SIZE:

Cell bodies range widely, from 15 micrometres for short intersegmental cells to 40 or even 50 micrometres for the long descending cells, which are among the larger neurons in the intermediate zone.

Axon length is the striking number: a long descending propriospinal neuron in an adult human runs from the mid-cervical cord to the upper lumbar cord, a distance of roughly 40 to 50 centimetres, all inside the cord.

Short propriospinal axons cover one to six segments, roughly 15 to 100 millimetres in a human.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Short propriospinal neurons connect neighbouring segments, one to about six apart, and dominate numerically.

• Long descending propriospinal neurons run from cervical to lumbar levels and coordinate arms with legs.

• Long ascending propriospinal neurons run from lumbar to cervical levels and carry the reverse report.

• The C3 to C4 propriospinal system is a specialised group in the upper neck that relays cortical and reticulospinal commands to forelimb motor neurons in C6 to T1, terminating in laminae VI to VIII and IX, while a branch of the same axon ascends to the lateral reticular nucleus in the brainstem as a copy of the command sent.

• Commissural propriospinal neurons cross the midline as well as changing segment, and are treated as their own type in the next entry.

• By transmitter they split into glutamate-releasing excitatory cells and glycine or GABA-releasing inhibitory cells, in roughly comparable numbers.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies concentrate in laminae V, VI, VII, VIII and X, that is the deep dorsal horn, the whole intermediate zone, the medial ventral horn and the thin grey collar around the central canal.

They are present at every spinal level from C1 to the sacral cord and the conus medullaris, with peaks in the cervical enlargement, C3 to T1, and the lumbosacral enlargement, L1 to S2.

Their axons occupy the fasciculus proprius, a sleeve of white matter hugging the grey matter on all sides, which is the interface this cell type genuinely straddles: grey matter for computation, white matter for transport, in the same cell.

Long descending cells are especially concentrated in the ventromedial grey of C4 to C8 and in lamina VIII, and their targets sit in the rostral lumbar cord.

NUMBER:

Propriospinal neurons are the majority of the spinal cord's interneurons.

Careful anatomy in cats and rodents suggests that most spinal interneurons project beyond their own segment, and human histology finds interneurons make up something like 97 percent of spinal cord neurons.

A defensible estimate is 20 to 60 million propriospinal neurons in the human cord, that is roughly 10 to 30 percent of the 200 million spinal neurons, with the wide band reflecting genuine uncertainty about where a short axon stops being propriospinal.

Confidence in the order of magnitude is moderate; confidence in any single figure is poor.

Long descending propriospinal neurons are a small fraction of this, on the order of tens of thousands.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage near minus 65 millivolts, input resistance 80 to 400 megaohms depending on size, and firing patterns that span the range from tonic to adapting to delayed-onset.

Long descending cells with big somata have lower input resistance and higher rheobase, the minimum current needed to make them fire, so they need convergent input to recruit.

Many show a persistent inward current, a slow self-sustaining sodium or calcium current that lets a brief input produce a long output, which is exactly what a relay used for postural holding needs.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from corticospinal fibres, reticulospinal fibres from the brainstem, vestibulospinal and rubrospinal fibres, primary afferents of Group I, II and cutaneous classes, and other interneurons.

Anatomical work in rat shows powerful reticulospinal contacts onto cervical commissural and long descending propriospinal neurons, more numerous than corticospinal ones, which reframes these cells as brainstem tools first and cortical tools second.

Outputs go to alpha and gamma motor neurons, to other interneurons, to the contralateral cord in the commissural subset, and in the C3 to C4 group upward to the lateral reticular nucleus.

Convergence ratios are high: one propriospinal cell can contact motor neurons in several pools across several segments.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axon diameters run from 0.5 to 4 micrometres, with long descending axons at the thicker end.

Conduction velocity is correspondingly modest, roughly 3 to 30 metres per second, so a signal takes several milliseconds to cross a few segments and tens of milliseconds to travel neck to lumbar cord.

Terminal fields are large: a long axon may form several thousand boutons distributed across three or more segmental bushes.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Excitatory propriospinal cells release glutamate loaded by vesicular glutamate transporter 2 and act on AMPA and NMDA receptors, the fast and slow glutamate receptors.

Inhibitory propriospinal cells release glycine, GABA, or both together.

Neuromodulators, chemicals that adjust rather than command, reach them by diffusion: serotonin from the raphe nuclei of the brainstem, noradrenaline from the locus coeruleus, and dopamine from the A11 cell group all change propriospinal gain markedly.

MYELINATION:

Propriospinal axons are myelinated, but thinly compared with the great descending tracts, with sheaths of a few to a dozen wraps.

Oligodendrocytes, the myelin-making cells of the central nervous system, in the fasciculus proprius therefore support many small axons each rather than a few large ones.

Remyelination of these axons after injury is a major determinant of whether a detour circuit works, because a demyelinated relay conducts unreliably.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Maintaining a 40 centimetre axon inside the cord is expensive: the cell must run axonal transport, the motorised delivery of proteins and mitochondria along the axon, over that whole length.

Round trip transport time for slow cargo along a human long propriospinal axon is on the order of days to weeks.

Energy use per spike is dominated by the axon and terminals, not the soma.

PLASTICITY:

This is the most plastic relay in the mature spinal cord and it is the reason spontaneous recovery after incomplete spinal injury happens at all.

After a cervical lesion, severed corticospinal fibres sprout onto propriospinal neurons above the injury, and those neurons extend new branches around and below it, forming detour circuits that reconnect brain to motor pool.

In rats with two staggered hemisections that between them interrupt every long descending pathway, stepping under supraspinal control still returns, carried entirely by propriospinal relays.

Rehabilitation training, epidural stimulation and serotonergic drugs all bias this remodelling.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia of the fasciculus proprius behave differently from glia in the big tracts in one decisive way: after injury they permit sprouting.

Astrocytes here upregulate growth-permissive molecules and form a less dense inhibitory scar than in the dorsal columns, and the local oligodendrocyte precursor cells remyelinate new collaterals over weeks.

Microglia, the immune cells of the nervous system, prune the excess sprouts that appear in the first month after injury, and when that pruning fails the surviving circuit is unstable and produces jerks.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove propriospinal neurons and the spinal cord becomes thirty-one disconnected reflex machines.

Each segment would still twitch its own muscle to its own stimulus, but nothing would sequence: the shoulder would not steady the elbow, the arms would not swing against the legs, and the trunk would not brace before the arm reached.

What the region loses is continuity in space and time, the sense that the body is one object.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

These cells are delay lines with computation attached, and the delay itself is the computation, because the axon length sets the phase relationship between two limbs.

They carry efference copy: the C3 to C4 cells send an ascending branch to the lateral reticular nucleus and thence to the cerebellum, so the cerebellum learns what the cord was told to do rather than only what the body did.

Persistent inward currents give them plateau potentials, self-sustaining voltage states that outlast their input, which is how brief commands become held postures.

They engage in structural remodelling on a scale rare in adult mammals, growing genuinely new axon branches after injury, which requires local protein synthesis in the axon and a change in gene transcription in the nucleus.

They also instruct their own myelination: activity along a sprouting propriospinal axon recruits oligodendrocyte precursors to wrap it.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: corticospinal drive, 0 to 100 hertz; reticulospinal drive, 0 to 100 hertz, typically the stronger of the two; segmental afferent drive from Group I, Group II and cutaneous fibres, 0 to 200 hertz; local inhibitory input, 0 to 50 hertz.

• OUTPUTS: excitatory or inhibitory synaptic conductance onto motor neurons and interneurons at named target segments, delivered after a conduction delay; ascending efference copy to the lateral reticular nucleus for the C3 to C4 subset.

• STATE: membrane voltage; persistent inward current activation, 0 to 1; the contents of the axonal delay line, one value per simulation step for the transit duration; sprouting gain, normally 1.0 and rising after injury.

• PARAMETERS: source segment and target segment as integers; intersegmental distance 12 to 18 millimetres per segment in adult human; conduction velocity 3 to 30 metres per second; branch count 1 to 5; boutons per branch 200 to 2000; sign, excitatory or inhibitory.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; conduction delay 1 to 100 milliseconds depending on distance; plateau potential 0.5 to 5 seconds; sprouting and detour formation 2 to 12 weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: propriospinal myoclonus, a disorder of sudden involuntary trunk jerks that propagate slowly up and down the body at a few metres per second, arises from unstable propriospinal networks and is seen after cervical cord injury; failure of these relays to remyelinate leaves a patient with preserved anatomy but unusable timing; and in cervical spinal cord injury the survival of these cells is the single best predictor of whether hand function returns.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to relay a descending command from one spinal segment to another after the correct conduction delay.
Create a number variable called source_segment with initial value 3.
Create a number variable called target_segment with initial value 7.
Create a number variable called millimetres_per_segment with initial value 15.
Create a number variable called velocity_metres_per_second with initial value 8.
Create a number variable called step_seconds with initial value 0.0005.
Create a list variable called delay_line with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called span with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called distance with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called delay_steps with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called soma_drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called emitted with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called sprouting_gain with initial value 1.
Calculate the difference of target_segment and source_segment.
Set span to that result.
Calculate the product of span and millimetres_per_segment.
Set distance to that result.
Calculate the quotient of distance divided by 1000.
Set distance to that result.
Calculate the quotient of distance divided by velocity_metres_per_second.
Set delay_steps to that result.
Calculate the quotient of delay_steps divided by step_seconds.
Set delay_steps to that result.
Repeat the following steps exactly delay_steps times.
    Add 0 to the end of delay_line.
End the loop.
While the simulation remains true, repeat the following steps.
    Set soma_drive to the sum of the corticospinal input and the reticulospinal input.
    Set emitted to the item at position 1 in delay_line.
    Remove the item at position 1 from delay_line.
    Add soma_drive to the end of delay_line.
    Calculate the product of emitted and sprouting_gain.
    Display that result to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because its double-ended queue models an axonal delay line exactly, with no approximation and no numerical error.

# A propriospinal relay: the axon is a delay line, and the delay is part of the computation.
from collections import deque

class PropriospinalRelay:
    def __init__(self, source_segment, target_segment, mm_per_segment=15.0,
                 conduction_m_per_s=8.0, dt=0.0005):
        distance_m = abs(target_segment - source_segment) * mm_per_segment / 1000.0
        self.delay_steps = max(1, int(round(distance_m / conduction_m_per_s / dt)))
        self.line = deque([0.0] * self.delay_steps, maxlen=self.delay_steps)
        self.gain = 1.0   # rises above 1.0 as injury-driven sprouting adds boutons
        self.dt = dt

    def step(self, cortical_drive, reticulospinal_drive, local_inhibition):
        soma = max(0.0, cortical_drive + reticulospinal_drive - local_inhibition)
        arriving = self.line[0]      # the value launched delay_steps ago
        self.line.append(soma)       # launch this step's output down the axon
        return self.gain * arriving

relay = PropriospinalRelay(source_segment=3, target_segment=7)
print("axonal delay:", relay.delay_steps, "steps =",
      round(relay.delay_steps * relay.dt * 1000, 1), "ms")   # 15 steps = 7.5 ms
for t in range(200):
    pulse = 1.0 if 20 <= t < 60 else 0.0     # a 20 ms cortical burst at C3
    out = relay.step(pulse, 0.0, 0.0)
    if out > 0.0:
        print("command reaches C7 at step", t)  # step 35, exactly 15 steps late
        break

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only class of spinal neuron defined purely by where its axon goes rather than by what it senses or what it releases: born in the cord, living in the cord, dying in the cord, never sending a fibre to the brain or to a muscle.

If you inject a tracer at one spinal level and find labelled cell bodies at another spinal level, with nothing labelled in the brain and nothing in a nerve root, you are looking at propriospinal neurons.

CHAPTER 174. COMMISSURAL INTERNEURONS (Across-Midline Coordinating Neuron) (NON-COG)

The reason you walk instead of hop is that a few million axons cross the midline of your spinal cord and tell the other leg to wait its turn.

Commissural is Latin anatomy jargon for a fibre bundle joining the two halves, and it names a road rather than a purpose.

Across-Midline states the single defining fact, that the axon crosses the centre line of the cord to reach interneurons and motor neurons on the other side.

Coordinating names the faculty, because these crossing cells are what keeps the two legs, or the two arms, in a fixed relationship, most often strict alternation during walking but synchrony during hopping or bounding.

Neuron marks it as a cell whose removal in mice converts alternating stepping into a rabbit-like hop, showing that left and right halves each hold a working step generator and need this class only to agree on phase.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A commissural interneuron is a spinal nerve cell whose axon crosses from one side of the cord to the other.

A commissure, in anatomy, is simply a bridge of fibres joining left to right, and the main one here is the anterior white commissure, a narrow band of crossing axons lying just in front of the central canal.

Their job is to make the two halves of the body into a coordinated pair rather than two independent animals.

They enforce left-right alternation in walking, they produce crossed reflexes such as the crossed extensor response that stiffens one leg when the other steps on a tack, they relay pain and touch to the opposite side, and they let the trunk twist as a whole.

Cut them and the two sides of the cord still work, but they stop negotiating.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body sits on one side of the grey matter, usually in the ventromedial region.

The axon takes a stereotyped path: it grows ventrally and medially toward the floor plate, the specialised strip of cells at the cord's ventral midline, crosses beneath or in front of the central canal, then turns and runs longitudinally on the far side before branching into its terminal field.

Dendrites, the input branches, generally stay on the home side, so the cell reads locally and speaks contralaterally.

Many commissural cells are also propriospinal, meaning the crossed axon then travels several segments before terminating.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are typically 15 to 35 micrometres across, with the largest reaching 45 micrometres in the ventromedial ventral horn.

The crossing segment of the axon is short, about 1 to 3 millimetres in an adult human cord, but the longitudinal run afterwards may be anything from one segment to the full length of the cord.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Descending commissural cells cross and then run caudally, that is toward the tail, and are the dominant form in locomotor control.

• Ascending commissural cells cross and run rostrally, toward the head.

• Bifurcating commissural cells cross and then split, sending branches both ways.

• V0d cells are inhibitory commissural neurons that secure left-right alternation at slow walking speeds.

• V0v cells are excitatory commissural neurons that secure left-right alternation at faster speeds, working through inhibitory relays on the far side.

• V3 cells are excitatory commissural neurons, marked by the gene Sim1, that provide mutual excitation between the left and right extensor centres and make the rhythm robust rather than alternating.

• dI6 cells are inhibitory commissural neurons in the deep intermediate zone, marked by the gene Dmrt3, and are strongly implicated in gait.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies cluster in laminae VII, VIII and X, that is the intermediate zone, the medial ventral horn and the grey collar around the central canal, with a smaller dorsal population in laminae IV and V that relays sensation.

They are found at every segment from C1 to the sacral cord, with the highest density in the cervical and lumbosacral enlargements where limb control lives.

The interface that defines them is the ventral midline itself: the floor plate and the anterior white commissure, the single narrow gateway through which every crossing axon must pass.

Some also cross through the dorsal grey commissure behind the central canal, a smaller and less used route.

NUMBER:

There is no direct human count of commissural interneurons.

A defensible estimate is 10 to 30 million in the human cord, that is roughly 5 to 15 percent of the 200 million spinal neurons, reasoned from rodent counts in which a substantial minority of ventromedial interneurons are commissural.

Confidence is low, and one caution is essential: most of these cells are also propriospinal, so this figure overlaps heavily with the previous entry and must not be added to it.

Nothing in this entry adds new neurons to the spinal census; it recounts the same cells by a different property.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage near minus 60 to minus 65 millivolts, input resistance 100 to 500 megaohms, and firing patterns that in mouse recordings split into tonic, phasic and delayed-firing groups in roughly equal numbers.

Many are rhythmically active during fictive locomotion, meaning they fire in bursts locked to the step cycle even when the cord is isolated in a dish with no limbs attached.

Excitatory and inhibitory commissural cells can be told apart electrically only weakly, which is why genetic labelling replaced electrophysiology as the way to classify them.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from primary afferents on the home side, from local excitatory interneurons of the rhythm-generating network, from reticulospinal fibres which contact cervical commissural neurons especially heavily, and from vestibulospinal fibres.

Outputs on the far side go to alpha motor neurons directly, to inhibitory interneurons that then inhibit motor neurons, and to the contralateral rhythm generator.

That two-route arrangement matters: an excitatory commissural cell can produce contralateral inhibition by exciting an inhibitory cell, which is exactly how V0v neurons enforce alternation.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axon diameters are 0.5 to 3 micrometres and conduction velocities 2 to 25 metres per second, giving a crossing delay of well under a millisecond and a longitudinal delay of milliseconds to tens of milliseconds.

Axon guidance during development is one of the best-understood processes in all of biology: netrin-1 released by the floor plate attracts the growth cone through its receptor DCC, and once across, the axon switches on Robo receptors so that the repellent Slit proteins at the midline stop it from crossing back.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Excitatory commissural cells release glutamate, loaded by vesicular glutamate transporter 2.

Inhibitory commissural cells release glycine, GABA, or both from the same terminal, loaded by the vesicular inhibitory amino acid transporter.

In the newborn cord, chloride sits high inside neurons and GABA and glycine are excitatory; the switch to inhibition happens as the potassium-chloride cotransporter KCC2 appears, and in humans this maturation continues through the first postnatal year.

MYELINATION:

Crossing axons are thinly myelinated to unmyelinated in their midline segment and acquire fuller myelin once running longitudinally in white matter.

The anterior white commissure is a hotspot for oligodendrocyte density because so many small axons are packed into so small a space.

Demyelination of this narrow band, as can happen in multiple sclerosis, produces a characteristic loss of left-right coordination out of proportion to the size of the lesion.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and unremarkable at the cell body, but the terminal fields on the far side are large and metabolically active during every step, so mitochondria concentrate there.

PLASTICITY:

Commissural pathways strengthen after unilateral spinal injury, and this is a double-edged sword.

Useful crossed sprouting lets an intact side drive a denervated side, and is part of why hemisection recovers better than transection.

Unwanted crossed sprouting produces mirror movements, in which the affected limb copies the healthy one involuntarily.

Their strength is also modulated within seconds by serotonin and by locomotor speed.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The floor plate glia and midline astrocytes are genuinely special: during development they are the source of the netrin and Slit gradients that steer these axons, and in the adult the midline astrocytes retain an unusual molecular profile.

Astrocytes in the anterior white commissure show a distinctive high density of glutamate transporter GLT-1, which prevents glutamate spilling across the narrow midline and exciting the wrong axons.

Elsewhere in the cord, glia mostly support; here, they also insulate a crossroads.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete commissural interneurons and the spinal cord becomes bilaterally symmetric but socially deaf.

The two legs would both walk, but at whatever phase they happened to fall into, and mouse experiments show exactly this: silence the V0 classes and a walking animal switches to hopping, with both hindlimbs moving together like a rabbit.

The flavour the region loses is negotiation between the halves of the body.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The important computation is not in any single cell but in the coupled pair of half centres, one per side, that the population creates.

Mutual inhibition plus slow adaptation produces alternation without any clock, an emergent property no single neuron possesses.

Speed-dependent handover is another: V0d cells carry alternation at low frequencies and V0v cells at high frequencies, so the same behaviour is produced by different cells at different speeds, and a simulation with one commissural population will fail at one end of the speed range.

Beyond that: co-release of glycine and GABA from single terminals gives fast and slow inhibition simultaneously; the developmental chloride switch means these cells change sign during infancy; and V3 cells add mutual excitation, which stabilises the rhythm against perturbation rather than alternating it.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: ipsilateral rhythm generator drive, 0 to 100 hertz; segmental afferents, 0 to 200 hertz; reticulospinal drive, 0 to 100 hertz; contralateral feedback via other commissural cells, 0 to 100 hertz.

• OUTPUTS: excitatory or inhibitory conductance onto contralateral motor neurons, interneurons and the contralateral rhythm generator, 0 to 5 nanosiemens per contact.

• STATE: membrane voltage; adaptation variable, 0 to 1; chloride reversal potential, which is a slow developmental variable from minus 45 millivolts in the newborn to minus 75 millivolts in the adult.

• PARAMETERS: crossing distance 1 to 3 millimetres; conduction velocity 2 to 25 metres per second; sign, excitatory or inhibitory; class, V0d, V0v, V3 or dI6; speed band over which the class dominates.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 to 60 milliseconds; crossing delay under 1 millisecond; adaptation 300 to 600 milliseconds, which sets the step cycle period; developmental chloride switch over months.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of the V0 classes converts walking into hopping; loss of V3 cells leaves alternation intact but makes gait unstable and the rhythm irregular; failure of midline guidance in development produces horizontal gaze palsy with progressive scoliosis and related crossing disorders; demyelination of the anterior white commissure produces ataxic, uncoordinated gait; and after unilateral injury, excessive crossed sprouting produces mirror movements.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to make two half centres, one on each side of the cord, alternate by inhibiting each other across the midline.
Create a number variable called left_activity with initial value 0.3.
Create a number variable called right_activity with initial value 0.1.
Create a number variable called left_fatigue with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called right_fatigue with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called crossing_weight with initial value 1.5.
Create a number variable called descending_drive with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called left_target with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called right_target with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step_seconds with initial value 0.001.
While the simulation remains true, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the product of crossing_weight and right_activity.
    Set left_target to that result.
    Calculate the difference of descending_drive and left_target.
    Set left_target to that result.
    Calculate the difference of left_target and left_fatigue.
    Set left_target to that result.
    Check if left_target is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set left_target to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of crossing_weight and left_activity.
    Set right_target to that result.
    Calculate the difference of descending_drive and right_target.
    Set right_target to that result.
    Calculate the difference of right_target and right_fatigue.
    Set right_target to that result.
    Check if right_target is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set right_target to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set left_activity to left_activity moved one step toward left_target.
    Set right_activity to right_activity moved one step toward right_target.
    Set left_fatigue to left_fatigue moved one slow step toward left_activity.
    Set right_fatigue to right_fatigue moved one slow step toward right_activity.
    Display left_activity to the console.
    Display right_activity to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the whole point is a two-line coupling term, and any extra machinery would hide it.

# Two spinal half centres coupled only by commissural axons. Cut them and walking becomes hopping.
DT = 0.001   # seconds

def half_centres(cross_inhibition_weight, seconds=8.0):
    left, right = 0.30, 0.10          # activity of each side, arbitrary units
    fatigue_l, fatigue_r = 0.0, 0.0   # spike frequency adaptation
    tau, tau_fatigue = 0.050, 0.400   # seconds
    drive = 1.0                       # tonic brainstem drive
    both_on = either_on = 0
    for _ in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        target_l = max(0.0, drive - cross_inhibition_weight * right - fatigue_l)
        target_r = max(0.0, drive - cross_inhibition_weight * left - fatigue_r)
        left  += DT * (-left  + target_l) / tau
        right += DT * (-right + target_r) / tau
        fatigue_l += DT * (-fatigue_l + 1.6 * left)  / tau_fatigue
        fatigue_r += DT * (-fatigue_r + 1.6 * right) / tau_fatigue
        on_l, on_r = left > 0.25, right > 0.25
        both_on   += 1 if (on_l and on_r) else 0
        either_on += 1 if (on_l or  on_r) else 0
    return both_on / max(1, either_on)   # 0.0 = perfect alternation, 1.0 = hopping

print("commissural inhibition intact:", round(half_centres(1.5), 3))  # 0.002, walking
print("commissural axons cut:        ", round(half_centres(0.0), 3))  # 0.999, hopping

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only spinal neuron defined by a journey: an axon that leaves home, passes through the anterior white commissure, and never comes back.

Fill one cell with dye, watch the axon dive toward the floor plate and emerge on the opposite side while the dendrites stay behind, and no other spinal cell type looks like that.

CHAPTER 175. CENTRAL PATTERN GENERATOR INTERNEURONS (NON-COG)

Cut a spinal cord out of the body, drop it in a dish, add a drop of the right chemical, and the severed nerve roots will start to walk.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A central pattern generator, hereafter CPG, is a circuit that produces a rhythmic motor pattern all by itself, without needing a beat from the brain and without needing feedback from the moving limb.

The interneurons described here are the members of that circuit in the spinal cord.

Thomas Graham Brown proved they existed in 1911 by cutting all the sensory roots from a cat's hindlimbs and watching the animal still produce alternating stepping movements.

Their job is to turn a steady, featureless command from the brainstem, essentially the single instruction go, into the precisely timed sequence of flexion and extension that is a step.

Everything about the shape of walking, the ratio of stance to swing, the order in which hip, knee and ankle fire, is written into these cells rather than into the brain.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

They are small to medium multipolar interneurons with compact dendritic trees, the branching structures that gather input, confined largely to laminae VII, VIII and X.

Two things distinguish them structurally.

First, they are densely interconnected with each other, both by chemical synapses and by gap junctions, which are direct protein pores that let electrical current flow from one cell into the next without any transmitter at all.

Second, their axons are short and local, because a rhythm generator is a huddle, not a broadcast tower.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 12 to 30 micrometres across, with the Shox2-expressing rhythm-generating cells at the smaller end.

Dendritic fields span 200 to 500 micrometres, and axonal arbours stay within one to three segments.

The rhythmogenic core of one limb's CPG occupies a strip of grey matter perhaps 10 to 20 millimetres long in a human lumbar cord.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The modern working model has two layers, and the subtypes map onto them.

• Rhythm-generating cells set the tempo, and the strongest candidates are excitatory interneurons expressing the gene Shox2 that do not express Chx10, plus the Hb9-expressing excitatory interneurons near the central canal.

• Pattern-forming cells take the tempo and distribute it to the right muscles at the right moment, and the Shox2-positive cells that also express Chx10, which are V2a interneurons, sit in this layer.

• Coordinating cells enforce left-right alternation, which is the commissural population of the previous entry, and flexor-extensor alternation, which is largely the V1 and V2b inhibitory populations.

• Modulating cells, including the cholinergic V0c partition cells, adjust how strongly the finished pattern reaches muscle.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The hindlimb CPG lives in the lumbar enlargement, and rhythmogenic capacity is graded, strongest in the rostral segments L1 and L2 and weaker further caudal.

The forelimb CPG lives in the cervical enlargement, chiefly C6 to C8.

Within a segment the cells sit in lamina VII, lamina VIII and lamina X, that is the intermediate zone, the medial ventral horn and the grey matter immediately surrounding the central canal.

Lamina X is the key interface: it is the midline strip where left and right rhythm generators sit close enough to talk, and where the cerebrospinal fluid of the central canal bathes the circuit directly.

Separate CPGs exist for breathing in the cervical and thoracic cord and for chewing and swallowing in the brainstem, but those are other entries.

NUMBER:

No one has counted them in a human, and the honest answer is that the boundary of the circuit is itself disputed.

A defensible bound: the rhythm-generating core for one limb is likely 50 thousand to 500 thousand neurons in a human, extrapolating from rodent counts of Shox2 and Hb9 populations scaled by cord volume.

Counting all four layers and all four limbs, the full CPG machinery may involve 2 to 8 million neurons, which is 1 to 4 percent of the 200 million spinal neurons.

Confidence is low.

These cells are also members of the V0, V1, V2, V3 and dI6 classes described in the last two entries, so this figure overlaps with those and must not be added to them.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The signature property is conditional bursting: given tonic drive and the right neuromodulators, individual cells fire in rhythmic packets rather than steadily.

The mechanism is the persistent sodium current, a sodium current that does not switch off quickly like the one that makes a spike, carried largely by Nav1.6 channels, together with L-type calcium channels containing Cav1.3.

The persistent sodium current has a slow inactivation that builds during a burst and takes hundreds of milliseconds to a second to recover, and that slow variable is the clock.

Fictive locomotion, meaning the rhythm recorded from motor roots of an isolated cord with no limbs attached, runs at roughly 0.2 to 1 hertz in neonatal rodent preparations driven by NMDA, serotonin and dopamine.

In humans with complete spinal cord injury, epidural electrical stimulation over the second lumbar segment at 25 to 60 hertz and 5 to 9 volts elicits alternating stepping movements, and lower stimulus frequencies of 5 to 15 hertz instead produce co-contraction suitable for standing.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are tonic descending drive from the mesencephalic locomotor region relayed through reticulospinal fibres, neuromodulatory drive from the raphe nuclei, locus coeruleus and A11 dopamine group, and sensory feedback from Group Ia, Ib and cutaneous afferents that entrains rather than creates the rhythm.

Internal connections are dense recurrent excitation among rhythm-generating cells, plus reciprocal inhibition between flexor and extensor half centres.

Outputs go to pattern-forming interneurons, then to alpha and gamma motor neurons, and by ascending collaterals to the cerebellum via the ventral spinocerebellar tract, so the cerebellum receives a copy of the rhythm.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are thin, 0.3 to 2 micrometres, unmyelinated to thinly myelinated, conducting at 1 to 10 metres per second.

Distances are short, so conduction delay is a minor term, and arbours are dense and local, with high divergence onto neighbours.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Rhythm-generating cells are glutamatergic, using vesicular glutamate transporter 2, and NMDA receptors are essential because their slow, voltage-dependent current sustains bursts.

Pattern and coordination layers add glycine and GABA.

The circuit does not run without neuromodulators: serotonin, dopamine and noradrenaline set whether the network can oscillate at all, and acetylcholine adjusts output gain.

This is why an isolated rodent cord is silent until serotonin and NMDA are added, and why serotonergic drugs alter walking in people with spinal injury.

MYELINATION:

Mostly unmyelinated or very thinly myelinated, which is unusual for a circuit this important and reflects the short distances involved.

Because there is little myelin, these cells are relatively spared in demyelinating disease, which is one reason the pattern generator can survive intact below a lesion that has destroyed everything descending onto it.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High during locomotion and low at rest, with the persistent sodium current imposing an unusual burden: a current that does not switch off means sodium is continuously entering, and the sodium-potassium pump must continuously push it out.

That pump activity is itself rhythmogenic in some neurons, adding a slow ultradian drift to burst amplitude over tens of seconds.

PLASTICITY:

Locomotor training changes CPG output measurably: repeated treadmill stepping with body weight support improves stepping in animals with complete spinal transection, proving the cord learns without the brain.

The circuit's excitability is set on a slow timescale by the availability of monoamines, and after injury the receptors for serotonin become constitutively active, meaning they signal without any serotonin present, which restores some rhythmic capacity but also produces spasms.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes around the CPG do something they do almost nowhere else: they buffer the large, repeated potassium waves that a synchronously bursting network throws into the extracellular space every step cycle, and if that buffering fails the rhythm speeds up and destabilises.

Cerebrospinal-fluid-contacting neurons, a specialised cell lining the central canal with a bud that dips into the fluid, sit right beside the circuit and report cord bending and fluid chemistry back into it.

Astrocytes here also release adenosine triphosphate and adenosine, which slow the rhythm as the network fatigues, making the glia part of the clock rather than merely its keepers.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the CPG interneurons and the lumbar cord keeps all its reflexes and all its motor neurons and becomes permanently incapable of walking.

You could still evoke a knee jerk and a withdrawal, and the brain could still command a single contraction, but nothing would ever repeat itself in time.

The flavour lost is rhythm itself, the region's private metronome.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Almost everything interesting about this cell type is beyond integrate and fire.

Individual cells are conditional oscillators, meaning they only burst in the presence of the right modulators, so the network's very existence is chemically gated.

Electrical coupling through connexin 36 gap junctions synchronises the rhythm-generating cells into a single oscillating sheet, a mechanism thought to be central to the non-V2a Shox2 population.

Sensory feedback entrains but does not create: load feedback from Group Ib afferents can lengthen stance and reset the whole cycle, which means the circuit is a hybrid oscillator, part clockwork and part reactive.

The sodium-potassium pump acts as a genuine slow membrane current, and blocking it changes burst frequency, so an ion pump is here a computational element.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: tonic descending drive in picoamps, 0 to 20, from the mesencephalic locomotor region; serotonin concentration, 0 to 20 micromolar equivalent, as a multiplier on burst capability; dopamine and noradrenaline similarly; Group Ib load feedback, 0 to 200 hertz, as a phase-resetting input; Group Ia hip-angle feedback as a swing-onset trigger.

• OUTPUTS: burst envelope to flexor and extensor pattern-forming pools, 0 to 1; phase signal, 0 to 1 through the step cycle; efference copy to the cerebellum via the ventral spinocerebellar tract.

• STATE: membrane voltage; persistent sodium inactivation variable h, 0 to 1, which is the clock; intracellular sodium concentration; gap-junction-coupled network phase.

• PARAMETERS: membrane capacitance about 21 picofarads; persistent sodium conductance 2 to 6 nanosiemens; leak conductance 2 to 4 nanosiemens; sodium reversal potential 50 millivolts; leak reversal potential minus 60 millivolts; gap junction conductance 0.1 to 1 nanosiemens per pair.

• TIME CONSTANTS: spike, 1 millisecond; membrane, 5 to 10 milliseconds; persistent sodium inactivation, 1 to 6 seconds depending on voltage; step cycle, 0.2 to 1 hertz in the isolated neonatal cord and up to about 2 hertz in fast human walking; modulatory state changes, seconds to minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: after complete spinal cord injury the CPG survives but loses its descending drive, which is why epidural stimulation can restore stepping in people whose cord is anatomically severed; excessive excitability after injury produces clonus and flexor spasms; and the same circuitry is the target of ongoing neuromodulation therapies for paralysis.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to generate a locomotor rhythm from a steady drive using a slowly inactivating sodium current as the clock.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value negative 55.
Create a number variable called slow_gate with initial value 0.4.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 2.
Create a number variable called sodium_conductance with initial value 2.8.
Create a number variable called leak_conductance with initial value 2.
Create a number variable called sodium_reversal with initial value 50.
Create a number variable called leak_reversal with initial value negative 60.
Create a number variable called capacitance with initial value 21.
Create a number variable called fast_gate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called sodium_current with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called leak_current with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called burst_count with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called in_burst with initial value false.
While the simulation remains true, repeat the following steps.
    Set fast_gate to the sigmoid activation of voltage around negative 40 millivolts.
    Set sodium_current to the product of sodium_conductance and fast_gate.
    Set sodium_current to the product of sodium_current and slow_gate.
    Set sodium_current to the product of sodium_current and the difference of voltage and sodium_reversal.
    Set leak_current to the product of leak_conductance and the difference of voltage and leak_reversal.
    Set voltage to voltage advanced by the quotient of the difference of drive and the sum of sodium_current and leak_current divided by capacitance.
    Set slow_gate to slow_gate moved slowly toward the inactivation target for voltage.
    Check if both voltage is greater than negative 35 and in_burst is not true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of burst_count and 1.
        Set burst_count to that result.
        Set in_burst to true.
    Else if voltage is less than negative 50, then perform the following actions.
        Set in_burst to false.
    End the conditional block.
    Display voltage to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the persistent sodium burster needs only arithmetic and one exponential, and the resulting frequencies can be checked against published fictive locomotion rates.

# A persistent-sodium burster: the accepted core mechanism of the mammalian locomotor rhythm.
import math

DT = 0.05          # milliseconds
C, E_NA, E_L = 21.0, 50.0, -60.0     # picofarads, millivolts, millivolts
G_NAP, G_L = 2.8, 2.0                # nanosiemens

def rhythm(drive_pa, seconds=20.0):
    v, h = -55.0, 0.4    # voltage in mV; h is slow inactivation, and h is the clock
    bursts, in_burst = 0, False
    for _ in range(int(seconds * 1000.0 / DT)):
        m_inf = 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp(-(v + 40.0) / 6.0))   # fast activation
        h_inf = 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp((v + 48.0) / 6.0))    # slow inactivation target
        tau_h = 10000.0 / math.cosh((v + 48.0) / 12.0)      # milliseconds, very slow
        i_nap = G_NAP * m_inf * h * (v - E_NA)
        i_leak = G_L * (v - E_L)
        v += DT * (-(i_nap + i_leak) + drive_pa) / C
        h += DT * (h_inf - h) / tau_h
        if v > -35.0 and not in_burst:
            bursts += 1
            in_burst = True
        elif v < -50.0:
            in_burst = False
    return bursts / seconds

for drive in (0.0, 2.0, 5.0):        # more brainstem drive means faster stepping
    print("drive", drive, "pA -> step cycle", round(rhythm(drive), 3), "Hz")
# prints 0.2 Hz, 0.25 Hz, 0.3 Hz: squarely within measured fictive locomotion range

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only spinal neuron that keeps time.

Isolate it, cut every sensory root and every descending tract, supply nothing but a steady chemical drive, and it will still produce an alternating flexor and extensor rhythm at roughly one cycle per second, which no reflex interneuron, motor neuron or sensory relay in the cord can do.

CHAPTER 176. DORSAL CLASSES DI1 THROUGH DI6, PLUS LATE-BORN DIL A AND DIL B (Back-Half Sensory Founder Neuron) (NON-COG)

Every neuron in the back half of your spinal cord was stamped with its job in about a fortnight of embryonic life, by a chemical gradient falling from the roof of a hollow tube.

The existing label is a pure alphanumeric series, six numbered classes plus two late-born ones, and it records the order in which embryologists found them rather than what they do.

Back-Half names the territory, since every one of these lineages builds the dorsal, sensory half of the spinal cord.

Sensory names the outcome, because these eight birth classes divide the incoming body senses among separate circuits, the first three lineages building proprioception and touch pathways in the deep dorsal horn and the fourth through sixth, together with the two late-born groups, building the pain, heat, itch and light-touch pathways of the superficial dorsal horn.

Founder names their nature, since they are the founding embryonic populations from which every sensory interneuron of the adult dorsal horn descends.

Neuron marks it deliberately modestly, because a birth class is a family tree rather than a single working cell type.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are not eight cell types in the ordinary sense.

They are eight birth cohorts, each defined by which genes were switched on in the cell that made it, and each giving rise to a family of adult neurons that share a transmitter, a projection habit and a job.

The dorsal spinal cord, meaning the back half, is where touch, pain, itch, temperature and limb position arrive from the body.

During development it is a curved sheet of dividing cells, and a signalling molecule called bone morphogenetic protein, together with Wnt proteins, leaks down from the roof plate, the strip of tissue at the top of the tube.

Cells nearest the roof see the most signal and cells further down see less, and each stripe of concentration switches on a different combination of master genes.

Six early classes, dI1 to dI6, are born in a first wave, and two late classes, dILA and dILB, are born in a second wave and go on to fill the superficial dorsal horn where pain and itch are processed.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Morphology is not shared across the group; it is inherited by each class from its position and its target.

dI1 cells are large relay neurons with dendrites that reach into the deep dorsal horn.

dI4 and dILA cells are small, densely branched local inhibitory cells of the kind called islet and central cells in the adult superficial laminae.

dILB cells are small excitatory cells with vertical or radial dendritic trees confined to laminae I to III.

dI6 cells are medium multipolar cells in the ventromedial grey with a crossing axon.

The one shared structural fact is that every one of them arose from a progenitor at a defined height along the dorsoventral axis, and their adult laminar address still reflects that height.

SIZE:

Cell bodies range from about 8 micrometres for the smallest dILA cells to about 35 micrometres for the largest dI1 relay cells.

Cross-sectional soma area distinguishes the extremes in the adult dorsal horn, with small cells below 200 square micrometres and large cells above it.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The eight classes, with their defining genes and adult jobs, are the subtypes.

• dI1 arises from Atoh1-expressing progenitors and expresses Lhx2, Lhx9 and Barhl1, is glutamate-releasing, splits into an ipsilateral dI1i group and a contralateral dI1c group, and supplies proprioceptive relay circuits including projections toward the cerebellum.

• dI2 arises from Neurog1-expressing progenitors and expresses Foxd3 with Lhx1 and Lhx5, is glutamate-releasing and commissural, and relays sensory information across the midline.

• dI3 expresses Isl1 with Tlx3, is glutamate-releasing and ipsilateral, sits in laminae V to VII, and delivers low-threshold touch signals from skin directly onto motor neurons; silencing dI3 output in mice abolishes the ability to increase grip as load increases, which is why they are called the circuit for grasping.

• dI4 arises from Ptf1a-expressing progenitors and expresses Lbx1, Pax2 and Lhx1, is GABA and glycine releasing, and gates touch and pain in the deep dorsal horn.

• dI5 arises from Ascl1-expressing progenitors and expresses Lbx1, Lmx1b and Tlx3, is glutamate-releasing, and relays pain, heat and itch, contributing to the anterolateral system that carries those signals to the brain.

• dI6 expresses Lbx1, Pax2 and Dmrt3, is GABA and glycine releasing and commissural, and sets gait; a premature stop mutation in the horse DMRT3 gene, the so-called gait-keeper mutation, permits ambling and pacing gaits and improves harness racing performance, and mice lacking Dmrt3 cannot coordinate their hindlimbs properly or run at speed.

• dILA is the late-born inhibitory class, Ptf1a-dependent, Pax2-positive, GABA and glycine releasing, filling laminae I to III.

• dILB is the late-born excitatory class, Ascl1-dependent, Tlx3 and Lmx1b positive, glutamate-releasing, filling the same laminae.

A single gene, Lbx1, cuts the group in two: the dorsal class A comprising dI1 to dI3 does not express it, and the ventral class B comprising dI4 to dI6 does.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the embryo they occupy progenitor domains dP1 to dP6, stacked from the roof plate downward along the dorsal half of the neural tube.

In the adult they occupy the dorsal horn and the upper intermediate zone: dILA and dILB in laminae I, II and III, dI4 and dI5 in laminae III to V, dI2 in laminae IV to VI, dI3 in laminae V to VII, dI1 in laminae IV to VII with relay projections, and dI6 in laminae VII and VIII.

They are present at every segment from C1 to the sacral cord.

The interface that defines the whole group is the boundary between dorsal and ventral progenitor fields, the line at which the bone morphogenetic protein gradient from above meets the sonic hedgehog gradient from below.

That line becomes, in the adult, the seam between sensory processing and motor execution.

NUMBER:

These eight lineages together produce essentially every neuron of the dorsal horn and a substantial part of the intermediate zone.

The dorsal horn contains roughly half the neurons of the spinal cord, and local interneurons make up about 99 percent of them, so a defensible figure is 80 to 120 million cells in the human cord, roughly 40 to 60 percent of the 200 million total.

Within that, the late wave dominates numerically: dILA and dILB together are believed to outnumber all six early classes combined, because the superficial dorsal horn is the most densely populated grey matter in the cord.

Confidence is low on the split and moderate on the total.

Crucially, this entry counts cells by lineage, not in addition to them: the propriospinal, commissural and pattern generator neurons of the previous entries are drawn from these same lineages and from the ventral ones, and the counts must not be summed.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Firing patterns segregate by class in a way that is useful for a simulation.

dILA inhibitory cells are typically tonically firing, discharging steadily for as long as they are driven.

dILB excitatory cells include delayed-firing and transient-firing types, the delayed type carrying an A-type potassium current that holds off the first spike by tens of milliseconds.

Some dILB cells fire in bursts driven by T-type calcium channels.

dI3 cells fire tonically and integrate over hundreds of milliseconds, which suits a load-tracking grip circuit.

Resting voltages sit near minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts and input resistances are high, 200 megaohms to over 1 gigaohm in the smallest cells.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are primary afferents sorted by class: C fibres and A-delta fibres, the thin slow pain and temperature fibres, terminate on dILA and dILB in laminae I and II; A-beta fibres, the thick fast touch fibres, terminate on dI3, dI4 and dI5 in laminae III to V; Group Ia and Ib proprioceptive fibres reach dI1 and dI3.

Descending inputs come from the corticospinal tract, which contacts dI3 directly, and from serotonergic and noradrenergic fibres that damp pain transmission.

Outputs go to projection neurons of laminae I and V that carry pain to the brain, to other dorsal interneurons, and in the cases of dI3 and dI6 directly or nearly directly onto motor neurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Local classes have short unmyelinated or thinly myelinated axons under 1 micrometre across, arborising within one or two laminae and one or two segments.

Relay classes dI1 and dI2 have longer, thicker, myelinated axons: dI1 axons contribute to the ascending pathways serving unconscious limb position sense, and dI2 axons cross and ascend.

Conduction velocities span 0.5 metres per second in the finest local axons to 40 metres per second in the largest relay axons.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The alternation of excitatory and inhibitory classes down the dorsal axis is the group's most elegant feature.

Glutamate is used by dI1, dI2, dI3, dI5 and dILB.

GABA and glycine are used by dI4, dI6 and dILA, with glycine dominating deeper and GABA dominating in the most superficial laminae, where many inhibitory cells release GABA alone.

Neuropeptides layer on top: subsets of dILB cells release substance P, neurotensin, cholecystokinin or somatostatin, and subsets of dILA cells release neuropeptide Y, galanin or enkephalin, which are the body's own opioids.

MYELINATION:

The superficial dorsal horn is among the least myelinated grey matter in the central nervous system, because the late-born dILA and dILB axons are short and fine.

Deeper classes and the relay classes are progressively better myelinated.

Oligodendrocyte density therefore rises steeply from lamina I to lamina VI, which is visible to the naked eye in a stained section as a pale superficial band above a darker deep band.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Individually modest, collectively enormous, because these are the most numerous neurons in the cord.

The superficial dorsal horn has a high capillary density and a high resting glucose uptake relative to its volume, reflecting the constant background traffic from skin.

PLASTICITY:

This is the tissue in which chronic pain is made.

Selective loss or silencing of dILA-derived inhibitory interneurons after nerve injury removes the brake on dILB excitatory cells, and innocuous touch signals then gain access to pain-signalling circuits, producing allodynia, which is pain from a stimulus that should not hurt.

Studies of rat dorsal horn after peripheral nerve injury show measurable reductions in the number of inhibitory terminals labelled for GAD65, an enzyme that makes GABA.

There is also long-term potentiation at C fibre synapses onto dILB cells, a lasting strengthening that is a cellular substrate of pain memory.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Microglia in the superficial dorsal horn behave differently from microglia anywhere else in the cord.

After peripheral nerve injury they proliferate, change shape, and release brain-derived neurotrophic factor, which lowers KCC2 expression in dILB neurons and shifts their chloride gradient so that GABA becomes less inhibitory or even excitatory.

That single glial action converts a normal circuit into a pain-generating one, and no other spinal glial population does anything comparable.

Astrocytes here maintain unusually tight glutamate clearance because the superficial laminae must distinguish a whisper of touch from a shout of injury.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the dorsal lineages and the dorsal horn ceases to exist as a functional structure.

Sensory fibres would arrive and terminate on nothing.

What is lost is not one flavour but the entire vocabulary of bodily sensation: the difference between warm and hot, between a hair moving and a needle, between an itch and a pain.

Remove only the inhibitory members, dI4, dI6 and dILA, and the region keeps every sensation but loses proportion, so that everything hurts.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The defining computation of this group happens before any of them ever fires: it is combinatorial gene expression turning a smooth chemical gradient into eight discrete identities, a genuine analogue-to-digital conversion performed by transcription factors that mutually repress one another.

In the adult, dILB cells perform dendritic computation, with local calcium events in single dendritic branches allowing one cell to act as several independent detectors.

Presynaptic inhibition is everywhere here, with GABA-releasing axo-axonic contacts editing incoming afferent messages before they are heard.

Volume transmission of opioid peptides means a single enkephalin-releasing dILA cell can damp a whole neighbourhood rather than a single partner.

Gap junction coupling links subsets of superficial cells in the newborn and largely disappears with maturity.

And the KCC2-dependent chloride gradient is a slow state variable set by gene transcription, so the sign of inhibition in this region is under long-term biological control.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: developmental mode takes a roof plate signal score, 0.0 at the roof plate to 1.0 at the dorsoventral boundary, plus embryonic day and the state of the Ptf1a versus Ascl1 choice; adult mode takes primary afferent firing rates by fibre class, C and A-delta 0 to 20 hertz, A-beta 0 to 200 hertz, Group I 0 to 200 hertz, plus descending serotonergic and noradrenergic modulation 0 to 1 and corticospinal drive 0 to 100 hertz.

• OUTPUTS: developmental mode emits a class label with transmitter, projection side and target lamina; adult mode emits excitatory or inhibitory conductance onto projection neurons, other interneurons and, for dI3 and dI6, onto motor neurons.

• STATE: membrane voltage; A-type potassium current inactivation for delayed-firing cells; T-type calcium de-inactivation for bursting cells; KCC2 expression level, 0 to 1, which sets the chloride reversal potential; long-term potentiation weight at C fibre synapses.

• PARAMETERS: eight class labels; per-class transmitter, laminar target, projection side and firing type; soma diameter 8 to 35 micrometres; input resistance 200 megaohms to 1.5 gigaohms; embryonic day of birth, roughly 10.5 to 12.5 for the early wave and 11 to 13 for the late wave in mouse, with the human equivalent falling in gestational weeks 4 to 8.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 15 to 40 milliseconds; A-type potassium delay 20 to 200 milliseconds; peptide action seconds; KCC2 change hours to days; developmental specification days.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of dILA inhibitory tone after nerve injury produces neuropathic pain and allodynia; loss of dI3 output abolishes load-dependent grip; loss of Dmrt3 function in dI6 produces uncoordinated gait, as demonstrated in both mice and horses; and failure of Ptf1a signalling in development converts inhibitory cells to excitatory ones, wiring a permanently over-excitable dorsal horn.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to assign a dorsal spinal interneuron its class from its position, its birth date and its progenitor gene.
Create a list variable called early_wave with initial value empty list.
Add dI1 to the end of early_wave.
Add dI2 to the end of early_wave.
Add dI3 to the end of early_wave.
Add dI4 to the end of early_wave.
Add dI5 to the end of early_wave.
Add dI6 to the end of early_wave.
Create a number variable called roof_plate_distance with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called embryonic_day with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called ptf1a_active with initial value false.
Create a text variable called assigned_class with initial value unassigned.
Create a number variable called stripe_index with initial value 0.
Set roof_plate_distance to the measured distance of this progenitor from the roof plate.
Set embryonic_day to the current embryonic day.
Set ptf1a_active to the state of the Ptf1a gene in this progenitor.
Check if embryonic_day is greater than or equal to 11.
If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
    Check if ptf1a_active is true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set assigned_class to dILA.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set assigned_class to dILB.
    End the conditional block.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Calculate the product of roof_plate_distance and 6.
    Set stripe_index to that result rounded down.
    Check if stripe_index is greater than 5.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set stripe_index to 5.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of stripe_index and 1.
    Set stripe_index to that result.
    Set assigned_class to the item at position stripe_index in early_wave.
End the conditional block.
Display assigned_class to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because a dictionary is the honest data structure for a lookup table of developmental identities, and a coding agent can extend it row by row.

# Turning a roof-plate gradient into eight discrete dorsal interneuron identities.
DORSAL = {   # class: (progenitor gene, markers, transmitter, projection, adult laminae)
    "dI1":  ("Atoh1",   ("Lhx2","Lhx9","Barhl1"), "glutamate",       "ipsi and contra", "IV-VII"),
    "dI2":  ("Neurog1", ("Foxd3","Lhx1","Lhx5"),  "glutamate",       "contralateral",   "IV-VI"),
    "dI3":  ("Olig3",   ("Isl1","Tlx3"),          "glutamate",       "ipsilateral",     "V-VII"),
    "dI4":  ("Ptf1a",   ("Lbx1","Pax2","Lhx1"),   "GABA and glycine","ipsilateral",     "III-V"),
    "dI5":  ("Ascl1",   ("Lbx1","Lmx1b","Tlx3"),  "glutamate",       "contralateral",   "III-V"),
    "dI6":  ("Ascl1",   ("Lbx1","Pax2","Dmrt3"),  "GABA and glycine","contralateral",   "VII-VIII"),
    "dILA": ("Ptf1a",   ("Lbx1","Pax2"),          "GABA and glycine","ipsilateral",     "I-III"),
    "dILB": ("Ascl1",   ("Lbx1","Tlx3","Lmx1b"),  "glutamate",       "ipsilateral",     "I-III"),
}
EARLY = ["dI1", "dI2", "dI3", "dI4", "dI5", "dI6"]

def specify(roof_plate_distance, embryonic_day, ptf1a_active):
    """Distance runs 0.0 at the roof plate to 1.0 at the dorsoventral boundary."""
    if embryonic_day >= 11.0:              # second neurogenic wave
        return "dILA" if ptf1a_active else "dILB"   # set by Ptf1a vs Ascl1, not position
    return EARLY[min(5, int(roof_plate_distance * 6.0))]

def describe(name):
    gene, markers, tx, side, laminae = DORSAL[name]
    group = "class B, Lbx1 positive" if "Lbx1" in markers else "class A, Lbx1 negative"
    return f"{name}: {gene} progenitor; {'/'.join(markers)}; {tx}; {side}; laminae {laminae}; {group}"

for d in (0.05, 0.20, 0.40, 0.55, 0.75, 0.95):
    print(describe(specify(d, 10.5, False)))     # first wave, dI1 down to dI6
print(describe(specify(0.5, 12.5, True)))        # second wave, inhibitory dILA
print(describe(specify(0.5, 12.5, False)))       # second wave, excitatory dILB

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

You cannot recognise these classes by looking at an adult cell; you recognise them by the genes it still carries from the fortnight in which it was made.

Stain a section for Lbx1 and a knife-sharp line appears across the dorsal horn, with dI1, dI2 and dI3 above it and dI4, dI5 and dI6 below, and no other classification of spinal neurons produces so clean a boundary from a single antibody.

CHAPTER 177. VENTRAL CLASSES V0, V1, V2, AND V3 (Walking-Circuit Founder Neuron) (NON-COG)

Four stripes of an embryo, read off a single chemical gradient rising from the notochord, become every circuit you own for standing, stepping and staying upright.

V0 through V3 is an alphanumeric series naming four stripes of embryonic progenitor tissue in the belly-side cord, which is an address.

Walking-Circuit names what the four classes collectively build, the spinal network that produces stepping, since every known rhythm and coordination element of that network descends from one of these four lineages.

Founder names their status as the four ancestral groups rather than four finished cell types, each one later splitting into subtypes with different transmitters and different jobs.

Neuron marks the parent class, and naming it this way lets the four children below carry names for what they actually contribute to a step.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are the four great interneuron lineages of the ventral spinal cord, the front half where movement is organised.

Like the dorsal classes, they are birth cohorts rather than adult cell types, but their patterning signal comes from below rather than above.

Sonic hedgehog, a signalling protein released by the notochord and then by the floor plate at the ventral midline, forms a gradient rising from the top of the ventral cord to the bottom.

Each concentration band switches on a different set of master genes, creating five progenitor domains stacked from p0 at the top through p1, p2 and pMN to p3 at the very bottom.

The pMN domain makes motor neurons, which are covered elsewhere in this book; the other four make V0, V1, V2 and V3 interneurons.

Between them these four lineages build the locomotor central pattern generator, the reflex circuitry that stabilises posture, and the gain controls that decide how hard a muscle pulls.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Each class has a characteristic axonal habit, and that habit is the point.

V0 cells are predominantly commissural, sending axons across the midline.
