V1 cells are ipsilateral and descending, meaning they stay on their own side and send short axons toward the tail, typically one to three segments.

V2 cells are ipsilateral, with V2a cells descending over several segments and V2b cells acting more locally.

V3 cells are mostly commissural and mostly ascending or bifurcating.

Somata are compact and multipolar with dendritic fields, the input branches, of 200 to 600 micrometres.

SIZE:

Cell bodies run from about 10 micrometres for the smallest V1 Renshaw cells to about 30 micrometres for the largest V2a and V3 cells.

Axons range from a fraction of a millimetre for local V1 cells to the full length of the cord for the long descending members of V0 and V3.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• V0 arises from Dbx1-expressing progenitors and splits four ways: V0d, dorsal and inhibitory and commissural; V0v, ventral and excitatory and commissural, marked by Evx1; V0c, cholinergic and marked by Pitx2, which forms the large C-bouton synapses on motor neurons; and V0g, glutamatergic and Pitx2-positive.

• V1 arises from p1 progenitors and is marked by the gene En1, is entirely inhibitory using GABA and glycine, and contains the Renshaw cells that provide recurrent inhibition back onto motor neurons, making up roughly 9 percent of the V1 population, together with a parvalbumin-expressing proprioceptive group comprising about 27 percent of V1 cells that includes the Ia reciprocal inhibitory interneurons; further clades are defined by the genes Foxp2, MafA, Pou6f2 and Sp8.

• V2 arises from Lhx3 and Foxn4 expressing progenitors and divides into V2a, excitatory and marked by Chx10 which is also called Vsx2; V2b, inhibitory and marked by Gata2 and Gata3; and a small V2c group marked by Sox1.

• V3 arises from Nkx2-2 expressing p3 progenitors, is marked by Sim1, is glutamatergic and largely commissural, and divides into a dorsal group in lamina VII and a ventral group in lamina VIII.

WHERE IT LIVES:

In the embryo, the progenitor domains p0, p1, p2 and p3 lie stacked in the ventricular zone, the dividing cell layer lining the neural tube.

In the adult, V0 cells occupy laminae VII, VIII and X; V1 cells occupy the ventral part of lamina VII with the Renshaw cells packed into a narrow band in lamina VII immediately medial and ventral to the motor pools; V2a and V2b cells occupy lamina VII; V3 cells occupy laminae VII and VIII; and V0c cells form a distinctive column beside the central canal in lamina X, which is why they are called partition cells.

They are present at every segment, densest in the cervical and lumbosacral enlargements.

The critical interface is the boundary between p2 and pMN, the line at which the sonic hedgehog gradient stops making interneurons and starts making motor neurons.

That single line is the most consequential boundary in the ventral cord, and it is held by mutual repression between the transcription factors Irx3 and Olig2.

NUMBER:

The four ventral lineages together produce the majority of neurons in the ventral horn and intermediate zone.

A defensible estimate is 40 to 70 million cells in the human cord, roughly 20 to 35 percent of the 200 million spinal neurons.

Within that, V1 and V2 are the largest classes and V3 the smallest.

Renshaw cells specifically, being about 9 percent of V1, are on the order of a few hundred thousand to a couple of million, and V0c cholinergic partition cells are the rarest of all at perhaps tens of thousands, since each motor neuron receives C-boutons from only a small pool.

Confidence is low, and as with the dorsal entry this is a re-cut of the same population: the propriospinal, commissural and pattern generator neurons described earlier are drawn from these lineages, and these counts must not be added to those.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Renshaw cells, the best-characterised members, are small, high-input-resistance cells that fire a high-frequency burst of up to several hundred hertz in response to a single motor neuron spike, then fall silent.

V2a cells are typically tonic firers recruited progressively as locomotor speed rises.

V1 cells as a population fire during the transition phases of the step cycle and are responsible for terminating bursts, which is why silencing them slows locomotion in both mice and zebrafish.

V3 cells fire tonically and broadly across the cycle.

Resting voltages sit near minus 60 millivolts and input resistances range from 100 megaohms in the largest to over 800 megaohms in Renshaw cells.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are motor neuron axon collaterals onto Renshaw cells, which is the only place in the body where a motor neuron's own output feeds back onto an interneuron; Group Ia afferents onto the reciprocal inhibitory V1 cells; rhythm generator drive onto V2a and V0v; descending reticulospinal and vestibulospinal fibres onto all four; and, remarkably, a brain-wide set of descending inputs onto V1 cells from motor cortex, red nucleus, vestibular nuclei and reticular formation.

Outputs go to motor neurons, to each other, and across the midline.

V0c cells make the C-bouton, an unusually large cholinergic synapse on the motor neuron cell body, sitting opposite a specialised stack of membrane called the subsurface cistern.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axon diameters are 0.3 to 2.5 micrometres and conduction velocities 1 to 20 metres per second.

V1 axons are short and heavily branched near their targets; V2a axons descend several segments with collaterals at each; V0 and V3 axons cross and then travel.

The C-bouton of a V0c cell is up to 8 micrometres across, among the largest synaptic terminals anywhere in the nervous system.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The assignment is clean and is the single most useful fact for a simulation.

V0d, V1 and V2b are inhibitory, releasing GABA and glycine.

V0v, V2a and V3 are excitatory, releasing glutamate.

V0c is cholinergic, releasing acetylcholine onto muscarinic M2 receptors clustered at the C-bouton.

Activation of those M2 receptors inhibits SK potassium channels, reducing the medium afterhyperpolarization that follows each motor neuron spike and thereby allowing the motor neuron to fire faster, which is a gain control rather than a command.

MYELINATION:

Ventral interneuron axons are thinly myelinated where they run in white matter and largely unmyelinated within grey matter.

Renshaw cell axons are essentially unmyelinated, which suits their extremely short path back to the motor pool.

The long commissural V0 and V3 axons acquire proper myelin sheaths once they enter the ventral funiculus.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Renshaw cells sustain very high firing rates in short bursts, which imposes a sharp transient sodium load, and they are correspondingly rich in mitochondria for their size.

V0c cells must synthesise and package large quantities of acetylcholine for their giant terminals.

The rest are metabolically ordinary.

PLASTICITY:

C-boutons undergo dramatic structural change in disease: in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, hereafter ALS, the C-boutons on surviving motor neurons enlarge substantially before the motor neuron dies, an early and consistent finding.

Renshaw cell input to motor neurons is reduced in ALS models and in spasticity.

V2a cells show injury-induced plasticity that is being actively exploited: after cervical spinal cord injury, V2a interneurons can be recruited to restore drive to the phrenic motor neurons that run the diaphragm, which is a route toward weaning patients off ventilators.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia around the C-bouton are genuinely unusual: astrocyte processes wrap the giant cholinergic terminal and the postsynaptic cistern in a tight sheath that limits acetylcholine spillover, and this arrangement is not seen at ordinary spinal synapses.

Renshaw cells are enclosed in dense perineuronal nets, cages of extracellular matrix built largely of chondroitin sulphate proteoglycans, which stabilise their synapses and mark them out under the stain Wisteria floribunda agglutinin.

Those nets also make Renshaw cell connections unusually resistant to change in adulthood, which is a design decision by the nervous system: the recurrent inhibition loop is meant to be a fixed constant, not a learned variable.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the ventral lineages and the ventral horn keeps its motor neurons and loses everything that shapes them.

A command from the brain would reach muscle unedited: no recurrent damping, no reciprocal switch-off of the opposing muscle, no left-right negotiation, no burst termination, no gain scaling.

The flavour lost is control, in the engineering sense.

Movements would be all-or-nothing, jerky, symmetrical when they should alternate, and impossible to hold steady.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The V0c C-bouton is a textbook case of neuromodulation rather than transmission: it does not tell the motor neuron to fire, it changes how much the motor neuron fires for a given input, by closing potassium channels through a second messenger cascade over hundreds of milliseconds.

V0c cells are additionally coupled to one another by connexin 36 gap junctions, so the partition cell column acts partly as a single unit.

Renshaw cells receive gap-junction coupling from motor neurons in the newborn and are among the earliest-born interneurons in the cord.

V1 cells regulate burst duration rather than burst existence, which means their computation is temporal shaping, not gating, and silencing them converts asymmetric flexor and extensor durations into a symmetric 50 percent duty cycle.

Across the whole group, transcription factor cross-repression during development performs a sharpening operation that turns a smooth gradient into hard boundaries, and the same factors continue to be expressed in adulthood, maintaining identity rather than merely establishing it.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: developmental mode takes a sonic hedgehog exposure score, 0.0 at the dorsoventral boundary to 1.0 at the floor plate, and returns a progenitor domain and class; adult mode takes motor neuron collateral firing for Renshaw cells, 0 to 100 hertz; Group Ia afferent rate for reciprocal V1 cells, 0 to 150 hertz; rhythm generator drive for V2a and V0v, 0 to 100 hertz; descending drive from cortex, red nucleus, vestibular nuclei and reticular formation, 0 to 100 hertz each.

• OUTPUTS: inhibitory conductance onto motor neurons and antagonist interneurons for V0d, V1 and V2b; excitatory conductance for V0v, V2a and V3; for V0c, a modulatory multiplier on motor neuron gain of 1.0 to about 1.6 rather than a synaptic current.

• STATE: membrane voltage; burst adaptation variable; for V0c targets, the fraction of SK potassium channels suppressed, 0 to 1; for V2a, a recruitment threshold that scales with locomotor speed.

• PARAMETERS: class label from V0d, V0v, V0c, V0g, V1, V2a, V2b, V2c, V3; transmitter per class; projection side and direction; soma diameter 10 to 30 micrometres; input resistance 100 to 800 megaohms; Renshaw fraction of V1 about 0.09; parvalbumin proprioceptive fraction of V1 about 0.27.

• TIME CONSTANTS: Renshaw burst 20 to 50 milliseconds; glycine current decay 5 to 10 milliseconds; GABA type A decay 20 to 50 milliseconds; muscarinic M2 modulation onset 100 to 500 milliseconds and decay over seconds; V2a recruitment changes over one step cycle; developmental specification over days.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of V0 classes converts walking to hopping; loss of V1 slows locomotion and flattens flexor and extensor burst durations toward equality; loss of V3 leaves alternation intact but makes gait unstable; C-bouton enlargement and Renshaw cell dysfunction are early features of ALS; and loss of glycinergic V1 output underlies part of the rigidity in hereditary hyperekplexia and in acquired spasticity.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to assign a ventral spinal neuron its class and transmitter from its exposure to sonic hedgehog.
Create a number variable called hedgehog_score with initial value 0.
Create a text variable called domain with initial value unassigned.
Create a text variable called class_name with initial value unassigned.
Create a text variable called transmitter with initial value unassigned.
Set hedgehog_score to the measured sonic hedgehog exposure of this progenitor.
Check if hedgehog_score is less than 0.35.
If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set domain to p0.
    Set class_name to V0.
    Set transmitter to mixed, glycine for V0d and glutamate for V0v and acetylcholine for V0c.
Else if hedgehog_score is less than 0.5, then perform the following actions.
    Set domain to p1.
    Set class_name to V1.
    Set transmitter to glycine and GABA.
Else if hedgehog_score is less than 0.65, then perform the following actions.
    Set domain to p2.
    Set class_name to V2.
    Set transmitter to glutamate for V2a and glycine for V2b.
Else if hedgehog_score is less than 0.8, then perform the following actions.
    Set domain to pMN.
    Set class_name to motor neuron.
    Set transmitter to acetylcholine.
Else if hedgehog_score is less than or equal to 1, then perform the following actions.
    Set domain to p3.
    Set class_name to V3.
    Set transmitter to glutamate.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Display the error message that the hedgehog score must lie between 0 and 1 to the console.
End the conditional block.
Display domain to the console.
Display class_name to the console.
Display transmitter to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because a single ordered table plus a linear scan is the clearest possible expression of a gradient being read as thresholds, and a coding agent can insert a new domain without touching the logic.

# Reading one sonic hedgehog gradient as five discrete ventral identities.
VENTRAL = [   # (domain, upper bound of Shh score, class, transmitter, axon, headline job)
    ("p0",  0.35, "V0", "V0d glycine/GABA, V0v glutamate, V0c acetylcholine",
     "commissural, V0c ipsilateral", "left-right alternation and motoneuron gain"),
    ("p1",  0.50, "V1", "glycine and GABA", "ipsilateral descending",
     "recurrent and reciprocal inhibition, burst termination, locomotor speed"),
    ("p2",  0.65, "V2", "V2a glutamate, V2b glycine and GABA", "ipsilateral",
     "left-right stability and flexor-extensor alternation"),
    ("pMN", 0.80, "motor neuron", "acetylcholine", "leaves the cord via the ventral root",
     "muscle contraction"),
    ("p3",  1.01, "V3", "glutamate", "mostly commissural",
     "rhythm robustness and gait stability"),
]
TF_CODE = {"V0": "Dbx1 then Evx1", "V1": "En1", "V2": "Lhx3 with Chx10 or Gata3",
           "motor neuron": "Olig2 then Isl1 and Hb9", "V3": "Nkx2-2 then Sim1"}

def specify_ventral(shh_score):
    if not 0.0 <= shh_score <= 1.0:
        raise ValueError("sonic hedgehog score must lie between 0.0 and 1.0")
    for domain, ceiling, cls, tx, axon, job in VENTRAL:
        if shh_score < ceiling:
            return domain, cls, tx, axon, job
    raise AssertionError("unreachable: the table covers the whole range")

for score in (0.10, 0.40, 0.58, 0.72, 0.95):     # dorsal-most to floor-plate-most
    domain, cls, tx, axon, job = specify_ventral(score)
    print(f"Shh {score:0.2f} -> {domain} -> {cls} [{TF_CODE[cls]}]")
    print(f"    transmitter: {tx}")
    print(f"    axon: {axon};  job: {job}")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Four lineages, four transcription factors, four jobs, and a rule you can hold in your head: V0 crosses the midline, V1 inhibits close to home, V2 runs down its own side, and V3 crosses to steady the whole machine.

Stain one section for Dbx1, En1, Chx10 and Sim1 and the ventral cord resolves into four clean bands stacked in the order they were painted by the gradient, a map that no amount of adult morphology or firing pattern could ever have revealed.

CHAPTER 178. V0 (Left-Right Alternating Neuron) (NON-COG)

Every step you take requires one leg to wait while the other swings, and the order to wait crosses the midline of your spinal cord along the axon of a V0 neuron.

V0 is a progenitor-domain code with no meaning to a reader.

Left-Right names the axis this lineage governs, because almost all of its members send axons across the midline and impose an out-of-phase relationship between the two sides of the body.

Alternating names the specific output, and the split within the class is itself instructive, since the inhibitory members carry alternation at slow speeds such as walking while the excitatory members carry it at fast speeds such as trotting.

Neuron marks it as a cell whose deletion is legible in gait, because removing the inhibitory members leaves a mouse that alternates only when running and removing the excitatory ones leaves one that hops as soon as it speeds up.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A V0 neuron is defined not by its shape but by a genetic switch thrown in the embryo, the gene Dbx1, short for developing brain homeobox 1.

Almost every cell that throws it sends its output fibre across the midline, and the family's job is left and right coordination during walking.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small many-armed cell body with four to eight dendrites, the short branching antennae that gather incoming signals.

One axon dives to the floor of the cord, crosses in the ventral commissure, the narrow fibre bridge under the central canal, then runs up and down the far side.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 10 to 20 micrometres across, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre, with the V0C subgroup larger at 20 to 30.

Dendritic fields span 150 to 400 micrometres, and crossed axons run two to eight segments, one to four centimetres in an adult.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• V0D, inhibitory, releasing glycine and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) directly onto motor neurons across the midline, and dominant at slow walking speeds.

• V0V, marked by the gene Evx1, excitatory, working across the midline through an intermediate inhibitory cell, and dominant at fast speeds.

• V0C, marked by the gene Pitx2, cholinergic, staying on its own side, and the source of the large C-bouton endings on motor neuron cell bodies.

• V0G, a small glutamatergic Pitx2 sibling of V0C with no assigned function yet.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Lamina VIII and medial lamina VII of the Rexed laminae, the ten layers of the spinal grey matter, with V0C partition cells in area X beside the central canal.

They run from the first cervical segment to the sacral segments, densest in the cervical and lumbar enlargements, and their defining interface is the ventral commissure itself, where an axon passes from one half of the nervous system into the other.

NUMBER:

Roughly 1 to 2 million in the human cord, about 0.5 to 1 percent of its approximately 200 million neurons.

Confidence is low, because the figure is scaled from mouse lineage counts and no human count exists; the firm bound is that V0 cells outnumber the roughly 200 thousand alpha motor neurons several times over and cannot be more than a few percent of the cord.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage near minus 60 millivolts, threshold near minus 45 millivolts, input resistance a high 200 to 600 megaohms.

During walking they fire in bursts locked to one phase of the step cycle, typically 5 to 40 spikes per second inside a burst.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from ipsilateral V2a and Shox2 rhythm cells, from descending reticulospinal fibres carrying the brainstem command to walk, and from muscle and skin afferents.

V0D endings land directly on contralateral motor neurons, V0V endings on contralateral inhibitory interneurons, and V0C endings on ipsilateral motor neuron somata through M2 muscarinic receptors.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Diameter 0.5 to 2 micrometres, conducting at 1 to 10 metres per second, so a crossing signal takes a few milliseconds.

Most branch after crossing into ascending and descending limbs, so one cell speaks to several segments.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

V0D releases glycine with GABA, both opening chloride channels that push a target away from firing.

V0V and V0G release glutamate onto AMPA receptors, named for alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid, and NMDA receptors, named for N-methyl-D-aspartate; V0C releases acetylcholine.

MYELINATION:

Bare inside grey matter, thinly wrapped once in the ventral funiculus by oligodendrocytes, the glia that make the fatty insulating sheath.

The thin sheath matches the modest conduction velocities measured.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and rhythmic, dominated by the sodium and potassium pump burning adenosine triphosphate (ATP) to repay the ions spent on each spike.

Bursting once or twice a second is far cheaper than firing continuously.

PLASTICITY:

Crossed connections are pruned by use in the first weeks of life, which is why newborn stepping is clumsy.

After spinal cord injury commissural pathways sprout into detour circuits, and V0C C-boutons enlarge in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS).

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes in lamina VIII carry glycine transporter 1 and so set how long V0D inhibition lingers, a genuinely local specialisation because glial glycine handling barely exists in the brain above.

Ventral commissure oligodendrocytes wrap an unusually dense crossing bundle in a tiny volume and are early casualties in contusion injury, which is why crossed coordination is often lost before strength is.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete V0 and the two halves of the cord stop negotiating.

Mice without Dbx1 neurons hop like rabbits, both hind legs together, because nothing tells one side to wait.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

V0C cells are electrically coupled through connexin 36, a protein forming direct pores between neighbours, so they act partly as one sheet.

Their C-bouton is not a plain synapse: the motor neuron builds a subsurface cistern opposite it packed with Kv2.1 and calcium-activated potassium channels, retuning the target rather than merely pushing it.

The handover from V0D to V0V as speed rises is a computation performed by the population, not by any cell.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: ipsilateral rhythm drive from V2a and Shox2, 0 to 1 normalised; reticulospinal drive, 0 to 1; afferent input, 0 to 100 spikes per second.

• OUTPUTS: V0D inhibitory conductance onto contralateral motor neurons, 0 to 5 nanosiemens; V0V excitatory conductance onto contralateral inhibitory cells; V0C muscarinic gain on ipsilateral motor neurons, multiplying output by 1.0 to 1.6.

• STATE: membrane voltage; adaptation level; crossed terminal depression; the speed estimate weighting V0D against V0V.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 60 millivolts; threshold minus 45 millivolts; input resistance 200 to 600 megaohms; crossing delay 1 to 5 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 30 milliseconds; adaptation 200 to 600 milliseconds; muscarinic action 0.1 to several seconds; step cycle 0.4 to 2 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: loss produces a hopping synchronous gait; partial loss produces alternation that drifts in and out as speed changes; in ALS the V0C interface enlarges and misregulates motor neuron excitability.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide how strongly a V0 population inhibits the opposite side of the cord on this time step.
Create a number variable called ipsilateralDrive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called locomotorSpeed with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called dorsalWeight with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called ventralWeight with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called crossedInhibition with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called delayBuffer with initial value empty list.
Set ipsilateralDrive to the excitation arriving from V2a and reticulospinal sources.
Set locomotorSpeed to the normalised step frequency between 0 and 1.
Set dorsalWeight to the difference of 1 and locomotorSpeed.
Set ventralWeight to locomotorSpeed.
Set crossedInhibition to the product of ipsilateralDrive and the sum of dorsalWeight and the product of 0.9 and ventralWeight.
Add crossedInhibition to the end of delayBuffer.
Check if the total number of items in delayBuffer is greater than 3.
If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set crossedInhibition to the item at position 0 in delayBuffer.
    Remove the item at position 0 from delayBuffer.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set crossedInhibition to 0.
End the conditional block.
Return crossedInhibition to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: the V0 story is a two body problem and Python shows every line of
# the update with no library at all.
import math

DT = 0.001  # seconds per step

def sigmoid(x):
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp(-x))

def crossed_weight(drive):
    v0v = sigmoid((drive - 0.5) * 12.0)   # V0V takes over as speed rises
    return (1.0 - v0v) + 0.9 * v0v        # V0D carries the slow end

def run(drive=0.4, seconds=4.0):
    left, right = 0.6, 0.0    # half centre activity on each side, 0 to 1
    al, ar = 0.0, 0.0         # spike frequency adaptation on each side
    w = crossed_weight(drive)
    for i in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        il = drive - w * right - al
        ir = drive - w * left - ar
        left += DT / 0.050 * (-left + sigmoid(8.0 * il))
        right += DT / 0.050 * (-right + sigmoid(8.0 * ir))
        al += DT / 0.400 * (-al + 1.2 * left)
        ar += DT / 0.400 * (-ar + 1.2 * right)
        if i % 200 == 0:
            print("t=%.2f s  left=%.3f  right=%.3f" % (i * DT, left, right))

run()

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A V0 is the cell whose axon you can watch cross the floor of the cord under the central canal, carrying an order from one half of the body to the other, with its ancestry readable from one gene switch thrown before birth.

APPENDIX 1. THE THREE-STAGE PROVING SYSTEM OF PREVIOUSLY UNNAMED CONSTRUCTS (PUC)


A1.1 Why the Names Must Be Proved

Eleven times in this work, a construct has been marked with the tag (PUC), meaning Previously Unnamed Construct, and given a name.

That is a strong act, and it carries a burden.

To claim that a thing is real, is used, and yet has never been named is to make three assertions at once, and each of them can be wrong.

The thing might not be real.

Clustering algorithms produce clusters whether or not nature put a boundary there, and a great deal of what looks like a cell type is a cut through a continuum, drawn where the analyst set a parameter.

It might already have a name that the author overlooked.

This is the commonest and most embarrassing failure, and it is the reason the second stage below carries the heaviest burden of the three.

Or the proposed name might be a poor one that the field is right to reject.

A responsible naming, therefore, is not a christening but a proof.

This appendix supplies that proof system, and the appendices that follow it apply the system to each Previously Unnamed Construct named in this volume.

No construct earns its name by assertion.

Each must survive the three stages.

The naming is never a claim of discovery.

Every construct named in this work was already real, already relied upon, already published in parts.

What was missing was the word, and a science cannot reason efficiently about a thing it cannot name.

The proving system is the discipline that ensures a proposed word deserves to exist.


A1.2 The Three Stages

Every construct is evaluated against three stages, in order.

A construct that fails any stage does not earn its name.

STAGE 1: CONCEPTUAL PRECISION.

The construct must be demonstrably real and currently relied upon in the neuroscience literature.

It must be physically or functionally distinct from every adjacent named construct, not merely a paraphrase of something already named.

And it must be functionally necessary, playing a specific causal role whose existence is documented, rather than being a convenience of description.

In short: the thing must exist, must be distinct, and must matter.

Applied to a cell type rather than to a brain region, this stage is sharpened into three specific tests.

• The convergence test, for reality.

Do independent methods agree that this population exists?

A cluster that appears only in gene-expression data, and that no anatomist, physiologist or connectivity study has ever separated from its neighbours, fails.

A population that appears as a distinct cluster in sequencing, a distinct shape under a microscope, a distinct firing pattern under an electrode, and a distinct projection target in a tracing study has passed the strongest test the field can apply.

• The boundary test, for distinctness.

Which named cell type is this one most likely to be confused with, and what specifically separates them?

The answer must be stated, not assumed.

• The ablation test, for necessity.

If this population is silenced, deleted, or lost to disease, what specifically fails?

Evidence that a cell is merely active during a behaviour is not enough.

Something must break.

STAGE 2: IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP, AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS.

The construct must lack an accepted, widely used proper name in the existing literature.

The burden here is high, and it is placed on the person claiming the gap rather than on the reader.

Informal descriptive phrases, pedagogical metaphors, and passing usages in single papers do not count as proper names.

An established proper name is one used consistently across the literature, appearing in standard textbooks, understood without further definition by the relevant community.

If any such name exists, even an imperfect or contested one, the construct does not qualify, and it is cited as a named concept instead.

For cell types one further clarification is needed, and it is the clarification that makes this exercise possible.

A label is not a name.

None of the following counts as an accepted proper name, however consistently it is used.

• A gene symbol, or a combination of gene symbols, such as Sst Chodl or Lamp5 Lhx6.

A gene symbol names a gene, designates the cell by a correlate, and changes when a better marker is found.

• An alphanumeric cluster identifier from any atlas or taxonomy, such as subcluster 0417 or MLI2.

• An anatomical coordinate, such as layer 6b or lamina II inner.

• A description of the method by which the cell was found, or of how it looks on a plot.

• A phrase that varies from paper to paper, so that two papers about the same cell cannot be retrieved by the same search.

An eponym, meaning a scientist's surname, does count as a proper name, and disqualifies a candidate.

A long-established functional term that appears in textbooks and is understood without definition, such as silent nociceptor or intrinsic primary afferent neuron, also counts and also disqualifies.

Each construct must state, by name, the strongest counter-candidate term and precisely why that term does not clear the bar.

STAGE 3: PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY.

The proposed name must be self-explanatory to an informed reader, must accurately describe the construct's identity and function, and must be more useful for teaching, research and clinical application than the current unnamed state.

Names that are opaque, misleading, excessively long, or redundant with existing terminology do not pass.

The name must earn its place.

Every name proposed in this work obeys six rules.

• Plain English, with no Latin, no Greek, no eponym, no gene symbol, no acronym and no alphanumeric string.

• The name tells the story of the cell's purpose, function and faculty, so that a first-year student meeting the name alone learns something true.

• Two to five words, ending in the word neuron or cell.

• Dignified enough to print in a journal and plain enough for a curious teenager.

• Not redundant with, and not confusable with, any existing neuron name, nor with the other names proposed alongside it.

• Compound modifiers hyphenated, so that the reader parses the name correctly on first reading.


A1.3 The Additional Considerations: Ten Properties of a Sound Construct

The three stages are the minimum.

To hold each construct to the maximum, each is also examined against ten properties, drawn from the qualities that distinguish a genuine construct from a mere label.

These are stated once here and then reported briefly for each construct.

REALITY: the construct denotes something that physically or functionally exists, not a modelling artefact.

NECESSITY: the construct is required to reason about the domain; remove it and something true becomes unsayable.

CONCEPTUAL PRECISION AND SPECIFICITY: the construct picks out one thing exactly, with sharp boundaries, not a fuzzy region.

IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS: there is a demonstrable hole in the field's vocabulary that this construct fills, and no accepted proper name already occupies it.

PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY: the name teaches; a student meeting it learns something true about the construct from the name alone.

DIRECTIONALITY: what the cell takes in, and where it sends its output, is definite and stated, not ambiguous.

HIERARCHICAL POSITION: the construct sits at a definite layer of the fifteen-layer framework, and its relation to the layers above and below it is specified.

Every construct named in this work sits at Layer 6, the Cellular Layer, and each entry states the Layer 8 microcircuit motif it participates in and the Layer 9 region or pathway it helps to build.

UNIVERSALITY WITHIN ITS CLASS: the construct is not a one-off; it is an instance of a recognisable class, and the class is populated.

THEORETICAL COMPLETENESS IN HIERARCHICAL MODELS: naming the construct closes a gap that a complete hierarchical model of the nervous system would otherwise leave open.

CLINICAL CONSEQUENCE: a person is different because of it, whether through its loss, its excess, or a drug in current use that acts upon it.

For brevity, where a property is fully satisfied and needs no elaboration, it is marked as satisfied; where it requires comment, the comment is given.

A construct for which a property genuinely does not apply is marked (NA), meaning Not Applicable, and is always accompanied by the reason for its non-applicability, so that a reader never mistakes a property that does not apply for one that was merely omitted.


A1.4 How These Eleven Were Selected

The eleven Previously Unnamed Constructs named in this work were not chosen.

They are what survived.

Two sweeps were run across the whole of neuroscience, the first across twenty territories and the second across eighteen more, and together they returned four hundred and sixty-three candidate cell types, which merged into two hundred and twenty-nine distinct constructs.

Forty-six of those were carried into the proving system.

Each of the forty-six was then attacked by two independent skeptics working separately, neither of whom saw the other's report.

The first was instructed to refute the claim of namelessness, to search the standard textbooks, the review literature, the Petilla terminology, the Cell Ontology and the major brain atlases, and to reject the construct if it found any qualifying proper name, or if it was merely uncertain.

The second was instructed to refute the claim of reality, distinctness and necessity, to look for clustering artefacts, arbitrary cuts through continua, single-laboratory findings that failed to replicate, and rebrandings of things already known, and to demand causal evidence rather than correlation.

A construct passed only if both skeptics tried and failed.

Eleven survived.

Thirty-five did not.

Twenty-one of the thirty-five failed because a proper name already existed and the proposer had not found it, which is the single most important practical lesson of the exercise: the hard part of claiming that something is unnamed is not the claiming, it is the looking.

The appendices that follow apply the three stages, and the ten additional considerations, to each Previously Unnamed Construct that appears in this volume.


APPENDIX 2. Hush-Lifting Learning Neuron, proved. (From CHAPTER 126.)

WHAT IT NAMES.

The Hush-Lifting Learning Neuron is a tiny cell in the molecular layer, the outer sheet of the cerebellum, the brain structure that calibrates movement.

It is star-shaped, sits at every depth of that sheet, is one in four of the sheet's short-range cells, or interneurons, and speaks with gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main quieting signal.

It aims not at the Purkinje cell, the cerebellar cortex's output cell, but at the interneurons quieting that Purkinje cell.

By quieting the quieteners it lifts a brake, raising Purkinje firing and letting calcium flood the Purkinje cell's dendrites, its branching input arms.

That calcium is the permission slip for long-term depression, the lasting weakening of connections that stores a corrected movement.

WHAT THE LITERATURE CALLS IT NOW.

The standard label, MLI2 (molecular layer interneuron type 2), is a cluster identifier, minted when a computer sorted gene profiles into numbered groups.

"Nxph1-positive MLI" and "Gjd2-negative MLI" are gene-symbol barcodes, and the Cell Ontology entry CL:4307012 welds a gene symbol to a cluster number.

The one functional label, "disinhibitory MLI", is an unstable phrase, elsewhere "disinhibitory interneuron" or "MLI-targeting MLI".

STAGE 1, CONCEPTUAL PRECISION.

The cell is real, found twice by methods sharing no assumptions.

Kozareva and colleagues (Nature, 2021) sequenced 611,034 mouse cerebellar nuclei, found two non-overlapping interneuron groups, and confirmed the split in intact tissue, where the marker genes proved "entirely mutually exclusive".

Lackey and colleagues (Neuron, 2024) recorded from cell pairs blind to their genes and found the same proportion in the wiring alone, three quarters aiming at Purkinje cells, one quarter at interneurons.

It is distinct from its neighbour type 1, which is joined to its own kind by gap junctions, direct electrical bridges, in 67 percent of tested pairs, while no pair involving this cell coupled at all, and the two push Purkinje firing in opposite directions.

It is also functionally necessary.

Park and colleagues (Nature Neuroscience, 2026), working independently in Korea and Colorado, cut inhibition between molecular layer interneurons and found that "disruption of MLI-to-MLI inhibition prevents CF-instructed motor learning".

No tool yet silences this cell alone, so necessity stands for the pathway it alone supplies.

STAGE 2, IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP, AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS.

Every neighbour has a textbook name: the Purkinje cell, granule cell, Golgi cell, basket cell, stellate cell, Lugaro cell and candelabrum cell.

What is unnamed is the one cell whose job is to inhibit an inhibitor.

The strongest counter-candidate, "stellate cell", fails on scope.

All six reconstructed cells of this type "had classic stellate cell morphologies", but so do type 1 cells in the outer two thirds of the layer, so the term names a superset.

That classical mapping is disputed (Schilling, 2023), evidence that no classical name ever attached.

The gap is genuine.

STAGE 3, PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY.

Brake names what is acted upon, the standing inhibition neighbouring interneurons hold against the Purkinje cell.

Lifting names the action, and teaches the hardest idea, that this cell helps by subtraction, removing a restraint rather than adding a push.

Lesson names the purpose, since the brake lifts exactly when an error signal arrives along a climbing fibre, and only then does the error stick.

Neuron marks it as one cell rather than a circuit.

The insight from the name alone is that a brain withholds learning by default and grants it deliberately, so instruction and permission are separate signals.

That unlocks motor calibration, by which a clumsy first attempt becomes a smooth one.

ADDITIONAL CONSIDERATIONS.

• Reality.

Satisfied: two laboratories on two continents, five methods, one discrete population.

• Necessity.

Satisfied at pathway level: cutting interneuron-to-interneuron inhibition abolishes climbing-fibre-instructed motor learning; cell-selective silencing is still outstanding.

• Conceptual precision and specificity.

Satisfied: fixed by its target, by the absence of electrical coupling, and by raising rather than lowering Purkinje firing.

• Identification of a gap and proof of namelessness.

Satisfied: every label in use is a cluster index, a gene symbol, or a phrase reworded from paper to paper.

• Pedagogical clarity.

Satisfied: the name gives mechanism, a brake is lifted, and purpose, a lesson is allowed.

• Directionality.

It takes in glutamate, the main excitatory messenger, spilling from climbing fibres, and outputs onto type 1 interneurons, whose output lands on the Purkinje cell.

• Hierarchical position.

Layer 6, the Cellular Layer, of the fifteen-layer framework; at Layer 8 the serial disinhibition motif, one inhibitory cell inhibiting another; at Layer 9 the olivocerebellar error pathway; Layer 10 makes cerebellar learning gated rather than automatic, and Layer 12 shows failed motor adaptation.

• Universality within its class.

Satisfied: found in every lobule and depth in mouse at a ratio near one in four, and recovered in human tissue by gene expression.

• Theoretical completeness in hierarchical models.

Satisfied: supervised-learning accounts assume the error signal arrives unopposed, and this cell is the permission step they omit.

• Clinical consequence.

None is yet known: NXPH1 is not an ataxia gene, no postmortem study implicates this cell, and all work so far is in the mouse.

SOURCES FOR THIS CONSTRUCT.

• Kozareva and colleagues, A transcriptomic atlas of mouse cerebellar cortex comprehensively defines cell types, Nature, 2021.

• Lackey and colleagues, Specialized connectivity of molecular layer interneuron subtypes leads to disinhibition and synchronous inhibition of cerebellar Purkinje cells, Neuron, 2024.

• Santos-Valencia and colleagues, Climbing fibres recruit disinhibition to enhance Purkinje cell calcium signals, Nature, 2026.

• Park and colleagues, Synchronous climbing fiber activity enables instructive signaling for cerebellar learning through modulation of disinhibitory circuits, Nature Neuroscience, 2026.

• Schilling, Revisiting the development of cerebellar inhibitory interneurons in the light of single-cell genetic analyses, Histochemistry and Cell Biology, 2023.


APPENDIX 3. Body-Alarm Broadcast Neuron, proved. (From CHAPTER 157.)

WHAT IT NAMES.

This neuron lives in the upper brainstem, the stalk joining brain to spinal cord, in a thin shell called the external lateral subnucleus of the parabrachial nucleus.

Its messenger is glutamate, the brain's main excitatory signal, and it also makes a peptide, meaning a short chain of amino acids, called calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP).

It sends output forward into the emotional forebrain, most densely to the central nucleus of the amygdala, and also to the extended amygdala, basal forebrain and visceral thalamus (Huang and colleagues, 2021).

It collects signals that all mean the body is in trouble, including pain, heat, itch, blood-borne bacterial toxin and rising carbon dioxide, and fires harder as the danger grows (Campos and colleagues, 2018).

That firing reaches many forebrain targets at once as one warning, which ends the meal, freezes the animal, writes an avoidance memory and wakes it from sleep.

WHAT THE LITERATURE CALLS IT NOW.

The working labels, verbatim, are "parabrachial CGRP neurons", "CGRP-PBN neurons", "PBel-CGRP neurons" and "Calca/CGRP-expressing parabrachial neurons".

Each is a gene-symbol barcode bolted onto an anatomical coordinate, since CGRP is the protein made by the gene Calca, while PBN means parabrachial nucleus and PBel its external lateral part.

Atlas work adds cluster identifiers such as "N15" and "N16", while "a general alarm" is an unstable descriptive phrase used as a review title, never as the cell's designation (Palmiter, 2018).

STAGE 1, CONCEPTUAL PRECISION.

The cell is real, not a statistical illusion, because it was seen by antibody staining in the 1980s, mapped in human post-mortem brainstem by de Lacalle and Saper in 2000, and confirmed since by mouse genetics and anatomical tracing.

It is distinct from the cell most easily confused with it, the neighbouring Foxp2-marked parabrachial neuron, which meets the same rising carbon dioxide by adjusting breathing rather than raising an alarm (Yang and colleagues, 2024).

It is functionally necessary, because when Kaur and colleagues silenced these cells and raised carbon dioxide around a sleeping mouse, the animal failed to wake on half of all trials.

STAGE 2, IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP, AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS.

What is named is the neighbourhood, not the cell: the parabrachial nucleus has a name, and one subdivision even carries an eponym, the Koelliker-Fuse nucleus.

What is not named is the cell itself, and the absence is formal: a direct query of the Cell Ontology returns zero terms for this population.

The strongest counter-candidate is "parabrachial CGRP neuron", which, unusually, is genuinely retrievable across independent laboratories.

It still fails three times over: it is a gene product plus a map reference, which the standard excludes; "CGRP neuron" alone is ambiguous, since about half of all trigeminal sensory neurons also make CGRP; and the peptide is not the working part, because mice lacking it still learn taste aversion (Zajdel and colleagues, 2021).

The gap is genuine.

STAGE 3, PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY.

"Body" teaches where the signal comes from, the flesh and organs rather than the distant world.

"Alarm" teaches what kind of signal it is, a warning that harm is happening, with urgency but no detail.

"Broadcast" teaches the circuit's shape, since many kinds of bad news funnel into one small group of cells that sends one message to many destinations.

The one insight a student gains is that the brain has cells whose job is not to report what is wrong, only that something is wrong, loudly and to everyone at once.

That insight unlocks obstructive sleep apnoea, in which the throat closes during sleep and the sleeper must be jolted awake by rising carbon dioxide.

ADDITIONAL CONSIDERATIONS.

• Reality.

Satisfied, since antibody staining showed the cell in human tissue decades before transcriptomic clustering existed.

• Necessity.

Satisfied, since silencing it stops a sleeping mouse waking to carbon dioxide and abolishes learned taste aversion.

• Conceptual precision and specificity.

Satisfied, since the population is compact and separable from the breathing-control Foxp2 cells beside it.

• Identification of a gap and proof of namelessness.

Satisfied, since forty years of study produced only gene-plus-coordinate labels and no Cell Ontology term.

• Pedagogical clarity.

Satisfied, since each modifier teaches something different: source, message type, and circuit shape.

• Directionality.

It takes in bodily danger signals from the spinal cord and the nucleus of the solitary tract, and sends output to the amygdala, basal forebrain and thalamus.

• Hierarchical position.

Layer 6, the Cellular Layer, of the fifteen-layer framework.

◦ At Layer 8 it forms a converge-then-diverge motif, many danger channels collapsing onto one hub that fans out again.

◦ At Layer 9 it builds the ascending interoceptive alarm pathway from brainstem to extended amygdala.

◦ At Layer 10 and Layer 12 the consequence is a defensive whole-body state and a shifted threshold for waking.

• Universality within its class.

Partly satisfied, since it occurs in mouse, rat and human, but it is a molecular class rather than one type.

• Theoretical completeness in hierarchical models.

Satisfied, since it supplies the single-cell substrate that circuit models of threat and arousal assumed.

• Clinical consequence.

No human genetic or lesion evidence yet implicates this cell, and the anti-CGRP migraine antibodies do not test it, since they barely enter the brain.

SOURCES FOR THIS CONSTRUCT.

• Palmiter, The parabrachial nucleus: CGRP neurons function as a general alarm, Trends in Neurosciences, 2018.

• Campos and colleagues, Encoding of danger by parabrachial CGRP neurons, Nature, 2018.

• Kaur and colleagues, A genetically defined circuit for arousal from sleep during hypercapnia, Neuron, 2017.

• Zajdel and colleagues, Calcitonin gene related peptide alpha is dispensable for many danger-related motivational responses, Scientific Reports, 2021.

• Huang and colleagues, Efferent projections of CGRP/Calca-expressing parabrachial neurons in mice, Journal of Comparative Neurology, 2021.


APPENDIX 4. Poison-Watch Nausea Neuron, proved. (From CHAPTER 158.)

WHAT IT NAMES.

This neuron lives in the area postrema, a small swelling on the floor of the fourth ventricle, low in the brainstem.

The area postrema lacks a blood-brain barrier, the seal that keeps blood chemicals out of brain tissue, so this cell bathes in circulating blood.

It is excitatory, signalling with glutamate, the brain's main "go" messenger, and carrying docking proteins for blood-borne sickness signals: the glucagon-like peptide 1 receptor (GLP1R), and the receptor for growth differentiation factor 15 (GDF15), called GFRAL.

Its fibres run forward onto the calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) alarm neurons of the parabrachial nucleus.

When it fires, an animal feels sick, stops eating, and afterwards refuses whatever flavour it tasted before falling ill.

WHAT THE LITERATURE CALLS IT NOW.

The working labels, verbatim, are "GLP1R neurons", "GFRAL neurons", "SLC6A2 neurons" and "excitatory clusters 1 to 4".

The first three are gene-symbol barcodes, naming receptor genes also switched on in gut and pancreas, so none names this neuron.

"Cluster 2" to "cluster 4" are alphanumeric cluster identifiers from one atlas, used nowhere outside the originating laboratory.

"Area postrema neuron" is an anatomical coordinate covering seven cell types, some doing the opposite job.

STAGE 1, CONCEPTUAL PRECISION.

The cell is real: single-cell ribonucleic acid (RNA) sequencing by Zhang and colleagues resolved it, independent sequencing by Dowsett, Lam and Yeo recovered the same populations, and two-photon imaging of living mice by Huang and colleagues confirmed it physiologically.

It is most easily confused with its neighbour, the amylin-receptor excitatory neuron labelled CALCR.

The separation is behavioural: activating GLP1R, GFRAL or SLC6A2 neurons produced learned flavour avoidance, while identical activation of CALCR neurons produced none.

It is functionally necessary: killing the area postrema GLP1R population with a targeted toxin abolished learned avoidance to three unrelated poisons, exendin-4, lithium chloride and bacterial lipopolysaccharide.

Avoidance of cisplatin survived, so the cell is required for a wide but not unlimited class of threats.

STAGE 2, IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP, AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS.

What is named here is the place and the phenomenon: the area postrema, the chemoreceptor trigger zone, nausea, and the downstream parabrachial CGRP alarm neuron.

What is not named is the cell that reads poison out of the blood and turns it into sickness and one-trial food avoidance.

The strongest counter-candidate, "chemoreceptor trigger zone", fails twice: it names a region, not a cell, and fails on coverage, because that zone also holds inhibitory GIPR neurons that suppress nausea.

"Noradrenergic cell group A2" is an alphanumeric identifier spanning the whole dorsal vagal complex and covering only the noradrenaline-making subgroup.

No eponym and no textbook functional term for these cells appeared in any source retrieved.

The gap is genuine.

STAGE 3, PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY.

"Blood" teaches what sets this cell apart, that it touches circulating blood directly, because the usual barrier is absent here.

"Sampling" teaches its job, continuous chemical surveillance rather than a reply to a nerve signal.

"Nausea" teaches what it produces, the queasy, food-refusing state that protects an animal that has swallowed poison.

"Neuron" teaches that a nerve cell, not a gland, does this sensing.

The insight from the name alone is that the brain keeps a deliberate hole in its own defences, staffed by cells that taste the blood and raise sickness.

That insight unlocks the nausea driving people off weight-loss drugs, the wasting of cancer cachexia, and the vomiting of pregnancy.

ADDITIONAL CONSIDERATIONS.

• Reality.

Satisfied: two independent sequencing atlases plus imaging in behaving mice.

• Necessity.

Satisfied: toxin ablation abolishes learned poison avoidance, and silencing GFRAL-bearing cells attenuates cancer cachexia.

• Conceptual precision and specificity.

Satisfied: the glutamate-releasing, GLP1R-bearing, aversion-driving population, excluding the non-aversive CALCR neuron and the inhibitory GIPR neuron.

• Identification of a gap and proof of namelessness.

Satisfied: every source retrieved uses gene symbols, cluster numbers or the region name only.

• Pedagogical clarity.

Satisfied: the name states where the cell looks and what it produces.

• Directionality.

It takes in blood-borne chemicals through windowed capillaries, including bacterial toxins, drugs and the hormones GDF15 and glucagon-like peptide 1.

It sends glutamate output to the parabrachial nucleus and solitary tract nucleus.

• Hierarchical position.

Layer 6, the Cellular Layer, of the fifteen-layer framework.

Its Layer 8 motif is a locally gated sensory relay, a neighbouring inhibitory neuron vetoing the sensor before its message leaves the region.

Its Layer 9 pathway is the area postrema to parabrachial interoceptive alarm route.

Layer 10 brings a whole-body sickness state, and Layer 12 one-trial flavour learning.

• Universality within its class.

Satisfied with a caveat: the population appears in rat and macaque atlases too, but no human atlas was retrieved.

• Theoretical completeness in hierarchical models.

Satisfied: models of interoception, the sensing of the body's internal state, assume a blood-reading first sensor but never name it.

• Clinical consequence.

Satisfied: it is the presumed source of the nausea limiting glucagon-like peptide 1 drugs and of GDF15-driven wasting treatable with ponsegromab.

Destruction of the region in people produces intractable nausea, vomiting and hiccups.

SOURCES FOR THIS CONSTRUCT.

• Zhang, Kaye, Cai, Wang, Prescott and Liberles, Area Postrema Cell Types that Mediate Nausea-Associated Behaviors, Neuron, 2021.

• Zhang, Vincelette, Reimann and Liberles, A Brainstem Circuit for Nausea Suppression, Cell Reports, 2022.

• Dowsett, Lam and Yeo, A Survey of the Mouse Hindbrain in the Fed and Fasted States Using Single-Nucleus RNA Sequencing, Molecular Metabolism, 2021.

• Huang and colleagues, Dissociable Hindbrain GLP1R Circuits for Satiety and Aversion, Nature, 2024.

• Sun and colleagues, Area Postrema Neurons Mediate Interleukin-6 Function in Cancer Cachexia, Nature Communications, 2024.

• Groarke and colleagues, Ponsegromab for the Treatment of Cancer Cachexia, New England Journal of Medicine, 2024.


APPENDIX 5. Dream-Sleep Stillness Neuron, proved. (From CHAPTER 159.)

WHAT IT NAMES.

This cell lives in a small patch of the pons, the bulge of brainstem below the midbrain, called the sublaterodorsal tegmental nucleus.

It is a projection neuron, a nerve cell whose output fibre travels far rather than staying local, and it signals with glutamate, the brain's main activity-raising messenger.

Its long descending fibres reach inhibitory relay cells in the medulla and interneurons beside the motor nerve cells of the spinal cord.

Those relays release glycine and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), quietening messengers that shut down the motor neurons driving muscle.

The result is the near-total limpness of the body during rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, the stage of vivid dreaming.

WHAT THE LITERATURE CALLS IT NOW.

The commonest labels, "sublaterodorsal tegmental nucleus (SLD) neurons", "subcoeruleus neurons" and "peri-locus coeruleus alpha neurons", are anatomical coordinates - addresses, not names.

"SLD Vglut2 neurons", "SLDGlut", "GluSLD" and "SubLDT Crhbp-positive neurons" are gene-symbol barcodes bolted onto an address.

"Pontine inhibitory area neurons", "dorsal pontine REM-on neurons" and "the REM-atonia generator" are unstable descriptive phrases, so two studies of one cell are not found by one search.

STAGE 1, CONCEPTUAL PRECISION.

The cell is real, because four independent laboratories reached it by four routes.

Chemical lesions of the region in rats produced REM sleep without atonia, dreaming sleep with muscle tone intact (Krenzer and colleagues, Boston, 2011).

Silencing glutamate release from these neurons alone cut REM sleep by thirty per cent and produced violent dream-enacting behaviour (Valencia Garcia and colleagues, Lyon, 2017).

Switching the cells on chemically produced instant atonia; switching them off produced dream enactment, in mice (Torontali and colleagues, Toronto, 2019).

Killing a molecularly defined subset reduced sleep and impaired REM atonia (Kashiwagi and colleagues, Japan, 2024).

The cell is distinct, and is most easily confused with the noradrenergic subcoeruleus, or A7, group next door, which shares half its name.

Those neighbours use noradrenaline and fall silent in REM sleep; these use glutamate and fire.

The cell is functionally necessary: silence its glutamate output and atonia fails, so the dreaming rat rears and lunges at nothing while the dream continues.

STAGE 2, IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP, AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS.

What is named is the address, the molecule and the disease.

The nucleus holds anatomy-ontology entries, the marker genes Slc17a6 and Crhbp are named, and the illness, REM sleep behaviour disorder, is named.

What is not named is the cell.

The Cell Ontology, the reference catalogue of cell types, holds no class for this neuron, and the textbooks are worse than silent: Purves attributes REM paralysis to unnamed "GABAergic neurons in the pontine reticular formation", the wrong transmitter.

The strongest counter-candidate is "REM-on neuron", genuinely textbook-established and used without definition.

It fails because it is a firing-pattern class covering acetylcholine-releasing, GABA-releasing and glutamate-releasing cells alike, naming a shape on a spike record, not a cell.

The gap is genuine.

STAGE 3, PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY.

"Body" teaches that the target is skeletal muscle, not the mind, which is left running.

"Stilling" teaches that the stillness is an active achievement, not a passive fading.

"Dream" teaches when it acts.

"Neuron" teaches that one class of nerve cell carries the whole job.

The insight from the name alone is that dreaming and moving are held apart by a dedicated cell, so the body is actively restrained while the mind runs free.

That insight unlocks REM sleep behaviour disorder, in which sleeping people punch, kick and leap from bed, the earliest visible sign of Parkinson's disease.

ADDITIONAL CONSIDERATIONS.

• Reality.

Satisfied, by lesion, gene silencing, chemical switching, fibre tracing and single-cell sequencing across four laboratories.

• Necessity.

Satisfied, since silencing their glutamate release alone abolishes REM atonia and produces dream enactment.

• Conceptual precision and specificity.

Satisfied for the descending, glutamate-releasing population, which the name separates from the GABA-releasing neurons of the same nucleus.

• Identification of a gap and proof of namelessness.

Satisfied, since no Cell Ontology class and no textbook term names the cell rather than the place.

• Pedagogical clarity.

Satisfied, since the three content words give target, action and state.

• Directionality.

It takes in excitatory pontine drive and release from REM-off inhibition by the ventrolateral periaqueductal grey, and sends output down to inhibitory relay cells of the medulla and spinal cord.

• Hierarchical position.

Layer 6, the Cellular Layer, of the fifteen-layer framework.

Its Layer 8 motif is state-gated feed-forward inhibition, a long-range excitatory command recruiting inhibitory interneurons to brake motor neurons, within the Layer 9 pontomedullary atonia pathway.

Layer 10 gains whole-brain state switching, and Layer 12 the separation of dream from deed.

• Universality within its class.

Satisfied in mammals, the cat, rat, mouse and human populations being homologous.

• Theoretical completeness in hierarchical models.

Satisfied, since no model otherwise explains why an active dreaming brain produces a motionless body.

• Clinical consequence.

Degeneration causes dream enactment, and in Parkinson's disease patients these human neurons are largely lost and carry pathological alpha-synuclein protein; clonazepam and melatonin help, but neither targets the cell.

SOURCES FOR THIS CONSTRUCT.

• Valencia Garcia and colleagues, Genetic inactivation of glutamate neurons in the rat sublaterodorsal tegmental nucleus recapitulates REM sleep behaviour disorder, Brain, 2017.

• Krenzer and colleagues, Brainstem and spinal cord circuitry regulating REM sleep and muscle atonia, PLOS One, 2011.

• Torontali and colleagues, The sublaterodorsal tegmental nucleus functions to couple brain state and motor activity during REM sleep and wakefulness, Current Biology, 2019.

• Kashiwagi and colleagues, A pontine-medullary loop crucial for REM sleep and its deficit in Parkinson's disease, Cell, 2024.

• St Louis and colleagues, Lesional REM sleep behavior disorder localizes to the dorsomedial pons, Neurology, 2014.
